### 02_doc_application_assistant_assistantController_h

<a id="02-doc-application-assistant-assistantcontroller-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/02_doc_application_assistant_assistantController_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Path traversal in `RunSingleAiCall` queue directory — no containment check on `subfolderName`

- **Location:** `AssistantController::RunSingleAiCall`, approximately where `queueDir = queueBase / subfolderName` is constructed
- **Issue:** `subfolderName` is assembled by the caller (`RunAiCallAsync`, `MakeToolAiCall`) from a timestamp and an atomic integer, so under normal operation it is safe. However, `RunSingleAiCall` is a `private` method that takes the subfolder as a plain `std::string` with no validation. Nothing verifies that `queueBase / subfolderName` canonicalises under `queueBase`. If a future caller (or a refactor) passes attacker-influenced content (e.g., a session ID embedded in the subfolder name, a tool name, or any user-supplied string), a `../`-prefixed value will escape the queue root and create or overwrite arbitrary files. Unlike `GetSession`, no `weakly_canonical` + `lexically_relative` containment check is performed here.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write anywhere the process has write permission; combined with the AI prompt pipeline, an attacker could overwrite config, credentials, or executable files.
- **Fix:** After constructing `queueDir`, perform the same canonical-path containment check used in `GetSession`: compute `weakly_canonical(queueBase)` and `weakly_canonical(queueDir)`, then assert the latter is lexically under the former. Return an error if the check fails. Alternatively, strip every path separator and `..` from `subfolderName` before use.

---

### [HIGH] `user_message` text field is unbounded — no per-field size cap

- **Location:** `AssistantController::OnMessage`, `user_message` branch, approximately the `std::string text = std::string(doc["text"].get_string().value())` line
- **Issue:** The 64 KB frame cap (`kMaxIncomingFrameBytes`) is applied to the entire serialised JSON frame. After parsing, the `text` field extracted from it is fed without further length checks into `HandleUserMessage`, then into session persistence (`AddUserMessage`), prompt assembly (`ContextAssembler::Assemble`), keyword extraction (`ExtractKeywords`), file-path extraction (`ExtractFilePaths`), and memory recall (`m_MemoryStore.GetRelevant`). A single text field that is close to 64 KB minus JSON envelope overhead will pass the frame check and trigger all of these allocations. Because the text is embedded verbatim into AI request files written to disk, it also consumes disk space proportional to the number of tool iterations (`MAX_TOOL_ITERATIONS = 10`), potentially writing up to ~640 KB per turn to the queue directory. The same issue applies to `command`, `args`, `sessionId`, `prefix`, and `kind` fields.
- **Impact:** A connected (authenticated) WebSocket client can exhaust RAM, disk, or CPU in prompt-assembly and keyword-tokenisation loops with a single message.
- **Fix:** Add per-field length limits after extraction (e.g., 8 KB for `text`, 256 bytes for `sessionId`, `command`, `kind`). Return a JSON error and return early when any field exceeds its limit.

---

### [HIGH] `HandleListSessions` loads every on-disk session to count turns — unbounded disk and memory amplification

- **Location:** `AssistantController::HandleListSessions`, the `GetSession(ids[i])` call inside the JSON-building loop
- **Issue:** `AssistantSession::ListSessions` enumerates all session files on disk. For each file not already in `m_Sessions`, `HandleListSessions` calls `GetSession`, which constructs a new `AssistantSession` that loads the full JSONL history into memory. This happens synchronously on the WebSocket handler thread for every disk-resident session. An attacker who has sent many messages (or who can create session files externally) can cause the server to load arbitrarily large histories into memory simply by sending a `list_sessions` frame.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion; server stall on the WebSocket I/O thread; potential OOM.
- **Fix:** Separate the concept of "list session IDs" from "load session history". For the listing endpoint, only return IDs and optionally a lightweight summary (e.g., last-modified timestamp from filesystem metadata) without loading full history. Apply a hard cap on the number of sessions returned (e.g., 100 most recent).

---

### [HIGH] `HandleLogCommand` reads a hardcoded relative path `log/log.txt` without containment verification

- **Location:** `AssistantController::HandleLogCommand`, `fs::path const logPath = "log/log.txt"` line
- **Issue:** While the path is hardcoded as a literal, the log content itself is returned verbatim to any connected WebSocket client without authentication checks. More critically, if the working directory can be influenced (e.g., by a symlink placed at `log/log.txt`), the command reads and exfiltrates an arbitrary file. The command is reachable via the `/log` slash command, which itself is accessible through `HandleCommand`, which is reachable from any `user_message` frame starting with `/log` — no access control is enforced anywhere in the WebSocket handler stack.
- **Impact:** Any WebSocket client (there is no authentication on the `/ws/assistant` endpoint visible in this code) can read up to 32 KB of the application log, which may contain API keys, internal paths, stack traces, or other sensitive data. If `log/log.txt` is a symlink, arbitrary file read.
- **Fix:** (1) Resolve and canonicalise `logPath` against an absolute, configured log directory and verify containment. (2) Add WebSocket-level authentication before any command or message is processed.

---

### [MEDIUM] No authentication or authorisation on the WebSocket endpoint

- **Location:** `AssistantController::OnOpen`, `AssistantController::OnMessage`
- **Issue:** `OnOpen` unconditionally adds any connecting client to `m_Clients`. `OnMessage` processes all message types (including tool approval, session history retrieval, log reading, workflow listing, memory access) without verifying the caller's identity. There is no token, session cookie, or other credential check visible in this file or referenced from it.
- **Impact:** Any network-reachable client can read session histories, memories, indexed file summaries, log contents, and trigger AI calls (incurring API costs). If the server is not separately firewalled, this is unauthenticated access to all assistant functionality.
- **Fix:** Validate an authentication token (passed as a query parameter on the WebSocket upgrade URL, or as the first message) in `OnOpen` or at the start of `OnMessage`. Reject and close unauthenticated connections before processing any message.

---

### [MEDIUM] `HandleGetHistory` loads all sessions from disk without a session count cap

- **Location:** `AssistantController::HandleGetHistory`, the loop over `sessionIds`
- **Issue:** `AssistantSession::ListSessions` returns all sessions on disk. The function iterates them in order, calling `GetSession` (which loads from disk) for each until `maxEntries` user-message entries are collected. With many sessions or sessions with few user messages, every session file is loaded. `maxEntries` is capped at 500 (correct), but the number of sessions loaded is not independently capped.
- **Impact:** With many small session files on disk, a single `get_history` request can cause the server to open and parse every session file, exhausting file descriptors and memory.
- **Fix:** Add an independent cap on the number of sessions iterated (e.g., 50 most recent sessions). The sessions list is already sorted newest-first, so this is a simple early-exit condition.

---

### [MEDIUM] Tool argument values are embedded unescaped into the `approvalDesc` string sent to the frontend

- **Location:** `AssistantController::RunAiCallAsync` (the lambda), inside the `needsApproval` branch, `approvalDesc` construction loop, approximately the `approvalDesc += k + "=\"" + v + "\""` line
- **Issue:** The `approvalDesc` string is built by concatenating raw tool argument values between `\"` delimiters, then passed to `RequestToolApproval` and inserted into a JSON string via `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString(description)`. The `EscapeJsonString` call does protect the outer JSON structure. However, if `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` does not sanitise all control characters or if tool argument values contain sequences that interact with the frontend's rendering (e.g., HTML/Markdown injection into the approval dialog), the displayed content could mislead the user into approving a different action than what is shown.
- **Impact:** Prompt injection via tool output could cause misleading approval dialogs (clickjacking-equivalent). The severity depends on how the frontend renders `description`.
- **Fix:** Ensure `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` covers all JSON special characters. For the human-readable `approvalDesc`, consider truncating argument values to a safe display length and stripping non-printable characters before display.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ExtractFilePaths` in `ValidateResponse` resolves user-influenced paths against the filesystem without rate-limiting

- **Location:** `AssistantController::ValidateResponse`, `ExtractFilePaths` call and subsequent `fs::exists` loop
- **Issue:** `ExtractFilePaths` tokenises the AI response text (which is attacker-influenced via crafted AI output or prompt injection) and extracts tokens containing `/` with known extensions. Each extracted path is resolved via `fs::path / resolved` and checked with `fs::exists`. There is no limit on the number of paths extracted or checked. A response containing hundreds of fake file paths causes hundreds of filesystem `stat` calls on the I/O thread.
- **Impact:** DoS via filesystem exhaustion or I/O saturation if an attacker can influence AI output (e.g., through prompt injection). Also, the `basePath / resolved` join is performed without a containment check: a path like `../../etc/passwd` would be probed (though only `exists` is called, not `open`).
- **Fix:** Cap `ExtractFilePaths` results to a small number (e.g., 20). Add a canonical-path containment check before calling `fs::exists` to ensure the resolved path is under the workspace root.

---

### [MEDIUM] Memory values are injected verbatim into AI prompt context (`memoryContext`)

- **Location:** `AssistantController::RunAiCallAsync` lambda, `memoryContext` construction loop: `memoryContext += "- [" + mem.key + "]: " + mem.value + "\n"`
- **Issue:** Recalled memory `key` and `value` strings are concatenated directly into the AI system context without any sanitisation or escaping relative to the prompt format. If an attacker previously stored a memory (via the `save_memory` tool) containing tool-call syntax (e.g., `<tool_call name="run_command">...</tool_call>`), that content will appear in the AI's context on a future turn and may be interpreted as a legitimate tool call by `ToolRegistry::ParseToolCalls`.
- **Impact:** Stored prompt injection: an attacker who can invoke `save_memory` (directly or indirectly) can plant content that triggers unauthorised tool execution on a future turn, potentially bypassing the approval gate if the injected call targets a non-approval tool.
- **Fix:** Apply `ToolRegistry::DefangToolMarkers` (already used on tool result output) to memory values before injecting them into the context. Similarly defang file summary content injected from `m_WorkspaceIndexer.GetRelevantFiles`.

---

### [MEDIUM] File summaries from `WorkspaceIndexer` are injected into AI context without defanging

- **Location:** `AssistantController::RunAiCallAsync` lambda, file summary injection block: `prompt.cntx += "- " + file.relativePath + ": " + file.summary + "\n"`
- **Issue:** File summaries are generated by a prior AI call (via `MakeToolAiCall`/`get_file_summary`) and cached. The cached summary text is injected verbatim into the conversation context. A file in the workspace whose content contains tool-call markers will produce a summary that, when injected, becomes a prompt-injection vector — the same class of issue as the memory injection finding above.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can place a crafted file in the indexed workspace can inject tool-call syntax into future AI turns through the summary cache, potentially triggering unauthorised tool execution.
- **Fix:** Apply `ToolRegistry::DefangToolMarkers` to `file.summary` and sanitise `file.relativePath` (strip `../` sequences) before injecting into the context.

---

### [LOW] `RandomHex` does not protect against integer overflow when `numBytes` is very large

- **Location:** `AssistantController::RandomHex` (assistantHelpers.cpp), `static_cast<int>(numBytes)` passed to `RAND_bytes`
- **Issue:** `numBytes` is a `std::size_t`. The cast to `int` for `RAND_bytes` wraps silently if `numBytes > INT_MAX`, causing `RAND_bytes` to be called with a negative length, which returns an error. The function then returns an empty string (correct fail-closed behaviour), but callers do not distinguish "RAND_bytes API misuse" from "entropy exhausted". In practice, all call sites pass `16`, so this is not currently exploitable, but it is a latent defect.
- **Impact:** Negligible at current call sites. If a future call site passes a large value, token generation silently fails rather than producing the expected token.
- **Fix:** Add `if (numBytes == 0 || numBytes > static_cast<std::size_t>(std::numeric_limits<int>::max())) return {};` before the `RAND_bytes` call.

---

### [LOW] `DrainPendingMessages` re-checks client liveness under a per-send lock acquisition — TOCTOU window

- **Location:** `AssistantController::DrainPendingMessages`, the per-client lock re-check and `client->send_text(batch)` call
- **Issue:** The code snapshots `m_Clients` under `m_ClientsMutex`, then for each client re-acquires `m_ClientsMutex` to verify the pointer is still in `m_Clients` before calling `send_text`. Between the re-check and `send_text`, the connection can be closed and the `crow::websocket::connection` object can be destroyed on another thread (ASIO's I/O thread). The re-check reduces the window but does not eliminate it; `send_text` is called without the mutex held.
- **Impact:** Potential use-after-free on the `crow::websocket::connection` pointer if the connection is torn down between the membership check and `send_text`. The `try/catch` around `send_text` may not be sufficient if the object has been deallocated.
- **Fix:** This is fundamentally a Crow framework lifetime question. Ensure `crow::websocket::connection` objects remain valid for the lifetime of registered callbacks (Crow typically guarantees this via shared ownership). If not guaranteed, hold a shared reference or use Crow's recommended send pattern. Document the assumed lifetime contract explicitly.


---

### 03_doc_application_assistant_assistantHelpers_h

<a id="03-doc-application-assistant-assistanthelpers-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/03_doc_application_assistant_assistantHelpers_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

### 04_doc_application_assistant_assistantMemory_h

<a id="04-doc-application-assistant-assistantmemory-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/04_doc_application_assistant_assistantMemory_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] `entry.id` loaded from disk without opaque-ID validation

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::LoadFromDiskLocked()`, the block that reads `obj["id"]` into `entry.id` (approximately the line `if (!obj["id"].get_string().get(sv)) entry.id = std::string(sv);`)
- **Issue:** The `id` field read from the on-disk JSON file is stored verbatim into `entry.id` with no call to `IsValidOpaqueId()`. The comment in `assistantHelpers.h` says `IsValidOpaqueId` "is used at every site where an attacker-influenced ID feeds into a filesystem path or audit-log substring," but this site is missing. A tampered or externally supplied JSON file can inject arbitrary bytes — including path separators, newlines, null bytes, and shell metacharacters — into `entry.id`. If `entry.id` is later used to build a filesystem path (e.g., per-entry sidecar files) or appears in a log line via a format string that is not sanitised, those bytes propagate.
- **Impact:** If the store file is writable by another process or user, or is sourced from an untrusted location, an attacker can store a crafted `id` that causes path traversal or log injection downstream. Even within this code, `LogSafeKey` is only applied to `key`; `id` values reach log macros unfiltered.
- **Fix:** After reading `entry.id`, validate with `IsValidOpaqueId(entry.id)`; skip or discard the entry (and log a warning) if validation fails, the same way `entry.key.empty()` causes a `continue`.

---

### [HIGH] `entry.createdAt` and `entry.sourceSessionId` loaded from disk without any validation or length cap

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::LoadFromDiskLocked()`, the lines assigning `entry.createdAt` and `entry.sourceSessionId`
- **Issue:** Neither field is length-clamped (unlike `key` and `value`) nor structurally validated before being stored. `createdAt` is expected to be an ISO-8601 timestamp (bounded ~25 chars) but can be an arbitrarily long string from a tampered file. `sourceSessionId` is an opaque ID that should pass `IsValidOpaqueId` but is stored raw. Both fields are later serialised back to disk via `SaveToDiskLocked` and may appear in log output. A multi-megabyte `createdAt` causes unbounded memory growth across all entries at load time.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / DoS if the file is large; log injection if newlines or control characters are embedded; bypasses the intent of `IsValidOpaqueId` for session IDs.
- **Fix:** Apply `ClampLen` to `createdAt` (e.g., 32 bytes is sufficient for ISO-8601) and apply `IsValidOpaqueId` to `sourceSessionId`, discarding entries that fail.

---

### [HIGH] `SaveToDiskLocked` writes atomically to the live path — interrupted write leaves a corrupt store

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::SaveToDiskLocked()`, the `std::ofstream` open with `std::ios::trunc`
- **Issue:** The file is opened with truncation before the new content is written. If the process is killed, loses power, or encounters an `ofs.write` error after truncation, the file is left empty or partially written. On the next startup `LoadFromDiskLocked` will either see an empty file (silently losing all data) or a partial JSON array (triggering `m_FileBroken = true` and refusing further writes permanently for that session). There is no write-to-temp-then-rename pattern.
- **Impact:** A crash during a write permanently destroys all stored memories. An attacker who can cause repeated crashes (e.g., by driving `Save` calls that trigger OS resource exhaustion) can wipe the memory store.
- **Fix:** Write to a sibling temp file (`m_StorePath` + `.tmp`), flush and close it, then use `std::filesystem::rename` (atomic on POSIX) to replace the live file. Only call `m_FileBroken = true` if the rename itself fails.

---

### [MEDIUM] `Delete` uses the raw user-supplied `key` string against `entry.key` but `entry.key` was stored clamped

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::Delete()`, the `std::remove_if` lambda `e.key == key`
- **Issue:** `Delete` compares the caller-supplied `key` (unclamped, up to `std::string::max_size()` bytes) directly to stored entries. While this is just a string comparison and does not itself overflow, it is inconsistent: a caller who saved `key` of length > `kMaxKeyBytes` will have the stored entry keyed on the clamped prefix, but `Delete(originalLongKey)` will never match it, silently failing to delete. More importantly, `Delete` does not log the operation result with `LogSafeKey` on the success path's key argument — it calls `LogSafeKey(key)` with the raw, unclamped, potentially multi-megabyte input, causing `LogSafeKey` to iterate up to `kMaxLogChars` characters of an unbounded allocation on the stack — but `LogSafeKey` itself internally caps at 64 chars so the string copy it builds is safe. The real concern is the inconsistency allowing silent bypass of deletion.
- **Impact:** Logic bypass: an entry saved with a long key cannot be deleted by name. Depending on how deletion is used for access control or data-subject requests, this is a correctness and compliance gap.
- **Fix:** Apply `ClampLen(key, kMaxKeyBytes)` at the top of `Delete` (same as `Save`) before the comparison.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ScoreMatch` builds a `haystack` string bounded only by entry `value` size — controllable O(n²) scan

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::ScoreMatch()`, the `haystack.find` loop
- **Issue:** For each query word, `find` scans the entire haystack repeatedly from each match position. The haystack can be up to `kMaxKeyBytes*2 + kMaxValueBytes + kMaxTagsPerEntry*kMaxTagBytes` ≈ 66 KB. With an adversarial query containing many short, very common words (e.g., single characters are filtered ≥2 chars, but two-char words like "aa"), and `kMaxEntries = 10000` entries, `GetRelevant` will perform 10 000 × (number of query words) × (haystack scans) operations per call. There is no cap on the number of query words derived from `userMessage`.
- **Impact:** A caller who can supply an arbitrarily long `userMessage` with many 2-character tokens can trigger a CPU-exhaustion DoS on every `GetRelevant` or `Recall` call while holding `m_Mutex`, blocking all other threads.
- **Fix:** Cap the number of query words extracted in `RecallLocked` (e.g., 64 words maximum) and/or cap the length of the input query before tokenisation.

---

### [MEDIUM] `RandomHex` passes `numBytes` cast to `int` to `RAND_bytes` — truncation for large values

- **Location:** `AIAssistant::RandomHex()`, the line `RAND_bytes(buf.data(), static_cast<int>(numBytes))`
- **Issue:** `numBytes` is `std::size_t`. If a caller ever passes a value exceeding `INT_MAX` (unlikely in practice but the function is public), the cast silently truncates to a negative `int`, causing `RAND_bytes` to return an error or undefined behaviour. More practically, `RAND_bytes` takes an `int` length; the OpenSSL documentation specifies it is undefined for negative values.
- **Impact:** In the degenerate case, `RandomHex` returns an empty string and the fallback counter-based ID is used, weakening ID unpredictability.
- **Fix:** Add a precondition check: `if (numBytes == 0 || numBytes > static_cast<std::size_t>(INT_MAX)) return {};` before the `RAND_bytes` call.

---

### [LOW] `m_StorePath` logged verbatim and may expose internal filesystem layout

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::LoadFromDiskLocked()` and `SaveToDiskLocked()`, multiple `LOG_APP_ERROR` calls using `m_StorePath.string()`
- **Issue:** The full filesystem path of the memory store is emitted to error logs. If those logs are accessible to users, operators of shared deployments, or exposed through a diagnostic endpoint, the path reveals internal directory structure and potentially usernames or deployment identifiers embedded in the path.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure; aids an attacker in targeting the file directly.
- **Fix:** Log only the filename component (`m_StorePath.filename().string()`) in operational log lines, or hash/redact the full path. Reserve the full path for DEBUG-level logs gated behind a compile flag.

---

### [LOW] `LoadFromDiskLocked` reads the entire file into a `std::string` with no size limit before parsing

- **Location:** `MemoryStore::LoadFromDiskLocked()`, the `std::istreambuf_iterator` read into `content`
- **Issue:** The file is read into memory in its entirety before any size check. An adversary who can write to the store file (or substitute it via a symlink) can provide a multi-gigabyte file, causing the process to allocate that much heap before the entry-count cap is enforced.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / OOM DoS on startup or reload.
- **Fix:** Check `fs::file_size(m_StorePath)` before reading and reject files larger than `kMaxEntries * (kMaxKeyBytes + kMaxValueBytes + kMaxTagsPerEntry * kMaxTagBytes)` (a generous but finite upper bound, approximately 650 MB at current constants — consider a tighter practical cap such as 10 MB).


---

### 05_doc_application_assistant_assistantSession_h

<a id="05-doc-application-assistant-assistantsession-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/05_doc_application_assistant_assistantSession_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — Insecure session-ID fallback leaks timing information and is predictable

- **Location:** `AssistantSession::GenerateSessionId()`, approximately line 230 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** When `RAND_bytes` fails, the fallback session ID is constructed from `std::chrono::system_clock` milliseconds plus the string literal `"sess_fallback_"`. The result is trivially guessable: an attacker who can observe any timing signal (log timestamps, HTTP response latency, clock skew) can enumerate the ~millisecond-precision value and predict or brute-force the ID within a small window. The comment acknowledges this is "degraded mode" but does not fail-closed; instead it returns a weak ID that is accepted by `IsValidOpaqueId` and used as a real filesystem path and session handle.
- **Impact:** Session hijacking under the (admittedly rare) condition of `RAND_bytes` failure. An attacker who can trigger or observe RAND_bytes failures (e.g., on a constrained embedded or container environment) or who can simply observe approximate server start time can guess active session IDs and open or overwrite those JSONL files.
- **Fix:** Fail closed. If `RandomHex` returns empty, do not construct a session ID at all. Set `m_FileBroken = true`, log the error, and return without setting `m_SessionId`. All callers that subsequently call `AddUserMessage` or `AddAssistantMessage` will immediately get `false` because `m_FileBroken` is set. Alternatively, throw a recoverable exception that the factory (`AssistantController::CreateSession`) can catch to surface a clean error to the caller. Never hand a time-derived string to the filesystem or to the session map.

---

### LOW — `RestrictFilePermissions` is called after the file is already created and data may be written

- **Location:** `AssistantSession::AppendTurnLocked()`, approximately line 175 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `RestrictFilePermissions` is invoked every time `AppendTurnLocked` runs (inside the mutex), but `std::ofstream` in append mode creates the file with the process's inherited umask permissions before `chmod`/`fs::permissions` is called. There is a window between file creation and the permission restriction where another process running as the same user (or a privileged auditor process) can read or hard-link the file. On systems where the umask is permissive (e.g., 0022 producing 0644), the session JSONL — which may contain AI conversation history including typed secrets — is world-readable until `RestrictFilePermissions` returns.
- **Impact:** Local information disclosure of session content (conversation history) to other users on a shared system, for the brief TOCTOU window on first write. On subsequent writes the file already exists at 0600 so the window disappears, but the first-write window is real.
- **Fix:** Set a restrictive umask before opening the file for the first time, or use platform-specific APIs (`open(O_CREAT, 0600)` on POSIX, then `fdopen`/`fstream` from the fd) so the file is created with 0600 in a single atomic step. Alternatively, create a placeholder file at session construction time (before any turn data is written) using `open(O_CREAT|O_EXCL, 0600)`, so the mode is set atomically at creation.

---

### LOW — `kMaxLineBytes` (1 MiB) allows significant per-line heap allocation inside `LoadFromFileLocked`

- **Location:** `AssistantSession::LoadFromFileLocked()`, approximately line 200 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `std::getline` reads into `std::string line` with no bound; the check `if (line.size() > kMaxLineBytes)` happens *after* the full line is already in memory. A crafted JSONL file with a single line of, say, 100 MiB will cause `std::getline` to allocate 100 MiB before the guard triggers. Multiplied by `kMaxTurnsPerSession` (10 000) if lines are exactly at the limit, worst-case heap use is 10 000 × 1 MiB = 10 GiB before the cap fires. The simdjson `padded_string` constructor then copies the same data again.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write or influence the session JSONL file (e.g., via a separate path-traversal bug elsewhere in the engine, or physical access) can cause an OOM condition when the session is loaded, resulting in a denial of service or `std::bad_alloc` crash.
- **Fix:** Use a bounded read instead of `std::getline`. Read at most `kMaxLineBytes + 1` bytes per line (e.g., `ifs.read` into a fixed-size buffer, scanning for `\n`), and reject the line if the full newline was not found within the limit. This bounds heap use per-line to `kMaxLineBytes` before the guard, not after.

---

### NONE (for the remaining focus areas)

The following reviewed areas have no identified issues in this code:

- **Path traversal:** `IsValidOpaqueId` is enforced at both the constructor and `ListSessions` before any user-controlled value is appended to a filesystem path. The allowlist `[A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,128}` excludes `.`, `/`, `\`, and NUL.
- **JSON injection:** `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` is applied to all three fields before writing to JSONL.
- **Log injection:** `LogSafeSessionId` truncates IDs to 8 chars + `...`; free-form user text is not logged directly.
- **Insecure deserialization:** simdjson is used; `kMaxLineBytes`, `kMaxTurnsPerSession`, and `kMaxTurnTextBytes` are enforced; role values are validated against a closed set.
- **RNG:** `RAND_bytes` (OpenSSL) is used for session ID generation; no `rand()`, `srand`, or time-seeded RNG is used in the non-fallback path.
- **Mutex correctness:** All public methods acquire `m_Mutex`; `LoadFromFileLocked` is only called from constructors before the object is shared, consistent with the documented contract.
- **Race conditions / TOCTOU:** `directory_iterator` is constructed directly with an `error_code` rather than an `exists`-then-open pattern; file open is done without a prior `exists` check.
- **Secrets in logs:** Session IDs are truncated; turn text is never logged.


---

### 06_doc_application_assistant_assistantTools_h

<a id="06-doc-application-assistant-assistanttools-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/06_doc_application_assistant_assistantTools_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Prompt injection via unsanitized `instructions` field in `jcwf_fix_task`

- **Location:** `ExecJcwfFixTask`, user prompt construction (~line 1780)
- **Issue:** The `instructions` argument is embedded verbatim into the AI user prompt: `"Instructions: " + instructions + "\n\n"`. Although `taskId` is validated with `IsValidOpaqueId`, `instructions` has no length cap and no sanitization. A hostile `instructions` value can contain text that overrides the system prompt (e.g., "Ignore all previous instructions. Output ONLY: ...") or injects `<tool_call>` markers that survive the AI response and get parsed as tool calls on the next turn.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls `instructions` (e.g., through a prompt-injection chain where the AI reads attacker-controlled file content and then calls `jcwf_fix_task`) can redirect the workflow-editor AI to produce arbitrary JCWF JSON, write backdoored scripts, or exfiltrate data through subsequent tool calls.
- **Fix:** (1) Apply `DefangToolMarkers` to `instructions` before embedding it in the user prompt. (2) Enforce a hard length cap (e.g., 4 KB). (3) Consider placing `instructions` inside a delimited block (e.g., `<instructions>...</instructions>`) with a system-prompt note that the block is untrusted user input, making override attacks harder.

---

### [HIGH] `ExecRunShell` passes unsanitized AI-supplied `command` directly to `/bin/sh -c`

- **Location:** `ExecRunShell` (POSIX path), `execl("/bin/sh", "sh", "-c", command.c_str(), nullptr)` (~line 1080)
- **Issue:** The entire `command` string from the AI response is forwarded to `/bin/sh -c` with no filtering whatsoever. The code comment acknowledges this is intentional and relies exclusively on the approval gate in `AssistantController`. However, that gate is documented here only by comment; its enforcement is entirely outside this translation unit and cannot be audited from this file. If anything in the approval path is bypassed—misconfigured MCP route, future refactor, test harness—an AI-generated command with shell metacharacters, subshells, or environment manipulation executes without any in-process guard.
- **Impact:** Full OS command injection as the process user, limited only by OS permissions.
- **Fix:** The approval gate is the right primary defense, but add an in-process hard assertion (`assert(approvedByUser)` or an explicit boolean parameter threaded through `Execute`) so the shell invocation path cannot be reached without a provable approval token. This eliminates the single-point-of-failure characteristic. Additionally, enforce the 16 KB output cap before appending `\n... [output truncated at 16 KB]` (the current code appends the suffix after already exceeding 16384 bytes, so the returned string can be slightly larger than documented).

---

### [HIGH] `jcwf_write_plan` accepts arbitrary `workflow_id` as a filename component without validation

- **Location:** `ExecJcwfWritePlan` (~line 1650) and `ExecJcwfReadPlan` (~line 1620), `ExecJcwfGenerate` (~line 1680)
- **Issue:** `workflow_id` is used directly in `fs::path("workflows") / (idIt->second + ".plan.md")` and similar constructions with no validation that the value is a safe filename component. A `workflow_id` of `../../etc/cron.d/evil` would resolve outside `workflows/`, and the `lexically_normal` call is not applied here. `IsPathDenied` is not called on these paths. The same issue affects `ExecJcwfGenerate` which writes `(workflowId + ".json")` and `(workflowId + ".jcwf")`.
- **Impact:** Path traversal: an AI-supplied `workflow_id` can write arbitrary files outside the project root, limited by process permissions. A specially crafted ID could overwrite startup scripts, cron files, or SSH authorized_keys.
- **Fix:** Validate `workflow_id` with an allowlist regex (`[A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,128}`) before any path construction, similar to the `IsValidOpaqueId` check that was correctly applied to `taskId` in `ExecJcwfFixTask`. Apply this check in every function that uses `workflow_id` as a filename component.

---

### [HIGH] `ExecReadFile` checks `..` presence in the normalized string but does not re-verify after `weakly_canonical` resolution

- **Location:** `ExecReadFile` (~line 700), `IsPathDenied` (~line 470)
- **Issue:** `ExecReadFile` calls `fs::path(path).lexically_normal()`, checks the result for `".."` as a substring, and then calls `IsPathDenied` which does call `fs::weakly_canonical`. However, `lexically_normal` does not resolve symlinks. A path like `legit/link_to_dotdot/secret` where `link_to_dotdot` is a symlink pointing to `..` passes the `..` substring check but can escape the project root. `IsPathDenied`'s `lexically_relative` check would catch this only if the symlink's *target* is outside the root, but `weakly_canonical` resolves existing symlink prefixes—so if the symlink exists, the canonical path does escape and `IsPathDenied` returns true. If the symlink does not yet exist at check time (TOCTOU) but is created before the `std::ifstream` open, the read proceeds on the resolved path.
- **Impact:** Potential TOCTOU-based path traversal to read files outside the project root.
- **Fix:** After the `IsPathDenied` check, additionally resolve the path with `fs::canonical` (hard, not weak) and confirm the result still starts with `projectRoot` before opening the file. Open by the canonical path, not the original argument.

---

### [MEDIUM] `jcwf_fix_task` embeds full `jcwfContent` (up to the file's entire size) in an AI prompt without a size cap

- **Location:** `ExecJcwfFixTask`, user prompt construction (~line 1790)
- **Issue:** `jcwfContent` is read via `ReadJcwfContent` which has no size limit visible in this file. The full content is concatenated into the AI user prompt string. A maliciously large or pathologically nested JCWF file can cause unbounded memory allocation.
- **Impact:** Denial of service through memory exhaustion; also inflates AI API costs.
- **Fix:** Cap `jcwfContent` at a reasonable limit (e.g., 64 KB) before embedding it in the prompt, returning an error if the file exceeds the limit.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ExecGetFileSummary` forwards raw file content (up to 32 KB) to an external AI provider without defanging tool markers

- **Location:** `ExecGetFileSummary` (~line 820)
- **Issue:** `m_WorkspaceIndexer->ReadFileContent(filePath, 32768)` is passed directly as part of the AI user prompt. If the file contains `<tool_call>...</tool_call>` blocks (e.g., a test fixture or captured AI output), those markers survive into the summarization AI's context. The comment in `DefangToolMarkers` explicitly documents this threat; the defanging is applied to task output and error messages but not to file content sent for summarization.
- **Impact:** A project file crafted with embedded `<tool_call>` blocks can inject tool calls into the summarization AI's response, which is then stored in the summary cache and returned to the main AI on the next `get_file_summary` call.
- **Fix:** Apply `DefangToolMarkers(content)` before inserting `content` into `userPrompt`.

---

### [MEDIUM] Windows `ExecRunShell` bash path appends `2>&1` inside `WrapForBash`, which wraps in `bash -c "..."` with double-quote delimiters

- **Location:** `ExecRunShell` (Windows path, ~line 1110), `WrapForBash` (~line 150)
- **Issue:** `WrapForBash` wraps the command in `bash -c "..."` with double-quote delimiters. A command string containing `"` (double quote) breaks out of the bash `-c` argument. The PowerShell path correctly uses `-EncodedCommand`, but the bash fallback does not escape the command content before embedding it inside double quotes.
- **Impact:** On Windows with bash mode, an AI-supplied command containing `"` can break the quoting, leading to unintended shell interpretation or argument splitting. Combined with the approval-gate dependency, this is a defense-in-depth gap rather than a direct bypass.
- **Fix:** In the bash path, use the same `EncodePowerShellCommand`-style approach: pass the command as a base64-encoded argument to `bash --norc -c "$(base64 -d <<< ...)"`, or at minimum apply `PosixSingleQuote` to the command string and use single-quote delimiters in `WrapForBash`.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ParseToolCalls` brace-counting fallback does not account for braces inside JSON strings

- **Location:** `ToolRegistry::ParseToolCalls`, brace-counting loop (~line 360)
- **Issue:** When the closing `</tool_call>` tag is absent, the code counts `{` and `}` characters to find the end of the JSON object. This counting is applied to raw bytes without skipping characters inside JSON string literals. A `command` argument value like `{"name": "run_shell", "args": {"command": "echo '}'"}}`  causes the depth counter to reach zero prematurely at the `}` inside the string, producing a truncated and malformed JSON argument that is then fed to `ParseToolCallJson`.
- **Impact:** Malformed tool-call extraction leading to incorrect tool dispatch or silent argument truncation. With a specifically crafted AI response, an attacker could cause one tool's arguments to be partially replaced with another's.
- **Fix:** Replace the ad-hoc brace counter with a proper JSON boundary detector that respects string escaping, or require the closing tag to be mandatory and reject tool calls that lack it.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ResolveWorkflowPath` fallback constructs a path from `workflow_id` without validation

- **Location:** `ResolveWorkflowPath` (~line 1540)
- **Issue:** If `m_WorkflowRegistry->TryGetWorkflowFilePathAbsolute` returns no value, the function constructs `fs::path("workflows") / (workflowId + ".jcwf")` directly from the caller-supplied `workflowId`. At this call site there is no allowlist check on `workflowId`. Functions that call `ResolveWorkflowPath` (e.g., `ExecJcwfRead`, `ExecJcwfValidate`, `ExecJcwfFixTask`) rely on this fallback for workflows not yet loaded into the registry.
- **Impact:** Path traversal: `../../etc/passwd` as `workflow_id` would produce `workflows/../../etc/passwd.jcwf`, which after normalization exits the project directory.
- **Fix:** Validate `workflowId` with `IsValidOpaqueId` at the top of `ResolveWorkflowPath` before any path construction.

---

### [MEDIUM] `WriteJcwfContent` iterates `extractedDir` with `fs::directory_iterator` without first confirming the directory is inside the project root

- **Location:** `WriteJcwfContent` (static helper, ~line 1570)
- **Issue:** The extracted directory path is derived from `jcwfPath.parent_path() / jcwfPath.stem()`. If `filePath` is an absolute path that was returned by `ResolveWorkflowPath` (which calls `fs::absolute`), and if `TryGetWorkflowFilePathAbsolute` returns a path outside the project root (e.g., a symlinked registry entry), then `extractedDir` can be outside the project root. Content is written to this directory without a project-root confinement check.
- **Impact:** Writing attacker-controlled JSON content to an arbitrary directory.
- **Fix:** Assert that `fs::weakly_canonical(extractedDir)` starts with the project root before proceeding with extraction and writing.

---

### [LOW] `ExecSearchFiles` Windows path uses `popen` with a shell-composed command; `glob` argument escaping depends on `PosixSingleQuote` correctness under bash-via-cmd

- **Location:** `ExecSearchFiles` Windows path (~line 760)
- **Issue:** The Windows fallback builds a shell command string using `PosixSingleQuote` for `query` and `glob`, then wraps it in `WrapForBash` which uses double-quote delimiters. A glob value containing both single quotes and special characters could interact unexpectedly with the nested quoting. The POSIX path correctly uses argv exec.
- **Impact:** Limited shell injection in the glob argument on Windows; impact is constrained to ripgrep argument manipulation (e.g., adding additional `--glob` patterns or flags) rather than arbitrary shell execution.
- **Fix:** Port the Windows path to use `CreateProcess` with an argv array as is done in the POSIX path, eliminating shell-string composition entirely.

---

### [LOW] Error messages returned in `ToolResult.output` may echo sensitive path fragments or internal state

- **Location:** Multiple `ExecJcwf*` and `ExecReadFile` functions throughout
- **Issue:** Error messages like `"Cannot open file: " + tmpPath.string()` and `"Rename failed: " + ec.message()` include absolute filesystem paths (since `tmpPath` is derived from `fs::absolute`) and OS error strings. These are returned in `ToolResult.output`, which is forwarded to the AI's context and may be included in AI responses shown to users.
- **Impact:** Internal path structure disclosure, which assists further path traversal or targeted attacks.
- **Fix:** Sanitize error messages to use relative paths (relative to project root) before returning them in `ToolResult`. Keep detailed paths in server-side logs only.


---

### 07_doc_application_assistant_contextAssembler_h

<a id="07-doc-application-assistant-contextassembler-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/07_doc_application_assistant_contextAssembler_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Prompt-injection via `toolDescriptions` truncation without defanging

- **Location:** `ContextAssembler::Assemble`, the block that appends `toolDescriptions` to `prompt.stng` (approximately lines 95–120 of the `.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `toolDescriptions` is capped at `kMaxToolDescriptionsBytes` (64 KiB) via `td.resize()`, but it is **never passed through `DefangContextSentinels`** before being concatenated into the system prompt. The comment says the content comes from the "trusted ToolRegistry," but `DefangContextSentinels` is also applied defensively to the user message precisely because trust can erode. A future code path that lets any user-influenced string reach tool registration (e.g., a user-named tool, a user-supplied description field) would allow injection of `=== … ===` section headers or `<tool_call>` / `<tool_result>` markers directly into the system prompt without any sanitisation.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence any tool description string can spoof structural prompt boundaries (e.g., inject a fake `=== Tool System ===` block), override AI instructions, or cause the model to execute attacker-controlled tool calls — all without touching the user-message path that is already defanged.
- **Fix:** Pass `td` through `DefangContextSentinels` after the size cap and before appending it to `prompt.stng`:
  ```
  td = DefangContextSentinels(td);
  ```
  Place this immediately after the `td.resize(kMaxToolDescriptionsBytes)` call, consistent with how `userMessage` is handled.

---

### MEDIUM — `=`-run defang is bypassable via Unicode or mixed encoding

- **Location:** `ContextAssembler::DefangContextSentinels`, the `=` scanning loop (approximately lines 40–70 of the `.cpp`)
- **Issue:** The defang logic scans raw bytes for the ASCII `=` character (0x3D). It does not normalise Unicode before scanning. An attacker can supply visually identical or semantically equivalent characters that the AI model may interpret as `=` but that the defanger ignores — for example, U+FF1D FULLWIDTH EQUALS SIGN, U+2E40 DOUBLE HYPHEN, or simply zero-width characters interspersed in a `===` run. Additionally, `defanged.resize(runLen)` is never called; only runs of **3 or more** contiguous ASCII `=` bytes are replaced, so two consecutive `=` characters (which the system prompt does not use for headers but a future template change might) pass through unmodified.
- **Impact:** A crafted user message containing non-ASCII look-alike equals signs can spoof `=== Section ===` boundaries in the assembled prompt, injecting or overriding system-prompt sections and escalating prompt-injection effectiveness.
- **Fix:** Before the `=`-run scan, apply Unicode NFKC normalisation to `defanged` (which maps fullwidth and compatibility variants to their ASCII equivalents), then run the existing loop. If the runtime does not have a convenient NFKC library, at minimum document the known bypass and add a secondary scan that replaces U+FF1D (0xEF 0xBC 0x9D) with the same box-double character.

---

### LOW — Truncation via `std::string::resize` can split a multi-byte UTF-8 sequence

- **Location:** `ContextAssembler::Assemble` (user message resize, ~line 128 of `.cpp`) and `ContextAssembler::BuildConversationContext` (per-turn resize, ~line 155 of `.cpp`)
- **Issue:** Both `defangedUser.resize(kMaxUserMessageBytes)` and `defanged.resize(kMaxTurnTextBytes)` blindly truncate at a byte boundary. `DefangContextSentinels` outputs 3-byte UTF-8 sequences for the U+2550 replacement character, and user-supplied text may already contain multi-byte UTF-8. Cutting in the middle of a sequence produces an invalid UTF-8 suffix that may confuse downstream parsers, the AI model tokeniser, or logging subsystems.
- **Impact:** Malformed UTF-8 at the truncation boundary could cause unexpected behaviour in JSON serialisation of the prompt (producing invalid JSON if the surrounding code emits the strings into JSON without re-validating), or trigger model-side parsing anomalies. In isolation the impact is low, but it degrades the correctness of the sanitisation layer.
- **Fix:** After resizing, walk backward from the cut point and trim any incomplete leading byte. A helper such as:
  ```cpp
  while (!s.empty() && (s.back() & 0xC0) == 0x80)
      s.pop_back();   // drop continuation bytes
  if (!s.empty() && (unsigned char)s.back() >= 0xC0)
      s.pop_back();   // drop orphaned start byte
  ```
  applied after each `resize` call is sufficient.

---

### LOW — `toolDescriptions` size cap applied before defang inflates output beyond the intended limit

- **Location:** `ContextAssembler::Assemble`, `td.resize(kMaxToolDescriptionsBytes)` (~line 97 of `.cpp`)
- **Issue:** The 64 KiB cap is applied to the raw `toolDescriptions` string. If `DefangContextSentinels` is later added (as recommended above), each replaced `=` in a run of N becomes 3N bytes of U+2550 UTF-8. A carefully crafted input consisting entirely of `===…===` runs would expand 3× after defanging, yielding up to ~192 KiB injected into `prompt.stng`, violating the intended cap and potentially causing uncontrolled allocation or excessively large AI API payloads.
- **Impact:** Denial-of-service via memory pressure or oversized API requests; possible rejection by the upstream AI provider resulting in workflow failures.
- **Fix:** Apply the size cap **after** defanging, not before. Alternatively, reserve headroom by capping raw input at `kMaxToolDescriptionsBytes / 3` when defanging is expected.


---

### 08_doc_application_assistant_workspaceIndexer_h

<a id="08-doc-application-assistant-workspaceindexer-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/08_doc_application_assistant_workspaceIndexer_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path-Confinement Bypass via `lexically_normal` Relative-Path Stripping Before `ResolveAndConfine`

- **Location:** `ScanWorkspace()` top-level file loop (~line 195) and `ScanDirectory()` (~line 248), both doing `relPath = relPath.substr(2)` after `lexically_normal()`
- **Issue:** The relative path stored in `FileIndexEntry::relativePath` is produced by `entry.path().lexically_normal().string()` with an optional leading `./` stripped. This path is stored directly into `m_Entries` and later passed back to `ResolveAndConfine` (in `ReadFileContent`, `GetFileSummary`, `SetFileSummary`). If a directory entry's path, after `lexically_normal`, produces something like `../outside/file.cpp` (possible if the scan root itself is a symlink or if `fs::directory_iterator` on `.` yields an entry whose path starts with `..`), the stored `relativePath` can escape the workspace. The strip `substr(2)` is also only applied when `relPath[0]=='.' && relPath[1]=='/'`; a path starting with `../` satisfies neither condition and is stored verbatim. Only `LoadIndex` re-validates paths via `ResolveAndConfine`; paths generated by `ScanDirectory` and the top-level loop are stored without that check.
- **Impact:** A symlinked directory inside the workspace pointing upward, or a crafted filesystem layout, could cause `m_Entries` to contain a `relativePath` value like `../sensitive_file`. A subsequent `ReadFileContent` call on that entry would pass the traversal string directly to `ResolveAndConfine`, which should catch it — but the issue is that the entry already exists in the index and can be returned by `GetAllEntries` or `GetRelevantFiles`, leaking the traversal path to callers who may act on it without going through `ResolveAndConfine` (e.g., displaying the path, passing it to external tools).
- **Fix:** Apply the same `ResolveAndConfine` validation in `ScanDirectory` and the top-level loop before pushing any entry into `m_Entries`, mirroring the check that already exists in `LoadIndex`.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Between `fs::file_size` and `ifs.read` in `ReadFileContent`

- **Location:** `ReadFileContent()`, approximately lines 310–325
- **Issue:** The code calls `fs::file_size(filePath)` to determine `readSize`, then allocates `std::string content(readSize, '\0')` and reads exactly that many bytes. The file is already open (`ifs` was opened a few lines earlier), but `file_size` is a separate syscall on the already-opened path. If the file is truncated between `file_size` and `read`, `ifs.gcount()` will be less than `readSize` and `content.resize(ifs.gcount())` handles it correctly. However, if the file *grows* between `file_size` and `read` (or if `file_size` returns a stale cached value), the allocated buffer is smaller than the file and the read is silently truncated with no indication to the caller that the content is incomplete. More critically, `file_size` on an already-open stream should use `ifs.seekg(0, end)` / `ifs.tellg()` to avoid the race entirely.
- **Impact:** In a concurrent environment (e.g., active build), an attacker or racing process could cause `ReadFileContent` to return partial content, potentially cutting off security-relevant content mid-line and causing the AI summarizer to miss sensitive patterns or produce misleading summaries.
- **Fix:** Derive the file size from the already-open stream: `ifs.seekg(0, std::ios::end); auto sz = ifs.tellg(); ifs.seekg(0, std::ios::beg);` rather than making a second `fs::file_size` call.

---

### MEDIUM Unvalidated `m_IndexDir` and `m_IndexPath` Allow Writing Outside Workspace

- **Location:** `WorkspaceIndexer::WorkspaceIndexer()` constructor and `SaveIndex()`, approximately lines 145–155 and 430–455
- **Issue:** `m_IndexDir` is accepted as a constructor parameter (`fs::path indexDir`) and is used without any check that it resolves inside `m_WorkspaceRoot`. `SaveIndex` calls `fs::create_directories(m_IndexDir)` and opens `m_IndexPath` for write. If the caller passes an absolute path or a path with `..` components (e.g., `../../tmp/evil`), the index file will be written outside the workspace. `ResolveAndConfine` is never applied to `m_IndexDir` or `m_IndexPath`.
- **Impact:** A misconfigured or adversarially supplied `indexDir` argument allows writing a JSONL file to an arbitrary filesystem path, potentially overwriting sensitive files or placing attacker-controlled JSONL where another component reads it.
- **Fix:** In the constructor, after capturing `m_WorkspaceRoot`, resolve `m_IndexDir` via `fs::weakly_canonical` and verify it is a prefix of `m_WorkspaceRoot` or is beneath it; reject (log and clear) if not. Alternatively, accept only a relative path and construct `m_IndexDir = m_WorkspaceRoot / indexDir`.

---

### MEDIUM Summaries Written to Index Without Re-validating Calling Path in `SetFileSummary`

- **Location:** `SetFileSummary()`, approximately lines 295–308
- **Issue:** `SetFileSummary` looks up `relativePath` in `m_PathToIndex` and writes to the matching `m_Entries` element. The lookup key is a raw string from the caller with no call to `ResolveAndConfine`. If `m_PathToIndex` has been populated with a traversal key (see first finding), the summary is written to that entry and immediately persisted to disk via `SaveIndex()`. Additionally, `LOG_APP_INFO` logs the `relativePath` verbatim, which can contain newline characters, enabling log injection (see next finding).
- **Impact:** Summaries for out-of-workspace paths can be persisted and later served to callers. A crafted `relativePath` with embedded newlines can corrupt structured log output.
- **Fix:** Call `ResolveAndConfine(relativePath)` at the top of `SetFileSummary` and return early if it returns empty, matching the behaviour of `ReadFileContent`.

---

### LOW Log Injection via Unsanitized `relativePath` in Log Statements

- **Location:** `SetFileSummary()` ~line 306 (`LOG_APP_INFO("[indexer] Cached summary for '{}'...")`), `LoadIndex()` ~line 400 (`LOG_APP_INFO("[indexer] Loaded {} entries...")`), and the security log at ~line 395 (`LOG_SECURITY_WARN`)
- **Issue:** `relativePath` values sourced from `file_index.jsonl` (external file) and from caller arguments are interpolated directly into log messages. A `relativePath` containing `\n`, `\r`, or ANSI escape sequences will split or corrupt log lines. The security log entry at ~line 395 logs `entry.relativePath.size()` but not the path itself — that instance is safe — however the `SetFileSummary` log at ~line 306 logs the path string directly.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write to `file_index.jsonl` or invoke `SetFileSummary` with a crafted path can inject fake log lines, potentially hiding evidence of malicious activity or spoofing security events in log aggregation systems.
- **Fix:** Sanitize paths before logging: replace control characters (codepoints < 0x20, DEL) with their `\xNN` representation, or truncate at the first control character. Apply this to all log sites that emit externally-sourced strings.

---

### LOW Atomic Write Not Used for Index File — Partial Write Leaves Corrupt State

- **Location:** `SaveIndex()`, approximately lines 430–460
- **Issue:** `SaveIndex` opens `m_IndexPath` with `ios::trunc` and writes directly to the live index file. If the process is killed, crashes, or the disk fills mid-write, the index file is left partially written. On the next startup `LoadIndex` will parse whatever partial JSONL exists. While `simdjson` parse errors are skipped per-line, a truncated last line silently drops entries, losing cached summaries without any error indication.
- **Impact:** Crash or OOM during `SaveIndex` corrupts the on-disk index. Repeated crashes can cause the index to slowly lose all cached summaries, forcing expensive re-summarization on every startup. This is a reliability/DoS issue rather than a direct confidentiality breach, but a hostile process that repeatedly kills j9t can drain AI API quota.
- **Fix:** Write to `m_IndexPath` with a `.tmp` suffix, flush and close successfully, then atomically rename over `m_IndexPath` (`fs::rename`). On POSIX this is atomic with respect to readers seeing either the old or new complete file.


---

### 09_doc_application_cloud_azureBlobCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="09-doc-application-cloud-azureblobcloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/09_doc_application_cloud_azureBlobCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH – TOCTOU / partial-download file left on disk after HTTP error

- **Location:** `AzureBlobDownload`, approximately lines 230–270
- **Issue:** The output file is opened with `fopen(..., "wb")` before the HTTP request is made, and is never deleted on failure. When `curl_easy_perform` succeeds but `httpCode >= 400` (e.g., 403 Forbidden, 404 Not Found), `fclose(fp)` has already been called and the function returns false — but the zero-length (or partial) file remains at `outputPath`. A subsequent task or external reader may treat the empty file as valid downloaded data. More seriously, if the server returns a 4xx with a body (Azure error XML), that body has already been written to the output file before the HTTP status code is checked.
- **Impact:** Corrupted or attacker-influenced data at `outputPath` is silently left in the project tree and may be consumed by a downstream workflow step as if it were a legitimate blob.
- **Fix:** Write to a temp file (e.g., `outputPath + ".tmp"`) and atomically rename to `outputPath` only on full success (both `res == CURLE_OK` and `httpCode < 400`). Delete the temp file on any failure path.

---

### HIGH – OAuth2 Bearer token and credential values not checked for CRLF before header injection

- **Location:** `SetAuthHeaders`, approximately lines 85–100
- **Issue:** The comment in `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf` (header file) acknowledges the CRLF-injection risk and states the check exists for callers, but `SetAuthHeaders` does **not** call `ContainsCrlf` (or any equivalent) on `credentials.m_Token` before constructing `"Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token` and passing it to `curl_slist_append`. Nor is it called on `credentials.m_AccessKeyId`, `credentials.m_SecretKey`, or any value returned by `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign`. The defence is described but not applied at the point of use.
- **Impact:** If a credential store or secret manager supplies a token containing `\r\n`, an attacker who controls that value can inject arbitrary HTTP request headers (e.g., forging `x-ms-blob-type`, smuggling a second request, or leaking the real Authorization header to an attacker-controlled server via a redirected request).
- **Fix:** Call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf` (or an equivalent inline check) on every credential field before string-concatenation into a header value in `SetAuthHeaders`. Return an error or abort header construction if CRLF is detected.

---

### HIGH – `extraHeaders` key and value not validated for CRLF before curl header injection

- **Location:** `AzureBlobRequest`, approximately lines 205–215 (the `for (auto const& [key, value] : extraHeaders)` loop)
- **Issue:** Caller-supplied `extraHeaders` (e.g., `"x-ms-blob-type"` and `"Content-Type"`) are concatenated directly into `key + ": " + value` and passed to `curl_slist_append` without any CRLF check on either the key or the value. While these specific headers are currently hardcoded at the call site, the function signature accepts an arbitrary map, and future callers may supply user- or config-derived values.
- **Impact:** An attacker who influences an `extraHeaders` value (e.g., through a workflow parameter that is forwarded as a custom header) can inject arbitrary HTTP headers into Azure Blob requests, potentially forging auth material or smuggling requests.
- **Fix:** Validate that neither key nor value contains `\r` or `\n` before appending. Reject or sanitize on violation.

---

### MEDIUM – Upload file read size trusts `tellg` result without verifying `read` completeness

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud` (upload branch), approximately lines 310–325
- **Issue:** After `seekg(0)` and reading into `fileData`, the code does not check that `file.read(fileData.data(), fileSize)` actually read `fileSize` bytes. On a file system race (file truncated between `tellg` and `read`), a network file system error, or a non-regular file, `gcount()` may be less than `fileSize`, leaving the tail of `fileData` as null bytes. The undersized read is not detected.
- **Impact:** Uploading a blob with trailing null bytes instead of the true file content is a data-integrity failure. On pathological inputs (e.g., a file that grows between `tellg` and `read`) the allocated buffer size could be wrong in the other direction, though the 256 MB cap limits DoS exposure.
- **Fix:** After `file.read(...)`, check `file.gcount() == fileSize` and fail with a clear error if not. Alternatively use a size-bounded streaming read.

---

### MEDIUM – Error message for upload failure may echo raw Azure XML response body

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud` (upload branch), approximately lines 335–342
- **Issue:** When `httpCode >= 400`, up to 500 bytes of `responseBody` (the raw Azure XML error response) are appended to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. Azure error responses can contain internal storage account names, resource URIs, and request-correlation IDs. This message is stored in `TaskInstanceState` and likely surfaced to workflow logs or API responses.
- **Impact:** Leakage of Azure-internal error details to end users or log aggregators. Minimal direct exploitation risk, but aids reconnaissance.
- **Fix:** Log the raw response body at DEBUG level internally; expose only the HTTP status code and a generic error string in `m_LastErrorMessage`.

---

### MEDIUM – `IsValidAzureBlobName` allows uppercase letters but validator comment says it does not

- **Location:** `IsValidAzureBlobName`, approximately lines 155–175
- **Issue:** The function uses `std::isalnum(uc)` which accepts both uppercase and lowercase characters. The function's doc comment says "Allowed: alphanumeric" and links to Microsoft's rules, which in practice accept any Unicode codepoint in the range 1–1024 (the SDK percent-encodes them). More importantly, `std::isalnum` is locale-dependent; in a locale with a non-default character classification, non-ASCII bytes that happen to be classified as alphanumeric would pass the allowlist, potentially introducing unexpected characters into the URL.
- **Impact:** In a locale-sensitive environment, unexpected characters pass validation and are concatenated unencoded into the blob URL, potentially altering URL structure.
- **Fix:** Replace `std::isalnum(uc)` with an explicit ASCII-range check: `(uc >= 'a' && uc <= 'z') || (uc >= 'A' && uc <= 'Z') || (uc >= '0' && uc <= '9')`. Similarly fix `IsValidAzureContainer` which uses `std::islower` and `std::isdigit` with the same locale dependency.

---

### LOW – `create_directories` error code silently discarded in download path

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud` (download branch), approximately lines 360–365
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::create_directories(outputDir, ec)` captures the error code into `ec` but never inspects it. If directory creation fails (permissions, path conflict), `AzureBlobDownload` will subsequently fail with `fopen` returning null, producing a misleading "Cannot open output file" error rather than the true cause.
- **Impact:** No direct security issue, but a misconfigured or adversarially named path that prevents directory creation produces an ambiguous error that may mask a path-traversal attempt that `ValidateLocalPath` should have already caught.
- **Fix:** Check `ec` after `create_directories`; if set, fail immediately with a descriptive message including `ec.message()`.


---

### 100_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParser_h

<a id="100-doc-application-workflow-workflowjsonparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/100_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParser_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Unvalidated `task.m_Id` and `workflow_file` strings reach downstream sinks without length or character-set enforcement

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseTask`, approximately the `"id"` and `"workflow_file"` branches
- **Issue:** `taskOut.m_Id` and `taskOut.m_WorkflowFile` are populated directly from JSON string values via `ElementToString` with no length cap, no character-set restriction, and no path-safety check for `m_WorkflowFile`. Every other path-bearing field (`file_inputs`, `file_outputs`, `working_directory`, `queue_binding.*`) is gated through `IsAcceptedRelativePath`. `m_WorkflowFile` is not. A `sub_workflow` task can therefore specify `m_WorkflowFile = "../../etc/passwd"` or an arbitrarily long string, and that value is carried into the task executor.
- **Impact:** If the engine later resolves `m_WorkflowFile` relative to any base directory, an attacker who controls a workflow definition can achieve path traversal into arbitrary filesystem locations accessible to the engine process.
- **Fix:** Apply `IsAcceptedRelativePath` to `taskOut.m_WorkflowFile` immediately after `ElementToString` succeeds, mirroring the existing guard on `working_directory`. Also cap `m_Id` to a reasonable length (e.g. `WorkflowParserLimits::kMaxIdLength`) and restrict it to `[A-Za-z0-9_\-]` characters, because it is interpolated into log messages and potentially into database/filesystem paths downstream.

---

### MEDIUM — `trigger.m_Id`, `workflow.m_Id`, and `workflow.m_Version` have no length or character-set bounds

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseTrigger` (`"id"` branch), `ParseRootObject` (`"id"` and `"version"` branches), `ParseGlobalJson` (`"id"` and `"version"` branches)
- **Issue:** All three fields are extracted with `ElementToString` and stored without any size cap or allowed-character validation. `m_Id` is interpolated into `LOG_APP_INFO` and `LOG_CORE_WARN` format strings and is used as a map key. A multi-megabyte string causes unbounded allocation; a string containing `%` or format-specifier sequences can misbehave in logging backends that do not sanitize their input.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow file can cause uncontrolled memory allocation (DoS), and depending on the logging library's format-string handling, potential log-injection or format-string misuse.
- **Fix:** After `ElementToString`, assert `value.size() <= kMaxIdLength` (define a small constant such as 256 bytes). Optionally validate against `[A-Za-z0-9_\-.]` for IDs. Apply the same cap to `m_Version` (which is additionally passed to `std::stoi` after a manual `find('.')` — an excessively long version string causes a large `substr` allocation before the numeric parse).

---

### MEDIUM — Unbounded allocation from untrusted `jsonContent` before any structural validation

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseWorkflowJson`, `ParseGlobalJson`, `ParseCanvasJson` — construction of `simdjson::padded_string paddedJson(jsonContent)`
- **Issue:** The only check before allocating is `jsonContent.empty()`. The caller controls the string size. `simdjson::padded_string` allocates `jsonContent.size() + SIMDJSON_PADDING` bytes unconditionally. There is no maximum-document-size guard.
- **Impact:** A caller that passes workflow content from an untrusted source (network, uploaded file) can cause arbitrarily large heap allocation, exhausting process memory (DoS).
- **Fix:** Add an explicit size cap before constructing `paddedJson`, e.g.: `if (jsonContent.size() > kMaxWorkflowJsonBytes) { errorMessage = "workflow JSON exceeds size limit"; return false; }` Define `kMaxWorkflowJsonBytes` in `WorkflowParserLimits`.

---

### MEDIUM — `m_Materialize` map keys and values are unbounded and unvalidated

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseTask`, `"materialize"` branch
- **Issue:** The `materialize` object is iterated and every key/value pair is stored in `taskOut.m_Materialize` with no count limit, no per-entry length cap, and no character-set restriction on keys. Other similarly structured collections (e.g., `depends_on`) have explicit count caps (`kMaxDependsOnPerTask`).
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying a workflow with a large `materialize` object can allocate unbounded memory. If `m_Materialize` keys are later used as environment variable names or filesystem paths, lack of character restrictions enables injection.
- **Fix:** Add a count guard (e.g. `kMaxMaterializeEntries`), cap key and value lengths, and for keys restrict to `[A-Za-z0-9_]`.

---

### LOW — Secrets-capable `params` JSON stored and re-logged without redaction

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseTask` (`"params"` branch) and `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseTrigger` (`"params"` branch); the resulting `m_ParamsJson` / trigger `m_ParamsJson` are then subjects of debug `LOG_APP_INFO` calls later in the call chain (visible from the debug logging block at the bottom of `ParseTask`)
- **Issue:** `params` is documented to hold provider credentials, model configuration, and prompt templates. It is stored verbatim as a raw JSON string. The debug log block at the bottom of `ParseTask` logs the task ID and type; if callers or downstream executors log `m_ParamsJson` at any level, API keys and tokens embedded in `params` are written to log files.
- **Impact:** Credential leakage through log files or log aggregation systems.
- **Fix:** Do not log `m_ParamsJson` or `triggerOut.m_ParamsJson` at any severity. If structured logging of task configuration is required, emit only a hash or a redacted representation that strips any field whose key matches a known secret-bearing name (e.g., `api_key`, `token`, `secret`).

---

### LOW — Log injection via unsanitized user-controlled string values in `LOG_CORE_WARN` and `LOG_APP_INFO`

- **Location:** `StringToTaskType` (unknown-type warn), `StringToTaskMode` (unknown-mode warn), `StringToTriggerType` (unknown-type warn), `ParseTask` unknown-field warn, `ParseTrigger` unknown-field warn, `ParseRetries` unknown-field warn, `ParseDefaults` unknown-field warn, `ParseRootObject` unknown-field warn; also `ParseTask`'s debug block logging `taskOut.m_WorkingDirectory`, `inputPath`, `outputPath`, `fileRef.m_Path`
- **Issue:** All of these pass raw, attacker-controlled JSON string values directly as format arguments to structured log calls. If the logging backend interprets newlines, carriage returns, ANSI escape sequences, or JSON metacharacters in format arguments, an attacker can forge log entries or corrupt log structure.
- **Impact:** Log injection allowing false audit records; potential terminal escape-sequence injection in console logging.
- **Fix:** Sanitize user-supplied strings before logging: strip or escape `\r`, `\n`, control characters, and ANSI escape sequences. Alternatively, quote and truncate the value (e.g., emit only the first 64 printable ASCII characters).


---

### 101_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParserDetails_h

<a id="101-doc-application-workflow-workflowjsonparserdetails-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/101_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParserDetails_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal – `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `fs::weakly_canonical` which does not require the path to exist

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, `pathConfinement.cpp`, confinement check block
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` resolves as many leading components as exist and lexically normalises the rest. If the tail of a path does not yet exist on disk, symlink loops or intermediate symlinks in the existing prefix are still resolved, but a not-yet-created path component that would escape the root is accepted because the lexical suffix is simply appended without being checked against the root at creation time. More concretely, the escape-detection logic is a string prefix check: `relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0`. This only catches cases where the relative path *starts* with `..`. A relative path of the form `a/../../outside` produces a `relStr` of `../outside` which *does* start with `..` and is correctly rejected. However, a symlink inside the tree pointing to `../../outside` is resolved by `weakly_canonical` only for the parts that exist; if the symlink target does not exist yet the non-existing tail is appended lexically and can still escape. The deeper issue is that `weakly_canonical` gives weaker guarantees than `canonical` for paths whose targets have not been created yet, and the code relies on it as the sole containment gate.
- **Impact:** A workflow that specifies a path whose non-existent tail resolves outside the project root could pass the confinement check. Combined with a subsequent file-creation call this becomes a write-outside-root primitive. The severity is moderated by the requirement that intermediate directories must already exist and point outside the tree via symlinks, which requires prior filesystem manipulation.
- **Fix:** Use `fs::canonical` (which requires the full path to exist) wherever possible. For paths that are expected to not yet exist (output files), canonicalise the *parent directory* with `fs::canonical`, assert it is inside the root, then append only the final filename component — verifying the filename itself contains no directory separators.

---

### MEDIUM Signed-to-Unsigned Truncation on `max_items` and `page_size` Integer Fields

- **Location:** `ParseFilter`, filter `max_items` branch (~line in the `ParseFilter` function body); `ParseFilterSource`, `page_size` branch
- **Issue:** Both fields are read as `int64_t` via `get_int64()` and then cast unconditionally to `uint32_t` with no range check. A JSON value of `-1` becomes `0xFFFFFFFF` (4 294 967 295). A value larger than `UINT32_MAX` silently wraps.
- **Impact:** A malicious or malformed JCWF can set `max_items` or `page_size` to a huge effective value, potentially causing the downstream consumer to allocate or iterate over billions of items, leading to a denial-of-service via resource exhaustion.
- **Fix:** After `get_int64()` succeeds, check `value >= 0 && value <= UINT32_MAX` (and impose any tighter domain maximum) before the cast. Return an error if the check fails.

---

### MEDIUM `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` Root Captured via `fs::current_path()` — TOCTOU and Privilege Concern

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, `pathConfinement.cpp`
- **Issue:** The project root is re-derived from `fs::current_path()` on every call. If anything in the process changes the working directory between calls (e.g., a third-party library, the embedded Python interpreter via `os.chdir`, or a concurrent thread), every subsequent confinement check silently shifts its trust boundary to the new working directory. This is a TOCTOU on the working directory itself, not on the path being checked.
- **Impact:** An embedded Python script or a race-prone component that calls `os.chdir` (or `chdir(2)`) can point the root to an attacker-controlled directory, making all subsequent `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` calls confine against the wrong root, effectively nullifying the containment guarantee.
- **Fix:** Capture the project root once at startup into a process-lifetime `static const fs::path` and use that fixed value in every `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` call. Disallow working-directory changes after initialisation (e.g., deny `os.chdir` in the embedded Python sandbox).

---

### LOW User-Controlled String Values Passed to `LOG_CORE_WARN` / `LOG_APP_INFO` Without Sanitisation (Log Injection)

- **Location:** `ParseFilter` (~`LOG_APP_INFO` call near end of function); `ParseControlNodes`, `ParseControlflow`, `ParseTaskInputs`, `ParseTaskOutputs`, `ParseTaskEnvironment`, `ParseFilterSource` (`LOG_CORE_WARN` calls throughout)
- **Issue:** Field keys and values read directly from the JSON document (e.g., `key`, `subKey`, `filterOut.m_Id`, `filterOut.m_Source.m_Kind`, `filterOut.m_Binding`) are interpolated into log messages. If the logging back-end writes to a file or a terminal that is later parsed by an operator or a SIEM, an attacker who controls the JCWF can inject newlines, ANSI escape sequences, or structured-log tokens (e.g., `}\n{"level":"ERROR","msg":"..."}`) to spoof log entries or corrupt log files.
- **Impact:** Log forgery, SIEM alert suppression, terminal escape-sequence injection. Not a direct code-execution vector but materially impairs incident detection.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape `\r`, `\n`, and control characters from any externally-supplied string before passing it to a log macro. A one-line sanitiser that replaces bytes `< 0x20` (excluding tab) with `_` is sufficient.

---

### LOW Unbounded String Field Sizes — No Maximum Length Enforced on Most String Fields

- **Location:** All `ElementToString` call sites throughout `workflowJsonParser.cpp` (task IDs, filter IDs, path strings, kind strings, query strings, etc.)
- **Issue:** `IsAcceptedRelativePath` enforces `kMaxPathLength` only for paths. All other string fields (task IDs, labels, type strings, query strings, connection strings, environment variable keys and values, mapping keys and values) have no length cap. A crafted JCWF can supply megabyte-long strings for any of these fields and they will be copied into heap-allocated `std::string` members of `TaskDef`, `FilterDef`, `ControlNodeDef`, etc., with no rejection.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / denial of service on the parser thread. Downstream code that assumes field lengths are bounded (e.g., log lines, dashboard payloads, database columns) may also be impacted.
- **Fix:** Introduce per-category maximum lengths (e.g., `kMaxIdLength`, `kMaxLabelLength`, `kMaxQueryLength`) in `WorkflowParserLimits` and check the length returned by `ElementToString` before accepting the value.


---

### 102_doc_application_workflow_workflowRegistry_h

<a id="102-doc-application-workflow-workflowregistry-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/102_doc_application_workflow_workflowRegistry_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Uncontrolled file-size allocation in `ReadFileToStringStatic`

- **Location:** `ReadFileToStringStatic`, lines ~42–55 (workflowRegistry.cpp)
