- **Issue:** The function calls `fileStream.seekg(0, std::ios::end)` then `outText.resize(static_cast<size_t>(size))` with no upper-bound check on `size`. A crafted or maliciously large `.jcwf` canvas JSON or `global.json` causes a single `resize` call of up to `SIZE_MAX` bytes.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can place a large file in the workflow directory (or supply one via `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson` before it is written to disk) can trigger a `std::bad_alloc`, crashing the engine process. On 32-bit builds the effective limit is ~4 GB; on 64-bit the OS OOM killer is the only backstop.
- **Fix:** Add a hard cap before `resize`, e.g. `if (size > MAX_WORKFLOW_FILE_BYTES) return false;` where `MAX_WORKFLOW_FILE_BYTES` is a reasonable constant (e.g. 64 MB).

---

### MEDIUM — `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` bypass via path separator edge cases on Windows

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, pathConfinement.cpp lines ~40–55
- **Issue:** The escape-check is string-based: `relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0`. This catches `../foo` but the check uses the platform's native separator. On Windows, `lexically_relative` may produce a relative path beginning with `..` using a backslash (`"..\foo"`), which the `rfind("..", 0)` prefix test still catches. However, the more subtle problem is that `relStr == "."` (i.e., the resolved path equals the root itself) is not rejected — a path exactly equal to the project root passes the confinement check and is returned as valid. Additionally, `weakly_canonical` is used instead of `canonical`; if the final path component does not yet exist (common during `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson` for a new workflow), `weakly_canonical` leaves non-existent suffix components un-resolved, meaning a symlink attack on an intermediate component that exists at canonicalization time but is later swapped remains possible (TOCTOU between `weakly_canonical` and the eventual `ofstream` / `create_directories` call).
- **Impact:** An attacker with the ability to create symlinks inside the project tree and race the write window could redirect a `create_directories` or `ofstream` write to an arbitrary location outside the project root.
- **Fix:** (1) Use `std::filesystem::canonical` (not `weakly_canonical`) for all path components that are expected to exist before the operation; apply `weakly_canonical` only to the final filename component. (2) After `create_directories`, re-verify the created directory is still under root before writing files into it.

---

### MEDIUM — JSON content of `workflowJson` written verbatim to disk without re-serialization

- **Location:** `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`, canvas-write block, lines ~310–320
- **Issue:** After parsing `workflowJson` into a `WorkflowDefinition`, the function writes the *original raw string* `workflowJson` directly to the canvas JSON file (`canvasStream.write(workflowJson.data(), ...)`), not the re-serialized parsed structure. The parser may accept and silently ignore or mangle content that the downstream canvas reader then re-interprets differently (parser differential). Any injection payload embedded in JSON string values (e.g. `\u0000` sequences, oversized keys, duplicate keys) that the parser normalizes away is preserved verbatim on disk and re-parsed later by `LoadContainer`.
- **Impact:** Persistently stored malformed JSON that passes the write-time parser but triggers a different code path on the read-time parser; in the worst case task definitions are injected that bypass intended workflow validation.
- **Fix:** Serialize the parsed `WorkflowDefinition` back to canonical JSON and write that, rather than the raw input string. This ensures the on-disk representation is always the output of the trusted serializer.

---

### MEDIUM — Sub-workflow ID collision via crafted folder name (`__` separator is attacker-controllable)

- **Location:** `LoadContainerSubWorkflows`, sub-ID generation, line ~490
- **Issue:** `subDef.m_Id = parentWorkflowId + "__" + folderName;` where `folderName` comes from `entry.path().filename().string()` — i.e., a directory name inside the extracted `.jcwf` zip archive. An attacker who supplies a `.jcwf` file can choose folder names that collide with existing workflow IDs (e.g., a folder named `foo__bar` under a parent `root` produces ID `root__foo__bar`, which may shadow a legitimate workflow). Additionally a folder name containing path metacharacters accepted by the filesystem but later treated as a key in HTTP routing could enable confusion attacks.
- **Impact:** Workflow shadowing — a malicious `.jcwf` can overwrite an already-registered workflow in `m_Workflows` by producing a colliding sub-workflow ID, replacing that workflow's definition with attacker-controlled task definitions.
- **Fix:** (1) Validate `folderName` against an allowlist (alphanumeric plus `-_`) before use. (2) Check whether the generated `subId` already exists in `m_Workflows` before inserting and reject/warn if so.

---

### MEDIUM — Removal of workflow from registry before confirming file deletion success may leave registry inconsistent; partial TOCTOU on delete

- **Location:** `RemoveWorkflow`, lines ~370–395
- **Issue:** `m_Workflows.erase(iterator)` is called unconditionally before the `deleteFile` branch attempts the filesystem `remove`. If `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` rejects the path (lines ~383–390), the function returns `false` with an error — but the workflow is already gone from the registry. The caller receives a failure indication but the registry no longer contains the entry, so the workflow is silently de-registered without its file being deleted.
- **Impact:** An inconsistency between registry state and filesystem state: the `.jcwf` file persists on disk (and will be re-loaded on the next `LoadDirectory`), but API callers between the failed `RemoveWorkflow` and the next `LoadDirectory` see the workflow as absent. Depending on the calling context this can be used to transiently hide a workflow from validation or scheduling.
- **Fix:** Perform path confinement and any pre-flight checks *before* erasing from `m_Workflows`. Only erase after all checks pass.

---

### LOW — `BrokenWorkflow::m_Error` may echo internal filesystem paths and exception messages to callers

- **Location:** `LoadDirectory` exception handler, lines ~155–165; `LoadContainerSubWorkflows` warn paths
- **Issue:** `ex.what()` from `std::exception` subclasses (which may be `std::filesystem::filesystem_error` including full absolute paths and OS error detail) is stored verbatim into `BrokenWorkflow::m_Error` and returned by `GetBrokenWorkflows()`, which is a public API.
- **Impact:** Internal absolute filesystem paths and OS-level error strings are disclosed to API consumers, potentially leaking deployment directory layout.
- **Fix:** Log `ex.what()` internally at ERROR level, but store only a sanitized message (e.g., `"Parse error — see application log"`) in the `BrokenWorkflow` struct that is returned to callers.

---

### LOW — `global.json` written with unsanitized field values via manual string concatenation

- **Location:** `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`, global.json write block, lines ~280–295
- **Issue:** When `global.json` does not already exist, the function writes it using a manually constructed string: `globalStream << "  \"id\": \"" << workflowId << "\",\n"` and `workflowDefinition.m_Version`. Neither `workflowId` nor `m_Version` are escaped for JSON. A workflow ID or version string containing `"`, `\`, or newline characters will produce malformed or injected JSON.
- **Impact:** If the parser later reads this file and a downstream consumer interprets keys beyond `id`/`version`/`manual_start`, injected content in those fields could alter parsed metadata. At minimum this produces a broken `global.json` that causes subsequent `LoadContainer` to fail.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (the codebase already uses one via `WorkflowJsonParser`) to write `global.json` rather than manual string concatenation.

---

### LOW — `weakly_canonical` called while holding `m_Mutex` on potentially blocking filesystem paths

- **Location:** `TryGetWorkflowIdByFilePathLocked`, `GetSubWorkflowDependencyGraph`, `LoadContainer`, `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson` — multiple call sites
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` performs synchronous filesystem I/O (stat calls). It is called while `m_Mutex` is held. On network-mounted filesystems or under high I/O load this can block the mutex for an unbounded duration, serializing all registry accessors.
- **Impact:** Denial of service — a caller on a slow filesystem stalls all other threads that need any registry operation, including workflow scheduling.
- **Fix:** Pre-canonicalize paths before acquiring the mutex where possible. For the locked lookup path (`TryGetWorkflowIdByFilePathLocked`), canonicalize the argument before lock acquisition in the public wrapper and pass the pre-canonicalized value to the locked helper.


---

### 103_doc_application_workflow_workflowRuntimeManager_h

<a id="103-doc-application-workflow-workflowruntimemanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/103_doc_application_workflow_workflowRuntimeManager_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — SSRF via DNS TOCTOU in callback URL validation

- **Location:** `IsCallbackUrlAllowed` / `FireCompletionCallback`, anonymous namespace
- **Issue:** `IsCallbackUrlAllowed` resolves the hostname with `getaddrinfo` and rejects private ranges, but the result is never bound to the subsequent `curl_easy_perform` call. curl performs its own DNS resolution at connection time. An attacker who controls a hostname can serve a public IP during validation and a private IP (e.g. 169.254.169.254) during curl's resolution (classic DNS rebinding). The two resolutions are not pinned to the same socket.
- **Impact:** An attacker-supplied `callbackUrl` in a workflow context can reach internal cloud-metadata services, intranet endpoints, or the local control plane after the pre-flight check passes.
- **Fix:** After the pre-flight check, pass the resolved numeric IP directly to curl via `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` (curl's DNS cache pinning), or use `CURLOPT_CONNECT_TO`. Build the override list from the `addrinfo` results collected during validation and supply them with `curl_slist_append` before `curl_easy_perform`. This pins curl to the same address that passed the private-range check.

---

### HIGH — Path traversal in per-item freshness output resolution (no confinement)

- **Location:** `FanOutPerItemChildren`, approximately the block that resolves `fileOutput` for freshness checking
- **Issue:** When checking whether per-item child outputs already exist, the code substitutes `{{binding.field}}` values directly from filter-item data into `fileOutput` template strings and calls `fs::exists(resolvedOutput)` with no call to `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`. Filter item field values (`item.m_Values`) are attacker-influenced (they come from external data sources such as CSV files, Polarion queries, or filesystem scans processed by `FilterEngine::Evaluate`). A crafted field value such as `../../../../etc/passwd` would resolve to an arbitrary path outside the project tree.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence filter source data can probe the existence of arbitrary files on the host filesystem by observing whether the child task is skipped (outputs exist) or executed (outputs absent). Combined with other primitives, this could be escalated to read-access.
- **Fix:** Wrap the `resolvedOutput` path through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` before calling `fs::exists`. Reject (treat as non-existent) any path that returns empty from the confinement check.

---

### HIGH — Unbounded file read in callback payload builder

- **Location:** `FireCompletionCallback`, the output-content loop inside the task-summary block
- **Issue:** The code checks `fs::is_regular_file` and then reads up to `kMaxOutputBytes` (65 536 bytes) of each output-file per succeeded task and per output slot. The `slotValue` strings come from `taskState.m_OutputValues`, which are populated by executor results and `InjectUpstreamOutputs`. A workflow definition can declare many output slots, and a run can have many tasks. No cap is placed on the total payload size, only on a single slot read. Additionally, `slotValue` is passed to `fs::path` without confinement; if it resolves outside the project root, the code reads an arbitrary file up to 65 536 bytes and POSTs it to the callback URL.
- **Impact:** Task output slots that store absolute paths outside the project tree (injected via upstream `InjectUpstreamAiStructuredOutput` or a crafted `response.json`) cause the callback to exfiltrate up to 65 536 bytes of arbitrary host files to an attacker-controlled HTTPS endpoint.
- **Fix:** (1) Run `slotValue` through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` before `fs::is_regular_file`; skip slots where confinement fails. (2) Add a total-payload byte budget (e.g. 512 KB) across all slots and truncate once the budget is reached.

---

### HIGH — `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `weakly_canonical` — symlinks not followed

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` in `pathConfinement.cpp`
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` does not resolve symlinks for path components that exist; it only resolves the portions of the path that exist on disk and leaves non-existent components normalised but unresolved. A symlink inside the project tree that points outside (e.g. `<project>/data/secret -> /etc`) will be canonicalised to `<project>/data/secret`, which passes the `lexically_relative` check. The comment says symlink targets that point out of tree are rejected, but `weakly_canonical` does not enforce this — only `fs::canonical` does.
- **Impact:** Any attacker who can create a symlink inside the project root (via a malicious JCWF extraction, a crafted filter output, or a compromised task working directory) can escape the confinement check and cause the runtime to read or delete files anywhere on the filesystem.
- **Fix:** Replace `fs::weakly_canonical` with `fs::canonical` (which throws or sets `ec` on missing paths or unresolvable symlinks). Handle the `ec` case by returning an empty path. If the path being confined need not exist yet (e.g. a new output file), canonicalize only the parent directory and append the filename separately, then re-verify the parent is inside the root.

---

### MEDIUM — `TryGetRunById` acquires `m_Mutex` after `TryGetActiveRun` releases it (double-lock TOCTOU)

- **Location:** `TryGetRunById`
- **Issue:** `TryGetActiveRun` acquires and releases `m_Mutex`, then `TryGetRunById` acquires it again to scan `m_LastRuns`. Between the two lock acquisitions, `Update()` can run on the main thread, complete the active run, move it to `m_LastRuns`, and erase it from `m_ActiveRuns`. The caller of `TryGetRunById` would then see neither snapshot — `TryGetActiveRun` returns false (run already moved), and the `m_LastRuns` scan finds the run (run now in `m_LastRuns`). This is a logic race, not a data race, but it can cause the REST handler to return HTTP 404 for a run that exists.
- **Impact:** Incorrect 404 responses to clients polling run status immediately after completion. Low direct security impact, but can mask run state from monitoring/audit tools.
- **Fix:** Implement `TryGetRunById` as a single locked operation that searches both `m_ActiveRuns` and `m_LastRuns` within one `scoped_lock`.

---

### MEDIUM — Secrets potentially logged in `InjectUpstreamOutputs` / `InjectUpstreamAiStructuredOutput`

- **Location:** `InjectUpstreamOutputs` and `InjectUpstreamAiStructuredOutput`, anonymous namespace; also `TryApplyAiCompletion` context-publish loop
- **Issue:** These functions flatten all JSON fields from `response.json` and AI structured-output files into `targetInputValues` / `workflowRun.m_Context` with log lines reporting the count. More critically, `ExecuteTaskOnWorker` builds `m_InputsJson` as a `key=value;` concatenation of all resolved input values and stores it in `TaskInstanceState::m_InputsJson`, which is serialised into the callback payload's task summary and the REST snapshot. If an upstream task's output or a context value contains an API key, token, password, or PII, it flows into these diagnostic fields and can be exfiltrated via the callback or read via the run-snapshot REST endpoint.
- **Impact:** Secrets injected into workflow context (e.g. via `EnqueueWorkflowRunWithContextAndGetRunId`) or produced as task outputs propagate into `m_InputsJson` / `m_OutputsJson` fields visible in the REST API and the completion callback.
- **Fix:** Introduce a `sensitive` flag on `ContextValue` and `OutputSlot`; redact flagged values in the `*Json` summary strings and in the callback task-outputs block. At minimum, document clearly that `callbackUrl` owners receive task input/output values and that secrets should not be passed through context.

---

### MEDIUM — Uncontrolled allocation via deferred AI completion queue

- **Location:** `DrainAiRequestCompletions`, the `m_DeferredAiCompletions` push-back
- **Issue:** The cap of 256 on `m_DeferredAiCompletions` guards only the new-arrival path. The `stillDeferred` vector produced each tick by re-testing the existing queue is rebuilt without a size cap. If 256 entries are deferred and none match any active run (e.g. all runs completed), the vector grows without bound across ticks because `TryApplyAiCompletion` returns `true` for completions whose run is in `m_LastRuns`, so they are dropped — but the loop only applies this logic to existing deferred entries sequentially. Under adversarial conditions (many completions arriving for runs that are never promoted to `m_LastRuns`), the deferred queue remains at 256 but each tick allocates a fresh 256-element vector for `stillDeferred`. This is minor but the cap also does nothing to limit the size of each `AiRequestCompletion` payload held in memory.
- **Impact:** Sustained DoS via memory pressure if AI completions arrive faster than runs can be matched. Low severity given the 256 entry cap on new arrivals.
- **Fix:** Also cap `stillDeferred.reserve` and enforce the same limit when re-inserting. Evict the oldest entries when the cap is hit rather than silently dropping only new arrivals.

---

### MEDIUM — `lastRun` ordering comparison uses lexicographic ISO-8601 string comparison

- **Location:** `Update()`, the `m_LastRuns` update block
- **Issue:** The guard `active.m_Run.m_StartedAtIso8601 >= existingIt->second.m_StartedAtIso8601` uses `std::string::operator>=` on ISO-8601 UTC strings. Lexicographic comparison is correct for the format `%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ` produced by `GetIso8601NowUTC` only if both strings use the same length and format. There is no validation that either string conforms. A crafted `m_StartedAtIso8601` supplied via `EnqueueWorkflowRunWithContextAndGetRunId` (context can be arbitrary) could replace a legitimate last-run record with an older or fabricated one.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can trigger runs can poison the `m_LastRuns` map so that a newer successful run is never recorded, causing REST queries for `last_run` to return stale or adversarial data.
- **Fix:** Store `m_StartedAtSteady` (already present) as the ordering key instead of the ISO-8601 string, and compare `std::chrono::steady_clock::time_point` values. `m_StartedAtSteady` is set by the runtime, not by external input.

---

### LOW — `parentTaskInstanceId` not validated against the ID allowlist in `RegisterSubWorkflowLink`

- **Location:** `RegisterSubWorkflowLink`
- **Issue:** `childRunId` and `parentRunId` are validated by `IsValidRunOrWorkflowId`, but `parentTaskInstanceId` is only checked for non-empty. The task instance ID can contain `#` (for per-item children), which is not in the `IsValidRunOrWorkflowId` charset. However, because the ID is used only as a map key and log field, the immediate injection risk is low. The broader concern is that `parentTaskInstanceId` is stored in `m_SubWorkflowLinks` and later used in `find()` against `m_Run.m_TaskStates` — a value that has already been validated at insertion. No secondary processing expands it unsafely.
- **Impact:** Minimal direct impact; a malformed task instance ID simply fails to match any task state entry, causing the sub-workflow link to be silently ignored.
- **Fix:** Validate `parentTaskInstanceId` with a function that also permits `#` and digits after it (or extend `IsValidRunOrWorkflowId` with a flag). This closes the gap if the field is ever used in path construction in the future.

---

### LOW — Detached thread in `FireCompletionCallback` may outlive curl global state

- **Location:** `FireCompletionCallback`, `callbackThread.detach()` call
- **Issue:** The detached thread captures `callbackUrl` and `payload` by value (safe for lifetime) but calls `curl_easy_init()` after the thread is detached. If `curl_global_cleanup()` is called during application shutdown before the thread finishes (race between detached thread and main-thread teardown), curl's global state is freed while the thread is still using it. `curl_easy_perform` behaviour is undefined after `curl_global_cleanup`.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free in the curl global state during shutdown. In practice, crash or undefined behaviour, not an exploitable security gap, but can suppress the delivery of a final callback that audit systems depend on.
- **Fix:** Use a thread pool or a `std::future` tracked by a shutdown-aware owner rather than `detach()`. Alternatively, ensure `curl_global_cleanup` is called only after all detached callback threads complete by tracking them with a semaphore or join handle.


---

### 104_doc_application_workflow_workflowTriggerBinder_h

<a id="104-doc-application-workflow-workflowtriggerbinder-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/104_doc_application_workflow_workflowTriggerBinder_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unsanitized File Watch Path

- **Location:** `ParseFileWatchParams` / `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll`, file-watch case, approximately the block beginning around line 310–345
- **Issue:** The `path` field read from the JSON params is accepted without any validation that it stays within an expected directory tree. `lexically_normal()` resolves `..` components syntactically, but the code only checks `watchedPath.front() != '/'` to decide whether to treat a path as relative. An attacker who can write workflow definitions (e.g., via a compromised workflow store or a misconfigured admin interface) can supply `../../etc/passwd`, `../../../proc/self/environ`, or an absolute path like `/etc/shadow` and the watcher will be registered against that location. `lexically_normal()` does not canonicalize against the filesystem and does not prevent escape above the base directory.
- **Impact:** An attacker with workflow-definition write access can watch arbitrary files on the host filesystem, causing file-change events to fire and potentially leaking the content of sensitive files to workflow steps that process those events.
- **Fix:** After computing `watchedPathAbsolute`, verify that the resolved path starts with the workflow base directory's absolute canonical path:

      std::filesystem::path canonical = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(watchedPathAbsolute);
      std::filesystem::path base = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(workflowBaseDirectoryPathAbsolute);
      if (!canonical.string().starts_with(base.string() + '/')) {
          LOG_APP_ERROR("...: path '{}' escapes workflow base directory", watchedPathAbsolute);
          break;
      }

  Use `std::filesystem::canonical` (or `weakly_canonical`) so that symlinks are resolved before the prefix check.

---

### MEDIUM Unvalidated poll_interval_seconds Allows DoS / Resource Exhaustion (S3Watch, AzureBlobWatch, GcsWatch, OneDriveWatch, EmailWatch)

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll`, all polling trigger cases (S3Watch ~line 390, AzureBlobWatch ~line 460, GcsWatch ~line 500, OneDriveWatch ~line 420, EmailWatch ~line 440)
- **Issue:** For `S3Watch`, `AzureBlobWatch`, and `GcsWatch`, `poll_interval_seconds` is read as `int64_t` and cast directly to `uint32_t` with no bounds checking (unlike `debounce_ms` in `ParseFileWatchParams` which correctly clamps). A value of `0` or `1` produces a tight polling loop; a negative value wraps to a very large `uint32_t` depending on implementation, but the immediate danger is zero/one-second intervals. For `OneDriveWatch` and `EmailWatch` the value is read as `uint64_t` and silently truncated via `static_cast<uint32_t>`, again with no minimum or maximum enforcement.
- **Impact:** A workflow author can trigger near-continuous polling against a remote storage provider, exhausting API rate limits, incurring cost, and consuming internal thread/connection resources (DoS).
- **Fix:** Apply the same clamping pattern used for `debounce_ms`: reject values ≤ 0, enforce a sensible minimum (e.g., 30 seconds) and cap at `UINT32_MAX`. Log and break if the value is out of range.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded JSON Input Allocation

- **Location:** `ParseCronParams`, `ParseFileWatchParams`, `ParseWebhookParams`, and the inline S3/OneDrive/Email/AzureBlob/GCS parsers — all call sites where `padded_string` is constructed from `paramsJson`
- **Issue:** `paramsJson` is passed in from `workflowTrigger.m_ParamsJson` with no size limit checked before constructing `simdjson::padded_string`. simdjson allocates `paramsJson.size() + SIMDJSON_PADDING` bytes on the heap. If `m_ParamsJson` is populated from an externally supplied workflow definition without a size cap, an attacker can supply a multi-megabyte or multi-gigabyte JSON blob, causing a large heap allocation per trigger registration call.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / OOM crash (DoS) during workflow registration, especially if `RegisterAll` is called on untrusted or user-controlled workflow definitions.
- **Fix:** Add an explicit maximum size guard before parsing, e.g., reject any `paramsJson` larger than a reasonable upper bound (e.g., 64 KB):

      if (paramsJson.size() > 65536) {
          LOG_APP_ERROR("ParseCronParams: params JSON too large ({} bytes)", paramsJson.size());
          return false;
      }

---

### MEDIUM Cron Expression and Timezone Passed to TriggerEngine Without Validation

- **Location:** `ParseCronParams` return values consumed in `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll` around the Cron case, then forwarded to `triggerEngine.AddCronTrigger`
- **Issue:** The cron expression string and timezone string are extracted from JSON and forwarded verbatim to `AddCronTrigger` with no syntactic validation of the expression and no allowlist check on the timezone string. Depending on the cron library used by `TriggerEngine`, a malformed or adversarially crafted expression could trigger a parse error in a third-party scheduler, or a timezone string could be used to construct a filesystem path (many timezone implementations look up `/usr/share/zoneinfo/<timezone>`), enabling a directory traversal in the timezone resolution layer.
- **Impact:** Crafted timezone strings containing `../` sequences may resolve to arbitrary files in the zoneinfo lookup path in some libc/ICU implementations. Malformed cron expressions may cause scheduler crashes.
- **Fix:** Validate the timezone against a closed allowlist of IANA timezone identifiers before forwarding. Validate the cron expression with the same parser the scheduler will use and reject it at this layer before passing it downstream.

---

### MEDIUM Debug Log Lines Expose Internal Filesystem Paths

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll`, file-watch case, two `LOG_APP_INFO("[paths debug]…")` calls approximately lines 330 and 350
- **Issue:** Two `LOG_APP_INFO` calls tagged `[paths debug]` emit `workflowBaseDirectory`, `workflowBaseDirectoryAbsolute`, `watchedPath`, and `watchedPathAbsolute` at INFO level. These are labeled as debug logging that was apparently not removed before release. Absolute filesystem paths of the workflow engine's data directories are written into the application log at every trigger binding operation.
- **Impact:** If logs are accessible to lower-privileged users, support staff, or external log aggregators, internal directory layout is exposed, aiding privilege escalation or targeted path-traversal attacks.
- **Fix:** Remove these two `LOG_APP_INFO` calls entirely, or downgrade to `LOG_APP_DEBUG` and ensure debug logging is disabled by default in production builds.

---

### LOW Integer Truncation on poll_interval_seconds Without Range Enforcement (OneDriveWatch / EmailWatch)

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll`, OneDriveWatch case ~line 425 and EmailWatch case ~line 450
- **Issue:** `poll_interval_seconds` is read as `uint64_t` and then truncated with `static_cast<uint32_t>(interval)`. Values above `UINT32_MAX` silently wrap to a small number, potentially setting the interval to near-zero.
- **Impact:** Unintended near-zero poll intervals leading to tight polling loops (see the DoS finding above); this is a contributing factor to that issue worth noting as a distinct coding flaw since it is a truncation without saturation rather than a missing bounds check.
- **Fix:** Before casting, check `interval > std::numeric_limits<uint32_t>::max()` and clamp or reject, consistent with how `debounce_ms` is handled in `ParseFileWatchParams`.

### 105_doc_application_workflow_workflowTypes_h

<a id="105-doc-application-workflow-workflowtypes-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/105_doc_application_workflow_workflowTypes_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

This file is a header defining plain data structures (POD-like structs, enums, a couple of inline pure-transformation string utilities, and an atomic watchdog helper). It contains no I/O, no cryptographic operations, no authentication logic, no network calls, no memory allocation beyond standard-library containers, no hardcoded secrets, and no deserialization logic. The two inline functions (`TruncateUtf8Safe`, `SanitizeUtf8`) are purely computational, operate on caller-supplied `const std::string&` inputs, produce new strings, and have no observable security-relevant side effects. There is nothing in this translation unit to flag.

### 106_doc_application_workflow_workflowValidator_h

<a id="106-doc-application-workflow-workflowvalidator-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/106_doc_application_workflow_workflowValidator_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path traversal mitigation is bypassable via URL-encoded or null-byte sequences in `ContainsPathTraversal`
- **Location:** `ContainsPathTraversal`, approximately line 130; consumed in `ValidateTaskParams` ~line 235 and `ValidateShellScriptContent` ~line 470
- **Issue:** The function checks only for the literal string `".."`. On some platforms and with some launchers, path traversal can be encoded as `%2e%2e`, `..%2f`, or similar sequences that survive to execution. More importantly, the guard is a substring scan on the raw user-supplied string before `lexically_normal()` is called; if any intermediate processing step decodes or canonicalises the string between validation time and execution time, the check is defeated. The existing `lexically_normal()` call at ~line 247 happens *after* the traversal check passes, meaning the two operations are not atomic relative to the validated value.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls `params.command` can supply a path that passes `StartsWith("scripts/")` and passes `ContainsPathTraversal` but resolves outside `scripts/` at execution time, allowing execution of arbitrary binaries on the host.
- **Fix:** Perform the traversal check on the *lexically normalised* path, not the raw string. After computing `scriptPath = (launchCwd / command).lexically_normal()`, verify that the normalised path starts with `(launchCwd / "scripts/").lexically_normal().string()`. This single check replaces both `StartsWith` and `ContainsPathTraversal` and is immune to encoding tricks.

---

### MEDIUM Information disclosure via `ValidateFileInputReachability` debug log emitting user-supplied paths and absolute filesystem layout
- **Location:** `ValidateFileInputReachability`, approximately line 720 (`LOG_APP_INFO` call)
- **Issue:** The log statement unconditionally emits `taskId`, `fileInput` (user-supplied), `resolvedRelative` (user-influenced), and `absoluteCheckPath` (absolute host filesystem path) at INFO level. In a multi-tenant or server-side deployment this leaks the host's absolute directory layout and reflects user-controlled strings into structured application logs with no sanitisation.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit workflow definitions can probe the server's filesystem layout through log exfiltration, and can inject log-forging payloads (newlines, ANSI escape sequences) into the log stream if the logger does not sanitise format arguments.
- **Fix:** Remove or guard the log statement behind a debug/trace build flag. If retained, sanitise `fileInput`, `resolvedRelative`, and `absoluteCheckPath` by stripping control characters before formatting, and do not log absolute paths in production builds.

---

### MEDIUM Absolute path accepted for `sub_workflow` `workflow_file` bypasses the `scripts/` containment model
- **Location:** `ValidateTaskParams`, approximately line 305 (`TaskType::SubWorkflow` branch)
- **Issue:** When `task.m_WorkflowFile` is an absolute path the code takes the `raw` path verbatim and calls `std::filesystem::exists` on it. There is no check that the resolved path is inside the expected workflow directory tree. The `ContainsPathTraversal` guard applied to shell commands is entirely absent here.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit a workflow definition can reference any absolute path on the host filesystem as a sub-workflow file, causing the engine to read (and potentially execute) arbitrary files.
- **Fix:** Reject absolute paths in `workflow_file` with an `Error`-severity issue. For relative paths, apply the same normalisation-then-prefix check described above: ensure the resolved absolute path starts with the workflow base directory.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled allocation / DoS in `ReadFirstLines` and JSON parsing of attacker-controlled `paramsJson`
- **Location:** `ReadFirstLines` ~line 390; `TryGetParamsString` ~line 165; JSON parsing in `ValidateTaskParams` ~line 270
- **Issue:** `ReadFirstLines` reads up to 50 lines of unbounded length from disk. A single line of many megabytes causes a proportional heap allocation per call. `TryGetParamsString` and the inline simdjson parser in `ValidateTaskParams` construct `simdjson::padded_string` from `paramsJson` with no size limit; a caller who provides a multi-megabyte `paramsJson` causes a large allocation and a full parse on the validator's thread. The validator is invoked synchronously from the AI-request path, so this is a request-level DoS vector.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow with a large `paramsJson` or a script file with very long lines causes unbounded memory allocation and CPU consumption, potentially stalling or crashing the engine.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum byte length on `paramsJson` before constructing `padded_string` (e.g., 64 KB) and add a per-line length cap in `ReadFirstLines` (e.g., `line.size() > 4096` → truncate or abort).

---

### MEDIUM `FunctionExistsInPendingScript` searches generated script content with a user-controlled substring pattern, enabling ReDoS-like CPU amplification
- **Location:** `FunctionExistsInPendingScript` ~line 540; called from `ValidatePythonScriptRegistry` ~line 590
- **Issue:** The pattern `"def " + functionName + "("` is formed from the user-supplied `function` field and then used in `std::string::find` against potentially large generated script content. While `find` itself is not a regex and cannot cause classical ReDoS, `functionName` is not validated for length or character content before this call. A `functionName` of several megabytes causes O(n·m) scanning across every pending script's content on every validation call.
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying a workflow with a very long `function` name (no length limit is enforced in the Python params validation) can cause quadratic CPU consumption during validation.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum length on `module` and `function` values extracted from `paramsJson` (e.g., 256 characters) before they are used in string operations. Add this cap immediately after `TryGetParamsString` returns.

---

### LOW `ValidateSubWorkflowGraph` DFS is unbounded-stack recursive on externally supplied graph
- **Location:** `WorkflowValidator::ValidateSubWorkflowGraph` ~line 950; inner `dfs` lambda ~line 975
- **Issue:** The DFS uses direct C++ recursion via `std::function`. The recursion depth is bounded by `maxDepth` (default 10), but `maxDepth` is a caller-supplied `uint32_t` with no upper bound enforced inside the function. A caller passing `maxDepth = UINT32_MAX` with a long-chain graph causes stack overflow. Additionally, the depth check fires *after* the recursive call is already on the stack for nodes exactly at `maxDepth`, not before.
- **Impact:** A caller with control over `maxDepth` or a graph with a branching factor large enough to exhaust the stack before the depth check fires can cause a stack overflow / process crash.
- **Fix:** Cap `maxDepth` to a reasonable constant (e.g., 100) inside `ValidateSubWorkflowGraph`. Move the depth check to the *entry* of the `dfs` lambda (before visiting children) so recursion is halted before the next stack frame is allocated.

---

### LOW `IsInPendingScripts` suffix-match logic can match unintended paths
- **Location:** `IsInPendingScripts` ~line 510
- **Issue:** The suffix match checks that `gs.path` ends with `filePath` and that the preceding character is `/` or the match is at offset 0. However, it does not verify that the non-suffix portion of `gs.path` is actually a valid path prefix. A pending script at path `evil_scripts/parseLog.py` would match a query for `scripts/parseLog.py` because the suffix `scripts/parseLog.py` is present and the preceding character is `/`.
- **Impact:** A generated script at a path the engine considers untrusted could be incorrectly accepted as satisfying a `scripts/`-namespace module check, suppressing a validation warning and causing the engine to reference a script it should have rejected.
- **Fix:** Require the non-suffix portion to end with a path separator and to be either empty or a recognised root prefix (e.g., the absolute launch CWD). Alternatively, normalise both paths to absolute before comparing.

---

### LOW Webhook secret presence checked but not strength-validated
- **Location:** `ValidateTrigger` ~line 210
- **Issue:** The validator checks that the webhook `secret` field is non-empty but performs no minimum-length or entropy check. A single-character secret satisfies the validation and would produce a trivially brute-forceable HMAC-SHA256 key.
- **Impact:** A workflow author can configure a webhook with a weak secret. If the engine enforces webhook authentication based on this secret, a short secret can be brute-forced, allowing an attacker to forge valid webhook payloads.
- **Fix:** Add a minimum length check (e.g., ≥ 32 characters) and emit an `Error` or `Warning` if the secret is shorter, matching NIST SP 800-107 guidance for HMAC keys.


---

### 107_doc_engine_auxiliary_file_h

<a id="107-doc-engine-auxiliary-file-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/107_doc_engine_auxiliary_file_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal – No Canonicalization or Prefix Validation on Caller-Supplied Paths
- **Location:** `FileExists`, `IsDirectory`, `CreateDirectory`, `CopyFile`, `FileSize`, `SetCurrentWorkingDirectory` — throughout the .cpp file
- **Issue:** Every function that accepts a caller-supplied `std::string` or `const char*` path passes it directly to `std::ifstream`, `std::ofstream`, `std::filesystem::create_directories`, or `std::filesystem::current_path` without calling `std::filesystem::canonical` / `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` and without checking that the resolved path stays within an intended root. Sequences such as `../../etc/passwd` or absolute paths outside the application's working tree are silently accepted.
- **Impact:** If any caller in the wider j9t engine passes an HTTP-sourced filename, webhook payload field, or AI-request parameter to these helpers, an attacker can read or overwrite arbitrary files accessible to the process, create directory trees anywhere on the filesystem, or redirect the process working directory to an attacker-controlled location.
- **Fix:** At the top of each function that takes a path from outside the process, resolve it with `fs::weakly_canonical` and assert it has an expected prefix, e.g.:
  `fs::path resolved = fs::weakly_canonical(p); if (resolved.string().rfind(allowedRoot, 0) != 0) return /* deny */;`
  For `SetCurrentWorkingDirectory`, restrict the allowed set at the call site, or remove the function from the public API.

---

### MEDIUM Unchecked Allocation / DoS via Unbounded `rdbuf` Copy in `CopyFile`
- **Location:** `CopyFile`, approximately line 195
- **Issue:** `destination << source.rdbuf()` reads the entire source file into an in-memory stream buffer before writing. There is no check on the file size before opening or during copying, and no size limit is enforced.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply a `src` path pointing to a very large file (e.g., `/dev/zero`, a sparse file, or a file grown by another process) can exhaust process memory, triggering an OOM crash and denying service.
- **Fix:** Call `FileSize` before copying and reject files above a defined maximum. Use a fixed-size loop buffer (`std::array<char, 65536>`) with `source.read` / `destination.write` instead of `rdbuf()` so memory usage is bounded regardless of file size.

---

### LOW Undefined Behavior / Crash on Empty String in `AddSlash`
- **Location:** `AddSlash`, approximately line 208
- **Issue:** `filename.substr(filename.size() - 1)` is called without first checking that `filename` is non-empty. When `filename.size() == 0`, `filename.size() - 1` wraps to `std::string::npos` on the unsigned arithmetic, and `substr` throws `std::out_of_range`; an attacker who can supply an empty path string triggers an unhandled exception that may crash the process or, if exceptions are disabled, produce undefined behavior.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via an empty path string reaching `AddSlash`.
- **Fix:** Guard the function: `if (filename.empty()) { filename = slash; return filename; }` before the `substr` call, or replace `substr` with `filename.back() != slash[0]`.

---

### LOW TOCTOU Race in `GetNewestTimestamp` and `FileExists` / `CreateDirectory`
- **Location:** `GetNewestTimestamp` (~line 220); `FileExists` + `CreateDirectory` pair
- **Issue:** `fs::exists(p)` followed by `fs::last_write_time(p)` is a classic check-then-use race. Similarly, checking `FileExists` and then opening or creating a file is non-atomic. Between the existence check and the subsequent operation, another process can replace the path with a symlink pointing to a sensitive target.
- **Impact:** On systems where the process has elevated privilege or where the workflow engine operates on shared directories (e.g., a secrets or webhook staging folder), a symlink-swap attack could redirect reads or writes to unintended files.
- **Fix:** For `GetNewestTimestamp`, wrap `fs::last_write_time` in its own try/catch and drop the preceding `fs::exists` check entirely — the exception already handles the missing-file case. For file creation, use `O_CREAT | O_EXCL` via POSIX `open` or equivalent platform APIs rather than the check-then-create pattern.


---

### 109_doc_engine_core_h

<a id="109-doc-engine-core-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/109_doc_engine_core_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Hardcoded log file path with predictable name and truncation

- **Location:** `Core::Core()`, approximately line 30 of the .cpp (the `filename = "log/log.txt"` block)
- **Issue:** The log file path is a hardcoded relative string `"log/log.txt"` opened with `std::ios::trunc`. Because the path is relative to the working directory at launch and is world-predictable, a local attacker can pre-create a symlink at `log/log.txt` pointing to an arbitrary file (e.g., `/etc/cron.d/evil`) before the process starts. The `open()` with truncation will then wipe or overwrite that target. No validation of the resolved path is performed before opening.
- **Impact:** Local privilege escalation or denial of service through symlink-assisted file truncation/overwrite if the engine runs with elevated privileges; log data loss if the symlink target is `/dev/null`.
- **Fix:** Resolve the canonical path with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical()` after constructing the path, verify it falls within an expected directory prefix, and use `O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX) or equivalent when opening. On Linux, open the parent directory with `open(dir, O_PATH|O_DIRECTORY)` then `openat(dirfd, "log.txt", O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC|O_NOFOLLOW, 0600)` to prevent symlink substitution.

---

### MEDIUM `Core::g_Core` raw global pointer — use-after-free and signal-handler race

- **Location:** `Core.h` static declaration; used in `Core::CheckSignalFlags()` (calling `g_Core->RequestQuit()` and `g_Core->PushEvent(...)`)
- **Issue:** `g_Core` is a raw `Core*` set to `this` in the constructor and never cleared to `nullptr` on destruction. `CheckSignalFlags()` is called from the main-loop polling path and dereferences `g_Core` without any null or liveness check. If a second `Core` instance is ever constructed, or if the destructor runs and `CheckSignalFlags()` is somehow re-entered (e.g., from a late-arriving signal handled on a background thread on Windows), the pointer is dangling. Additionally, because `g_Core` is a plain `Core*` (not `std::atomic<Core*>`), the signal-handler path (which calls `_exit` rather than touching `g_Core`) is safe, but the main-loop consumer path has no memory ordering guarantee on platforms where the compiler can cache the pointer.
- **Impact:** Crash (denial of service) or, in adversarial scenarios where memory layout is controllable, potential exploitation of the dangling pointer.
- **Fix:** Null `g_Core` in the destructor. Guard the dereferences in `CheckSignalFlags()` with a null check. Declare `g_Core` as `std::atomic<Core*>` (or protect with a mutex) if any code path other than the single main thread reads or writes it.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled thread-pool size from external config — uncontrolled allocation / DoS

- **Location:** `Core::Start()`, the line `m_ThreadPool.Reset(m_EngineConfig.m_MaxThreads + THREADS_REQUIRED_BY_APP)`
- **Issue:** `m_MaxThreads` comes directly from `ConfigParser::EngineConfig` (externally supplied configuration file) with no visible upper-bound check in this file. An arbitrarily large value causes spawning of an unbounded number of OS threads, exhausting stack address space and OS thread limits.
- **Impact:** Local or config-injection denial of service; if the config file is writable by a less-privileged actor who can cause the engine to reload, it becomes a resource-exhaustion vector.
- **Fix:** Enforce a compile-time or startup-time maximum (e.g., `std::min(m_EngineConfig.m_MaxThreads, static_cast<size_t>(512))`) and log a warning when the cap is applied. The check should occur before `Reset()` is called.

---

### MEDIUM `cerr` redirected to the same custom `streambuf` as `cout` — log-integrity / post-shutdown use-after-free

- **Location:** `Core::Core()` (~line 38 of .cpp) and `Core::~Core()`
- **Issue:** `std::cerr.rdbuf(m_TerminalBuf.get())` installs a pointer to `m_TerminalBuf` in the global `std::cerr`. The destructor restores `cout` but points `cerr` to `m_OriginalCoutBuffer` (the original cout buffer), not the original cerr buffer, which is a distinct object. This is a correctness bug with a security consequence: any third-party library or OS-level code that writes to `stderr`/`cerr` after the original cerr buffer is lost will write to the wrong sink, potentially suppressing security-relevant error output or, if the original cerr buffer's lifetime ends first, causing a write through a dangling pointer.
- **Impact:** Silent loss of security-relevant error output (e.g., TLS certificate errors, libcurl warnings written to stderr); potential use-after-free write if shutdown ordering permits.
- **Fix:** Save the original `cerr` rdbuf separately (`m_OriginalCerrBuffer = std::cerr.rdbuf()`) and restore it independently in both the destructor and `Shutdown()`.

---

### LOW `JarvisRedirect` extern "C" entry point — unsanitised external string written to log

- **Location:** `JarvisRedirect()`, top of .cpp, approximately line 20
- **Issue:** This `extern "C"` function is callable from C and from embedded Python (given the project description). It accepts a raw `const char*` from an external runtime and forwards it verbatim to `std::cout` without length limits, encoding validation, or sanitisation. If the logging back-end formats this string into a structured log (e.g., spdlog JSON sink or a terminal with ANSI escape processing), a caller can inject ANSI escape sequences, newlines, or control characters to corrupt log output or spoof log entries.
- **Impact:** Log injection allowing an attacker with control over the Python environment or any native caller to forge log lines, hide malicious activity, or trigger terminal emulator vulnerabilities via escape sequences.
- **Fix:** Strip or reject non-printable characters and ANSI escape sequences from `message` before forwarding. Consider imposing a maximum length (e.g., 64 KB) to prevent a single call from flooding the log sink. Use `LOG_CORE_INFO("{}", message)` with a format string literal rather than passing `message` as the format string (the current code uses `std::cout << message` so format-string injection is not present here, but escape-sequence injection is).

---

### LOW Sleep duration read from config accepted without re-validation at use-site — assertion-based DoS

- **Location:** `Core::Run()`, the `CORE_ASSERT` and `sleep_for` block near the bottom of the run loop
- **Issue:** `m_EngineConfig.m_SleepDuration` is checked only via `CORE_ASSERT`, which in release builds is typically compiled out. If `m_SleepDuration` is zero or negative (or very large, e.g., `INT64_MAX` milliseconds) and the assert is disabled, the sleep becomes either a busy spin (CPU exhaustion) or an indefinitely blocked main loop (denial of service). The config value flows in from an external file without a runtime guard here.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: either 100% CPU utilisation or a permanently unresponsive main loop if the config is tampered with or malformed.
- **Fix:** Replace `CORE_ASSERT` with a runtime clamp: `auto sleepMs = std::clamp(m_EngineConfig.m_SleepDuration, std::chrono::milliseconds(1), std::chrono::milliseconds(256));` and use that clamped value in `sleep_for`. Log a warning if clamping was necessary.

### 10_doc_application_cloud_connectorHttp_h

<a id="10-doc-application-cloud-connectorhttp-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/10_doc_application_cloud_connectorHttp_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — `OpensocketStrictCallback` opens a real socket for non-IPCXN purposes without an SSRF guard

- **Location:** `OpensocketStrictCallback`, the `purpose != CURLSOCKTYPE_IPCXN` branch (~line 195)
- **Issue:** When `purpose` is anything other than `CURLSOCKTYPE_IPCXN` (e.g., `CURLSOCKTYPE_ACCEPT`), the callback calls `socket()` directly and returns it without running `IsLocalNetworkHost`. The `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION` documentation says the callback is responsible for creating *all* sockets for the handle. In practice libcurl only calls this for outgoing connections today, but relying on that undocumented assumption means a future libcurl version or an unusual protocol path (e.g., FTP active mode, if ever enabled) could open a socket to a local address that bypassed the post-resolve check.
- **Impact:** An attacker who somehow triggers a non-IPCXN socket creation (e.g., a redirect to an FTP URL if protocol restrictions are ever loosened) could bypass the SSRF guard entirely for that socket.
- **Fix:** Return `CURL_SOCKET_BAD` for any `purpose` other than `CURLSOCKTYPE_IPCXN` instead of calling `socket()` and passing through. The current `ApplyHardenedDefaults` path already disables redirects, so no legitimate caller needs a non-IPCXN socket from this callback.

---

### MEDIUM — `ApplyExecutorRedirectDefaults` does not install `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION` for the *redirect* targets

- **Location:** `ApplyExecutorRedirectDefaults`, curl options block (~lines 330–348)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_OPENSOCKETFUNCTION` (i.e., `OpensocketStrictCallback`) is installed only when the *initial* URL starts with `https://`. When libcurl follows a `301`/`302` redirect, it resolves and connects to the redirect target using the same handle and therefore the same opensocket callback — that part is fine. However, `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS_STR` is set to `"https"` but `CURLOPT_UNRESTRICTED_AUTH` is not explicitly set to `0L`. By default libcurl does not send `Authorization` headers after a cross-origin redirect, but the posture is not explicitly hardened. More critically, `MAXREDIRS=10` combined with the post-resolve IP check means 10 DNS round-trips can occur before the connection attempt that triggers the socket callback; a TOCTOU window exists between DNS resolution cached by libcurl and a second resolution if TTL expires mid-redirect chain.
- **Impact:** A carefully timed DNS TTL expiry during a redirect chain (DNS rebinding) could cause the post-resolve check to pass on first resolution but the actual connection to reach an internal host on a subsequent hop where the OS resolver returns a different IP.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to a lower value (3 is sufficient for the stated S3/Graph use cases). Add `curl_easy_setopt(handle, CURLOPT_UNRESTRICTED_AUTH, 0L)` to prevent credential forwarding on cross-origin redirects. Document that DNS rebinding during multi-hop redirects is a residual risk that cannot be fully mitigated at the libcurl layer without per-hop IP inspection.

---

### MEDIUM — `IsLocalNetworkHost` does not cover all loopback addresses or the `0.0.0.0` catch-all

- **Location:** `IsLocalNetworkHost`, entire function (~lines 383–440)
- **Issue:** The function checks `127.0.0.1` and the `127.` prefix, but `0.0.0.0` (which on Linux routes to the local machine when used as a connect target in some contexts) and the full `127.0.0.0/8` range beyond `127.*.*.*` strings are covered only by prefix match — that is actually correct for IPv4. However, the IPv6 loopback `::1` is checked by exact string match only. Compressed IPv6 forms of the same address (e.g., `0:0:0:0:0:0:0:1`, `::0001`) are not `IsIPv6Literal`-classified as local because they pass the hex+colon test but don't start with `fc`, `fd`, or `fe80`. Additionally `0.0.0.0` is not checked and can be a valid connect target on some platforms.
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying `0.0.0.0` or a non-canonical IPv6 loopback form as a host value could bypass the SSRF guard in `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` (which calls `IsLocalNetworkHost`) and potentially reach a local service.
- **Fix:** Add `host == "0.0.0.0"` to the exact-match block. For IPv6 loopback, after confirming `IsIPv6Literal`, also check that the string is not all-zeros-plus-one by normalizing via `inet_pton` + `inet_ntop` and re-running the check, or by adding an explicit comparison against known compressed forms (`"::1"` is already there, but `"0:0:0:0:0:0:0:1"` etc. are not).

---

### MEDIUM — `BoundedStringWriteCallback` is susceptible to integer overflow on `size * nmemb`

- **Location:** `BoundedStringWriteCallback`, ~line 222
- **Issue:** `size * nmemb` is computed as `std::size_t` multiplication with no overflow check. libcurl's documentation guarantees `size` is always `1`, but this is a documentation convention, not an ABI guarantee. If both values are attacker-influenced (e.g., via a malicious server in a test-connection scenario), a product overflow could produce a small `incoming` value that passes the cap check while `buf->append` writes a much larger region.
- **Impact:** If `size` and `nmemb` are crafted to overflow, the cap `kMaxConnectorResponseBytes` is bypassed and an unbounded `append` proceeds, causing heap exhaustion or a crash (DoS).
- **Fix:** Add an overflow-safe check before the multiplication: `if (nmemb > kMaxConnectorResponseBytes || size > kMaxConnectorResponseBytes / nmemb) return 0;` before computing `incoming`.

---

### LOW — `UrlEncodePathSegments` silently truncates paths ending with `/`

- **Location:** `UrlEncodePathSegments`, segment-loop boundary condition (~lines 285–315)
- **Issue:** The loop condition is `pos <= path.size()`. When a path ends with `/`, the last iteration sets `segment` to `path.substr(path.size(), 0)` — an empty string — which triggers the `segment.empty()` error path and returns an empty string with an error. This is documented as intentional ("reject empty segment"), but a trailing `/` is a valid path component in some vendor APIs (e.g., S3 key prefixes). More importantly, the check happens *after* `firstSegment` manipulation, so the error message "empty path segment" could be surfaced to a caller who passes a semantically valid path, causing a silent functional failure where a non-empty `outError` is returned but the caller may not check it.
- **Impact:** If a caller does not check `outError` on a non-empty return value, it may use an empty string as a URL path segment, resulting in a malformed request rather than a rejected one. No direct security bypass, but contributes to unpredictable behavior at the security boundary.
- **Fix:** Audit all callers to ensure they check `outError` when the return value is empty. Consider documenting explicitly that trailing slashes are rejected, or add a separate validation path that strips a single trailing slash before processing.

---

### LOW — Counter exposure via `/api/debug/signals` leaks gate-firing frequency to non-admin observers if the endpoint is insufficiently access-controlled

- **Location:** Header comment on `GetDnsResolvedIpRejectionCount` et al. (~header lines 120–140)
- **Issue:** The header documents that these counters are "surfaced via `/api/debug/signals` for live operator monitoring." The implementation itself is correct, but if the `/api/debug/signals` endpoint is not gated behind a strong authorization check, an attacker can query rejection frequencies to learn the rate of SSRF probing being blocked, calibrate timing attacks, or confirm whether a particular probe pattern triggers the DNS-resolved-IP counter.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: an attacker learns the effectiveness of their SSRF probes in near-real time.
- **Fix:** Ensure `/api/debug/signals` is protected by an admin-level authorization check (not just authentication). This is an architectural note since the access control is implemented elsewhere, but the header comment should explicitly state the access-control requirement rather than leaving it implicit.


---

### 110_doc_engine_curlWrapper_authSigner_h

<a id="110-doc-engine-curlwrapper-authsigner-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/110_doc_engine_curlWrapper_authSigner_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM HTTP Header Injection via Unsanitized API Key
- **Location:** `BearerSigner::Apply`, `XGoogApiKeySigner::Apply`, `AnthropicXApiKeySigner::Apply`, `AzureApiKeySigner::Apply` — all `Apply` methods in `authSigner.cpp`
- **Issue:** `q.m_ApiKey` is concatenated directly into HTTP header strings (e.g., `"Authorization: Bearer " + q.m_ApiKey`) without any sanitization of CR (`\r`), LF (`\n`), or NUL characters. If the API key value originates from user-controlled input (workflow configuration, secrets store, etc.) and contains CRLF sequences, an attacker can inject arbitrary additional HTTP headers into the outgoing request.
- **Impact:** Depending on how libcurl handles injected CRLF in header strings passed via `curl_slist_append`, an attacker who can influence the API key value could inject headers such as `X-Forwarded-For`, override `Content-Type`, or smuggle second requests. Even if libcurl rejects the header internally, the failure may be silent, sending a request without the auth header — a partial auth bypass.
- **Fix:** Before constructing the header string, validate that `q.m_ApiKey` contains no characters outside the printable ASCII range excluding `\r` and `\n` (i.e., bytes 0x20–0x7E minus `\r`/`\n`). Concretely, extend `ValidateApiKey` to scan for `\r`, `\n`, and `\0` and return false with a descriptive error if found. This check belongs in `ValidateApiKey` so all four signers inherit it automatically.

### LOW Unhandled `AuthStyle` Throws Across ABI Boundary / Uncaught Exception DoS
- **Location:** `IAuthSigner::Get`, bottom of function — `authSigner.cpp`
- **Issue:** `IAuthSigner::Get` throws `std::logic_error` for unrecognized `AuthStyle` values. If the `AuthStyle` enum value arrives from deserialized/external input (e.g., a workflow configuration decoded from JSON) and the caller does not catch this exception, the exception propagates up the call stack. If any caller site is `noexcept` or the exception crosses a C-linkage boundary (e.g., a Python embedding layer or a C callback), the result is `std::terminate` — a crash of the entire engine process.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit a workflow configuration with an out-of-range integer for `AuthStyle` can crash the engine process, causing denial of service.
- **Fix:** Validate the `AuthStyle` value against known enum members at the deserialization/ingestion point before it reaches `IAuthSigner::Get`, so invalid values never arrive here. Additionally, audit all call sites of `IAuthSigner::Get` to ensure the exception is caught and converted to a structured error response rather than propagating to `noexcept` or C-linkage frames.

### LOW Secret Value Exposure in `errorMessage` Propagation Path
- **Location:** `ValidateApiKey` — `authSigner.cpp`; all `Apply` overrides
- **Issue:** The comment block on `IAuthSigner::Apply` states the caller is responsible for structured ERROR logging. The `errorMessage` string produced by `ValidateApiKey` (e.g., `"Bearer: API key is empty or whitespace"`) is safe on its own, but the pattern establishes a convention where callers log `errorMessage` verbatim. If a future signer (or a current one extended under pressure) includes credential material in `errorMessage` — for instance, a SigV4 canonical string or a partial key for debugging — that string will be emitted to the run dashboard log. There is no interface-level constraint preventing this.
- **Impact:** Future maintainers following the existing pattern could inadvertently log secret material to the structured run log, making credentials visible to anyone with dashboard read access.
- **Fix:** Add a code comment to the `IAuthSigner` interface declaration explicitly prohibiting inclusion of credential values in `errorMessage`, and consider a type-level wrapper (e.g., a `SafeErrorMessage` struct) that serves as a documentation anchor. This is a defense-in-depth measure against future regression.


---

### 111_doc_engine_curlWrapper_awsSigV4_h

<a id="111-doc-engine-curlwrapper-awssigv4-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/111_doc_engine_curlWrapper_awsSigV4_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — HTTP header injection via unvalidated URL-derived host value

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Apply` and `SigV4Signer::Sign`, approximately the block starting at `outHeaders.push_back("Host: " + out.m_Host)`; host originates from `ParseUrl(in.m_Url)` with no sanitization.
- **Issue:** `ParseUrl` extracts `m_Host` directly from the caller-supplied `q.m_Url` without stripping or rejecting CR (`\r`), LF (`\n`), or NUL characters. The extracted host is then concatenated verbatim into an HTTP header string: `outHeaders.push_back("Host: " + out.m_Host)`. If the URL contains an embedded CRLF sequence (e.g., `https://evil.com\r\nX-Injected: bad/path`), the resulting `outHeaders` entry splits into two headers when libcurl or any HTTP/1.1 layer serializes the header list. The same raw value is also embedded in the SigV4 canonical request and the `Authorization` header's `Credential` scope, but those are HMAC-protected and the damage is to the transport layer, not the signature.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the URL (e.g., via a misconfigured workflow that passes user-supplied endpoint strings into `q.m_Url`) can inject arbitrary HTTP headers into the outgoing Bedrock request. This could be used to override security-relevant headers, poison request routing, or, if the host value is reflected anywhere in a response log, facilitate log injection.
- **Fix:** After `ParseUrl` returns, validate `out.m_Host` (and `url.m_Path`, `url.m_Query`) against a strict allowlist: reject any character outside the set valid for a DNS hostname or IP literal (alphanumerics, `.`, `-`, `[`, `]`, `:` for IPv6/port). At minimum, assert the absence of `\r`, `\n`, and NUL before constructing any header string. A one-liner reject: `if (out.m_Host.find_first_of("\r\n\0") != std::string::npos) { /* error */ }`.

---

### MEDIUM — Signing-key material not cleared from `RunSelfTest` stack/heap

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::RunSelfTest`, self-test block (2), approximately the lines declaring `kSecret`, `kDate`, `kRegion`, `kService`, `kSigning` as bare `std::vector<unsigned char>`.
- **Issue:** In `Sign()` the intermediate key material is wrapped in `ScopedSecretBytes` which calls `OPENSSL_cleanse` on destruction. In `RunSelfTest`, the equivalent key derivation chain uses raw `auto` variables (`auto kSecret = StringToBytes(...)`, etc.) that are plain `std::vector<unsigned char>` with no cleansing on scope exit. The AWS-published example key (`wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG+bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY`) is a well-known public constant, so the direct exposure of *that specific* secret is negligible. However, the omission establishes an inconsistent pattern: if a developer copies this block and replaces the example key with a real key (e.g., for a more targeted self-test), the real key material will survive in heap memory after the function returns.
- **Impact:** Residual key bytes in heap memory are readable via a heap-dump, use-after-free read, or exposure through a memory-disclosure vulnerability elsewhere in the process. Low immediate risk given the test key is public, but a latent hygiene gap.
- **Fix:** Wrap all intermediate vectors in `ScopedSecretBytes` in `RunSelfTest`, mirroring `Sign()`. Alternatively, call the existing `Sign()` function with the known-answer inputs directly rather than re-implementing the derivation inline (which also reduces duplicated logic).

---

### LOW — `m_AmzDate` substring without length guard can produce silent wrong output

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, line `std::string const dateStamp = out.m_AmzDate.substr(0, 8);`
- **Issue:** `out.m_AmzDate` is taken directly from `in.m_AmzDate` (which is caller-supplied) without validating that it is exactly 16 characters in the expected `YYYYMMDDTHHMMSSz` format. `substr(0, 8)` on a string shorter than 8 characters does not throw (it returns as many characters as are available), so a truncated or malformed date produces a silently wrong `dateStamp` that becomes part of the `credentialScope` and signing key derivation, resulting in a request that will fail at AWS with a 403—not a local error. No `IsBlank` check is performed on `m_AmzDate` when it is caller-supplied and non-empty.
- **Impact:** A misconfigured or adversarially crafted `m_AmzDate` produces a request signed with an incorrect credential scope. AWS will reject it (403), but the failure is silent and hard to diagnose. There is no amplification beyond a failed API call.
- **Fix:** When `in.m_AmzDate` is non-empty, validate it matches the regex `^\d{8}T\d{6}Z$` (16 chars, exact format) before use. Return an error (empty `SignedHeaders`) if validation fails, and log the malformed value.

---

### LOW — Signature of well-known test credentials logged on self-test failure

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::RunSelfTest`, `LOG_CORE_ERROR` calls in tests (3) and (4) that emit `a.m_Authorization` (which contains the full HMAC signature over the example key).
- **Issue:** On a self-test failure the full `Authorization` header value, including the computed HMAC signature, is emitted to the error log. For the example key (`wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG+bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY`) this is harmless because the key is public. If the test is ever extended with real credentials (or if this logging pattern is copied to `Apply()`), the signature—and by extension the key material it was derived from—would appear in logs.
- **Impact:** Negligible for current test vectors; relevant as a pattern risk if propagated to production paths.
- **Fix:** On self-test failure, log only the first N characters of the authorization value (e.g., the algorithm and credential scope prefix) rather than the full header including the signature hex. Keep the expected value in the log for comparison but mask or omit the actual computed signature bytes.


---
