### 114_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlMultiDispatcher_h

<a id="114-doc-engine-curlwrapper-curlmultidispatcher-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/114_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlMultiDispatcher_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH TLS Certificate Verification Disabled in Debug Builds with Host-Controlled Bypass

- **Location:** `SetupEasyHandle`, approximately lines 310–320 (the `#ifdef DEBUG` block)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are set to 0 when the extracted host equals `"localhost"`, `"127.0.0.1"`, or `"::1"`. The host is derived from `req.m_Url`, which ultimately comes from caller-supplied `QueryData::m_Url`. If an attacker or misconfigured config causes a URL like `https://localhost/redirect-to-evil` to be submitted, or if the URL parser in `ExtractHost` can be confused, verification is silently disabled. More critically, the guard is purely string-based on the URL the *client* sends — not on the actual peer the TLS stack connected to. Any debug build deployed to a shared or staging environment carries this permanently disabled verification for those host strings.
- **Impact:** In a debug build deployed outside a developer laptop (CI, staging, internal shared server), an attacker who can influence the `m_Url` field (e.g., via a crafted workflow config or SSRF-triggering input) can force requests through a TLS-stripped proxy while the client believes it is talking to localhost, bypassing all certificate checks.
- **Fix:** Remove the `#ifdef DEBUG` block entirely. For hermetic tests, use a test CA bundle and set `CURLOPT_CAINFO` to the test CA instead of disabling verification. Never disable `SSL_VERIFYPEER`/`SSL_VERIFYHOST` based on caller-supplied host strings.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled Retry Queue Growth Leading to Memory Exhaustion (DoS)

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, approximately lines 460–500 (429 retry block) and lines 510–540 (transient error retry block); `m_RetryQueue` declaration in header
- **Issue:** `m_RetryQueue` is a `std::vector<RetryEntry>` with no enforced upper bound. Every 429 response that still has retry budget pushes a new `RetryEntry`. Every 502/503/500/400 response does the same. A hostile or misbehaving AI provider that continuously returns 429 or 503 with valid-looking rate-limit headers will cause the queue to grow without limit because `m_RetryCount` resets with each new `Submit()` call — and the inbox itself is also unbounded. Combined with `kDefaultMaxRetries429 = 10` and `kMaxActivePerHost = 48`, a sustained flood of 429s from 48 concurrent streams produces up to 480 retry entries before any request is finally dropped, and new `Submit()` calls keep refilling the inbox.
- **Impact:** Sustained memory growth leading to OOM, which crashes the engine process or triggers the bad_alloc recovery loop, effectively making the dispatcher unresponsive.
- **Fix:** Add a hard cap on `m_RetryQueue.size()` before pushing. When the cap is exceeded, fail the oldest (or the new) entry immediately via its callback with a retriable error code rather than silently growing unbounded.

---

### HIGH Inbox Unbounded: Denial of Service via Unlimited Submit() Calls

- **Location:** `Submit()`, approximately lines 210–240; `m_Inbox` declaration in header
- **Issue:** `m_Inbox` is a `std::queue<PendingRequest>` with no capacity limit. `Submit()` holds `m_InboxMutex` only to check `m_Stopping` and push; it never checks the queue depth. Any caller (including the workflow engine itself processing adversarial input) can call `Submit()` in a tight loop and exhaust heap memory. Each `PendingRequest` contains a copy of `QueryData` which holds URL, POST body, headers, and auth material — potentially kilobytes each.
- **Impact:** Heap exhaustion / OOM crash of the engine process. An attacker with the ability to trigger AI requests (e.g., via a malicious workflow or webhook) can cause a denial of service.
- **Fix:** Impose a maximum inbox depth (e.g., 10,000 entries). When the inbox is full, `Submit()` should immediately invoke the callback with a failure result rather than enqueuing.

---

### HIGH Use-After-Move of `req` After Erase in `DrainCompleted`

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, approximately lines 555–590 (the normal completion path, inner lock block)
- **Issue:** After the `responseBody`, `callback`, and `hdrs` are moved/copied out of `req`, the code takes a second `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex>` on `m_DebugMutex` (which is already held by the outer lock earlier in the same call stack — but the outer lock went out of scope before this inner block). Inside that second lock block, `curl_multi_remove_handle`, `curl_slist_free_all`, and `m_Active.erase(it)` are called. The iterator `it` was obtained from `m_Active.find(easy)` before the first lock was released. However, `DrainCompleted` itself is called from `IoThreadFunc` which does not hold `m_DebugMutex` on entry; `DrainCompleted` acquires it per-message. Between releasing the outer per-message lock (when the `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex> debugLock` goes out of scope) and re-acquiring it for the cleanup block, another thread calling `GetDebugSnapshot()` could read `m_Active` in an inconsistent state where the handle has been cleaned up by curl but is still present in `m_Active`. More concretely: `it` is a `std::unordered_map` iterator; `m_Active.erase(it)` in the nested lock block is safe only if no concurrent modification occurred — but `DrainPendingCancellations` can erase from `m_Active` while holding `m_DebugMutex` from a different call on the same I/O thread. Since `m_DebugMutex` is recursive, the nested lock succeeds, but if `DrainPendingCancellations` (called earlier in the same loop iteration via the `try` block) already erased this entry, `it` is now a dangling iterator and `m_Active.erase(it)` is undefined behavior.
- **Impact:** Undefined behavior / heap corruption if a cancellation races with natural completion for the same handle within one I/O thread loop iteration.
- **Fix:** Re-validate the iterator after re-acquiring the lock: use `m_Active.find(easy)` again inside the cleanup block rather than reusing the pre-move `it`.

---

### MEDIUM HTTP Header Injection via Unsanitized Auth Headers

- **Location:** `SetupEasyHandle`, approximately lines 285–295 (the `authHeaders` loop calling `curl_slist_append`)
- **Issue:** Auth headers returned by `IAuthSigner::Get(...).Apply(...)` are appended directly to the curl slist without any sanitization for embedded newlines (`\r\n`). If the `IAuthSigner` implementation constructs headers from external input (e.g., a configured API key or signing credential containing a newline), a CRLF sequence in the header value could inject additional HTTP headers into the outbound request. libcurl does strip bare `\n` in modern versions, but the defense depends entirely on the libcurl version linked.
- **Impact:** If an attacker controls the API key or signing material stored in config, they can inject arbitrary headers into outbound AI provider requests — potentially smuggling requests or bypassing provider-side controls.
- **Fix:** Validate each string in `authHeaders` before appending: reject or strip any string containing `\r` or `\n`. This should be enforced in `SetupEasyHandle` as a defense-in-depth measure regardless of what `IAuthSigner` does.

---

### MEDIUM SSRF: No Validation of `QueryData::m_Url` Against an Allowlist

- **Location:** `SetupEasyHandle`, approximately line 300 (`curl_easy_setopt(easy, CURLOPT_URL, req.m_Url.c_str())`); `DrainInbox`, approximately line 380 (`req->m_Url = pending.m_QueryData.m_Url`)
- **Issue:** The URL passed to `CURLOPT_URL` is taken directly from `QueryData::m_Url` with no validation against any allowlist of permitted AI provider hostnames. Any caller that can populate `QueryData::m_Url` with an arbitrary value — including workflow engine steps that may be influenced by user-supplied workflow definitions or webhook payloads — can direct the dispatcher to make outbound HTTP requests to any reachable host, including internal services (`http://169.254.169.254/` for cloud metadata, internal microservices, etc.).
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: an attacker who can submit a workflow step can probe internal network topology, reach cloud metadata endpoints to retrieve IAM credentials, or attack internal services that trust requests from the engine host.
- **Fix:** Maintain a compile-time or config-time allowlist of permitted hostnames/prefixes. Validate `QueryData::m_Url` against this allowlist in `Submit()` before enqueuing, and reject with an immediate callback failure if the URL does not match.

---

### MEDIUM Retry Delay Integer Overflow in Exponential Backoff

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, approximately line 460: `int delayMs = baseRetryMs * (1 << (retryCount - 1));`
- **Issue:** `retryCount` can reach up to `kDefaultMaxRetries429` (10) or a caller-supplied value. `1 << (retryCount - 1)` with `retryCount = 10` produces `1 << 9 = 512`; with `baseRetryMs = 1000`, `delayMs = 512000` (about 8.5 minutes), which fits in an `int`. However, if a caller sets `m_MaxRetries429` to a value ≥ 32 (which is not bounds-checked anywhere in this file), `1 << 31` is undefined behavior in C++ for signed `int`, and `1 << 32` or higher is also UB. The `m_BaseRetryMs` field is also caller-controlled and unchecked; a large value multiplied by the shift result could overflow before the cast to `std::chrono::milliseconds`.
- **Impact:** Undefined behavior manifesting as a very short or negative delay (retry storms) or a very long delay (effective denial of retry service).
- **Fix:** Cap `retryCount - 1` to a safe maximum (e.g., 20) before the shift, use unsigned arithmetic or `std::min` with a hard ceiling on `delayMs` (e.g., 120,000 ms as already partially enforced in the reset-time branch), and bounds-check `m_MaxRetries429` and `m_BaseRetryMs` on ingestion.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets Potentially Logged in Auth Error Path

- **Location:** `SetupEasyHandle`, approximately lines 275–282 (the `LOG_CORE_ERROR` on auth signer failure)
- **Issue:** When `IAuthSigner::Apply` fails, `authError` is logged verbatim: `LOG_CORE_ERROR("... : {}", authError)`. The content of `authError` is produced by the signer implementation and is not reviewed in this file. If the signer includes credential fragments (partial API key, signing string, canonical request with auth header) in its error message — which some signing implementations do for diagnostics — these will appear in the application log.
- **Impact:** API keys or signing material written to log files, log aggregation systems, or the dashboard run analyzer, accessible to anyone with log read access.
- **Fix:** Define a contract that `IAuthSigner::Apply` error messages must never include credential material. Additionally, consider logging only the error *category* here (e.g., `"auth signer rejected"`) and discarding the full `authError` string from the structured log, or routing it only to a scrubbed audit log.

---

### LOW `DrainCompleted` Holds `m_DebugMutex` While Invoking User Callback

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, approximately lines 550–600 (the cleanup inner lock block and `callback(result, ...)` call at the end)
- **Issue:** Looking at the normal completion path: `responseBody`, `callback`, and `hdrs` are moved out of `req` *before* the second `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex>` block. The `callback` invocation happens *after* the cleanup lock is released (callback is called after the inner block). This is correct. However, the 429-retry path and transient-error retry path both acquire `m_DebugMutex` (via `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex> lock(m_DebugMutex)`) and erase from `m_Active` while still inside the outer `debugLock` scope at the top of `DrainCompleted`. Since `m_DebugMutex` is recursive, this is not a deadlock — but it means `GetDebugSnapshot()` callers will block for the entire duration of the retry processing including the `m_RetryQueue.push_back`. This is a latency concern that becomes a correctness concern if any callback (even in the cancel path) attempts to call `GetDebugSnapshot()` or `ResetTestState()` re-entrantly.
- **Impact:** Potential lock contention causing API threads to stall; unlikely deadlock given recursive mutex, but re-entrant callback scenarios are fragile.
- **Fix:** Move data out of `m_Active` under the lock, release the lock, then invoke callbacks — which is already done correctly in the normal completion path. Apply the same pattern consistently to the 429 and transient-error retry branches.


---

### 115_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlWrapper_h

<a id="115-doc-engine-curlwrapper-curlwrapper-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/115_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlWrapper_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Verbose mode leaks full request payload and URL (including sensitive prompt content) to logs

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, the `if (Core::g_Core->Verbose())` block, approximately lines 290–294
- **Issue:** When verbose mode is enabled, `LOG_CORE_INFO("url: {}, data: {}", url, data)` writes the full POST body (`queryData.m_Data`) to the application log. The POST body is the JSON payload sent to AI providers, which in a workflow engine context routinely contains user prompts, file contents, injected secrets, or other sensitive workflow data. Additionally, `curl_easy_setopt(m_Curl, CURLOPT_VERBOSE, 1L)` instructs libcurl to dump full request and response headers — including the `Authorization: Bearer <key>` or `x-api-key: <key>` headers built by the auth signer — to stderr or the libcurl debug callback. Without an explicit `CURLOPT_DEBUGFUNCTION` installed, libcurl writes to stderr, which may end up in systemd journals, log aggregators, or crash dumps.
- **Impact:** Any party with access to application logs or stderr output (log aggregation services, other tenants on shared infrastructure, log exfiltration via a separate vulnerability) obtains API keys for every configured AI provider as well as the full content of every AI request made while verbose mode is on.
- **Fix:** (1) Never log `data` directly; if payload debugging is needed, log a truncated, redacted form (e.g., first 200 characters, with any field named `api_key`, `secret`, `token` replaced by `[REDACTED]`). (2) Install a `CURLOPT_DEBUGFUNCTION` that explicitly skips `CURLINFO_HEADER_OUT` and `CURLINFO_HEADER_IN` lines containing `Authorization`, `x-api-key`, or `api-key` before passing through to the logger, or suppress header dump entirely in debug output.

---

### HIGH Unvalidated URL passed directly to libcurl enables SSRF

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, `curl_easy_setopt(m_Curl, CURLOPT_URL, url.c_str())`, approximately line 260
- **Issue:** `queryData.m_Url` is accepted and passed to `curl_easy_perform` without any validation of scheme, host, or port. `QueryData::IsValid()` only checks that the string is non-empty. libcurl supports dozens of schemes (`file://`, `gopher://`, `dict://`, `tftp://`, `ldap://`, `smtp://`, `ftp://`, etc.) in addition to `https://`. If any caller-controlled or config-derived data influences `m_Url`, an attacker who can write a workflow config or inject a URL can direct the engine to reach internal services (cloud metadata endpoints such as `http://169.254.169.254/`, internal Kubernetes API servers, internal databases via `dict://`), read local files via `file:///etc/passwd`, or perform port scanning.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: access to cloud instance metadata (credential theft), internal service enumeration and exploitation, local file read depending on libcurl build options.
- **Fix:** (1) Enforce an allowlist of permitted schemes (only `https://` for AI provider calls). (2) Optionally enforce an allowlist of permitted hostnames/domains drawn from config. (3) Use `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS_STR` (libcurl ≥ 7.85) or `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to restrict libcurl to HTTPS only: `curl_easy_setopt(m_Curl, CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTO_HTTPS)`. (4) Set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to the same restriction to block SSRF via redirect chains.

---

### HIGH No explicit TLS peer verification enforcement

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::CurlWrapper()` and `CurlWrapper::Query()` — no `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` setopt call appears anywhere in the visible code
- **Issue:** The code sets a CA bundle path via `CURLOPT_CAINFO` when one is found, but never explicitly sets `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L`. While libcurl's compiled-in defaults are typically peer-verification enabled, this relies on build-time defaults rather than explicit policy. More critically, if no CA bundle is found (`caBundle.empty()`), the code silently falls through to libcurl's compiled-in default CA path — which may not exist on the deployment system, causing libcurl to disable verification in some configurations. There is no assertion or fallback that verification is actually active.
- **Impact:** A network-positioned attacker can perform TLS MITM against all AI provider API calls, intercepting API keys (sent in headers) and all request/response content.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set both options unconditionally on every `m_Curl` handle after `curl_easy_init()`: `curl_easy_setopt(m_Curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(m_Curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)`. If no CA bundle path is resolved, either abort initialization or log a `CRITICAL` and fail-open only after deliberate operator override.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled response body size cap does not prevent header-based DoS; timeout of 0 is unsafe default

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, `m_TimeoutMs > 0` branch, approximately line 275; `kMaxResponseBodyBytes` cap, approximately line 240
- **Issue:** Two related issues: (1) The `m_TimeoutMs` field defaults to `0` (no timeout), and the code explicitly sets `CURLOPT_TIMEOUT_MS, 0L` in the else branch. A slow or adversarially throttled server can hold a connection open indefinitely, consuming a thread from the engine's thread pool permanently. (2) The 32 MiB cap on the response body prevents body-based OOM but does not bound response header size, connect time, or time-to-first-byte. An attacker controlling a server (possible if SSRF is exploitable) can cause indefinite hangs.
- **Impact:** Thread pool exhaustion leading to denial of service of the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** (1) Set a non-zero default timeout (e.g., 300 seconds) as the floor; treat `m_TimeoutMs == 0` as "use default" rather than "no timeout". (2) Add `CURLOPT_CONNECTTIMEOUT_MS` and `CURLOPT_LOW_SPEED_LIMIT` / `CURLOPT_LOW_SPEED_TIME` to catch stalled transfers the transfer timeout alone misses.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU in CA bundle path resolution via `std::filesystem::exists`

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()`, all `std::filesystem::exists()` calls, approximately lines 170–195
- **Issue:** The function checks `std::filesystem::exists(path)` for both the environment-variable-supplied paths and the hardcoded candidates, then returns the path string. The file is not opened and its inode is not locked at check time; it is only consumed later when libcurl performs TLS connections. Between the existence check and the actual TLS handshake, a privileged local attacker could replace the CA bundle file with a malicious one containing attacker-controlled trust anchors. This is a classic TOCTOU / symlink race.
- **Impact:** A local attacker with write access to the filesystem path could substitute a CA bundle containing attacker-controlled root certificates, allowing MITM of all TLS connections made by the engine.
- **Fix:** In addition to existence check, verify that the resolved path is owned by root and is not world-writable (using `stat(2)` directly). Consider pinning the CA bundle path to a single compile-time constant rather than accepting it from environment variables in security-sensitive deployments.

---

### MEDIUM API key presence logged at ERROR level via `IsValid()`, facilitating enumeration of key absence

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::QueryData::IsValid()`, approximately lines 218–232
- **Issue:** `IsValid()` logs `"CurlWrapper::QueryData::IsValid(): API key empty"` at ERROR level. While it does not log the key value, emitting a per-request error-level log entry for every request where the key is absent creates a reliable, machine-readable signal in log aggregators. Combined with the URL (logged on the error path in `Query()`), this pinpoints exactly which provider integration is misconfigured, aiding an insider or attacker with log access in targeting credential theft.
- **Impact:** Low-grade information disclosure: reveals which AI providers lack configured API keys.
- **Fix:** This is a minor issue; acceptable as-is in most deployments. If stricter log hygiene is required, rate-limit or deduplicate this log entry rather than emitting it per-request, and ensure it never appears in externally-accessible log streams.

---

### LOW `curl_slist_append` failure leaves `CurlSlist` in silent broken state

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::CurlSlist::Append()`, approximately lines 207–213
- **Issue:** When `curl_slist_append` returns `nullptr` (allocation failure), the code logs a CRITICAL message but does not set a failure flag and does not prevent the subsequent `Query()` call from proceeding with an incomplete or null header list. The `Get()` call on a list where a prior `Append` failed returns whatever partial state exists. In particular, if the first `Append` (an auth header) fails and `m_List` is left null, the request is dispatched to the AI provider without authentication headers — `CURLOPT_HTTPHEADER` receives a null pointer, which libcurl treats as "no custom headers".
- **Impact:** Under memory pressure, an AI API request could be sent without authentication headers, leaking the request payload to the provider unauthenticated (or receiving a 401 that reveals the target endpoint). Not directly exploitable by a remote attacker, but is a correctness and data-exposure risk.
- **Fix:** Add a boolean `m_Failed` member to `CurlSlist`, set it on null return from `curl_slist_append`, and check it in `Query()` before proceeding (treat it as a fatal pre-flight error and return `QueryResult::Fail`).


---

<a id="117-doc-engine-curlwrapper-ratelimitobservation-h"></a>

### 118_doc_engine_curlWrapper_rateLimitStrategy_h

<a id="118-doc-engine-curlwrapper-ratelimitstrategy-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/118_doc_engine_curlWrapper_rateLimitStrategy_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW: Uncontrolled integer accumulation in ParseOpenAIDurationMs
- **Location:** `ParseOpenAIDurationMs`, approximately lines 80–115
- **Issue:** The `num` and `ms` variables are plain `int`. A header value such as `"2147483647m2147483647s2147483647ms"` causes `num * 60000` to overflow signed `int` (undefined behaviour), and repeated additions to `ms` can wrap. The result is silently used to compute a `std::chrono::milliseconds` reset deadline.
- **Impact:** A malicious or compromised upstream provider can send a crafted `x-ratelimit-reset-requests` or `x-ratelimit-reset-tokens` header to produce an arbitrarily large or negative reset time, causing the rate-limit controller to either block all outbound requests indefinitely or skip rate limiting entirely.
- **Fix:** Use `int64_t` for both `num` and `ms`, add per-iteration overflow guards (e.g. reject any single component exceeding a sane ceiling such as 3,600,000 ms), and cap the final value before use.

### LOW: Negative/overflowing stoll/stoi header values accepted without range checks
- **Location:** `RateLimitStrategyOpenAI::Parse` and `RateLimitStrategyAnthropic::Parse`, lambda bodies, approximately lines 175–260
- **Issue:** `std::stoll` and `std::stoi` are called on provider-supplied header values. Negative values (e.g. `"-1"`) are not rejected before being stored in `m_RemainingRequests`, `m_RemainingCombinedTokens`, `m_RemainingInputTokens`, `m_RemainingOutputTokens`. The downstream `ShouldAdmit()` logic (not shown) presumably compares these against zero; a negative "remaining" count may bypass admission gating.
- **Impact:** A compromised or spoofed provider endpoint can inject negative token counts, potentially causing the admission controller to treat an exhausted quota as having ample headroom and submit unlimited requests.
- **Fix:** After parsing, reject or clamp values below zero before storing them in the observation struct.

### LOW: `retry-after` integer seconds not validated against implausibly large values before stoi
- **Location:** `RateLimitStrategyOpenAI::Parse` and `RateLimitStrategyAnthropic::Parse`, `retry-after` branch, approximately lines 230 and 295
- **Issue:** The upper bound check `seconds < 7200` is correct, but `std::stoi` will throw `std::out_of_range` for values larger than `INT_MAX` — and those exceptions are silently swallowed by the surrounding bare `catch(...)`. The real risk is that `stoi` on a very long numeric string (e.g. 10 000 digits) performs significant work before throwing, and bare `catch(...)` discards the failure silently, leaving `m_RetryAfter` unset when it should have been flagged.
- **Impact:** Minor: a crafted header can suppress retry-after enforcement, causing unlimited immediate retries after a 429.
- **Fix:** Pre-validate that the header value string is short (e.g. ≤ 10 characters) and contains only ASCII digits before calling `stoi`/`stoll`.

### LOW: Header buffer parsed without a size cap (uncontrolled allocation / DoS)
- **Location:** `IterateHeaderLines`, approximately lines 55–100
- **Issue:** `headerBuffer` is iterated and `line` substrings are allocated without any limit on the total buffer size or on individual line length. If the caller accumulates a very large response header buffer (e.g. from a slow or malicious server sending gigabytes before `\n`), `headerBuffer.substr` will allocate correspondingly large strings on the heap.
- **Impact:** A compromised or SSRF-reachable upstream endpoint can cause unbounded heap allocation in the I/O thread, leading to OOM or extreme latency.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum header buffer size (e.g. 64 KB) before calling `IterateHeaderLines`, or add a line-length guard inside the loop before calling `substr`.

### NONE (remaining areas)
No issues found in: cryptography use, authentication/authorization, shell/SQL/log injection, path traversal, SSRF, TLS configuration, hardcoded secrets, memory safety (no raw pointers, no manual allocation), race conditions (singletons are stateless and `const`), or insecure deserialization.


---

### 119_doc_engine_engine_h

<a id="119-doc-engine-engine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/119_doc_engine_engine_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Silent no-op of all security-relevant log macros in distribution builds

- **Location:** Preprocessor block near line 90–130 (`#else` branch of `#ifdef LOGGING_AND_ASSERTS`)
- **Issue:** When `DISTRIBUTION_BUILD` is defined, `LOG_SECURITY_INFO`, `LOG_SECURITY_WARN`, `CORE_HARD_STOP`, and every other log macro expand to empty statement blocks `{}`. Security-significant events (authentication decisions, key-load failures, connection-file parse errors, master-password unlock) are silently swallowed. An operator deploying a distribution build gets zero audit trail.
- **Impact:** Intrusion, misconfiguration, and key-management failures become invisible to operators and SIEM systems in the exact builds shipped to production.
- **Fix:** Maintain a separate, always-on security-log sink that is not gated on `LOGGING_AND_ASSERTS`. `LOG_SECURITY_INFO` and `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` should be unconditionally active regardless of build type. The debug/trace macros may be no-ops, but security events must not be.

---

### MEDIUM — `CORE_ASSERT` does not halt execution; assertion body is also silently dropped in distribution

- **Location:** `CORE_ASSERT` macro definition, approximately line 66 and line 103
- **Issue:** In debug builds `CORE_ASSERT(x, str)` only logs a critical message when `x` is false — it does **not** stop execution. Code that relies on `CORE_ASSERT` to enforce a security invariant (e.g., a null-pointer check, a bounds check, an authorization pre-condition) will log the failure and then continue executing with the violated invariant. In distribution builds the check disappears entirely.
- **Impact:** A violated security pre-condition (null key manager, un-initialized logger pointer) will be silently bypassed, potentially leading to use of uninitialized state or skipped access controls.
- **Fix:** `CORE_ASSERT` should `std::abort()` or `throw` after logging. For truly hard invariants that must be enforced even in distribution builds, use an unconditional check rather than a logging macro.

---

### MEDIUM — Plaintext `keys.json` loaded without any warning or user-visible indication of the risk

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately line 195–204
- **Issue:** The code loads a plaintext API-key file from the working directory as a "development convenience" with no runtime warning visible to end-users or operators. The comment says "development convenience" but there is no mechanism that prevents this path from being reached in production. There is no check that verifies the file has restricted permissions, and its path (`keys.json`) is hardcoded and fixed.
- **Impact:** If a plaintext `keys.json` is present on a production host (accidentally committed, left from a migration, or created by an attacker with write access), API keys are loaded silently with no indication in a distribution build's output (because `LOG_CORE_WARN` is a no-op there). An attacker who can place a file in the working directory can supply their own key material.
- **Fix:** Emit an unconditional `stderr` warning (not a log macro) when plaintext key loading succeeds, e.g. `fprintf(stderr, "WARNING: loaded plaintext keys.json — not suitable for production\n")`. Also verify the file's permission bits on POSIX (mode 0600 or stricter) and refuse to load if they are wider.

---

### MEDIUM — TOCTOU between `std::filesystem::exists` / `file_size` and subsequent `std::ifstream` open for `connections.json`

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately line 213–247
- **Issue:** The code calls `std::filesystem::exists`, then `std::filesystem::file_size`, then `std::ifstream(connectionsPath)` as three separate syscalls. Between the size check and the open, the file can be replaced with a larger or differently-structured file (symlink swap, race by another process). The in-memory `std::string` constructed from `istreambuf_iterator` has no secondary size cap — the cap only gates entry into the block, but the actual read uses an uncapped iterator.
- **Impact:** On a system where the working directory is writable by another process or user, an attacker can substitute a multi-gigabyte file between the stat and the open, causing unbounded heap allocation and OOM/DoS. A symlink swap can also redirect the read to an arbitrary path.
- **Fix:** Open the file first (`O_NOFOLLOW` on POSIX to block symlink attacks), then check its size via `fstat` on the open file descriptor before reading, and enforce the size cap on the actual bytes read (e.g., read at most `kMaxConnectionsFileBytes + 1` bytes and reject if that many arrive).

---

### MEDIUM — Watchdog thread detached with references to stack-allocated synchronization objects

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately line 258–270
- **Issue:** `watchdogMutex`, `watchdogCV`, and `watchdogDiffused` are local variables on `engine()`'s stack frame. The watchdog thread is immediately `detach()`ed. If `_exit` is never called by the watchdog and `engine()` returns normally, the stack frame is destroyed. The detached thread may still be executing its destructor or the `wait_for` at the point the stack is reclaimed by the caller, causing undefined behavior (use-after-free on stack objects).
- **Impact:** In pathological timing the watchdog thread reads from freed stack memory. This is exploitable only if the thread outlives the function, but the design makes that possible: after `notify_one()` there is no `join` to confirm the thread has exited before the function returns.
- **Fix:** Do not detach the watchdog. Instead join it after `notify_one()`, or allocate the synchronization objects on the heap (e.g., `std::shared_ptr`) so their lifetime outlives the function frame even if the thread is detached.

---

### LOW — `unknownOption` echoed verbatim to `std::cerr` without sanitization

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately line 155–159
- **Issue:** `argv[i]` (attacker-controlled on some deployment models such as web CGI wrappers, sudoers with `NOPASSWD`, or process-spawning APIs) is written directly to `stderr` via `std::cerr`. While `std::cerr` is a byte stream and not a terminal command interpreter, if stderr is connected to a log aggregator that interprets ANSI escape sequences or terminal control codes, a crafted argument containing escape sequences could cause log injection or terminal manipulation (e.g., cursor repositioning, color spoofing of preceding log lines).
- **Impact:** Log injection / terminal control-character injection when stderr is forwarded to a logging pipeline or displayed in a terminal emulator.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `unknownOption` before printing — strip or escape any byte below 0x20 (except newline/tab) and above 0x7E, or limit the printed string to printable ASCII only.

---

### LOW — `SigV4Signer::RunSelfTest()` gated on `NDEBUG`, absent in release builds

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately line 277
- **Issue:** The self-test for the AWS SigV4 signing implementation runs only when `NDEBUG` is not defined (i.e., debug builds). Release builds ship without any runtime validation that the signing code produces correct output. If the signing algorithm is broken or regressed in a release build, requests will silently use malformed signatures.
- **Impact:** Not a direct attack vector, but a broken signing implementation in production could silently accept or generate invalid signatures, potentially bypassing HMAC-based authentication with AWS services.
- **Fix:** Run `SigV4Signer::RunSelfTest()` unconditionally (or gate it on a dedicated `ENABLE_SELF_TESTS` flag that is on by default) so release builds also validate crypto primitive correctness at startup.


---

### 11_doc_application_cloud_azureBlobConnector_h

<a id="11-doc-application-cloud-azureblobconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/11_doc_application_cloud_azureBlobConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated custom endpoint in BuildEndpointUrl

- **Location:** `AzureBlobConnector::BuildEndpointUrl`, approximately lines 130–142
- **Issue:** `BuildEndpointUrl` returns `connection.m_Endpoint` verbatim after only stripping a trailing slash. There is no scheme check, no hostname allowlist, and no call to `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint`. The SSRF guard in `TestConnection` (lines ~165–170) calls `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` only when `m_Endpoint` is non-empty *and* the `TestConnection` path is taken. Any other caller of `BuildEndpointUrl` — present or future — receives the raw value. Even within `TestConnection` itself, the URL passed to `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_URL, ...)` is built from `BuildEndpointUrl` *after* the validation check, so the validated value and the used value are coupled only by convention, not enforcement.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply a `CloudConnection` (e.g., through the config API or a deserialized workflow) can point the connector at `http://169.254.169.254/`, internal RFC-1918 addresses, `file://`, or `gopher://` URIs. This enables cloud-metadata exfiltration, internal service scanning, or credential theft.
- **Fix:** Move the SSRF validation into `BuildEndpointUrl` itself so every caller is protected regardless of call site. Validate scheme (require `https` for non-loopback), call `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` before returning, and return an empty string (or throw) on failure. Do not rely on call-site discipline.

---

### HIGH HTTP header injection via unsanitized container name in URL

- **Location:** `AzureBlobConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 185
- **Issue:** `containerIt->second` is concatenated directly into the URL string: `endpointUrl + "/" + containerIt->second + "?restype=container"`. The container value is sourced from `connection.m_Params` without any sanitization for `/`, `?`, `#`, `%`, newline, or carriage-return characters. `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_URL, url.c_str())` passes the fully assembled string to libcurl, which will honor embedded path segments, query strings, or (in older libcurl versions) embedded `\r\n` sequences that can break the HTTP request line.
- **Impact:** An attacker controlling the container parameter can redirect the request to a different path or resource, inject additional query parameters, or — on libcurl versions before the 2023 HTTP/1.1 request-splitting mitigations — inject arbitrary HTTP headers.
- **Fix:** Percent-encode the container name using `curl_easy_escape` before concatenation, or validate it against Azure's documented container naming rules (lowercase alphanumeric and hyphens, 3–63 characters) and reject anything that does not match.

---

### HIGH CRLF injection into Authorization header for AzureSharedKey path

- **Location:** `AzureBlobConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 195–199
- **Issue:** The CRLF check (`ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf`) is applied only when `credentials.m_AuthType == CloudAuthType::OAuth2`. When the auth type is `AzureSharedKey`, the signed header values returned by `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign` are appended directly to the `curl_slist` without any CRLF validation. If the account name or secret key contains `\r\n` (possible if the credential store or a future code path allows it), the resulting header string passed to libcurl could inject arbitrary HTTP headers.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence the account name or key value could inject rogue HTTP headers into the outbound Azure request, potentially poisoning responses or bypassing logging.
- **Fix:** Apply `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf` (or an equivalent check) to every header key and value in the `signed_.m_Headers` map before calling `curl_slist_append`, not just to the OAuth2 bearer token.

---

### MEDIUM Endpoint URL injected into log without sanitization

- **Location:** `AzureBlobConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 167–170
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` and `errorMessage` (which may itself contain the rejected endpoint URL) are interpolated directly into `LOG_SECURITY_WARN`. If the logging backend renders ANSI escape sequences or if the log is consumed by a SIEM that parses structured fields, an attacker-controlled `m_Name` or endpoint string containing newlines, escape sequences, or JSON-special characters can corrupt log integrity or inject false log entries.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery; potentially misleading security audit trails.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or truncate `connection.m_Name` and `errorMessage` before logging — strip control characters and enforce a reasonable length cap. Prefer structured logging fields that quote or escape values automatically.

---

### MEDIUM Response body reflected into errorMessage without size-bounding on path to caller

- **Location:** `AzureBlobConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 218–222
- **Issue:** `responseBody` is capped at 500 bytes before being appended to `errorMessage`, but `responseBody` itself is collected by `ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback` whose actual size limit is not visible here — it is enforced inside an opaque function. If `BoundedStringWriteCallback` has a generous or absent cap, a malicious Azure-compatible server (reachable via SSRF or a misconfigured endpoint) can send a large response body that is buffered entirely before the 500-byte truncation in the error path. This is an allocation amplification / DoS vector.
- **Impact:** Forced large heap allocation; potential denial of service if many connections are tested concurrently.
- **Fix:** Ensure `BoundedStringWriteCallback` enforces a tight cap (e.g., 64 KB) and returns an error to abort the transfer when exceeded. Document and unit-test that limit. The 500-byte display truncation is not a substitute for a transfer-level cap.

---

### LOW Account name injected into constructed endpoint URL without validation

- **Location:** `AzureBlobConnector::BuildEndpointUrl`, approximately lines 144–147
- **Issue:** When no custom endpoint is set, the account name from `connection.m_Params["account_name"]` is interpolated verbatim into `"https://" + accountName + ".blob.core.windows.net"`. If `accountName` contains `@`, `/`, `?`, or other URL-special characters, the resulting URL is malformed and may be parsed differently by libcurl than intended (e.g., `accountName` containing `@evil.com#` could redirect the request).
- **Impact:** Request misdirection to an attacker-controlled host; mild SSRF.
- **Fix:** Validate `accountName` against Azure's documented storage account name rules (3–24 lowercase alphanumeric characters only) and reject the connection if it does not conform, before using the value in URL construction.


---

### 12_doc_application_cloud_azureSharedKeySigner_h

<a id="12-doc-application-cloud-azuresharedkeysigner-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/12_doc_application_cloud_azureSharedKeySigner_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH HMAC-SHA256 result not checked for failure
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::HmacSha256`, ~line 78
- **Issue:** The return value of `HMAC()` is not checked. If `HMAC()` fails (returns `NULL`), `resultLen` remains 0 and `result` contains uninitialized stack bytes. The function then constructs and returns a string from that uninitialized memory, which gets used as the signature material.
- **Impact:** A failing HMAC call (e.g., due to an OpenSSL FIPS policy rejection or a NULL EVP_MD) produces a signature derived from garbage bytes. Depending on context, this may lead to an authentication token being issued that appears valid to callers but is cryptographically meaningless, or it may leak stack bytes into the Authorization header.
- **Fix:** Check the return value of `HMAC()`: `if (!HMAC(...)) { /* throw or return empty/error */ }`. Prefer the EVP_MAC API (OpenSSL 3.x) which has cleaner error propagation.

---

### HIGH Canonicalized header values not sanitized — HTTP header injection in StringToSign
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::CanonicalizedHeaders`, ~line 155; `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign`, ~line 235
- **Issue:** Header values from `extraHeaders` (caller-supplied) are concatenated verbatim into the StringToSign and ultimately into the `Authorization` header value. No stripping or rejection of embedded `\n`, `\r`, or `:` characters is performed. The Azure spec requires header values to be normalized (fold LWS, trim whitespace); the code does none of this.
- **Impact:** A caller passing a crafted header value containing `\n` can inject additional lines into the StringToSign, potentially causing signature confusion. More critically, if the Authorization string is later written into an HTTP response or log, the embedded newline constitutes an HTTP header injection.
- **Fix:** For each header value in `CanonicalizedHeaders`, strip or reject characters outside the printable ASCII range and replace any `\r`, `\n`, or sequences of whitespace with a single space before concatenation.

---

### HIGH Base64Decode silently returns empty string on malformed key — no error propagation
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Base64Decode`, ~line 101; `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign`, ~line 257
- **Issue:** When `BIO_read` fails (malformed Base64 input for `accountKey`), `Base64Decode` returns an empty string. `Sign` then calls `HmacSha256` with an empty raw key and proceeds to produce and return a signed request. No exception is thrown and no error is signalled to the caller.
- **Impact:** A misconfigured or attacker-influenced `accountKey` value causes HMAC to run with a zero-length key, producing a deterministic, trivially reproducible signature. Any code that accepts `SignedRequest` output without re-validating may forward requests authenticated with a known-weak key.
- **Fix:** Throw `std::invalid_argument` (or return an error type) from `Base64Decode` when `decodedLen <= 0`, and propagate the error out of `Sign` before the HMAC step.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled allocation in Base64Decode — input size not bounded
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Base64Decode`, ~line 95
- **Issue:** `std::vector<unsigned char> buffer(encoded.size())` allocates a buffer proportional to the caller-supplied `encoded` string with no upper bound check. The function is called with `accountKey` which is expected to be small (~88 bytes for a 64-byte key), but nothing enforces this.
- **Impact:** If `accountKey` is sourced from an untrusted configuration store or an API parameter, a multi-megabyte or gigabyte string triggers a large heap allocation, potentially causing OOM or DoS.
- **Fix:** Add a maximum-length check before allocation: if `encoded.size() > 256 /* or appropriate bound */`, throw or return an error.

---

### MEDIUM Query parameter values not URL-decoded in CanonicalizedResource
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::CanonicalizedResource`, ~line 175
- **Issue:** The Azure Shared Key spec requires query parameter values to be URL-decoded before being included in the canonicalized resource string. The code uses raw (percent-encoded) values directly. This is a correctness issue that also has a security dimension: a carefully crafted URL with a percent-encoded separator character could cause the StringToSign to differ from what Azure computes, silently producing an invalid or bypassable signature.
- **Impact:** Signature verification at Azure will fail for URLs with encoded query values, but more subtly, an attacker controlling the URL could craft inputs where the locally-computed StringToSign diverges from Azure's in a predictable way.
- **Fix:** Apply percent-decoding to each query parameter value (and key) before inserting into `params`.

---

### MEDIUM `accountName` injected into StringToSign and Authorization header without validation
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign`, ~line 260; `AzureSharedKeySigner::CanonicalizedResource`, ~line 163
- **Issue:** `accountName` is concatenated directly into the Authorization header value (`"SharedKey " + accountName + ":"`) and into the canonicalized resource path with no validation that it contains only alphanumeric characters (Azure account names are restricted to `[a-z0-9]{3,24}`). A value containing `:`, `\n`, or `/` corrupts the Authorization header or the StringToSign.
- **Impact:** A newline in `accountName` splits the Authorization header, injecting an arbitrary HTTP header. A colon splits the SharedKey credential field.
- **Fix:** Validate `accountName` against `^[a-z0-9]{3,24}$` at the top of `Sign` and throw `std::invalid_argument` on mismatch.

---

### LOW `getHeader` lookup uses exact case but callers may supply mixed-case keys
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign`, lambda `getHeader`, ~line 222
- **Issue:** `getHeader("Content-Type")` does a case-sensitive lookup in `allHeaders`, which is populated from the caller-supplied `extraHeaders` map. If a caller supplies `"content-type"` instead of `"Content-Type"`, the value is silently omitted from the StringToSign, producing a signature that Azure will reject. While not directly exploitable for privilege escalation, a missing Content-Type in the StringToSign when the actual request carries one allows a MITM to swap the body type without invalidating the signature.
- **Impact:** Body content-type is not covered by the signature, weakening the integrity guarantee of the signed request.
- **Fix:** Normalize all keys in `allHeaders` to lowercase at the point of insertion, and use lowercase literals in the `getHeader` calls.


---

### 130_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h

<a id="130-doc-engine-json-configchecker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/130_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — URL allowlist check is prefix-only and does not validate host or path structure

- **Location:** `checkUrl` lambda inside `ConfigChecker::Check`, approximately line 85
- **Issue:** The only gate on `m_Url` is `url.starts_with("https://")` plus a trivial length check. Any value that begins with `https://` passes — including `https://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` (cloud IMDS), `https://localhost/`, `https://internal-service/`, or any other RFC-1918/link-local/loopback address. No hostname allowlist, no rejection of IP literals, no path sanitization.
- **Impact:** If a compromised or malicious config file reaches this checker, the engine will accept an SSRF-capable URL and make outbound AI calls to attacker-controlled or internal infrastructure. In a cloud environment this trivially exposes instance metadata credentials.
- **Fix:** After the prefix check, parse the host component (everything between `://` and the next `/`, `?`, or end of string) and enforce an explicit allowlist of permitted hostnames or at minimum reject loopback (`127.*`, `::1`), link-local (`169.254.*`, `fe80::*`), and RFC-1918 ranges. Consider using a URL-parsing library rather than hand-rolling string checks.

---

### LOW — Filesystem paths logged verbatim from untrusted config without sanitization

- **Location:** `ConfigChecker::Check`, `LOG_CORE_ERROR` calls for `ok1` and `ok2`, approximately lines 110–117
- **Issue:** `queueFolderFilepath` and `workflowsFolder` are taken directly from the parsed config and emitted into the structured log without stripping control characters or newlines. A config value containing `\n`, `\r`, ANSI escape sequences, or other control bytes will be written as-is to `log/log.txt`.
- **Impact:** Log injection: an attacker who controls the config file can forge or corrupt subsequent log lines, potentially hiding malicious activity or confusing log-analysis tooling. If the log is forwarded to a SIEM, this can also cause log parsing failures.
- **Fix:** Before logging any externally sourced string, replace or strip non-printable characters and ensure the value contains no embedded newlines. A small helper such as `sanitize_for_log(str)` that removes `\n`, `\r`, and non-printable bytes is sufficient here.

---

### LOW — `m_MaxFileSizekB` upper-bound clamp is 256 kB — silently allows arbitrarily large files if field is absent and default is 0

- **Location:** `ConfigChecker::Check`, approximately lines 140–146
- **Issue:** The clamp condition is `m_MaxFileSizekB <= 0 || m_MaxFileSizekB > 256`. A value of exactly 256 passes unmodified and the comment implies this is a kB unit, meaning 256 kB is a reasonable ceiling. However, if a config omits the field and the default-constructed value happens to be any positive value ≤ 256, it passes silently with no log warning. More importantly, the unit is "kB" but the limit of 256 kB is very small; the concern is that someone reading the code might later change the upper bound without understanding that it is the sole DoS backstop on file reads, removing the protection entirely. This is on the boundary of a security-relevant misconfiguration rather than a purely stylistic issue.
- **Impact:** If the guard is accidentally widened or bypassed, the engine could read arbitrarily large files into memory, enabling a local denial-of-service through memory exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Document the security intent of this field explicitly in a comment adjacent to the clamp. Add a static assertion or `spdlog`-level note that the value is a resource-consumption limit. Ensure the field is treated as required (not silently defaulted) so misconfiguration is surfaced rather than silently corrected.


---

### 131_doc_engine_json_configParser_h

<a id="131-doc-engine-json-configparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/131_doc_engine_json_configParser_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path traversal via unsanitized `keys_file` and `mcp_keys_file` config values

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, `keys_file` and `mcp_keys_file` branches (~lines 440–465 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The values of `keys_file` and `mcp_keys_file` are accepted verbatim from config.json and stored directly into `engineConfig.m_KeysFilePath` and `engineConfig.m_McpKeysFilePath` without any path normalization or containment check. If an attacker can write or influence config.json (e.g., via a misconfigured deployment pipeline, a symlink swap on the config file, or a supply-chain substitution), they can point these fields to arbitrary filesystem paths such as `../../etc/shadow` or `//remote/share/evil.enc`.
- **Impact:** If downstream code opens `m_KeysFilePath` or `m_McpKeysFilePath` to read the encrypted key store and feeds the decrypted content into the engine, an attacker-controlled path means the engine will attempt to decrypt and load an attacker-chosen file. Depending on error handling in the key-loader, this could leak file content in error messages, cause the engine to load attacker-controlled keys, or enable TOCTOU-based symlink attacks.
- **Fix:** After reading the string, canonicalize it with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` and assert the resolved path starts with the expected base directory (e.g., the application's working directory or a configured secrets directory). Reject and abort parsing if the check fails.

---

### MEDIUM Path traversal via unsanitized `queue folder` and `workflows folder`

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, `queue folder` and `workflows folder` branches (~lines 320–345)
- **Issue:** Both folder paths are accepted without validation and stored in `engineConfig.m_QueueFolderFilepath` and `engineConfig.m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath`. These paths are later used by the file watcher and workflow dispatcher to enumerate and load `.jcwf` files. An attacker who controls config.json can point either folder to `/etc`, `/proc`, or any world-readable directory.
- **Impact:** The workflow engine will attempt to parse every file in the attacker-specified directory as a workflow. This can cause information leakage through parse-error log messages (which echo file content fragments), unexpected workflow execution if a crafted file passes parsing, or DoS through a directory with a very large number of entries.
- **Fix:** Canonicalize both paths and confirm they resolve to expected subdirectories. Enforce that neither path is an ancestor of the application's own binary or config directory.

---

### MEDIUM Path traversal via unsanitized `TlsCert` and `TlsKey` paths

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, `TlsCert` and `TlsKey` branches (~lines 465–490)
- **Issue:** The TLS certificate and private-key file paths are stored without canonicalization or containment checks. The private key path in particular is high-value: if an attacker redirects `m_TlsKey` to a world-readable attacker-controlled PEM file, they substitute the server's TLS identity. If redirected to `/dev/urandom` or a large file, they can cause a startup crash or hang.
- **Impact:** TLS private key substitution defeats all transport security guarantees. An attacker who MITMs config delivery and replaces the key path owns every TLS session.
- **Fix:** Canonicalize and pin both paths to a known secrets directory. Additionally, after loading, verify the certificate and key form a matching pair before accepting the configuration.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled `MaxRequestBodyMB` allows trivial memory-exhaustion DoS

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, `MaxRequestBodyMB` branch (~line 500); `EngineConfig::m_MaxRequestBodyMB` default is 10 MB
- **Issue:** The field is read as a `uint64_t` with no upper-bound check and stored directly. A config value of, e.g., `18446744073709551615` (UINT64_MAX) will be accepted and later used as the HTTP body allocation limit. Even a value of `65536` (MB) creates a 64 TB allocation attempt.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can modify config.json (or who submits a request to a REST endpoint that hot-reloads config) can cause the server to attempt an enormous allocation on the first HTTP request, crashing the process.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard ceiling (e.g., 2048 MB) and reject or clamp values above it with a loud warning. Document the ceiling in the config schema.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded `max_concurrency`, `max_retries_429`, and `base_retry_ms` enable resource exhaustion

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::ParseInterfaces`, `rate_limit` sub-object parsing (~lines 600–650)
- **Issue:** `m_MaxConcurrency`, `m_MaxRetries429`, `m_MaxRetriesTransient`, and `m_BaseRetryMs` are accepted from config with only positivity checks, no upper-bound validation. A config with `max_concurrency: 2147483647` and `max_retries_429: 2147483647` instructs the engine to open an unbounded number of concurrent outbound connections and retry indefinitely.
- **Impact:** An attacker who influences config can exhaust file descriptors, thread pools, and memory, taking down the engine or causing it to hammer a rate-limited upstream until banned.
- **Fix:** Apply reasonable ceilings (e.g., `max_concurrency ≤ 1000`, `max_retries ≤ 100`, `base_retry_ms ≤ 60000`) and reject values exceeding them.

---

### MEDIUM `TlsKey` file path logged at INFO level

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, `TlsKey` branch (~line 482)
- **Issue:** `LOG_CORE_INFO("TlsKey: {}", engineConfig.m_TlsKey)` emits the full private-key file path. While this is a path rather than key material itself, in environments where logs are aggregated, searchable, or forwarded to third-party SIEM systems, broadcasting the exact location of the TLS private key assists an attacker who has read access to logs but not the filesystem.
- **Impact:** Reduces the effort required to locate and exfiltrate the private key file in a post-compromise scenario.
- **Fix:** Log only the filename (last path component), not the full path, or log at DEBUG level gated behind `m_Verbose`.

---

### LOW TOCTOU between `FileExists` check and `padded_string::load`

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, ~lines 280–295
- **Issue:** The code calls `EngineCore::FileExists` and `EngineCore::IsDirectory` and then, in a separate operation, calls `padded_string::load`. Between the existence check and the load, a symlink swap can replace the config file with a different target. This is a classic TOCTOU.
- **Impact:** An attacker with local write access to the config directory can swap the config file between the check and the read, causing the engine to load an attacker-controlled configuration while the existence check passed on the legitimate file.
- **Fix:** Remove the pre-check and rely solely on the error return of `padded_string::load`. On POSIX, open the file with `O_NOFOLLOW` if symlinks in the config directory are not required, or pin the config directory with an `O_PATH` file descriptor and use `openat` to open the config file by name relative to that descriptor.

---

### LOW Unbounded number of API interfaces allows memory exhaustion

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::ParseInterfaces`, the outer `for (auto element : jsonArray)` loop
- **Issue:** There is no limit on how many entries the `"API interfaces"` array may contain. Each entry is pushed into `engineConfig.m_ApiInterfaces` without a cap. A config file with millions of interface objects will exhaust heap memory.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls config.json can crash the process at startup through memory exhaustion before any request is served.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum count (e.g., 256 interfaces) and return `FileFormatFailure` if exceeded.

---

### LOW Duplicate field processing without de-duplication allows last-write-wins confusion

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, `FieldOccurances` tracking throughout
- **Issue:** `fieldOccurances` counts field occurrences but the parser does not reject configs with duplicate top-level keys. Duplicate `keys_file`, `TlsKey`, or `TlsCert` entries silently overwrite the previously stored value. The last value wins, which means a carefully crafted config with a benign first occurrence and a malicious second occurrence bypasses any config-review tooling that inspects only the first occurrence.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can append to or partially control config.json can override security-sensitive fields (key paths, TLS paths, trusted headers) while the file superficially appears correct.
- **Fix:** Treat any `fieldOccurances[field] > 1` for security-sensitive fields (`KeysFile`, `McpKeysFile`, `TlsCert`, `TlsKey`, `TrustedProxyHeader`, `TrustedRoleHeader`) as a `FileFormatFailure` and abort parsing.


---

### 132_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h

<a id="132-doc-engine-json-jsonhelper-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/132_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h/docu.output.md`
