- **Fix:** Prefer `PQconnectdbParams` (keyword/value arrays) over a concatenated string so the password is passed as a discrete parameter value and never appears as a single flat string. Never log `connStr`. Audit `TruncateLibpqError` callers to ensure libpq error messages do not contain reflected credential fragments; consider stripping anything matching `password=...` patterns from the truncated output.

---

### HIGH: `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `fs::weakly_canonical` — does not resolve existing symlinks atomically (TOCTOU)

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, entire function body
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` resolves symlink components that currently exist at the time of the call but does **not** guarantee the path still resolves to the same target when the subsequent `std::ofstream` (or other filesystem operation) opens it. An attacker with write access to any directory component between the project root and the output file can swap a real directory for a symlink pointing outside the root between the confinement check and the `std::ofstream` open. Additionally, `weakly_canonical` by design leaves non-existent trailing components unresolved — so if the output file does not yet exist (normal case for a fresh task run), the final component is accepted verbatim without symlink resolution, and only the existing prefix is checked.
- **Impact:** A race or a pre-placed symlink in the task working directory could redirect output file writes to an arbitrary path on the filesystem, enabling overwrite of sensitive files (e.g., `/etc/cron.d/...`, SSH `authorized_keys`, or application config).
- **Fix:** Open the output file using `O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX) or equivalent to refuse symlinks at the final component. Alternatively, open the parent directory with `open(dir, O_DIRECTORY | O_NOFOLLOW)` and then `openat(dirfd, filename, O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC|O_NOFOLLOW, 0600)`. Replace `std::ofstream` at the output path with a file-descriptor-based write obtained through this hardened open so the confinement and the open are effectively atomic at the final segment.

---

### HIGH: `output_file` path-separator check does not block null bytes, Windows alternate data streams, or names with leading dots that bypass extension checks

- **Location:** `DbQueryCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, output_file validation block (~line where `find('/')` is called)
- **Issue:** The check rejects `/`, `\`, `.`, and `..` but accepts filenames containing null bytes (`\0`), which truncate the effective filename on some OS interfaces. It also accepts names like `....` (four dots), which `std::filesystem` normalises differently across platforms. A filename containing `\0` passed through `std::filesystem::path` may be silently truncated, causing the actual file created to differ from what the confinement check evaluated. Additionally, names with leading dots (e.g., `.bashrc`) are accepted, which could overwrite dotfiles in the working directory if the working directory is ever a home directory.
- **Impact:** Filename confusion leading to writing to an unintended file; on platforms where NUL truncation applies, partial path traversal bypassing the later `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` gate.
- **Fix:** Add an explicit check: `if (outputFile.find('\0') != std::string::npos)` — reject. Enforce a strict allowlist (e.g., `[A-Za-z0-9._-]+` with a maximum length) rather than a denylist. Reject leading dots if dotfiles in the working directory are sensitive.

---

### MEDIUM: `getStringParam` lambda captures `doc` by reference; simdjson ondemand document is iterated via multiple independent `doc[key]` accesses — key-order dependency is silently wrong

- **Location:** `DbQueryCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `getStringParam` and `getUint64Param` lambdas
- **Issue:** simdjson's `ondemand` API is forward-only. Accessing `doc["query"]` then `doc["format"]` then `doc["output_file"]` requires the JSON object to have keys in that exact order; out-of-order keys silently return an error that is indistinguishable from a missing key, causing the lambda to return an empty string / default value without any error indication. A workflow JSON with keys in a different order (e.g., `{"output_file":"x","query":"SELECT 1"}`) will silently fail to parse `query` and trigger the "Missing required 'query'" error path, which is a functional but confusing failure. More critically for security: if a future parameter (e.g., a privilege-relevant flag) is added and a hostile workflow sends it early in the JSON before the currently-first key, it will be silently ignored and the default (potentially more permissive) value will be used.
- **Impact:** Silent parameter misreading: security-relevant parameters could fall back to defaults when a workflow author or attacker reorders JSON keys, causing unexpected behavior. Not currently exploitable with existing parameters but is a latent architectural flaw.
- **Fix:** Use simdjson's `dom` parser (which allows random-access) for this use case, or pre-collect all expected key-value pairs in a single forward pass before acting on any of them.

---

### MEDIUM: JSON output emits all database values as JSON strings — numeric/boolean columns lose type fidelity and boolean `true`/`false` from DB is emitted as the string `"true"`

- **Location:** `DbQueryCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, JSON output branch, inner `col` loop
- **Issue:** Every non-null column value is unconditionally wrapped in `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString(...)` and output as a JSON string literal (`"..."`). PostgreSQL boolean columns return `"t"` or `"f"` via `PQgetvalue`; integer and float columns return their decimal representation. These are all emitted as JSON strings. Downstream consumers that trust the JSON type (e.g., parse `{"count": "42"}` vs `{"count": 42}`) may mishandle results. More importantly from a security perspective: if a downstream task performs type-based access control decisions on the JSON output (e.g., `if row["is_admin"] == true`) and receives `"t"` or `"true"` as a string, the comparison may behave unexpectedly depending on the consumer language, potentially resulting in authorization bypass in downstream workflow steps.
- **Impact:** Type confusion in downstream consumers; potential authorization logic bypass if downstream code trusts column types from this JSON.
- **Fix:** Use `PQftype(res.get(), col)` to check the OID of each column and emit unquoted JSON numbers (`INT4OID`, `INT8OID`, `FLOAT4OID`, `FLOAT8OID`, `NUMERICOID`) and JSON booleans (`BOOLOID`, emitting `true`/`false`) instead of always quoting. At minimum, document prominently that all values are strings.

---

### MEDIUM: `checkByteCap` relies on `std::ofstream::tellp()` which may return stale values for buffered streams

- **Location:** `DbQueryCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `checkByteCap` lambda
- **Issue:** `std::ofstream` is buffered by default. `tellp()` returns the stream's logical position, which includes unflushed buffered data not yet written to disk, so the byte-count tracking is functionally correct for the cap logic. However, if `tellp()` returns `-1` (stream error), the lambda returns `true` (treats it as safe) and continues writing without any cap enforcement. A disk-full or I/O error condition that sets the stream's `failbit` will cause all subsequent `tellp()` calls to return `-1`, and the cap check silently no-ops for the rest of the write loop, potentially writing an unbounded amount of data to the (now error-state) stream.
- **Impact:** If the output stream enters an error state (e.g., disk full), the byte-cap enforcement is silently disabled for all subsequent rows. In practice the writes themselves would also fail, limiting actual damage, but the cap logic provides a false safety guarantee.
- **Fix:** Check `out.good()` (or `out.fail()`) alongside `tellp()`. If the stream is in an error state, treat it as a cap failure (return false) rather than silently continuing. Also check the return value of the final write operations.

---

### LOW: `CsvEscape` does not handle CSV formula injection (leading `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`)

- **Location:** `CsvEscape`, entire function
- **Issue:** RFC 4180-compliant CSV escaping is implemented correctly for the standard. However, values beginning with `=`, `+`, `-`, or `@` are interpreted as formulas by Microsoft Excel, LibreOffice Calc, and Google Sheets when the CSV is opened interactively. A database column containing `=HYPERLINK("http://evil.com","Click")` will execute that formula when an operator opens the exported CSV. This is a client-side risk, not a server-side one, but the server is the origin of the file.
- **Impact:** If a database row contains attacker-controlled data starting with formula metacharacters and an operator opens the CSV in a spreadsheet application, formula injection executes in their local context (SSRF-like exfiltration, local file reads via DDE on Windows).
- **Fix:** Prepend a tab character (`\t`) or single-quote (`'`) to field values that begin with `=`, `+`, `-`, or `@` before CSV-escaping, or document this limitation explicitly and recommend operators not open raw CSV exports in spreadsheet applications without sanitization.

---

### LOW: `LOG_APP_INFO` at successful query completion logs the full `outputPath` — path may contain operator-sensitive directory structure

- **Location:** `DbQueryCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, final `LOG_APP_INFO` call
- **Issue:** The success log line emits `outputPath.string()`, which is the full absolute filesystem path. This is a low-severity information-disclosure concern if application logs are forwarded to a SIEM or third-party log aggregator — it reveals the project root directory structure and working directory naming convention to anyone with log access.
- **Impact:** Internal path structure disclosure in log aggregation systems; useful for an attacker enumerating the host's layout.
- **Fix:** Log only `outputFile` (the bare filename) or a path relative to the project root rather than the full canonical absolute path.


---

### 21_doc_application_cloud_emailCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="21-doc-application-cloud-emailcloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/21_doc_application_cloud_emailCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Uncontrolled body size read from `body_file`
- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, body_file branch (~line 390–410 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The file at `fullBodyPath` is opened with `std::istreambuf_iterator` after `ValidateLocalPath` confirms it stays inside `workDir`, but there is no size cap before the read. A valid workflow file could be arbitrarily large (e.g. a 10 GB log file placed in workDir). The entire contents are slurped into `body` (a `std::string`) unconditionally, then concatenated into `emailMessage` via `BuildEmailMessage`, then copied again into `EmailUploadContext::data`. Three full-size copies reside in memory simultaneously.
- **Impact:** Any workflow participant who can write a large file to a task working directory (or configure `body_file` to point at a large legitimate file) can exhaust process memory and cause a DoS.
- **Fix:** After opening `fullBodyPath`, call `file.seekg(0, std::ios::end)`, check `file.tellg()` against the same `kMaxAttachmentBytes` cap (25 MB) used for attachments, and fail the task if the file exceeds it before seeking back and reading.

---

### HIGH — SMTP URL is never validated before being passed to libcurl
- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, ~line 430 (`smtpUrl = EmailConnector::BuildSmtpUrl(connection)`)
- **Issue:** `BuildSmtpUrl` is called on a `CloudConnection` whose params come from user-supplied workflow definitions. The resulting URL string is handed directly to `CURLOPT_URL`. If `EmailConnector::BuildSmtpUrl` does not rigorously enforce the `smtp://` or `smtps://` scheme and reject non-SMTP schemes (e.g. `file://`, `gopher://`, `dict://`), libcurl will cheerfully use the alternate protocol, potentially exfiltrating data or performing internal-network probing (SSRF). The analogous `imapBaseUrl` has the same exposure.
- **Impact:** Operator or AI-generated connection configs with a crafted `smtp_host` value could redirect libcurl to internal services, read local files, or probe RFC-1918 addresses.
- **Fix:** After constructing the URL, assert it starts with `smtp://` or `smtps://` (and `imap://` / `imaps://` for the IMAP path) and reject with a hard error before any `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, ...)` call. Additionally set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS_STR` (libcurl ≥ 7.85) or `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to the expected bitmask.

---

### HIGH — `subjectFilter` applied client-side after fetching all messages; no size bound
- **Location:** `ExecuteEmailRead`, `subjectFilter` use (~line 310–315)
- **Issue:** `subjectFilter` is taken verbatim from task params and used in `subject.find(subjectFilter)`. There is no length bound on `subjectFilter`. A caller can supply a multi-MB string; `std::string::find` with a huge needle against every fetched message subject is O(n·m) and will block the thread for a significant time, and the needle string itself occupies memory proportional to its length. Additionally, the filter is applied *after* all messages are fetched and partially assembled into `summaryJson`, meaning the full `rawEmail` fetch for every UID still occurs.
- **Impact:** Unbounded `subjectFilter` input causes CPU/memory exhaustion proportional to `maxMessages × subjectFilter.size()`.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum length on `subjectFilter` (e.g. 1 KB) immediately after parsing, failing the task if exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM — Credentials exposed in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` via IMAP error propagation
- **Location:** `ExecuteEmailRead`, IMAP failure path (~line 290): `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage = "IMAP SEARCH failed: " + imapError`; and the per-UID fetch warning (~line 330): `LOG_APP_WARN("[email_read] failed to fetch UID {}: {}", uid, imapError)`
- **Issue:** `imapError` is produced by `EmailConnector::ImapCommand`, which internally calls libcurl. libcurl error strings for authentication failures often echo the URL (which contains the host and sometimes the path), and verbose error buffers can include fragments of server responses that echo back supplied credentials or authentication challenges. If `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` is persisted to a database or surfaced in an API response, and `LOG_APP_WARN` writes to a log accessible to non-privileged users, credential material leaks.
- **Impact:** IMAP/SMTP credentials partially exposed in logs or task state records.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `imapError` before logging or storing: strip or replace substrings matching the username pattern, and never log the password. Consider categorizing errors (authentication failure vs. network error vs. protocol error) and exposing only the category in `m_LastErrorMessage`.

---

### MEDIUM — MIME boundary is predictable / not cryptographically random
- **Location:** `BuildEmailMessage`, ~line 155: `"j9t-boundary-" + std::to_string(std::chrono::system_clock::now().time_since_epoch().count())`
- **Issue:** The MIME boundary is derived entirely from the current wall-clock time in nanoseconds. This is deterministic and guessable. If an attacker can influence the `body` content (e.g. via `body_file` pointing to attacker-writable content), they can craft a body that contains the boundary string, splitting the MIME structure and potentially smuggling additional MIME parts (including attachments) into the message, or corrupting the message structure.
- **Impact:** MIME structure injection via predictable boundary collision in attacker-influenced body content.
- **Fix:** Generate the boundary using a cryptographically random source: read 16 bytes from `RAND_bytes` (OpenSSL, already linked) and hex-encode them. The result is unguessable and collision-resistant.

---

### MEDIUM — `body` content is not sanitized against the MIME boundary
- **Location:** `BuildEmailMessage`, body-part emission (~line 170–180)
- **Issue:** Even with a random boundary (see above), the `body` string is written directly into the MIME message without checking whether it happens to contain the boundary string. RFC 2045 §6.4 allows binary content only in encoded parts; the body part here is `Content-Transfer-Encoding: 7bit` (implicit). An attacker-supplied or AI-generated body containing the literal boundary string will corrupt the MIME structure. Combined with the predictable boundary, this becomes exploitable.
- **Impact:** Malformed or attacker-structured outgoing emails; potential content-type spoofing in the delivered message.
- **Fix:** After generating the boundary, scan `body` for it and regenerate until no collision, or use `quoted-printable` / `base64` content transfer encoding for the body part.

---

### MEDIUM — No cap on total number of attachments
- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, attachment loop (~line 450–490)
- **Issue:** The JSON `attachments` array is iterated without any limit on the number of entries. Each attachment passes the 25 MB size check individually, but with no count cap an attacker can specify hundreds of attachment paths. Even at zero bytes each, the `std::vector<std::pair<std::string,std::string>>` grows unboundedly, and the loop performs a `ValidateLocalPath` + filesystem open per entry. With many small files, total allocation across all `content` strings plus `Base64Encode` output can still exceed the 25 MB-per-file cap in aggregate.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion or slowdown via a large attachment list in the task params JSON.
- **Fix:** Break out of the attachment loop once `attachments.size()` reaches a reasonable ceiling (e.g. 20 attachments), logging a warning for the truncation.

---

### LOW — `Base64Encode` uses a bare `BIO` chain with no error-return path
- **Location:** `Base64Encode`, ~line 95–108
- **Issue:** `BIO_new(BIO_f_base64())` and `BIO_new(BIO_s_mem())` both return `nullptr` on allocation failure. The code immediately calls `BIO_push(b64, bmem)` and `BIO_write` without checking for null. On OOM, this dereferences a null pointer (UB / crash).
- **Impact:** Process crash (DoS) if memory is exhausted during base64 encoding of a large attachment.
- **Fix:** Check both `BIO_new` return values; if either is null, free any successfully allocated BIO and return an empty string (or propagate an error code to the caller so the attachment can be skipped).

---

### LOW — `use_ssl` flag for SMTP re-declares a shadowing variable
- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, ~line 445: `auto sslIt = connection.m_Params.find("use_ssl")` — this is the second declaration of `sslIt` in the same function; the first appears in `ExecuteEmailRead`. In `ExecuteCloud` specifically, the `sslIt` for IMAP (inside `ExecuteEmailRead`) is fine, but within `ExecuteCloud` the SMTP `sslIt` lookup is the only one, so there is no actual shadowing bug in the current code. However, if `ExecuteCloud` is ever extended to also call `ImapCommand` directly, the repeated `auto sslIt` pattern will silently shadow and the wrong SSL flag will be used for one branch.
- **Impact:** Low currently; latent risk of incorrect TLS posture if code is refactored.
- **Fix:** Name the SMTP-specific variable `smtpSslIt` (or similar) to make the intent explicit and eliminate the shadowing risk.


---

### 22_doc_application_cloud_emailConnector_h

<a id="22-doc-application-cloud-emailconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/22_doc_application_cloud_emailConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — IMAP folder allowlist excludes legitimate RFC 3501 characters, silently blocking valid inputs; but more critically, square brackets are blocked while being required for Gmail-style folders

- **Location:** `EmailConnector::IsValidImapFolder`, approximately lines 95–120
- **Issue:** The comment in the header explicitly says `[Gmail]/Sent Mail` should validate, but the character allowlist in the implementation (`isalnum`, `.`, `_`, `-`, `/`) rejects `[`, `]`, and space. This is a correctness gap, but the security dimension is that an operator working around the rejection may disable validation entirely or switch to a permissive fallback, reintroducing the IMAP URL injection vector that the allowlist is designed to close. Additionally, `]` is not among the characters blocked by `IsValidEmailHost` logic, so the mismatch between documented intent and implemented allowlist creates a maintenance hazard.
- **Impact:** Operators whose legitimate folder names are silently rejected may disable or bypass the validation layer, reintroducing IMAP URL path injection.
- **Fix:** Extend the allowlist to include `[`, `]`, and space (space is legal in IMAP mailbox names per RFC 3501 and appears in common Gmail folders). Since these characters are safe in the URL path component when percent-encoded, apply `curl_easy_escape` (or equivalent) to the folder segment before appending it to the IMAP URL in `CheckForNewMail` rather than relying solely on character filtering. URL-encode the folder at the point of interpolation; keep the structural checks (no leading/trailing `/`, no `//`).

---

### MEDIUM — `ParseSearchUids` performs only a first-byte digit check; multi-byte non-digit UIDs reach `stoull`

- **Location:** `EmailConnector::ParseSearchUids`, approximately lines 310–335; `EmailConnector::CheckForNewMail`, approximately lines 390–415
- **Issue:** `ParseSearchUids` pushes a UID into the result vector if only `uid[0]` is a digit — it does not validate the remaining bytes. A malformed IMAP server response such as `* SEARCH 1abc 2 3` would produce `"1abc"` in the vector. `CheckForNewMail` then calls `IsValidImapUid` inside the loop and skips those, so the crash path is closed for the per-UID comparison. However, `highestUid` is taken as `uids.back()` **before** the per-element validation loop, so if the last element from `ParseSearchUids` is malformed (e.g., `"99xyz"`), `highestUid` (and therefore the returned watermark) will be that malformed string. On the next polling cycle that value is passed in as `lastSeenUid`; `IsValidImapUid` will reject it, the poll will log a warning and return `{}`, and the trigger will stall permanently (DoS of the polling loop for that connection).
- **Impact:** A compromised or malicious IMAP server can permanently stall an email_watch trigger by returning a single malformed UID as the last element of a SEARCH response.
- **Fix:** Apply `IsValidImapUid` inside `ParseSearchUids` before appending to the result, or re-validate `highestUid` before returning it from `CheckForNewMail` and fall back to `lastSeenUid` if it fails validation.

---

### MEDIUM — TLS disabled path in `TestConnection` does not set `CURLOPT_USE_SSL` / `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER`; libcurl build defaults apply

- **Location:** `EmailConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 230–255
- **Issue:** When `smtpUseTls` is false the code emits a `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` but does **not** call `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_USE_SSL, CURLUSESSL_NONE)` or explicitly set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER 0L`. This is inconsistent with `ImapCommand`, which explicitly sets `CURLUSESSL_NONE` on the non-TLS path. The behaviour falls through to libcurl's compiled-in defaults, which vary by build. On some builds the default `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` is 1L and the default `CURLOPT_USE_SSL` may be `CURLUSESSL_TRY`, meaning the SMTP connection may silently downgrade to plaintext after a failed STARTTLS without raising an error, giving a false "test passed" result that masks a MitM-susceptible configuration.
- **Impact:** A test-connection result of "OK" can be returned for a plaintext SMTP session that a network attacker can intercept, giving operators false assurance about connection security.
- **Fix:** Mirror the `ImapCommand` pattern: in the `else` branch explicitly set `CURLOPT_USE_SSL` to `CURLUSESSL_NONE` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `0L` so the behaviour is deterministic regardless of libcurl build configuration.

---

### MEDIUM — `IsValidImapFolder` used for IMAP URL path construction without percent-encoding; space and bracket bypass at construction site

- **Location:** `EmailConnector::CheckForNewMail`, approximately line 358: `std::string searchUrl = imapBaseUrl + "/" + folder;`
- **Issue:** Even with the current restrictive allowlist, the folder string is concatenated directly into the libcurl URL string. If the allowlist is later correctly extended to permit spaces and brackets (as the header doc requires), those characters are not percent-encoded before being handed to `CURLOPT_URL`. libcurl may parse them differently across versions — older libcurl versions do not auto-encode path segments, and a folder name containing a space could truncate the URL at the space or be interpreted as multiple URL components.
- **Impact:** After any allowlist expansion, a folder name with a space or bracket could corrupt the constructed IMAP URL, causing either a failed connection or, in adversarial scenarios, URL component injection.
- **Fix:** Use `curl_easy_escape` on the folder string before appending it to the base URL, or construct the URL via `CURLU` (libcurl's URL API, `curl_url_set`) which performs encoding automatically.

---

### LOW — `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns non-empty; silent fallback to system trust store on failure

- **Location:** `EmailConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 240–245; `EmailConnector::ImapCommand`, approximately lines 295–300
- **Issue:** If `GetCaBundlePath()` returns an empty string (path not configured, file missing, or lookup failure), neither function sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO`. libcurl falls back to its compiled-in trust store location. On platforms where that location is absent or stale (e.g., minimal container images), `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER 1L` will cause all TLS connections to fail — **but this failure is silent at the configuration level**. More critically, if the CA bundle path is attacker-influenced (e.g., via a config injection that empties the path), the fallback can be exploited to substitute a system-level trust store that accepts attacker-controlled certificates.
- **Impact:** In a misconfigured or tampered deployment, TLS peer verification may silently fall back to an unintended trust store, weakening the certificate validation that the code otherwise correctly enforces.
- **Fix:** If the intent is to use an explicit, pinned CA bundle, treat an empty `GetCaBundlePath()` as a fatal configuration error when `useSsl` is true, rather than silently falling back to the system store. Log an error and fail the connection attempt.

---

### LOW — Error messages returned to callers include `curl_easy_strerror` output which may embed internal URL fragments

- **Location:** `EmailConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 265: `errorMessage = std::string("Email SMTP test failed: ") + curl_easy_strerror(res);`; `EmailConnector::ImapCommand`, approximately line 310: `errorMessage = std::string("IMAP request failed: ") + curl_easy_strerror(res);`
- **Issue:** `curl_easy_strerror` returns static strings and does not include dynamic URL content, so this is low severity. However, the `errorMessage` output parameter is propagated to callers (and likely to API responses or UI) with no sanitization. If any future change substitutes `curl_easy_strerror` with `curl_easy_perform`'s verbose error buffer (`CURLOPT_ERRORBUFFER`), that buffer can contain the full URL including embedded credentials. The current code does not set `CURLOPT_ERRORBUFFER`, but the pattern establishes a path where a future maintainer might add it and inadvertently expose credentials in error responses.
- **Impact:** Low currently; if `CURLOPT_ERRORBUFFER` is added without sanitization, SMTP/IMAP passwords embedded in URLs could leak through error messages to API consumers or logs.
- **Fix:** Document explicitly in the code that `CURLOPT_ERRORBUFFER` must **not** be set (or if set, the buffer must be scrubbed before populating `errorMessage`). Add a comment at each `curl_easy_perform` call site stating this constraint.


---

### 23_doc_application_cloud_gcsCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="23-doc-application-cloud-gcscloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/23_doc_application_cloud_gcsCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Partial file read silently treated as full file content

- **Location:** `GcsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch, approximately where `file.read(fileData.data(), fileSize)` is called
- **Issue:** After `file.read(fileData.data(), fileSize)`, the number of bytes actually read is never checked against `fileSize`. On a short read (disk error, race with file truncation, etc.), the remaining bytes of `fileData` are zero-filled and that zero-padded buffer is uploaded to GCS as if it were the complete file. An attacker who can race a file truncation between the `tellg()` and `read()` calls gets silent data corruption, or can cause a TOCTOU where a file that passed `ValidateLocalPath` is swapped for a symlink/different file between the path-resolution step and the open.
- **Impact:** Silent upload of a zero-padded or attacker-substituted file; integrity of cloud-stored data cannot be trusted.
- **Fix:** After `file.read(...)`, assert `file.gcount() == fileSize` and fail the task if not. Additionally, open the file with `O_NOFOLLOW` (or use a pre-opened file descriptor from the validation step) to close the TOCTOU window between path validation and open.

---

### HIGH — `endpointUrl` injected into GCS URL without validation

- **Location:** `GcsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, URL construction for both upload and download, approximately the lines building `url` from `endpointUrl + "/upload/storage/v1/b/" + bucket + ...`
- **Issue:** `endpointUrl` comes from `GcsConnector::BuildEndpointUrl(connection)`, which derives it from the caller-supplied `connection` object. The bucket is validated by `IsValidGcsBucket`, and `objectName` is URL-encoded, but `endpointUrl` is used verbatim with no validation that it begins with `https://storage.googleapis.com` (or any allowlisted scheme/host). A workflow definition with an attacker-controlled connection endpoint can point GCS requests at an arbitrary internal host (SSRF) or a plaintext `http://` URL.
- **Impact:** SSRF against internal infrastructure; bearer token exfiltration to an attacker-controlled server over cleartext HTTP if the scheme is not forced to `https`.
- **Fix:** Validate `endpointUrl` against an explicit allowlist (e.g., must start with `https://storage.googleapis.com`) before URL construction, and reject or log-and-fail any other value. Do not rely solely on `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults` since that function is called after the URL is already set.

---

### HIGH — GCS error response body appended verbatim to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`

- **Location:** `GcsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload error branch, approximately `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + responseBody`
- **Issue:** When the HTTP response code is ≥ 400, up to 499 bytes of the raw GCS response body are appended directly to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` with no sanitization. This string is subsequently written to workflow state and likely surfaced in logs or API responses. If `responseBody` contains CRLF sequences or log-injection characters (e.g., `\n[ERROR]`), an attacker who controls the endpoint (see the SSRF finding above) can inject false log entries, or can embed control characters that corrupt structured log output.
- **Impact:** Log injection, structured log corruption, potential secondary parsing issues in downstream consumers of the error message.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape CRLF and non-printable characters from `responseBody` before appending it to any error/log string. Apply the same `ContainsCrlf` check that is already used for bearer tokens, or use a dedicated sanitizer.

---

### MEDIUM — `UrlEncode` called with a potentially null `encoderCurl`; fallback uses raw `objectName` in URL

- **Location:** `GcsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately `CURL* encoderCurl = curl_easy_init(); std::string encodedObjectName = encoderCurl ? UrlEncode(...) : objectName;`
- **Issue:** If `curl_easy_init()` returns null (OOM or library failure), `encodedObjectName` is set to the raw, un-encoded `objectName` and used directly in URL construction. An object name containing `?`, `#`, `&`, or `/` would alter the URL structure: a `?` would terminate the path and inject query parameters, and `#` would inject a fragment.
- **Impact:** URL structure injection into GCS API calls; depending on object name source, partial SSRF or parameter injection.
- **Fix:** Treat `curl_easy_init()` failure as a fatal error and return `false` immediately rather than falling back to the unencoded value. Do not proceed with URL construction if encoding cannot be performed.

---

### MEDIUM — Bearer token value included in header string built on the stack; no zeroization

- **Location:** `GcsRequest` and `GcsDownload`, approximately `curl_slist_append(headers, ("Authorization: Bearer " + accessToken).c_str())`
- **Issue:** The full bearer token is concatenated into a `std::string` that lives on the stack/heap for the duration of the function. It is never explicitly zeroed before deallocation. On systems without memory overwrite protections, the token value may persist in freed heap memory and be recoverable from a core dump or heap inspection.
- **Impact:** Credential recovery from process memory dumps or crash reports.
- **Fix:** After `curl_slist_free_all(headers)` (which does not zero memory), explicitly overwrite the header string buffer before it goes out of scope, e.g., using `explicit_bzero` or `SecureZeroMemory` on the underlying storage. Consider using a custom allocator for credential-bearing strings.

---

### MEDIUM — `fopen`/`fclose` output file left on disk on partial download if `curl_easy_perform` fails

- **Location:** `GcsDownload`, approximately after `std::fclose(fp)` in the failure path
- **Issue:** When `curl_easy_perform` returns an error or the HTTP code is ≥ 400, the function returns `false` but the output file created by `std::fopen(outputPath.c_str(), "wb")` remains on disk in a partial or empty state. The caller in `ExecuteCloud` creates parent directories unconditionally before calling `GcsDownload`, so the partial file is left at the validated `fullLocalPath`. A subsequent retry or different task reading that path gets stale/truncated content with no indication of truncation.
- **Impact:** Silent data integrity failure; downstream tasks consuming the output file get incomplete data.
- **Fix:** On any failure path in `GcsDownload`, delete the partial output file before returning (e.g., `std::remove(outputPath.c_str())` after `fclose`).

---

### LOW — `contentType` value appended to `Content-Type` header without CRLF validation

- **Location:** `GcsRequest`, approximately `curl_slist_append(headers, ("Content-Type: " + contentType).c_str())`
- **Issue:** `contentType` is validated for emptiness but not checked for embedded CR/LF before being spliced into the HTTP header. The call site in `ExecuteCloud` passes the literal `"application/octet-stream"`, which is safe, but the parameter is `std::string const&` and the function is a non-member static — future callers passing a user-influenced content type would be vulnerable to HTTP header injection. The existing `ContainsCrlf` check is applied only to `accessToken`.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection if a future call site passes a user-controlled content type string.
- **Fix:** Apply `ContainsCrlf` (or equivalent) to `contentType` before appending it to the header list, consistent with how `accessToken` is validated.


---

### 24_doc_application_cloud_gcsConnector_h

<a id="24-doc-application-cloud-gcsconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/24_doc_application_cloud_gcsConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated endpoint in ExchangeJwtForAccessToken

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ExchangeJwtForAccessToken`, gcsConnector.cpp ~line 75
- **Issue:** The `endpoint` parameter controls whether token exchange is skipped (non-production path), but the JWT itself is sent to the hardcoded `kGoogleTokenUrl`. The real SSRF risk is in `TestConnection`: `BuildEndpointUrl` returns any caller-supplied `connection.m_Endpoint` verbatim, and `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is only called in `TestConnection`. `ResolveCredentials` calls `BuildEndpointUrl` and passes the result directly to `ExchangeJwtForAccessToken` without any prior validation. An attacker who can write a connection config (e.g., via the workflow engine's config API) can set `m_Endpoint` to an internal metadata service URL (e.g., `http://169.254.169.254`) and have the connector issue authenticated GCS bucket probes to arbitrary internal hosts.
- **Impact:** Internal network scanning, cloud metadata exfiltration, credential leakage to attacker-controlled endpoints.
- **Fix:** Call `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` inside `ResolveCredentials` (or inside `BuildEndpointUrl`) before using the endpoint, not only in `TestConnection`.

---

### HIGH JWT payload injection via unescaped service_account_email

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ResolveCredentials`, gcsConnector.cpp ~line 195
- **Issue:** `serviceAccountEmail` is taken directly from `connection.m_Params` and interpolated into a raw JSON string using `ostringstream` with no escaping. A value containing `"` or `\` characters will produce malformed or attacker-controlled JWT payload JSON. For example, an email value of `x@x.com","exp":9999999999,"iss":"admin` rewrites the entire claims object.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can control connection parameters can forge arbitrary JWT claims, potentially escalating privileges or impersonating a different service account.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (e.g., nlohmann/json or simdjson builder) to construct the payload object rather than manual string concatenation, or at minimum reject any `service_account_email` value that contains characters outside the RFC 5321 allowed set.

---

### HIGH TLS peer verification not enforced in ExchangeJwtForAccessToken

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ExchangeJwtForAccessToken`, gcsConnector.cpp ~lines 95–105
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never explicitly set to `1L`/`2L`. The CA bundle is only applied if `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. If that path is empty (mis-configuration, stripped deployment image), libcurl uses its compiled-in default, which on some platforms/builds may not verify the peer certificate at all. There is no explicit assertion that verification is on.
- **Impact:** On affected deployments an attacker with network position can MitM the OAuth2 token exchange, obtaining the service-account JWT and exchanging it themselves for a real GCS access token.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)`. If `GetCaBundlePath()` returns empty, fail the operation rather than continuing with an unverified TLS connection.

---

### HIGH TLS peer verification not enforced in TestConnection

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::TestConnection`, gcsConnector.cpp ~line 270
- **Issue:** `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults` is called, but there is no visible guarantee it sets `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER`/`CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST`. For the test-connection path that hits a user-supplied endpoint, this is the same class of issue as above. Even if `ApplyHardenedDefaults` does set these options, the direct-curl path in `ExchangeJwtForAccessToken` does not call it.
- **Impact:** Same as above for the test path; also applicable to any non-production endpoint that happens to use HTTPS.
- **Fix:** Ensure TLS verification options are set explicitly in every raw `curl_easy_init` block, or route all CURL usage through a hardened wrapper that enforces them.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded response body accumulation in ExchangeJwtForAccessToken

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ExchangeJwtForAccessToken`, gcsConnector.cpp ~lines 88–91
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` lambda appends all received bytes into `responseBody` with no size limit. A malicious or misbehaving token endpoint can return a multi-gigabyte body, causing unbounded heap growth.
- **Impact:** Remote denial of service; memory exhaustion of the workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Use `ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback` (already used in `TestConnection`) or add an explicit byte counter inside the callback that returns 0 (abort) once a threshold such as 64 KB is exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM Token cache race — TOCTOU between cache check and cache write

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ResolveCredentials`, gcsConnector.cpp ~lines 155–175 (read) and ~lines 220–228 (write)
- **Issue:** The cache is read under `m_CacheMutex`, released, and then a new token is minted and the lock is re-acquired to write. Between the two critical sections multiple threads can simultaneously observe a cache miss, each mint a JWT, and each perform a token exchange. This is a correctness/DoS concern (unnecessary token minting) and, more importantly, if key rotation happens between the two lock acquisitions, an old token could overwrite a fresher one.
- **Impact:** Excess OAuth2 token requests (rate-limit risk); stale token stored after key rotation.
- **Fix:** Either hold the lock for the full resolve-and-cache operation, or use a per-key condition variable / future to serialise concurrent misses for the same key.

---

### MEDIUM HTTP header injection via bucket name in TestConnection

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::TestConnection`, gcsConnector.cpp ~line 258
- **Issue:** `bucketIt->second` (the bucket name from config) is concatenated directly into the URL without percent-encoding or character validation. GCS bucket names are constrained by Google's naming rules, but the code does not enforce those rules. A bucket name containing `/../`, `?`, `#`, or other URL-special characters can alter the request path or query string. While less likely than header injection, combined with `connection.m_Endpoint` this can redirect the request to an unintended path.
- **Impact:** Path traversal in the constructed URL; unexpected API endpoints targeted.
- **Fix:** Validate that the bucket name matches the GCS naming regex (`[a-z0-9][a-z0-9\-_.]{1,61}[a-z0-9]`) before use, or percent-encode it with `curl_easy_escape`.

---

### LOW Sensitive data in error messages returned to callers

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ExchangeJwtForAccessToken` (~line 125) and `GcsConnector::TestConnection` (~line 295)
- **Issue:** When HTTP status >= 400, up to 500 bytes of the raw response body are appended to `errorMessage` and returned to the caller. The Google OAuth2 and GCS APIs occasionally include token hints, partial credential information, or internal identifiers in error responses.
- **Impact:** Partial credential or internal system information may propagate into logs or be returned to end-users through higher-level error handlers.
- **Fix:** Log the raw response body at DEBUG level internally, but return only the HTTP status code and a generic message to callers. Alternatively strip fields other than `error` and `error_description` from the parsed JSON error response.

---

### LOW Local test mode bypasses all authentication (JWT used raw as Bearer token)

- **Location:** `GcsConnector::ExchangeJwtForAccessToken`, gcsConnector.cpp ~lines 78–82
- **Issue:** When `endpoint != kDefaultGcsEndpoint`, the raw service-account JWT is returned as the access token without exchange. If `connection.m_Endpoint` is set to any non-default value in a non-local environment (misconfiguration, staging environment pointing at a real GCS-compatible store), a long-lived signed JWT is transmitted as a Bearer token rather than a short-lived access token.
- **Impact:** Extended token lifetime exposure; JWT claims (including private key signature) sent to potentially unintended endpoints.
- **Fix:** Restrict the bypass to an explicit opt-in flag (e.g., `allow_local_test: true` in `m_Params`) rather than any non-default endpoint string, and log a prominent warning when the bypass is active.


---

### 25_doc_application_cloud_gitHubCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="25-doc-application-cloud-githubcloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/25_doc_application_cloud_gitHubCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Unbounded response body allocation (uncontrolled memory growth / DoS)

- **Location:** `GitHubRequest`, `writeCallback` lambda, approximately line 185
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends every byte libcurl delivers into `responseBody` with no size cap. A malicious or compromised GitHub endpoint, or a network attacker on a non-TLS path that somehow passes validation, can stream an arbitrarily large response body, causing unbounded heap growth until the process OOMs or the OS kills it.
- **Impact:** Denial of service against the workflow engine host; potential swap exhaustion affecting co-hosted services.
- **Fix:** Track accumulated size inside the lambda and return 0 (signalling abort to libcurl) once a ceiling is reached, e.g. 16 MB for normal issue/comment payloads, a separately tunable limit for `get_file`. Example: `if (buf->size() + size * nmemb > kMaxResponseBytes) return 0;`

---

### HIGH — `get_file` decoded content written without size limit

- **Location:** `GitHubCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `get_file` branch, approximately line 370–395
- **Issue:** `Base64Decode` returns a `std::string` of arbitrary length derived from `responseBody`, which itself is uncapped (see finding above). Even if a response-body cap is added at the HTTP layer, `decoded` can be up to ¾ the size of the base64 blob. The file write (`outFile.write`) then materialises that data fully on disk with no file-size guard.
- **Impact:** Disk exhaustion on the host; potential to fill log or temp partitions; can pair with the memory finding for a two-stage resource exhaustion.
- **Fix:** After decoding, check `decoded.size()` against a configurable maximum (e.g. `kMaxFileWriteBytes`) before opening the output file. Additionally cap the HTTP response body as described above.

---

### HIGH — `operation` value reflected verbatim into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `GitHubCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, else-branch at approximately line 465
- **Issue:** The string `"Unknown github_issue operation '" + operation + "'. Valid: ..."` is set on `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. `operation` is taken directly from the task-params JSON with no length cap and no character filtering. If `m_LastErrorMessage` is subsequently written to a log file, database field, or surfaced in a UI/API response, an attacker who can supply task params can inject arbitrary content into those outputs (log injection, stored XSS, or second-order injection depending on the consumer).
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forging; potential stored XSS if the message is rendered in a web UI; pollutes audit trails.
- **Fix:** Cap `operation` at a reasonable maximum length (e.g. 64 chars) immediately after `getStringParam`, and strip or reject non-printable and non-ASCII bytes before it is used in any user-visible string. Alternatively, only place a fixed literal in the error message without echoing the raw input.

---

### HIGH — `responseBody` surfaced in `m_CapturedStdout` and written to disk via `WriteResponseJson` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `GitHubCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately lines 472 and 480
- **Issue:** The raw GitHub API response body (up to `kMaxCaptureChars` chars for stdout, full body for the JSON file) is written to `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` and to disk. If this data is later rendered in a browser-facing UI or logged, it can contain attacker-controlled content from GitHub (e.g. issue body, label names, file content) that becomes a second-order injection vector. More immediately, secrets embedded in the file content (e.g. a `.env` file fetched via `get_file`) are captured verbatim.
- **Impact:** Second-order XSS or log injection; inadvertent capture and persistence of secrets retrieved via `get_file`.
- **Fix:** For `get_file`, do not capture the file content in `m_CapturedStdout` or `response.json`—only capture the metadata (HTTP status, filename, size). For other operations, ensure the UI layer HTML-encodes before rendering, and that logging strips or truncates the body.

---

### MEDIUM — `credentials.m_Token` not checked for CRLF before header injection

- **Location:** `GitHubRequest`, approximately line 212
- **Issue:** `authHeader` is constructed as `"Authorization: Bearer " + token` and appended to the curl header list without calling `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf` (which is declared in the base class and described as exactly this guard). A token value containing `\r\n` would inject an additional HTTP header. The base class declares this check as public and explicitly documents the risk, but `GitHubRequest` (a file-static function) does not call it.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection; an attacker who can place a CRLF-containing string in `credentials.m_Token` (e.g. via a compromised credential store or misconfigured connection) can add arbitrary headers to GitHub API requests, potentially overriding `Accept`, adding `X-GitHub-*` headers, or poisoning proxy cache entries.
- **Fix:** Call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf(token)` in `ExecuteCloud` before calling `GitHubRequest`, and fail the task if the check fires. Alternatively add the check at the top of `GitHubRequest` itself.

---

### MEDIUM — `apiBase` from `GitHubConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` spliced into URL without validation

- **Location:** `GitHubCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately line 270; used in every operation branch
- **Issue:** `apiBase` is retrieved from the connection object via `GitHubConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` and concatenated directly into every URL. There is no visible validation that it starts with `https://` or is confined to a known GitHub API host. A misconfigured or attacker-modified connection record could supply an `http://` URL (stripping TLS), an internal host (SSRF), or an `ftp://` scheme. `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults` is called after `CURLOPT_URL` is set, but its scope and whether it validates the scheme/host is opaque from this file.
- **Impact:** SSRF to internal services; TLS downgrade; credential (Bearer token) leakage to an attacker-controlled host.
- **Fix:** In `ExecuteCloud`, validate that `apiBase` matches the pattern `https://api.github.com` (or a customer-approved GHE hostname from an allowlist) before constructing any URL. Reject and fail the task if validation fails.

---

### MEDIUM — `Base64Decode` casts `clean.size()` to `int` without overflow check

- **Location:** `Base64Decode`, approximately line 163
- **Issue:** `static_cast<int>(clean.size())` is passed to `BIO_new_mem_buf` and `BIO_read`. On a 64-bit host `std::string::size_type` is 64-bit; if `clean.size()` exceeds `INT_MAX` (~2 GB) the cast wraps to a negative value. `BIO_new_mem_buf` interprets a negative length as "NUL-terminated" (OpenSSL documented behaviour for `len == -1`) which would read past the intended buffer, and `BIO_read` with a negative `nbytes` is undefined. Without the response-body cap from finding 1 this is reachable.
- **Impact:** Out-of-bounds BIO read; undefined behaviour; potential information disclosure from heap memory adjacent to the buffer.
- **Fix:** Assert or enforce `clean.size() <= INT_MAX` before the casts, or enforce the HTTP response-body cap so inputs of that size are impossible.

---

### MEDIUM — `outFile` write failure is silently ignored

- **Location:** `GitHubCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `get_file` branch, approximately line 400
- **Issue:** `outFile.write(decoded.data(), ...)` is called with no check of `outFile.fail()` or `outFile.bad()` afterward, and the enclosing `if (outFile.is_open())` block does not verify the write succeeded. A disk-full condition, permission error, or I/O error is silently swallowed and the task reports success.
- **Impact:** Silent data loss; the workflow proceeds under the false assumption that the file was written, potentially causing downstream tasks to operate on a missing or partially written file, leading to unpredictable behaviour.
- **Fix:** After `outFile.write(...)`, check `outFile.good()` (or `!outFile.fail()`). On failure, set `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`, set state to `Failed`, and return `false`.

---

### LOW — `path` logged verbatim in `get_file` success log line

- **Location:** `GitHubCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately line 405
- **Issue:** `LOG_APP_INFO("[github] retrieved file {}/{}/{}", owner, repo, path)` logs the user-supplied `path` directly. While `path` has been validated by `ConnectorHttp::UrlEncodePathSegments`, it may still contain non-ASCII or unusual characters. Depending on the log sink this can be a low-severity log-injection vector (e.g. ANSI escape sequences causing terminal misrepresentation of log output).
- **Impact:** Log terminal injection / log forging in terminal-based log viewers.
- **Fix:** Log `pathEnc` (the percent-encoded form) rather than the raw `path`, or strip non-printable bytes before logging.


---

### 26_doc_application_cloud_gitHubConnector_h

<a id="26-doc-application-cloud-githubconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/26_doc_application_cloud_gitHubConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated endpoint in GetApiBaseUrl

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` (~line 75) and `GitHubConnector::TestConnection` (~line 115)
- **Issue:** `GetApiBaseUrl` returns `connection.m_Endpoint` verbatim after only stripping a trailing slash. In `TestConnection`, the endpoint validation via `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is only performed when `connection.m_Endpoint` is non-empty, but `GetApiBaseUrl` is called unconditionally afterward and appends `/user` to whatever raw URL was stored. If the validation function has any bypass (redirect, DNS rebinding, IPv6 literals like `http://[::1]/`, `http://0.0.0.0/`, encoded characters) the constructed URL goes directly into `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_URL, ...)`. There is also no check that the scheme is `https://`; a value of `file:///etc/passwd` or `http://169.254.169.254/` would pass through if `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is not airtight.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply or modify a `CloudConnection` record (via config injection, a malicious workflow, or an admin API) can cause the server to issue authenticated requests to internal cloud metadata endpoints (AWS IMDSv1, GCP metadata, Kubernetes API), internal services, or local files, potentially exfiltrating credentials or pivoting internally.
- **Fix:** After `GetApiBaseUrl` returns, re-validate the composed URL (not just the raw endpoint) against an allowlist of schemes (`https` only) and, if private-range rejection is needed, parse and block RFC-1918/loopback/link-local addresses before calling `curl_easy_perform`. Do not rely solely on a pre-check of the raw endpoint field. Additionally set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` so libcurl itself enforces the scheme regardless of redirect or URL tricks.

---

### HIGH Bearer token appended to error message and potentially reflected in responseBody

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection` (~line 165)
- **Issue:** When the HTTP response code is ≥ 400 and the body is under 500 bytes, `responseBody` is concatenated directly into `errorMessage` and returned to the caller. The GitHub API often echoes request metadata (including partial token fragments in some error types) in its response body. More critically, the error message pipeline is unknown here—if any caller logs `errorMessage` or sends it back over an HTTP response to a UI, the raw server body (which could contain security-sensitive context) leaks.
- **Impact:** Secrets or internal path information embedded in a GitHub API error response are forwarded to whoever receives `errorMessage`, which may be a less-privileged caller, an audit log that is broadly readable, or a UI response.
- **Fix:** Strip or sanitize `responseBody` before inclusion in `errorMessage`. At minimum, JSON-parse the body and extract only the known `"message"` field rather than including the raw body verbatim. Cap the included text and log the full body only at a debug level visible solely to operators.

---

### HIGH PAT stored as `std::string` in CloudCredentials (no secure erasure)

- **Location:** `CloudCredentials` struct (~line in cloudConnector.h), `GitHubConnector::ResolveCredentials` (~line 105)
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` is a plain `std::string`. The PAT is copied into it via `std::string(api->m_ApiKey.Get())` and the `CloudCredentials` object is stack-allocated in `TestConnection`. `std::string` does not zero memory on destruction; the token value will persist in stack memory until overwritten by subsequent stack frames. The `authHeader` string built from the token also lives until after cleanup.
- **Impact:** A process memory dump, crash report, or heap/stack inspection (e.g., via a vulnerability in another component of the engine) can recover live Personal Access Tokens.
- **Fix:** Use a dedicated secure-erase string type (or at minimum call `explicit_bzero`/`SecureZeroMemory` on the underlying buffer before destruction). Overwrite `authHeader` similarly before `curl_slist_free_all`. The same applies to `m_SecretKey` and `m_Password` in `CloudCredentials`.

---

### MEDIUM HTTP header injection via PAT (CRLF check insufficient scope)

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection` (~line 127 and ~line 148)
- **Issue:** The CRLF check (`ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf`) is applied to `credentials.m_Token`, which is correct. However, `connection.m_Name` is interpolated into `errorMessage` strings without any sanitization. If `errorMessage` is subsequently written into an HTTP response header by the web server layer (e.g., as an `X-Error` header or similar), a name containing `\r\n` could inject additional headers. Additionally, `GetApiBaseUrl` builds a URL from `connection.m_Endpoint` without CRLF-checking it; some libcurl versions before 7.86 were vulnerable to CRLF injection in URLs passed to `CURLOPT_URL`.
- **Impact:** Response header injection if `errorMessage` is placed in an HTTP header; in older libcurl, request header injection via the URL.
- **Fix:** Apply CRLF (and general control-character) sanitization to `connection.m_Name` and `connection.m_Endpoint` before use. Pin to a libcurl version ≥ 7.86.0 and set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` explicitly.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled response body size (DoS / memory exhaustion)

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection` (~line 145)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` is set to `ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback` and `CURLOPT_WRITEDATA` points to `responseBody`. The name suggests a bound exists inside the callback, but there is no `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` set on the handle and no visible size cap in this function. If the callback's internal limit is absent or generous, a server (or a DNS-rebinding attack redirecting to a malicious server) can return a gigabyte-scale response, causing unbounded heap growth.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / denial of service of the j9t engine process.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` to a small value (e.g., 1 MB) on the curl handle before `curl_easy_perform`, in addition to whatever cap `BoundedStringWriteCallback` enforces internally.

---

### MEDIUM Missing TLS certificate verification enforcement

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection` (~line 140, call to `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults`)
- **Issue:** TLS peer and host verification are assumed to be enabled by `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults`, but this function is not visible in the provided code and its correctness cannot be confirmed. There is no explicit `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L` set in `TestConnection` itself. If `ApplyHardenedDefaults` fails or is called after options that override it, or if it simply doesn't set these options, requests will proceed with unverified certificates.
- **Impact:** Man-in-the-middle interception of Bearer token and response data.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L` in `TestConnection` after `ApplyHardenedDefaults`, so the security-critical TLS settings are unconditional and cannot be accidentally removed by refactoring `ApplyHardenedDefaults`.

---

### LOW connection.m_Name reflected into errorMessage without sanitization (log injection)

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::ResolveCredentials` (~lines 90–105) and `TestConnection` (~line 127)
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is a user-controlled string concatenated directly into `errorMessage` (e.g., `"No credential key specified for GitHub connection '" + connection.m_Name + "'"`). If `errorMessage` is written to a structured log, a name containing log-format metacharacters (newlines, JSON special chars, format verbs) can corrupt log entries or inject false log lines.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log spoofing; forged audit-log entries.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `connection.m_Name` before embedding it in log strings and error messages: strip control characters and enforce a maximum length at the point where the connection object is deserialized from config.


---

### 27_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="27-doc-application-cloud-googlesheetscloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/27_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: API key appended to URL in plain text, logged by curl debug and proxy infrastructure

- **Location:** `SheetsRequest()`, approximately line 240 (`finalUrl += "key=" + credentials.m_Token`)
- **Issue:** When `useApiKeyParam` is true the raw API key is concatenated directly into the final URL string. URLs are routinely captured in curl verbose logs, server-side access logs, HTTP `Referer` headers on any redirect, and reverse-proxy/CDN access logs. `CURLOPT_URL` with a secret embedded in the query string is a standard credential-leakage vector.
- **Impact:** Any log aggregation system, proxy, or CDN that sees the outbound request obtains a valid Google API key and can impersonate the application against the Sheets API.
- **Fix:** Prefer the `Authorization: Bearer <key>` header path for API keys, or, if the key=… query parameter is mandatory, use `CURLOPT_XFERINFOFUNCTION` / `CURLOPT_DEBUGFUNCTION` with a redaction filter. At minimum, ensure `CURLOPT_VERBOSE` is off and audit every log path that might capture `finalUrl`.

---

### HIGH: `valueInputOption` accepted without whitelist, injected raw into URL query string

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud()`, approximately line 450 (`"?valueInputOption=" + valueInputOption`)
- **Issue:** `valueInputOption` is read from the task-params JSON and appended verbatim to the URL. `IsValidSheetsRange` and `IsValidSpreadsheetId` guards are applied to other fields but not here. A value such as `USER_ENTERED&key=attacker_key` or `USER_ENTERED&valueRenderOption=UNFORMATTED_VALUE&fields=*` would inject additional query parameters. No `ContainsCrlf` check is applied either.
- **Impact:** URL query-parameter injection allows manipulating the Sheets API request (altering response format, adding keys, suppressing fields). CR/LF in the value would allow HTTP response splitting against any HTTP/1.1 intermediary.
- **Fix:** Accept only the two documented values: `if (valueInputOption != "USER_ENTERED" && valueInputOption != "RAW") valueInputOption = "USER_ENTERED";` — no encoding needed after that.

---

### HIGH: Unbounded CSV file read into memory before JSON serialisation

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud()` write path, approximately line 430–445 (`while (std::getline(in, line))`)
- **Issue:** The CSV input file is read line-by-line into `std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> values` with no size cap. `requestBody` is then built by string concatenation of every cell. A workflow definition that references a very large CSV (or a symlink to `/dev/zero` or a FIFO) will exhaust process memory. There is no equivalent of `kMaxSheetsResponseBytes` on the inbound side.
- **Impact:** Denial-of-service via memory exhaustion; if a FIFO or special file is used the process hangs indefinitely blocked in `std::getline`.
- **Fix:** Add a row-count limit and a per-row character limit (e.g. 50 000 rows, 64 KB per row), and set a total `requestBody` byte cap matching `kMaxSheetsResponseBytes`. Open the file with `O_NOFOLLOW` (or `std::filesystem::is_symlink` check) before `std::ifstream` to block symlink attacks, consistent with the path-traversal mitigation already present.

---

### MEDIUM: Bearer token not checked for CR/LF before header injection

- **Location:** `SheetsRequest()`, approximately line 256 (`std::string authHeader = "Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token`)
- **Issue:** `ContainsCrlf()` exists as a utility on `ICloudTaskExecutor` and the header-comment on that method explicitly calls out this risk, but it is never invoked on `credentials.m_Token` before the token is spliced into the `Authorization` header string passed to `curl_slist_append`. If the token value contains `\r\n` an attacker who can influence stored credentials can inject arbitrary HTTP headers into the outbound request.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection against the Google Sheets API endpoint (or any intervening proxy); potential for cache poisoning or request smuggling depending on intermediary behaviour.
- **Fix:** Call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf(credentials.m_Token)` before constructing `authHeader`; fail the request with an appropriate error if the check triggers.

---

### MEDIUM: Raw Google API error body reflected into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud()`, approximately lines 380–383 and 470–473 (`taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + responseBody`)
- **Issue:** Up to 499 bytes of the raw HTTP response body from Google are concatenated into the error message. If that message is later written to a log, database, or returned to a client, it may contain control characters, ANSI escape codes, or JSON content that could enable log injection or terminal escape injection. The 500-byte gate prevents bulk leakage but does not sanitise the content.
- **Impact:** Log injection; if the error message is surfaced in a web UI, reflected XSS depending on escaping in the rendering layer.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape control characters (`\r`, `\n`, `\t`, ESC) from `responseBody` before appending it to `m_LastErrorMessage`. A simple ASCII-printable filter suffices.

---

### MEDIUM: `ValidateLocalPath` result trusted but path subsequently constructed again, potential TOCTOU

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud()` read path ~line 355 and write path ~line 430 (`std::filesystem::path inputPath = workDir / inputFile`)
- **Issue:** `ValidateLocalPath` validates that `inputFile`/`outputFile` does not escape `workDir`, but the `std::filesystem::path` is re-constructed after validation (`workDir / outputFile`, `workDir / inputFile`) rather than using a canonical path returned by the validator. Between validation and file open, a race (e.g. a symlink swap) could redirect the open to a different target if the working directory is world-writable. Additionally, `ValidateLocalPath`'s implementation is not visible here — if it uses `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` without subsequently confirming the canonical path still starts with `workDir`, the check may be insufficient for multi-component relative paths.
- **Impact:** TOCTOU path traversal; overwrite or read of arbitrary files if the working directory is shared or has lax permissions.
- **Fix:** Have `ValidateLocalPath` return the canonicalised absolute path (or an `std::optional<std::filesystem::path>`), and use that returned path directly for all subsequent file operations. On POSIX, open `workDir` as a directory file descriptor and use `openat(dirfd, relativePath, O_NOFOLLOW|...)` to make the operation atomic.

---

### MEDIUM: Sheets API response body reflected to `response.json` without size check in `WriteResponseJson`

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud()`, last line before `return true` (`WriteResponseJson(workDir, taskState, responseBody)`)
- **Issue:** `responseBody` is bounded to `kMaxSheetsResponseBytes` (64 MB) by the write callback, but `WriteResponseJson` writes the entire body to disk unconditionally. 64 MB per task run, multiplied by concurrent runs, can fill the disk. There is no per-task or global disk-quota guard visible here.
- **Impact:** Disk exhaustion denial-of-service if many tasks run concurrently or a single response approaches the 64 MB cap.
- **Fix:** Either reduce `kMaxSheetsResponseBytes` to a value appropriate for Sheets API responses (typical maximum response is well under 10 MB), or enforce a separate disk-write cap in `WriteResponseJson`.

---

### LOW: `LOG_APP_INFO` logs the `range` string after validation but without truncation

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud()`, approximately lines 395 and 480 (`LOG_APP_INFO("[sheets] read {} rows from range '{}'", rows.size(), range)`)
- **Issue:** `range` is validated by `IsValidSheetsRange` which limits it to 256 characters and a restricted character set. The allowed set includes `'` (single quote) and space, which are benign for structured loggers but could cause issues with log parsers that treat single-quoted strings specially. More importantly, if the log sink is a terminal or file viewer that interprets ANSI, any future relaxation of the allowlist would immediately become a log-injection vector since there is no separate log-safe rendering step.
- **Impact:** Low-severity log injection if the range allowlist is ever widened; no immediate impact given current validation.
- **Fix:** Log `range.size()` and a truncated, printable-only rendering rather than the raw value, consistent with the pattern used when logging rejected inputs (which only logs the length).


---

### 28_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsConnector_h

<a id="28-doc-application-cloud-googlesheetsconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/28_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: API key exposed in URL query parameter (sent in plaintext and logged by servers)
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, line building `url += "&key=" + credentials.m_Token`
- **Issue:** When `useApiKeyParam` is true the raw API key is appended to the URL string. URLs are routinely written to server access logs, proxy logs, browser history, and TLS terminator logs. The key is also visible in `curl` debug output if that is ever enabled.
- **Impact:** Any system that records or proxies the outbound HTTP request captures the API key in plaintext, allowing an attacker with log access to impersonate the application and access or modify Google Sheets data.
- **Fix:** Send the API key in a request header (e.g., `X-Goog-Api-Key: <key>`) rather than in the URL. Google Sheets API v4 accepts this header. If a query-param path must be kept for compatibility, ensure the URL is never logged and that the token is scrubbed before any error messages are constructed.

---

### HIGH: SSRF — `connection.m_Endpoint` only validated in `TestConnection`, not in helpers called by other code paths
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` (header, ~line 20); `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection` (~line 130)
- **Issue:** `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is called on `connection.m_Endpoint` only inside `TestConnection`. `GetApiBaseUrl` is a `static` public helper that any other code can call with an arbitrary `CloudConnection`; it unconditionally returns `connection.m_Endpoint` without any validation. Any caller that builds a URL from `GetApiBaseUrl` without separately validating the endpoint can be directed to an internal network address.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply a crafted `CloudConnection` (e.g., through the config API or a deserialized workflow) can point the connector at `http://169.254.169.254/` (cloud metadata), internal services, or loopback addresses.
- **Fix:** Move the `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` check into `GetApiBaseUrl` itself so that every caller is protected regardless of call site. Return an empty string or throw on validation failure, and update all callers to handle that case.

---

### HIGH: Spreadsheet ID injected into URL without encoding
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, line `std::string url = apiBase + "/" + spreadsheetIdIt->second + "?fields=properties.title";`
- **Issue:** `spreadsheetIdIt->second` (the value of the `spreadsheet_id` parameter read from `connection.m_Params`) is concatenated into the URL without any percent-encoding or character-set validation. A value containing `/../`, `?`, `#`, or `@` can alter the effective URL path or authority.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the `spreadsheet_id` config value can redirect the request to an arbitrary path on the Google API host, or—when combined with the SSRF issue above—to an entirely different host.
- **Fix:** Apply `curl_easy_escape` (or equivalent URL-path encoding) to `spreadsheetIdIt->second` before interpolating it into the URL, and reject values containing path-separator characters (`/`, `\`, `..`).

---

### MEDIUM: Credential token not CRLF-checked when used as Bearer header
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, CRLF check (~line 148) and header construction (~line 175): `std::string authHeader = "Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token;`
- **Issue:** `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf(credentials.m_Token)` is checked and the function returns early if CRLF is found. However, the check gates on `credentials.m_Token` which at that point holds the value from `ResolveCredentials`. If `ResolveCredentials` is refactored or called again elsewhere without the surrounding guard, the header injection protection is absent. More concretely, the CRLF check fires before the `useApiKeyParam` branch is decided, so the path that appends the key to the URL also benefits—but if the ordering ever changes the URL injection risk re-emerges.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection allowing an attacker to split the HTTP request, inject additional headers, or perform response splitting.
- **Fix:** Enforce the CRLF check immediately before header construction, or enforce it inside `ResolveCredentials` / the credential store so no caller can receive a token with embedded CRLF. Defense-in-depth: use `curl_slist_append` with a pre-validated string and verify libcurl's header-validation setting (`CURLOPT_HEADEROPT`).

---

### MEDIUM: `responseBody` has no enforced size cap in this call site
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION, ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback)` (~line 165)
- **Issue:** The callback name implies a bound, but no `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or equivalent libcurl option is set at this call site to enforce a hard byte limit on the response. Whether `BoundedStringWriteCallback` itself enforces a limit is not visible in this file. If the callback does not limit, and the endpoint (possibly an SSRF target) returns a very large body, the entire response is buffered into `responseBody`.
- **Impact:** An attacker-controlled endpoint can cause unbounded heap growth, leading to OOM / denial of service of the engine process.
- **Fix:** Set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE, (curl_off_t)65536)` (or a similarly small limit appropriate for the expected JSON response) before `curl_easy_perform`. Confirm that `BoundedStringWriteCallback` also truncates internally.

---

### MEDIUM: API key material logged indirectly via error path
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, block starting `if (httpCode >= 400)` (~line 190)
- **Issue:** When the HTTP status is ≥ 400, `responseBody` (up to 500 bytes) is appended to `errorMessage`. The `errorMessage` is returned to the caller and may be logged or surfaced in API responses. If `useApiKeyParam` is true, the server's error response body may echo the API key back (Google's error JSON sometimes echoes request metadata), causing it to appear in log sinks.
- **Impact:** API key disclosure in application logs or error responses visible to users who can read workflow diagnostic output.
- **Fix:** Never include raw response bodies in user-visible or logged error messages for authenticated requests. Log only the HTTP status code. If the response body must be inspected, sanitize or parse it first and emit only safe fields.

---

### LOW: `connection.m_Name` echoed into error messages without sanitization
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::ResolveCredentials` (~line 110) and `TestConnection` (~line 160, the `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` calls)
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is a user-controlled string that is interpolated directly into `errorMessage` strings and into structured log entries via the `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` macro. If the logging backend renders these as structured text (e.g., JSON, syslog), embedded special characters could corrupt the log record.
- **Impact:** Log injection: an attacker who controls the connection name can forge log entries or corrupt structured logging output, potentially evading detection or injecting false audit events.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `m_Name` before interpolating into log messages (strip or escape control characters, newlines, and format-string metacharacters). Use structured logging fields rather than string concatenation where the logging framework supports it.


---

### 29_doc_application_cloud_jiraCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="29-doc-application-cloud-jiracloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/29_doc_application_cloud_jiraCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Unbounded response body accumulation (DoS via uncontrolled allocation)

- **Location:** `JiraRequest`, `writeCallback` lambda, approximately line 175
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends every byte libcurl delivers into `responseBody` with no size cap. A malicious or compromised Jira server can stream an arbitrarily large body; the engine will keep allocating until the process runs out of memory or the OS kills it.
- **Impact:** Denial of service against the workflow engine process; potential OOM-kill of the host if many concurrent tasks hit the same endpoint.
- **Fix:** Track accumulated length inside the lambda and return 0 (causing libcurl to abort with `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`) once a configured maximum (e.g., 10 MB) is reached. Use `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as a complementary server-side hint, but do not rely on it alone because it is not enforced for chunked transfers in all libcurl versions.

---

### HIGH — Unvalidated `fields` JSON passed verbatim into the request body (injection)

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `operation == "update"` branch, approximately line 335
