- **Issue:** `fieldsJson` is read from the task params as a raw string via `getStringParam("fields")` and spliced directly into `requestBody = "{\"fields\":" + fieldsJson + "}"` without any structural validation that it is well-formed JSON or that it does not contain attacker-controlled keys that override sensitive fields (e.g., `"reporter"`, `"assignee"`, `"project"`). Any workflow author or upstream AI output that controls this parameter can inject arbitrary JSON into the Jira REST body.
- **Impact:** Privilege escalation within Jira (e.g., reassigning issues, changing project), or malformed JSON that causes undefined server-side behaviour.
- **Fix:** Re-parse `fieldsJson` with simdjson before embedding it, confirm it is an object type, and reject or strip known-sensitive top-level keys. Alternatively, require individual named params and build the object programmatically, as is done for `create`.

---

### HIGH — `baseUrl` used in URLs without validation (SSRF)

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, multiple operation branches; `JiraRequest`, approximately line 195
- **Issue:** `baseUrl` is obtained from `JiraConnector::GetBaseUrl(connection)` and concatenated directly into the request URL (e.g., `baseUrl + "/rest/api/3/issue"`). If the connection configuration (which may be supplied by a workflow definition or an AI-assisted config path) can be set to an internal address such as `http://169.254.169.254` or `http://internal-service`, the engine will issue authenticated HTTP requests to that target. No scheme, host, or allowlist check is visible in this file.
- **Impact:** Server-Side Request Forgery — credential exfiltration against cloud metadata services, internal service enumeration, or pivot attacks.
- **Fix:** Validate `baseUrl` before first use: enforce an `https://` scheme, compare the resolved hostname against a configured allowlist (e.g., `*.atlassian.net`), and reject private/loopback RFC-1918 ranges. This validation belongs in `JiraConnector::GetBaseUrl` or immediately after the call.

---

### HIGH — Bearer token and Basic Auth password not checked for CRLF before header injection

- **Location:** `JiraRequest`, approximately lines 215–225
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` is concatenated into `"Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token` and appended to the curl slist without calling `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf`. The base class explicitly documents `ContainsCrlf` as the defence against header injection, and provides it as a `static` method for exactly this purpose, but it is never called in `JiraRequest`. A token value containing `\r\n` can inject additional HTTP headers into every request made by this function.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection — an attacker who controls the stored credential value could inject `Host:`, `X-Forwarded-For:`, or other headers, or terminate the header block and inject a request body prefix.
- **Fix:** Call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf(credentials.m_Token)` (and `credentials.m_Username` / `credentials.m_Password` for BasicAuth) before constructing headers; fail the request if either check triggers.

---

### MEDIUM — Error responses from Jira included in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, multiple `httpCode >= 400` checks, approximately lines 295, 360, and similar
- **Issue:** The raw Jira response body (`responseBody`) is appended to `m_LastErrorMessage` when its size is below 500 bytes. Jira error payloads can contain attacker-controlled strings (e.g., from issue content that triggered the error), which will be surfaced to whatever system reads `m_LastErrorMessage` — logs, UIs, or downstream task params.
- **Impact:** Log injection if `m_LastErrorMessage` is written to a structured log; reflected content injection if the message is rendered in a UI without escaping.
- **Fix:** Strip or encode newlines and non-printable characters from `responseBody` before appending it to the error message. At minimum, remove `\r` and `\n` to prevent log injection.

---

### MEDIUM — `description_file` read after `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` but before confirming the path was not modified by a concurrent rename (TOCTOU)

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `operation == "create"` / `descriptionFile` block, approximately lines 272–290
- **Issue:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` resolves and validates the path, then `std::ifstream in(confined)` opens it. Between these two operations a concurrent process could rename or replace the confined path with a symlink pointing outside the project root (classic TOCTOU). The check and the open are not atomic on POSIX.
- **Impact:** An attacker with local filesystem write access could read arbitrary files (e.g., `/etc/shadow`, TLS private keys) and embed them as the Jira issue description.
- **Fix:** Open the file with `O_NOFOLLOW` via a raw `open(2)` call (rejecting symlinks), then verify the opened file's realpath is still within the project root using `fstat` and path comparison. Alternatively, open the project-root directory with `open`, then use `openat` with `O_NOFOLLOW` for the relative path.

---

### MEDIUM — `description_file` content size is unbounded

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `operation == "create"` / `descriptionFile` block, approximately line 285
- **Issue:** `oss << in.rdbuf()` reads the entire file into `description` with no size limit. A 1 GB file in the project directory will be read entirely into memory, JSON-escaped, and sent in the request body.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion DoS; extremely large HTTP request body may also exhaust Jira API limits and generate confusing error paths.
- **Fix:** Read at most a configured maximum (e.g., 128 KB) from the file; fail the task with a clear error if the file is larger.

---

### MEDIUM — `responseBody` written to disk via `WriteResponseJson` without size cap applied before the write

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, end of function, approximately lines 445–455
- **Issue:** `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` is correctly capped to `kMaxCaptureChars`, but `responseBody` — which at this point may be the full, unbounded Jira response — is passed raw to `WriteResponseJson`. If the DoS guard in `JiraRequest` (noted above) is not fixed, this write is also unbounded and could exhaust disk space.
- **Impact:** Disk exhaustion on the workflow engine host.
- **Fix:** Cap `responseBody` before passing it to `WriteResponseJson`, or have `WriteResponseJson` enforce its own write limit. This fix becomes critical once the `writeCallback` size cap recommended above is in place, but should be applied independently in case `WriteResponseJson` is called from other code paths with large buffers.

---

### LOW — `operation` value reflected into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `else` branch at end of operation dispatch, approximately line 435
- **Issue:** The unrecognised `operation` string is embedded verbatim into the error message: `"Unknown jira_issue operation '" + operation + "'"`. While `operation` comes from the task params JSON and is not user HTTP input, a workflow definition or AI-supplied param value could inject log-meaningful characters (newlines, ANSI codes) into the logged/surfaced message.
- **Impact:** Log injection; minor information disclosure to workflow authors who can control the operation field.
- **Fix:** Truncate `operation` to a reasonable length (e.g., 32 chars) and strip non-printable characters before embedding it in the error string.


---

### 30_doc_application_cloud_jiraConnector_h

<a id="30-doc-application-cloud-jiraconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/30_doc_application_cloud_jiraConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: CRLF injection check bypassed for BasicAuth credentials

- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, approximately the block starting after `ResolveCredentials` returns
- **Issue:** The CRLF guard (`ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf`) is only applied when `credentials.m_AuthType != CloudAuthType::BasicAuth`. For BasicAuth, `credentials.m_Username` and `credentials.m_Password` are passed directly to `CURLOPT_USERNAME` and `CURLOPT_PASSWORD` with no CRLF inspection. A newline embedded in either field could allow injection into the raw HTTP request, depending on the libcurl version and transport in use.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the stored username or password (e.g., via a malicious config import or compromised KeyManager entry) could inject arbitrary HTTP headers into the outgoing request.
- **Fix:** Apply the same `ContainsCrlf` check to `credentials.m_Username` and `credentials.m_Password` before passing them to libcurl, and reject with a security warning if either contains CR or LF.

---

### HIGH: Unvalidated server response body reflected into error messages

- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, the `httpCode >= 400` block (~line 175-180)
- **Issue:** `responseBody` is appended directly into `errorMessage` when its size is under 500 bytes. The content comes from the remote Jira server and is not sanitized. If `errorMessage` is subsequently written to a log, displayed in a UI, or included in a downstream response, a malicious server (or a SSRF-redirected response) can inject log-forging sequences, HTML, or other payload content.
- **Impact:** Log injection/forgery; potential XSS if the error message surfaces in a web UI; useful for an attacker who can control the endpoint (e.g., via a misconfigured or SSRF-reachable target).
- **Fix:** Strip or escape control characters and any HTML/JSON-special characters from `responseBody` before appending it to `errorMessage`. At minimum, remove all CR, LF, and non-printable bytes.

---

### MEDIUM: `GetBaseUrl` blindly concatenates attacker-controlled endpoint into URL

- **Location:** `JiraConnector::GetBaseUrl` and its call site in `TestConnection` (~line 130)
- **Issue:** `GetBaseUrl` strips a trailing slash from `connection.m_Endpoint` and returns it verbatim. The result is then concatenated with a fixed path (`/rest/api/3/myself`) and passed to `CURLOPT_URL`. Although `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is called first, if that validation is insufficiently strict (e.g., does not reject `javascript:`, `file://`, or URL-encoded variants), an attacker-controlled endpoint value could redirect the request. The validation is opaque in this file and its guarantees cannot be confirmed.
- **Impact:** SSRF: depending on `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint`'s implementation, an attacker who can set `m_Endpoint` could cause the server to make requests to internal services or non-HTTP schemes.
- **Fix:** After `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint`, additionally assert that the normalized base URL begins with `https://` (strictly) and does not contain query strings, fragments, embedded credentials, or path-traversal sequences before constructing the request URL.

---

### MEDIUM: `responseBody` has no enforced size cap at the write-callback level visible here

- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` / `CURLOPT_WRITEDATA` setup (~line 143-144)
- **Issue:** `ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback` is used, which implies a cap exists, but the cap value and enforcement are entirely opaque in this translation unit. If the bound is large or absent, a slow/large response from a hostile server could cause unbounded memory growth in `responseBody` on this thread.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / DoS if the cap is not enforced correctly inside `BoundedStringWriteCallback`.
- **Fix:** Document and enforce the maximum response size explicitly. Consider setting `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as a defense-in-depth layer independent of the write callback.

---

### LOW: `CloudCredentials` secrets not zeroed on destruction

- **Location:** `struct CloudCredentials` in `cloudConnector.h`
- **Issue:** `m_Password`, `m_Token`, and `m_SecretKey` are plain `std::string` members. When a `CloudCredentials` object goes out of scope its destructor does not explicitly zero those fields. The compiler may optimize away a naive `memset` in a destructor, leaving plaintext secrets in freed heap memory.
- **Impact:** Secrets can be recovered from a process memory dump or heap inspection after the object is destroyed.
- **Fix:** Use a secure string type that overwrites memory on destruction (e.g., a wrapper calling `OPENSSL_cleanse` or `explicit_bzero`), or implement a destructor that zeroes each sensitive field using a technique the compiler cannot elide (e.g., `OPENSSL_cleanse(m_Password.data(), m_Password.size())`).


---

### 31_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="31-doc-application-cloud-onedrivecloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/31_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — TOCTOU: file written before HTTP response code validated in `GraphDownload`

- **Location:** `GraphDownload`, approximately lines 195–230
- **Issue:** `std::fopen(outputPath.c_str(), "wb")` opens and truncates the destination file before the HTTP transaction completes. If the server returns a 4xx/5xx, the function deletes (truncates) the existing file and then returns `false` with an error, but the on-disk file is now zero bytes. More importantly, no atomic write-then-rename is used, so a partial or error response body is already persisted when the HTTP status check runs after `curl_easy_perform` returns.
- **Impact:** An error response body (potentially attacker-influenced content from a CDN redirect or an injected error payload) is written to the workflow's local path. If a downstream task reads that file and the workflow continues despite the error, attacker-controlled content enters the pipeline. Additionally, a legitimate file at that path is unconditionally destroyed before the operation is confirmed to succeed.
- **Fix:** Write to a temporary file in the same directory (e.g. `outputPath + ".tmp"`), verify `httpCode < 400` and `res == CURLE_OK`, then atomically rename to `outputPath`. Delete the temporary on failure. This also eliminates the truncation-before-confirm race.

---

### MEDIUM — `contentType` header value not validated for CRLF injection in `GraphRequest`

- **Location:** `GraphRequest`, approximately lines 155–165
- **Issue:** The `bearerToken` is checked with `ContainsCrlf` before being placed in the `Authorization` header, but the `contentType` parameter is accepted without any CRLF check and concatenated directly into a `Content-Type:` header string: `"Content-Type: " + contentType`. Any caller that supplies a `contentType` containing `\r\n` would inject additional HTTP headers.
- **Impact:** If any call site passes a `contentType` derived from user-controlled or JSON-parsed input, an attacker can inject arbitrary HTTP request headers (e.g. `X-Custom: evil\r\nAuthorization: Bearer attacker-token`). The current call site uses the literal `"application/octet-stream"`, but the parameter is public-facing and the absence of validation is a latent injection point for future callers.
- **Fix:** Apply `ContainsCrlf(contentType)` (and reject if true) immediately after the `bearerToken` CRLF check, before any `curl_slist_append` call for `Content-Type`.

---

### MEDIUM — Unvalidated `operation` field logged verbatim into error message

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately line 335
- **Issue:** The `else` branch sets `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage = "Unknown onedrive operation '" + operation + "'..."`. The `operation` value comes directly from the JSON params with no length cap or character sanitization before being embedded in the error message, which is likely written to logs or returned to a caller.
- **Impact:** A very long or specially crafted `operation` string could bloat log entries (log-flooding DoS) or, if the error message is ever rendered in a UI or downstream system that interprets control characters, cause log injection. The `getStringParam` helper returns an unbounded `std::string` from simdjson.
- **Fix:** Cap `operation` (and similarly `localPath`, `remotePath` before the validator runs) to a reasonable length (e.g. 64 bytes) immediately after extraction. For the error message, either truncate or only emit `operation.substr(0, 64)`.

---

### MEDIUM — `GraphDownload` does not validate HTTP status before reporting success when `res == CURLE_OK` but redirect goes to non-HTTPS

- **Location:** `GraphDownload`, approximately lines 215–230
- **Issue:** The function returns `true` (success) when `res == CURLE_OK && httpCode < 400`. `ApplyExecutorRedirectDefaults` is documented to restrict redirects to HTTPS, but if that function has any bypass or is misconfigured, a redirect to an HTTP URL would be silently followed (curl follows redirects transparently), the bearer token would be sent over plaintext, and the download would succeed from the perspective of `GraphDownload`. There is no explicit check in this function that the final effective URL is HTTPS.
- **Impact:** Bearer token disclosure over an unencrypted channel if redirect enforcement in `ApplyExecutorRedirectDefaults` fails or is bypassed.
- **Fix:** After `curl_easy_perform`, retrieve `CURLINFO_EFFECTIVE_URL` and verify it starts with `https://`. Reject and return `false` otherwise.

---

### LOW — File path included verbatim in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` on open failure

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately line 285, and `GraphDownload`, approximately line 193
- **Issue:** `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage = "Cannot open file for upload: " + fullLocalPath.string()` and `errorMessage = "Cannot open output file: " + outputPath` expose the resolved absolute filesystem path in error messages that may be returned to API callers, logged to external sinks, or stored in workflow run records.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: an attacker who can trigger these errors learns the absolute path layout of the server's filesystem, which aids further attacks.
- **Fix:** Log the full path internally at DEBUG level only. Return a generic message to the task error state (e.g. `"Cannot open local file for upload"`), or at most include the relative `localPath` that came from the task params.

---

### LOW — `simdjson::ondemand::document` `getStringParam` lambda captures `doc` by reference; re-use after iteration may be undefined

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately lines 250–265
- **Issue:** `getStringParam` is a lambda that captures `doc` by reference and calls `doc[key].get_string()`. `simdjson::ondemand` documents are single-pass iterators; calling `doc[key]` multiple times (for `operation`, `remote_path`, `local_path`) on an `ondemand::document` is not guaranteed to be safe. Depending on the simdjson version, accessing a key that was already iterated past returns an error or silently returns stale/invalid data, which could mean a missing value is treated as an empty string and the subsequent emptiness check is bypassed.
- **Impact:** In the worst case, a carefully ordered JSON object could cause `remotePath` or `localPath` to appear empty (triggering an early return) or to silently return a previous key's value. This is a correctness issue that can lead to logic bypass in input validation.
- **Fix:** Use `simdjson::ondemand` with `find_field` in key-order or switch to `dom::parser` for random-access multi-key extraction. Alternatively, parse all required keys in document order and cache the results.


---

### 32_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveConnector_h

<a id="32-doc-application-cloud-onedriveconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/32_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Tenant ID injected into URL without validation

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::GetAuthorizeUrl` and `OneDriveConnector::GetTokenUrl`, approximately lines 95–102 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** The `tenantId` string is taken directly from `connection.m_Params["tenant_id"]` (via `GetOAuth2ProviderInfo`, ~line 110) and concatenated into `https://login.microsoftonline.com/<tenantId>/oauth2/v2.0/authorize` and the corresponding token URL with no character validation. A tenant ID containing `/../`, `%2F`, or a full hostname with embedded path (e.g., `evil.example.com/inject`) turns the constructed URL into an arbitrary target, enabling SSRF or open-redirect to an attacker-controlled OAuth endpoint that returns a hostile token.
- **Impact:** Attacker who can control the config (or inject a connection record) redirects token-exchange traffic to their own server, harvesting authorization codes or injecting crafted access tokens. Even without full control, unexpected URL shapes can bypass TLS hostname pinning or reach internal metadata services.
- **Fix:** Validate `tenantId` against a strict allowlist — either a UUID/GUID regex (`[0-9a-fA-F]{8}-[0-9a-fA-F]{4}-…`) or one of the reserved literal strings (`common`, `organizations`, `consumers`) before using it in URL construction. Reject and log any value that does not match.

---

### HIGH — Unvalidated `m_Endpoint` used as SSRF vector in `GetGraphBaseUrl`

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::GetGraphBaseUrl`, ~line 80 in the .cpp; consumed in `TestConnection`, ~line 155
- **Issue:** `TestConnection` calls `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` only when `!connection.m_Endpoint.empty()`, which is correct for `TestConnection` itself. However, `GetGraphBaseUrl` is `static` and `public`, and nothing prevents other callers (present or future) from calling it directly and then issuing HTTP requests to the unvalidated URL. More immediately, the validation occurs *before* credentials are resolved but the validated URL is then re-derived inside `TestConnection` via `GetGraphBaseUrl` without re-checking — so if `m_Endpoint` could change between the check and the use (TOCTOU on a shared mutable config structure) the validated value is not the one used.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can mutate the connection config between validation and use, or any future caller of `GetGraphBaseUrl` that skips validation, can direct authenticated Bearer-token requests to internal infrastructure (metadata services, internal APIs).
- **Fix:** Store the output of `GetGraphBaseUrl` into a local after validation succeeds, pass that local to the curl setup rather than calling `GetGraphBaseUrl` a second time. Make `GetGraphBaseUrl` package-private or document explicitly that callers must validate before use.

---

### MEDIUM — Bearer token appended to error message and potentially logged

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, ~lines 183–192 in the .cpp (the `httpCode >= 400` block)
- **Issue:** `responseBody` from the server is appended directly to `errorMessage` when it is under 500 bytes. Microsoft Graph error responses routinely echo back parts of the request context. If the error body contains token fragments, correlation IDs, or other sensitive data, these flow into `errorMessage`, which callers typically log. Additionally `credentials.m_Token` is live in the same stack frame; any future logging of the full error context in callers risks including token material.
- **Impact:** OAuth access tokens or sensitive diagnostic data appear in application logs, which are often less protected than credential stores.
- **Fix:** Do not include raw server response bodies in `errorMessage`. Log the HTTP status code only. If response detail is needed for diagnostics, write it to a structured log at DEBUG level with explicit redaction markers, keeping it out of the string passed back to callers.

---

### MEDIUM — CRLF check on bearer token is post-resolution, not pre-header-build

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, ~lines 145–152 and ~lines 162–165 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** The CRLF check on `credentials.m_Token` is done before the header is built, which is good. However the check uses `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf`, a utility whose implementation is not visible here. If that function checks only for bare `\r\n` but not for encoded variants (`%0d%0a`, Unicode line separators U+2028/U+2029, or null bytes that some HTTP stacks treat as terminators), a crafted token could still inject additional headers. There is no equivalent check on `connection.m_Name` which appears in log statements and error messages but not in headers here — low risk in this file but a pattern that should be consistent.
- **Impact:** HTTP response/request splitting if the CRLF detection is incomplete; limited to scenarios where the token store is compromised or the OAuth server returns a malformed token.
- **Fix:** Ensure `ContainsCrlf` also rejects null bytes (`\0`) and percent-encoded CRLF sequences. Consider using libcurl's structured header API (`curl_slist_append` already handles this for well-formed inputs, but the defense-in-depth check should be comprehensive).

---

### LOW — `curl_slist_append` failure not checked

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 164 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `curl_slist_append` returns `nullptr` on allocation failure. If it fails, `headers` remains `nullptr`, `CURLOPT_HTTPHEADER` is set to `nullptr` (silently sending no Authorization header), and `curl_slist_free_all(nullptr)` is a no-op. The request proceeds unauthenticated.
- **Impact:** Under memory pressure, the test request is sent without a Bearer token. Depending on the server's response to an unauthenticated request, `TestConnection` could return `true` erroneously (if the endpoint returns 200 for anonymous requests), creating a false sense of connectivity validation.
- **Fix:** Check the return value of `curl_slist_append`; if `nullptr`, call `curl_easy_cleanup(curl)` and return `false` with an appropriate error message.


---

### 33_doc_application_cloud_polarionConnector_h

<a id="33-doc-application-cloud-polarionconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/33_doc_application_cloud_polarionConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — CRLF injection check applied only in TestConnection, not in ResolveCredentials or at HTTP send time

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 60–67 (the `ContainsCrlf` guard); `PolarionConnector::ResolveCredentials` has no equivalent check.
- **Issue:** The CR/LF check on `credentials.m_Token` is performed only inside `TestConnection`. Any other caller of `ResolveCredentials` (e.g., a scheduled sync task or a direct API call through `ICloudTaskExecutor`) receives the `CloudCredentials` struct with an unchecked token and could pass it straight into an HTTP `Authorization: Bearer <token>` header, splitting the header and injecting arbitrary HTTP headers or a second request.
- **Impact:** If an attacker controls the stored PAT value (e.g., through a compromised config or a malicious admin action), they can inject HTTP response-splitting or request-smuggling payloads into any outbound Polarion request that bypasses `TestConnection`.
- **Fix:** Move the `ContainsCrlf` guard into `ResolveCredentials` itself, returning false immediately if the resolved token contains CR or LF, so every caller is protected regardless of code path.

---

### MEDIUM — `project_id` parameter is URL-encoded but the base endpoint is concatenated without any structural validation after stripping the trailing slash

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 76–82 (URL construction block).
- **Issue:** `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is called on `connection.m_Endpoint` before the trailing-slash strip, but the resulting `url` variable is mutated (slash stripped, path appended) and then passed to `client.HttpGet`. If `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` validates only the original string and the mutated form introduces a different effective URL (e.g., the endpoint itself contains URL-encoded path components that decode to `/../` sequences after normalization by the HTTP library), the SSRF guard may not cover the final request target.
- **Impact:** Partial SSRF mitigation bypass: a crafted endpoint value that passes the validator but resolves to an internal address after HTTP-library normalization.
- **Fix:** Validate `url` (the final assembled string, after the trailing-slash strip and path concatenation) rather than, or in addition to, `connection.m_Endpoint`. Alternatively, parse the endpoint with a URL library, enforce scheme and host allowlists on the parsed result, and construct the path separately.

---

### LOW — Secrets present in `CloudCredentials` struct members are plain `std::string` with no zeroization on destruction

- **Location:** `struct CloudCredentials` in `cloudConnector.h`, approximately lines defining `m_Token`, `m_SecretKey`, `m_Password`.
- **Issue:** `std::string` members holding Bearer tokens, SigV4 secret keys, and Basic-Auth passwords are destroyed by the default destructor, which does not guarantee the memory is zeroed. Heap memory containing these values may be readable in core dumps, swap files, or by other processes on the same host before the allocator reuses the pages.
- **Impact:** Post-mortem or in-process memory disclosure of live credentials.
- **Fix:** Implement a destructor (or a custom allocator / a `SecureString` wrapper) that explicitly zeroes the backing buffer before deallocation, e.g. using `OPENSSL_cleanse` or `explicit_bzero` on `m_Token.data()` etc. prior to the string going out of scope.

---

### LOW — `connection.m_Name` logged directly without sanitization in security log lines

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 53 and 67 (`LOG_SECURITY_WARN` calls).
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is user-supplied and is interpolated verbatim into structured security log entries. If the logging backend writes to a text-based SIEM or log file and does not escape newlines or control characters, a crafted connection name (e.g., containing `\n`, `\r`, or ANSI escape sequences) can forge or corrupt log lines.
- **Impact:** Log injection — an attacker with the ability to create or rename connection entries can falsify security audit records.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `connection.m_Name` before logging: strip or escape control characters (at minimum CR, LF, and ESC). A helper such as `SanitizeLogField(connection.m_Name)` applied at every log call-site is sufficient.


---

### 34_doc_application_cloud_polarionWriteTaskExecutor_h

<a id="34-doc-application-cloud-polarionwritetaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/34_doc_application_cloud_polarionWriteTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via `file_path` and `field_value_file` Parameters

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `upload_attachment` branch (~line 195) and `update` branch (`field_value_file` read, ~line 130)
- **Issue:** Both `filePath` (for upload and download) and `fieldValueFile` are taken directly from task params JSON and passed to `std::ifstream`, `client.UploadAttachment`, and `client.DownloadAttachment` without any path canonicalization or confinement check. The declared `ValidateLocalPath` static helper exists on `ICloudTaskExecutor` but is never called in `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud` for any of these paths.
- **Impact:** Any workflow definition or injected task params that control `file_path` or `field_value_file` can read arbitrary files from the server filesystem (e.g., `field_value_file: "../../../../etc/passwd"`) or write downloaded content to arbitrary locations (e.g., overwriting SSH authorized_keys, cron jobs, etc.).
- **Fix:** Before opening or passing any path, call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ValidateLocalPath(filePath, allowedBaseDir, taskDefinition.m_TaskId)` and abort with a `Failed` state if it returns false. The allowed base directory should be the resolved task working directory or a dedicated upload/download staging directory, not the full filesystem root.

---

### HIGH Unbounded File Read into Memory (`field_value_file`)

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `update` branch, ~line 133
- **Issue:** The entire contents of the file at `fieldValueFile` are read into `fieldValue` via `istreambuf_iterator` with no size limit. There is no check on file size before or during the read.
- **Impact:** An attacker (or a misconfigured workflow) pointing `field_value_file` at `/dev/zero`, a large file, or a named pipe can cause unbounded heap growth, leading to OOM and process crash (DoS). Combined with the path traversal above, the target file need not be within any expected directory.
- **Fix:** Check file size before reading (e.g., `std::filesystem::file_size`) and enforce a reasonable maximum (the existing `kMaxCaptureChars = 1024` is not applied here; a separate larger but bounded limit, e.g., a few MB, should be chosen and enforced). Use a loop that accumulates bytes up to the limit and fails if the limit is exceeded.

---

### HIGH `credentials.m_Token` Potentially Leaked via `errorMessage` Propagation into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, all operation branches (e.g., ~lines 165, 178, 213, 228, 247)
- **Issue:** The `errorMessage` string returned by all `PolarionClient` methods (`UpdateWorkItem`, `CreateWorkItem`, `UploadAttachment`, `DownloadAttachment`, `FetchLinkedWorkItems`) is concatenated verbatim into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. If the underlying HTTP client (e.g., libcurl) includes request details—headers, URLs with tokens, or the bearer token itself—in error strings, that secret propagates into the task state, which is typically persisted and/or returned to API callers.
- **Impact:** Bearer token (`credentials.m_Token`) exposure in logs, workflow run state storage, or API responses visible to any user who can read workflow run results.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `errorMessage` before assignment: strip or redact any substring matching the token value. Better, ensure `PolarionClient` methods never embed credentials in error strings—pass only HTTP status codes and non-sensitive diagnostic text. Add a redaction pass: replace occurrences of `credentials.m_Token` in `errorMessage` with `"[REDACTED]"` before assigning to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`.

---

### HIGH `workItemId` and `attachmentId` Not Validated Before URL Construction

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, all operation branches that call `client.UpdateWorkItem`, `client.UploadAttachment`, `client.DownloadAttachment`, `client.FetchLinkedWorkItems` (~lines 160, 211, 227, 244)
- **Issue:** `workItemId` and `attachmentId` are extracted from task params and passed directly to `PolarionClient` methods without any validation of their format or character content. These values are almost certainly interpolated into URL path segments inside `PolarionClient`. A value such as `../../admin/settings` or one containing `?`, `#`, or `%2F` can alter the request URL.
- **Impact:** SSRF-adjacent URL manipulation: an attacker controlling task params can redirect Polarion API calls to arbitrary paths on the same host, potentially accessing or modifying unintended resources on the Polarion server.
- **Fix:** Validate `workItemId` and `attachmentId` against a strict allowlist pattern (e.g., `^[A-Za-z0-9_\-\.]+$`) immediately after extraction. Reject with `Failed` state on mismatch. Ensure `PolarionClient` percent-encodes path components independently.

---

### MEDIUM `operation` Value Reflected into Error Message Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, unknown-operation `else` branch, ~line 256
- **Issue:** The raw `operation` string from task params JSON is interpolated directly into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. If this message is logged (which `LOG_APP_INFO` calls elsewhere suggest it is), and if the logging destination is a structured log viewer or SIEM, an attacker-controlled `operation` value containing newlines, JSON special characters, or log-injection sequences will be written as-is.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forging: an attacker can inject fake log entries or corrupt structured log records.
- **Fix:** Either truncate `operation` to a safe maximum length (e.g., 64 characters) and strip non-printable/non-ASCII characters before embedding it in the error message, or avoid reflecting untrusted input into messages entirely (e.g., log "Unknown operation" without the value, or log it under a structured field that the log framework quotes/escapes).

---

### MEDIUM `fileName` Derived from Attacker-Controlled `filePath` Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `upload_attachment` branch, ~line 203
- **Issue:** When `file_name` is not supplied, `fileName` is derived via `std::filesystem::path(filePath).filename().string()`. The resulting name is passed to `client.UploadAttachment` and logged. If `filePath` is something like `/etc/passwd` (already a path traversal issue), `fileName` becomes `passwd`. Beyond that, if `filePath` contains Unicode normalization tricks or null bytes accepted by the filesystem but not by the Polarion API, the derived name may be unexpected. More concretely, the `LOG_APP_INFO` at ~line 214 logs `fileName` which is user-controlled.
- **Impact:** Log injection via `fileName`; unexpected attachment names on the Polarion server if the client does not further sanitize.
- **Fix:** After computing `fileName`, validate it: ensure it contains no path separators, no null bytes, no CRLF, and is within a reasonable length. Apply the same CRLF check (`ContainsCrlf`) already defined on the base class.

---

### MEDIUM `ContainsCrlf` Available but Not Called on Any Parameter

- **Location:** `ICloudTaskExecutor` (declared, ~cloudTaskExecutor.h), `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud` (never invoked)
- **Issue:** The base class explicitly documents and exposes `ContainsCrlf` for header-injection prevention. None of the string parameters extracted in `ExecuteCloud` (`workItemId`, `attachmentId`, `filePath`, `fieldName`, `fieldValue`, `body`, `operation`) are checked with it before being passed to `PolarionClient`, which presumably constructs HTTP headers (including `Authorization: Bearer <token>` and Content-Disposition headers for attachments).
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection: a `workItemId` or `fileName` containing `\r\n` can terminate a header and inject arbitrary headers into the outgoing Polarion API request, potentially forging host, content-type, or authorization headers.
- **Fix:** Call `ContainsCrlf` on every string extracted from params that is used in an HTTP context (`workItemId`, `attachmentId`, `fileName`, `fieldName`, `operation`) and return `Failed` state if it triggers.

---

### LOW `credentials.m_Token` Passed to `LOG_APP_INFO` Indirectly via `connection.m_Name` in Error

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, ~line 110
- **Issue:** The error message `"Connection '" + connection.m_Name + "' missing project_id param"` logs the connection name. While this is low sensitivity, `connection.m_Name` originates from external configuration and could be set to a value that causes log noise or injection if log output is structured.
- **Impact:** Minor log injection if `connection.m_Name` contains newlines or structured-log metacharacters.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `connection.m_Name` (strip non-printable characters, enforce length limit) before including in error strings. This is the same pattern as the `operation` finding above.


---

### 35_doc_application_cloud_postgresConnector_h

<a id="35-doc-application-cloud-postgresconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/35_doc_application_cloud_postgresConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — libpq connection-string injection via `escape()` for unquoted keyword `connect_timeout`

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::BuildConnectionString`, approximately line where `connect_timeout=10` is appended
- **Issue:** The `connect_timeout` keyword/value pair is appended as a raw unquoted literal (`" connect_timeout=10"`). This is not itself injectable since it is a hardcoded integer, but the `escape()` function used for all other values uses single-quote quoting per the libpq keyword/value format. libpq's keyword/value parser does accept single-quoted values, however the escaping function only escapes `\` and `'`. A value containing a space or a keyword name followed by `=` embedded inside a single-quoted value would be parsed correctly by libpq — that is fine. The actual injection risk is: libpq's keyword/value connection string format treats an **unquoted** value that contains whitespace as a terminator, splitting into a new keyword. The `escape()` function wraps in single quotes and escapes backslash and single-quote, which is correct for libpq's single-quote quoting rules. However, a `\` in the input is escaped to `\\`, and then a `'` is escaped to `\'` — but in libpq's parser a backslash before a single-quote **is not** a universally documented escape in all libpq versions. The PostgreSQL documentation for the keyword/value format states that within a single-quoted value, backslash escapes a following backslash or single-quote. This appears correct. **The real gap:** `host`, `port`, and `database` can contain attacker-influenced content from `connection.m_Endpoint` and `connection.m_Params`. If `escape()` is insufficient (e.g., a libpq version that does not treat `\'` as an escape inside single-quoted values), an attacker who controls the endpoint could inject additional keywords such as `sslmode=disable` or `host=attacker`. The safer and definitive fix is to use `PQconnectdbParams` (the keyword/value array form) instead of building a string, which eliminates all injection surface entirely.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can set `m_Endpoint` or `m_Params["database"]` to a crafted string could inject additional libpq connection parameters, potentially overriding `sslmode` to `disable` and forcing a plaintext connection, bypassing the `IsValidSslMode` gate that ran on the *pre-build* values.
- **Fix:** Replace `BuildConnectionString` + `PQconnectdb` with `PQconnectdbParams`, passing keywords and values as separate null-terminated arrays. This is injection-proof by construction because libpq never parses the values for keyword syntax.

---

### HIGH — Port number not range-validated; unbounded string accepted

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::ParseHostPort`, digit-check loop, approximately lines checking `isPort`
- **Issue:** `ParseHostPort` verifies that the port string contains only decimal digits, but does not check that the numeric value falls in [1, 65535]. A value like `"99999999"` passes the digit check and is forwarded to libpq as the port value. Additionally, there is no maximum length check on the port string, so an arbitrarily long all-digit string is accepted.
- **Impact:** Malformed port values passed to libpq may trigger unexpected behavior, crash the connection attempt in unexpected ways, or (if libpq truncates/wraps) cause a connection to an unintended port.
- **Fix:** After the digit check, parse `afterColon` with `std::stoul` (or `strtoul`) and reject if the value is 0 or > 65535. Also cap the digit string length at 5 characters before the numeric check.

---

### MEDIUM — Credentials (password) copied into a `std::string` with no zeroing on destruction

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::ResolveCredentials`, line `credentials.m_Password = std::string(basic->m_Password.Get());`, and `CloudCredentials` struct definition
- **Issue:** `CloudCredentials::m_Password` is a plain `std::string`. Once the `CloudCredentials` object goes out of scope the password material sits in heap memory until the allocator reuses it. There is no guaranteed zeroing. If `BuildConnectionString` is also called, a second copy of the password appears in the `connStr` heap allocation, again without zeroing.
- **Impact:** A process memory dump, core file, or swap image can expose the plaintext database password.
- **Fix:** Use a zeroing string type (a `std::string` wrapped with a custom allocator that calls `explicit_bzero`/`SecureZeroMemory` on deallocation) for `m_Password` and `m_SecretKey` in `CloudCredentials`. Overwrite `connStr` before it goes out of scope in `TestConnection` (e.g., `std::fill(connStr.begin(), connStr.end(), '\0')`).

---

### MEDIUM — `PQerrorMessage` / `PQresultErrorMessage` returned verbatim to caller

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::TestConnection`, lines setting `errorMessage` from `PQerrorMessage(conn)` and `PQresultErrorMessage(res)`
- **Issue:** libpq error messages can include the connection string fragment (host, dbname, user) and, in some PostgreSQL versions, portions of the query or server-side detail including schema names, table names, and constraint names. These are returned in `errorMessage` which is likely surfaced to API callers or logged at a non-security level.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: internal topology (host, port, database name, schema) and potentially credential-adjacent data exposed to the caller or in logs.
- **Fix:** Log the raw libpq error at `DEBUG` level internally (behind a dev-mode flag), and return a sanitised, generic error string to the caller such as `"PostgreSQL connection failed"` without the libpq detail. Ensure the sanitised message does not include `host`, `port`, `user`, or `dbname` values.

---

### MEDIUM — `IsLocalNetworkHost` trust boundary: RFC 1918 / link-local bypass

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::IsValidSslMode` (delegates to `ConnectorHttp::IsLocalNetworkHost`), enforced in `TestConnection`
- **Issue:** The plaintext-fallback sslmode gate is bypassed for any host that `ConnectorHttp::IsLocalNetworkHost` classifies as local (loopback, RFC 1918, link-local). This function is not shown, but the documented intent is that an operator-supplied hostname like `"192.168.1.200"` causes the gate to accept `sslmode=disable`. If an attacker can influence `m_Endpoint` (e.g., through a crafted config import, a compromised admin credential, or a SSRF-style config injection), they can point the connection at any RFC 1918 address — including a rogue server — with plaintext enabled.
- **Impact:** A rogue server on a private subnet can receive cleartext credentials and queries when sslmode is weakened via the local-network exemption.
- **Fix:** Restrict the local-network opt-out to loopback only (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`) at the security boundary. If RFC 1918 plaintext is genuinely required in development, gate it on an explicit compile-time or environment flag rather than on the address family of the endpoint.

---

### MEDIUM — `connection.m_Name` reflected unsanitised into `errorMessage`

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::ResolveCredentials` and `TestConnection`, multiple `errorMessage =` assignments that concatenate `connection.m_Name`
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is user-controlled config data and is interpolated into `errorMessage` strings without sanitisation. If `errorMessage` is later written to logs via a format-string logger or reflected in an HTTP response, a crafted name such as one containing log-injection sequences (`\n`, ANSI codes) or HTML can corrupt log integrity or produce XSS.
- **Impact:** Log injection; potential XSS if error messages are reflected in a web UI without escaping.
- **Fix:** Sanitise `m_Name` before interpolation (strip or escape newlines and non-printable characters). Ensure the web layer HTML-escapes all error text before rendering.

---

### LOW — Missing `PQfinish` on early-exit path if `PQexec` is never reached

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::TestConnection`, between `PQconnectdb` and `PQexec`
- **Issue:** There is no early-exit path between the successful `PQconnectdb` call and the `PQexec` call in the code shown, so this is currently not a leak. However, if future code adds a check between those two points and returns without calling `PQfinish(conn)`, the connection handle leaks. The pattern in the current code correctly calls `PQfinish` on all three exit paths (connection failure, query failure, success), but there is no RAII wrapper guarding `conn`.
- **Impact:** Connection handle and associated memory/fd leak under future code changes.
- **Fix:** Wrap `conn` in an RAII guard immediately after `PQconnectdb`: `struct PGconnDeleter { void operator()(PGconn* c){ PQfinish(c); } };` and `std::unique_ptr<PGconn, PGconnDeleter> connGuard(conn);`. This makes all future exit paths leak-safe by construction.


---

### 36_doc_application_cloud_providerRateLimitPolicy_h

<a id="36-doc-application-cloud-providerratelimitpolicy-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/36_doc_application_cloud_providerRateLimitPolicy_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.
