### 37_doc_application_cloud_redmineCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="37-doc-application-cloud-redminecloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/37_doc_application_cloud_redmineCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Unbounded response body accumulation enables memory exhaustion DoS

- **Location:** `RedmineRequest` — the `writeCallback` lambda, approximately line 175
- **Issue:** The CURL write callback appends every byte returned by the server into `responseBody` with no size cap. There is no `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE`, no manual length check, and no per-append guard. A malicious or compromised Redmine server (or a DNS/SSRF redirect to an attacker-controlled host) can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap growth until the process is killed by OOM or the host system is destabilized.
- **Impact:** Denial of service; potential forced OOM-kill of the workflow engine process, dropping all in-flight workflows.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside the write callback: if `buf->size() + (size * nmemb)` exceeds a defined cap (e.g. 4 MB for API responses), return 0 to signal failure to libcurl, which will then surface `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`. Additionally set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as a belt-and-suspenders limit.

---

### HIGH: `assigned_to_id` read from file is never validated as numeric before use in JSON body

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `update_issue` branch, approximately lines 330–360
- **Issue:** When `assigned_to_id_file` is supplied, the file content is read, whitespace-trimmed, and stored in `assignedToId`. The code then checks `std::all_of(... c >= '0' && c <= '9')` to decide whether to emit the value unquoted or quoted. If the content is non-numeric it is passed through `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` and emitted as a JSON string — but Redmine expects a numeric field. More critically, there is no validation that the value is a well-formed Redmine user ID (e.g. length cap, positive value check) before it is embedded in the request. An attacker who controls the file content (e.g. via a symlink race inside the project root, or a previously written workflow output file) can inject a very long or specially crafted string. The non-numeric path reaches `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString`, but if that function has any gap, arbitrary JSON structure can be injected into the request body. By contrast, `issue_id` is run through `IsValidRedmineIssueId` — `assigned_to_id` from file is not.
- **Impact:** Potential JSON injection into the Redmine API request body; sending unexpected or malformed user IDs to the Redmine server; possible server-side confusion depending on Redmine version.
- **Fix:** Apply the same `IsValidRedmineIssueId`-style validation to `assignedToId` after resolving it from any source (inline or file), before it is used in the body builder. Reject any value that is not 1–10 decimal digits.

---

### HIGH: `limitVal` integer is used in URL without range validation

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `list_issues` branch, approximately line 280
- **Issue:** `limitVal` is read directly from the task JSON as an `int64_t` and converted with `std::to_string` into the query string with no bounds check. A negative value (e.g. `-1`) or an extremely large value (e.g. `9999999999`) is appended verbatim to the URL and sent to Redmine. While `UrlEncodeComponent` on a digit string is a no-op, the lack of a range check means:  (1) the Redmine server may interpret a huge limit as a bulk data dump, leaking information; (2) a negative value could confuse some Redmine versions; (3) it violates the documented default of 25 and any server-side pagination assumption.
- **Impact:** Attacker-controlled workflow JSON can force bulk data extraction from Redmine (e.g. all issues in a project) and increase response sizes beyond reasonable bounds, compounding the unbounded allocation issue above.
- **Fix:** Clamp `limitVal` to `[1, 100]` (or whatever the maximum your policy allows) immediately after parsing: `if (limitVal < 1 || limitVal > 100) limitVal = 25;`.

---

### MEDIUM: API key is not checked for CRLF before header injection

- **Location:** `RedmineRequest`, approximately line 195
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` is concatenated directly into `"X-Redmine-API-Key: " + apiKey` and appended to the curl header list. The code comments in `ICloudTaskExecutor` acknowledge that CRLF in caller-supplied strings enables HTTP header injection and that `ContainsCrlf` exists for this purpose. However, `ContainsCrlf` is never called on `credentials.m_Token` (or on the `apiKey` parameter) inside `RedmineRequest` or at the `ExecuteCloud` call site. libcurl does strip embedded newlines in many versions, but the code itself documents reliance on this as a known risk and provides a check that goes unused here.
- **Impact:** If a credential value containing `\r\n` reaches an older libcurl build, an attacker who controls a stored credential can inject additional HTTP headers into every Redmine request made with that connection.
- **Fix:** Call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf(credentials.m_Token)` (or inline an equivalent check) before building `apiKeyHeader`, and fail the request with a logged error if the check fires.

---

### MEDIUM: Notes file content size is not bounded before embedding in HTTP request body

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `update_issue` branch, approximately line 310
- **Issue:** `ReadFileToString` reads the entire file referenced by `notes_file` into memory with no size limit. The resulting string is then passed through `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` and embedded in the Redmine PUT request body. A very large file causes: (1) unbounded heap allocation for the file read; (2) a correspondingly large HTTP request body sent to Redmine, which may reject it or cause network-level issues; (3) the escape step itself doubling the string's size in the worst case.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write a large file inside the project root and reference it via a workflow can cause significant memory pressure or forced request failures.
- **Fix:** After `ReadFileToString` returns, check the length against a reasonable cap (e.g. 1 MB) and fail the task if exceeded, before `TrimWhitespace` and embedding.

---

### MEDIUM: Error response body appended to `m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, both the `list_issues` and `update_issue` error paths, approximately lines 293 and 390
- **Issue:** When the HTTP response code is ≥ 400, up to 499 bytes of the raw server response body are appended to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. This string is presumably surfaced to operators, stored in workflow state, or logged. The body is not sanitised for control characters, embedded newlines, or log-injection sequences. A malicious Redmine endpoint (reachable via SSRF or a misconfigured connection) can craft a response body containing log-forging payloads or terminal escape sequences.
- **Impact:** Log injection; potential confusion of log analysis tools; if the message is later rendered in a UI without escaping, stored XSS or display corruption.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape non-printable characters and newlines from `responseBody` before appending it to `m_LastErrorMessage`. A simple approach: replace any `\r`, `\n`, or non-ASCII byte with a space or hex escape.

---

### MEDIUM: TOCTOU between `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` and `ReadFileToString`

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `update_issue` branch, approximately lines 305–320 (notes_file) and 340–355 (assigned_to_id_file)
- **Issue:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` resolves and validates the path, returning a canonical confined path. `ReadFileToString` then opens that path independently. Between these two operations, a privileged local process or a symlink could replace the target file or redirect the path to something outside the intended tree. This is a classic TOCTOU (time-of-check/time-of-use) race.
- **Impact:** In a multi-tenant or shared-filesystem deployment, an attacker with local write access could swap the file between the confinement check and the read, causing the engine to read an unintended file.
- **Fix:** Open the file descriptor immediately after confinement validation (ideally passing the `O_NOFOLLOW` flag and using `openat` with the directory fd held open) so the resolved path cannot be substituted between check and use. Alternatively, use `std::filesystem::canonical` on the opened stream's path and re-validate after open.

---

### LOW: `simdjson` `ondemand` document is re-queried after top-level iteration has advanced

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `getStringParam` lambda and subsequent `doc["limit"].get_int64()` call, approximately lines 240–285
- **Issue:** `simdjson::ondemand` is a streaming/forward-only parser. Once `getStringParam` has iterated certain keys, re-querying `doc["limit"]` or `doc["issue_id"]` as a different type through a separate `doc[key]` call can yield undefined behaviour or silently return a wrong value depending on field order and what has already been consumed. While this is not directly exploitable in isolation, a carefully ordered JSON object could cause `limitVal` to retain its default of 25 even when a large value was supplied, masking the bounds-violation described above; or cause `issue_id` to be missed, leading to an empty-string rejection rather than the correct field value.
- **Impact:** Logic errors in parameter parsing; in combination with the missing limit cap, an attacker could exploit ordering sensitivity to bypass or trigger unexpected behaviour.
- **Fix:** Either switch to `simdjson::dom` (random-access), parse the document once into a local struct capturing all expected fields, or use `ondemand` strictly in forward field order and document the required JSON key ordering constraint explicitly.


---

### 38_doc_application_cloud_redmineConnector_h

<a id="38-doc-application-cloud-redmineconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/38_doc_application_cloud_redmineConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: HTTP header injection via unsanitized API key

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, around the line `std::string apiKeyHeader = "X-Redmine-API-Key: " + credentials.m_Token;`
- **Issue:** Although `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf` checks for `\r` and `\n`, that check happens only once in `TestConnection`. The token is then used directly to construct a raw header string passed to `curl_slist_append`. If the CRLF check is incomplete (e.g., does not cover `\r\n` in all encodings, or if the check is bypassed in other call paths that reuse `credentials.m_Token`), an attacker who can control the API key value stored in `KeyManager` could inject arbitrary HTTP headers into the outbound Redmine request. More critically, the CRLF guard protects this specific site, but any other executor code that builds this header from `credentials.m_Token` without repeating the check is unprotected.
- **Impact:** Attacker-controlled credential content could inject additional HTTP headers (e.g., `Host:`, `Authorization:`, `Content-Type:`) into requests sent to the Redmine server, enabling request smuggling or auth bypass on the remote side.
- **Fix:** Use `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_XOAUTH2_BEARER, ...)` or, for custom headers, use `curl_easy_escape`-style validation. At minimum, assert that the token contains no octets ≤ 0x1F or 0x7F before any header construction, not just `\r`/`\n`. Centralise the check so it is enforced at header-build time, not only at the call site that happens to remember to call it.

---

### HIGH: SSRF — endpoint URL passed unsanitized to libcurl without scheme enforcement

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::GetBaseUrl` and `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, line constructing `url`
- **Issue:** `GetBaseUrl` only strips a trailing slash; it performs no scheme validation itself. The SSRF guard is delegated entirely to `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint`, whose implementation is not visible here. The comment says it "allows local-net for the http scheme, blocks it for https." This means `http://169.254.169.254/users/current.json` (AWS IMDSv1) or `http://192.168.x.x/...` are explicitly permitted by design for the `http` scheme. If this connector is reachable by any multi-tenant or user-supplied input, that design decision is itself an SSRF vector.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can create or edit a Redmine connection record can point the endpoint at internal metadata services (AWS IMDS, GCP metadata, internal APIs) and trigger credential exfiltration or internal network reconnaissance via the `TestConnection` call.
- **Fix:** Remove the local-network exemption for `http` scheme connections unless there is a hard deployment constraint preventing it, or gate it behind an explicit administrator-only allow-list. Additionally enforce that the scheme is exactly `http` or `https` (reject `file://`, `gopher://`, `dict://`, etc.) inside `GetBaseUrl` before any use, not only inside the validator.

---

### HIGH: Response body reflected into `errorMessage` without sanitisation (log/error injection)

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, block starting `if (httpCode >= 400)`
- **Issue:** `responseBody` (content received from the remote Redmine server) is appended directly into `errorMessage` when the HTTP status is ≥ 400 and `responseBody.size() < 500`. This string is likely logged and/or surfaced to the UI. A malicious or compromised Redmine server (or a MitM on an `http://` endpoint) can craft a response body containing newlines, ANSI escape codes, or structured log tokens that corrupt log files, inject spurious log entries, or confuse log-parsing tooling.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery. If error messages are also displayed in a web UI, partial control of UI content.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape all control characters (characters with code point < 0x20, DEL, and non-UTF-8 sequences) from `responseBody` before appending it to `errorMessage` or passing it to any logger.

---

### MEDIUM: TLS certificate verification status is opaque / not confirmed at this layer

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults(curl, url)`
- **Issue:** TLS configuration is delegated entirely to `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults`, whose implementation is not in scope. There is no visible assertion or compile-time guarantee at this call site that `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are set to their secure values. For `http://` endpoints the comment explicitly permits local-net hosts, meaning many real deployments will use plain HTTP and skip TLS entirely, leaving the API key exposed in transit.
- **Impact:** API key disclosure to network observers; MitM attacks on `http://` Redmine endpoints.
- **Fix:** Add an explicit `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L` at this call site rather than relying solely on a helper whose implementation may change. Document explicitly in the security model that `http://` connections transmit the API key in cleartext.

---

### MEDIUM: `credentials.m_Token` (API key) copied into a heap `std::string` with no zeroing on destruction

- **Location:** `CloudCredentials` struct (`cloudConnector.h`), fields `m_Token`, `m_SecretKey`, `m_Password`
- **Issue:** Secret material (`m_Token`, `m_SecretKey`, `m_Password`) is stored in `std::string` objects. `std::string`'s destructor does not zero memory before deallocation. On heap-using implementations the secret bytes may remain in freed memory and be recoverable via memory dumps, core files, or allocator reuse.
- **Impact:** Post-free secret recovery from process memory or crash dumps.
- **Fix:** Use a `SecureString` type whose destructor calls `OPENSSL_cleanse` or `explicit_bzero` over the buffer before releasing it. At minimum, explicitly zero these fields in `CloudCredentials`'s destructor or in `ResolveCredentials` immediately after the credential is consumed.

---

### LOW: `connection.m_Name` logged in security warning without sanitisation

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` calls
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is a user-supplied string and is interpolated directly into structured log output. It may contain newlines, tab characters, or log-format metacharacters (e.g., `{}` if the logger uses fmt-style formatting).
- **Impact:** Log injection or log-parser confusion if an attacker controls the connection name.
- **Fix:** Sanitise or quote `connection.m_Name` before logging (strip control characters; if using a structured logger, pass it as a typed field rather than embedding it in the format string).


---

### 39_doc_application_cloud_s3CloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="39-doc-application-cloud-s3cloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/39_doc_application_cloud_s3CloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Unvalidated bucket name injected into S3 endpoint URL

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, bucket resolution block (~line 240–260 of the .cpp) and `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl` (called with that value)
- **Issue:** The `bucket` value is taken directly from task params JSON (`getStringParam("bucket")`) or from `connection.m_Params["bucket"]` without any validation. It is then passed to `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl`, which presumably interpolates it directly into a URL (e.g., `https://<bucket>.s3.amazonaws.com` or `https://s3.amazonaws.com/<bucket>`). A bucket name containing characters such as `/`, `?`, `#`, `@`, or a full hostname like `evil.com/..` can redirect the request to an arbitrary host — a classic SSRF via URL construction.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls task params (or the connection config) can redirect S3 API calls to an arbitrary server, leaking SigV4-signed Authorization headers (including the secret-key-derived signature and the access key ID) to that server.
- **Fix:** Validate `bucket` against the S3 bucket naming rules (lowercase alphanumeric, hyphens, 3–63 chars, no dots for path-style endpoints that embed bucket in hostname) before calling `BuildEndpointUrl`. Reject and fail the task on any violation. The same validation should be applied to the bucket value read from `connection.m_Params`.

---

### HIGH — `region` value injected into SigV4 signing and request URL without validation

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, region resolution (~line 263) and every `S3Request`/`S3Download` call
- **Issue:** `region` is taken from `connection.m_Params.find("region")` with no format validation, then passed to `SigV4Signer::Sign` and embedded in the signed `Host` header and/or URL. A region string containing newlines, spaces, or URL metacharacters can corrupt both the canonical request used for signing and the actual HTTP headers sent by libcurl.
- **Impact:** Header injection into the outgoing S3 request; potential bypass of the SigV4 signature check on the receiving end if the canonical string is manipulated; at minimum, undefined/unexpected behavior.
- **Fix:** Validate `region` against a strict allowlist pattern (e.g., `^[a-z0-9-]{2,25}$`) before use.

---

### HIGH — `endpointUrl` built from unvalidated connection params used as CURLOPT_URL

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud` (~line 266, `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl`) and `S3Request`/`S3Download` where `CURLOPT_URL` is set
- **Issue:** `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl` receives the unvalidated bucket and connection params (which may include a custom endpoint override for S3-compatible stores like MinIO). The resulting URL string is set directly as `CURLOPT_URL`. If the connection params contain a `endpoint` key with a value like `http://169.254.169.254/` or `file:///etc/passwd`, libcurl will happily follow it.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF to internal metadata services (AWS IMDSv1, GCP metadata, etc.), local file read via `file://`, or credential theft via redirect to attacker-controlled host.
- **Fix:** Validate that the constructed URL's scheme is `https` (or `http` only if explicitly allowed for private/test endpoints) and that the host component matches an expected pattern (e.g., `*.amazonaws.com`, `*.backblazeb2.com`, or a configured allowlist). Apply this check inside `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl` or immediately after the call, before passing the URL to any libcurl function.

---

### HIGH — SigV4 credentials (access key ID) logged at INFO level on every operation

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload log line (~line 345), download log line (~line 385), list log line (~line 430), delete log line (~line 453)
- **Issue:** `LOG_APP_INFO` calls include the bucket and key but `credentials.m_AccessKeyId` is passed to `S3Request`/`SigV4Signer::Sign`. More critically, on error paths the `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` is set with S3 HTTP error details and the bucket name, and at the `upload` error path (~line 338) the raw `responseBody` (up to 500 bytes) is appended directly to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. S3 error XML responses can contain sensitive context; more critically if a custom endpoint returns an error body that includes a reflected copy of the `Authorization` header value, it leaks into task state which may be stored or returned to clients.
- **Impact:** Partial credential and request context leakage into task state storage and log aggregators.
- **Fix:** Strip or truncate the S3 error XML body before including it in `m_LastErrorMessage`; do not include raw S3 response bodies in user-visible error messages. Ensure `m_AccessKeyId` is never written to logs (it currently is not directly, but audit `SigV4Signer::Sign` for any internal logging).

---

### MEDIUM — `UrlEncodeS3Key` silently returns unencoded key on `curl_easy_init` failure

- **Location:** `UrlEncodeS3Key`, ~line 80
- **Issue:** When `curl_easy_init()` returns null, the function returns the raw, unencoded key string with the comment "Safe fallback … the actual request will also fail." This is not a safe fallback: the subsequent `curl_easy_init` in `S3Request` or `S3Download` may succeed (CURL handle pool state can be transient), and the unencoded key will then be interpolated into the URL, potentially injecting `?`, `#`, `&`, `=`, or whitespace characters that subvert the request URL structure.
- **Impact:** URL injection via unencoded S3 key when the CURL encoder handle allocation transiently fails; in adversarial conditions this could be triggered deliberately via resource exhaustion to enable injection.
- **Fix:** Return an empty string (and let the caller fail the task) rather than the raw key. Alternatively, implement percent-encoding without relying on a CURL handle.

---

### MEDIUM — `getStringParam` lambda captures `doc` by reference; simdjson `ondemand` single-pass invalidation

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `getStringParam` lambda definition (~line 215) and all call sites
- **Issue:** `simdjson::ondemand` documents are single-pass iterators. The lambda captures `doc` by reference and calls `doc[key]` repeatedly across multiple operations. After the first access to a key, re-accessing earlier keys or re-calling `doc[key]` in a different order from the JSON structure is undefined behavior in `ondemand` mode and can return stale/garbage `string_view` values backed by the `paddedJson` buffer. The returned `std::string` copies happen immediately, so the values themselves are safe after copy — but if `simdjson` internally advances the tape past the key on the first call, subsequent calls to the same or an earlier key silently return empty strings, silently omitting required parameters.
- **Impact:** Required parameters (e.g., `key`, `file_path`) could silently resolve to empty strings depending on JSON key ordering, causing logic errors. In the worst case an operation proceeds with an empty key (e.g., S3 delete with key="" targeting the bucket root, or upload to an unintended path).
- **Fix:** Use `simdjson::ondemand` correctly by reading all required fields in document order in a single pass, or switch to `simdjson::dom` parser which supports random access.

---

### MEDIUM — Path traversal in `workDir / filePath` after `ValidateLocalPath` — TOCTOU on `workDir`

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch (~line 300) and download branch (~line 360)
- **Issue:** `ValidateLocalPath` is called with `filePath` (the raw string) and `workDir`, returning a bool. Then `workDir / filePath` is constructed and used directly. If `workDir` itself is a symlink or has been replaced between the `ValidateLocalPath` call and the `std::filesystem::path` construction + `std::fopen`/`std::ifstream` call, the resolved path can escape the intended directory. Additionally, it is not shown that `ValidateLocalPath` calls `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` or equivalent on the *joined* path; validating the relative component alone without canonicalizing the join is insufficient when `filePath` contains sequences that only become traversal after joining (e.g., on some platforms).
- **Impact:** Path traversal to read arbitrary files (upload leak) or write arbitrary files (download overwrite), e.g., overwriting cron jobs or SSH authorized_keys.
- **Fix:** Inside `ValidateLocalPath` (or immediately after), compute `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(baseDir / localPath)` and assert it has `baseDir` as a prefix. Open the file via the canonicalized path. Consider opening `workDir` as a directory fd and using `openat`-style operations to eliminate TOCTOU.

---

### MEDIUM — Unchecked `std::filesystem::create_directories` on attacker-influenced path

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, download branch (~line 372–377)
- **Issue:** After the path traversal check passes, `std::filesystem::create_directories(outputDir, ec)` is called where `outputDir = fullFilePath.parent_path()`. The error code `ec` is captured but never checked. More critically, this call runs *after* `ValidateLocalPath` but the directory creation happens at the OS level with no verification that intermediate symlinks haven't been placed since the validation. A TOCTOU window exists between validation and `create_directories`.
- **Impact:** An attacker could place a symlink at any intermediate path component between validation and directory creation, causing directories to be created outside the intended tree, or triggering unexpected filesystem side effects.
- **Fix:** Check `ec` after `create_directories` and fail the task on error. Use canonical path resolution after creation and re-validate the final path before opening the file for writing.

---

### LOW — `operation` value echoed into `m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitization

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, unknown-operation error path (~line 460)
- **Issue:** The raw `operation` string from user-supplied JSON params is concatenated into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`: `"Unknown s3 operation '" + operation + "'"`. If task state error messages are written to logs, stored in a database, or returned to a UI, a long or specially crafted `operation` value (e.g., containing log-injection sequences like `\n`, ANSI escape codes, or JSON-breaking characters) could corrupt log output or stored records.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forging; potential stored XSS if the error message is rendered in a web UI without escaping.
- **Fix:** Truncate `operation` to a safe maximum length (e.g., 32 characters) and strip or escape non-printable/non-ASCII characters before including it in any error message or log statement.


---

### 40_doc_application_cloud_s3Connector_h

<a id="40-doc-application-cloud-s3connector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/40_doc_application_cloud_s3Connector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Unvalidated bucket name and region injected into HTTP URL (SSRF / header injection)

- **Location:** `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl` and `S3Connector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 100–115 and 130–135
- **Issue:** The `bucket` value (from `connection.m_Params["bucket"]`) and `region` value (from `connection.m_Params["region"]`) are concatenated directly into the constructed AWS URL (`"https://" + bucket + ".s3." + region + ".amazonaws.com"`) and into the query string (`endpointUrl + "/?list-type=2&max-keys=1"`) without any validation or encoding. A bucket name containing characters such as `@`, `/`, `?`, `#`, or a region string containing `\r\n` can redirect the request to an arbitrary host (SSRF) or inject extra HTTP headers/query parameters.
- **Impact:** An attacker with the ability to supply or modify the `bucket` or `region` configuration parameter (e.g., via the REST API that manages connections) can redirect the signed S3 request to an internal metadata endpoint (`169.254.169.254`), a private network host, or inject arbitrary HTTP headers into the libcurl request.
- **Fix:** Validate `bucket` against the S3 bucket naming rules (lowercase alphanumeric and hyphens, 3–63 chars, no dots for path-style, no IP-address form) and validate `region` against an allowlist of known AWS region strings (e.g., `[a-z]{2}-[a-z]+-[0-9]`). Reject any value that does not match before building the URL. Apply `curl_easy_escape` for any user-controlled value placed in a query string.

---

### HIGH — Custom endpoint accepted without validating scheme; SSRF to internal services

- **Location:** `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl`, approximately lines 93–100; `S3Connector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 120–125
- **Issue:** `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` is called in `TestConnection` when a custom endpoint is set, which is good. However, `BuildEndpointUrl` is a public `static` method that any other caller can invoke directly without that guard. Any caller that passes a `CloudConnection` with a non-empty `m_Endpoint` receives the raw concatenation `base + "/" + bucket` with no validation, bypassing the SSRF check entirely. Additionally, the comment documents that `m_Endpoint` may be empty to mean "AWS default," but there is no enforcement that the scheme is `https`; a caller could supply `http://` and the signed request (including the `Authorization` header containing the HMAC-derived signing key) would be sent in cleartext.
- **Impact:** Callers of `BuildEndpointUrl` outside `TestConnection` (e.g., an upload/download task executor not shown in this file) would route requests to arbitrary internal IPs. A downgrade to `http://` exposes the AWS secret access key material embedded in the `Authorization` header to network observers.
- **Fix:** Move the `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` call into `BuildEndpointUrl` (or into a shared validation helper called by both). Enforce that the scheme is `https` before constructing the URL; reject or upgrade `http` endpoints.

---

### HIGH — SigV4 secret key material stored as a plain `std::string` in `CloudCredentials`

- **Location:** `CloudCredentials` struct, `cloudConnector.h`, approximately lines 20–30; populated in `S3Connector::ResolveCredentials`, approximately lines 55–80
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_SecretKey` (and `m_Password`, `m_Token`) are plain `std::string` fields. `std::string` does not zero its buffer on destruction and the string data may be copied freely by value. The secret key ends up on the heap with an indeterminate lifetime and is never explicitly zeroed. In the same file, `CloudCredentials` is passed by reference into `TestConnection`, which creates it as a stack variable; the compiler may leave the key bytes on the stack indefinitely.
- **Impact:** A memory-disclosure vulnerability elsewhere in the process (heap spray, use-after-free read, core dump, `/proc/self/mem` read by a compromised subprocess) can expose long-lived AWS secret keys.
- **Fix:** Replace `std::string m_SecretKey` with a type that zeroes its buffer on destruction (similar to the `SecureString` pattern already used by `AwsCredential::m_SecretAccessKey.Get()` upstream). At minimum, call `explicit_bzero` / `SecureZeroMemory` on the string data before the `CloudCredentials` object is destroyed, using a RAII wrapper.

---

### MEDIUM — Error message returned to caller includes S3 XML response body without sanitisation

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 175–180
- **Issue:** When the HTTP response code is ≥ 400 and the response body is under 500 bytes, the raw S3 XML error body is appended to `errorMessage` and returned to the caller. Depending on how the caller surfaces this string (REST API response, log line, UI), it becomes a vector for log injection (`\r\n` sequences in the XML can forge log lines) and, if rendered in a web UI, a reflected XSS channel.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the S3-compatible endpoint (e.g., a malicious MinIO server they pointed the connection at) can craft a response body that injects log lines or, if the error is rendered as HTML, injects script content.
- **Fix:** Sanitise the response body before appending it: strip control characters (especially `\r`, `\n`, `\0`), limit the character set to printable ASCII, and HTML-encode if the string will be placed in an HTML context. Do not append raw third-party HTTP response bodies to user-facing or log-facing strings.

---

### MEDIUM — `responseBody` string has no size cap enforced at the libcurl layer

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 145–155
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` is set to `ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback` and `CURLOPT_WRITEDATA` points to `responseBody`, implying a bounded write. However, no `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or equivalent libcurl option is set to cap the total download at the transport layer. If `BoundedStringWriteCallback` itself does not enforce a hard cap (its implementation is not shown), a slow-loris or large-response attack from a malicious endpoint can cause unbounded heap allocation.
- **Impact:** A malicious or misconfigured S3-compatible endpoint can exhaust process heap memory, causing an OOM crash (DoS).
- **Fix:** Set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE, 65536L)` (or an appropriate small limit for a list-1-object response) in addition to relying on the write callback. This ensures libcurl aborts the transfer before the callback is even invoked if the Content-Length or actual data exceeds the limit.

---

### LOW — `connection.m_Name` included in error messages without sanitisation

- **Location:** `S3Connector::ResolveCredentials`, multiple error-message construction sites, approximately lines 45–85
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is a user-supplied string that is embedded verbatim into `errorMessage` strings that are likely logged and may be returned via API responses. A name containing `\r\n` can inject spurious log lines; a name containing `<script>` could be reflected into a web UI.
- **Impact:** Log injection or reflected content injection when error messages are surfaced to external consumers.
- **Fix:** Sanitise `m_Name` (strip or encode control characters) before embedding it in log/error strings, or log it through a structured logging facility that handles escaping automatically.


---

### 41_doc_application_cloud_sigV4Signer_h

<a id="41-doc-application-cloud-sigv4signer-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/41_doc_application_cloud_sigV4Signer_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Header Injection via Unsanitized extraHeaders Values

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, canonical header building loop (~line 280–295)
- **Issue:** Values from `extraHeaders` are folded into `canonicalHeaders` with no stripping of embedded newlines (`\r` or `\n`). A caller passing a header value containing `\nX-Injected: evil` will produce a malformed canonical-request string and, more critically, a malformed `Authorization` header or additional HTTP headers when the returned `m_Headers` map is serialized onto the wire by the HTTP client.
- **Impact:** If the HTTP client emits headers verbatim, an attacker who controls an extraHeaders value can inject arbitrary HTTP request headers into the S3 call, potentially forging X-Amz-Security-Token or other sensitive headers, or manipulating the request in ways the signature does not cover.
- **Fix:** Before inserting any key or value into `headersToSign`, reject or strip characters outside the printable ASCII range and explicitly reject CR (`\r`) and LF (`\n`). For example:

    auto sanitize = [](std::string const& s) {
        return s.find_first_of("\r\n") == std::string::npos;
    };
    if (!sanitize(key) || !sanitize(value)) throw std::invalid_argument("invalid header");

---

### MEDIUM Header Injection via Unsanitized extraHeaders Keys

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, same loop (~line 283)
- **Issue:** Header names from `extraHeaders` are lower-cased but otherwise unvalidated. A key containing `:` or `\n` corrupts the canonical-headers block and the signed-headers list, breaking the SigV4 signature in a way that could be exploited to make the signature cover a different set of headers than actually transmitted.
- **Impact:** Signature covers an incorrect header set, potentially enabling request tampering that survives server-side signature verification depending on tolerances in the S3-compatible endpoint.
- **Fix:** Validate header names against RFC 7230 token characters (`[a-zA-Z0-9!#$%&'*+\-.^_` backtick `|~]+`) before accepting them.

---

### MEDIUM Return Value of OpenSSL EVP Calls Not Checked

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sha256Hex` (~lines 100–108)
- **Issue:** `EVP_DigestInit_ex`, `EVP_DigestUpdate`, and `EVP_DigestFinal_ex` all return `int` (1 on success, 0 on failure). None of the return values are checked. If any call fails, `hash` is uninitialized or partially written, and `hashLen` remains 0, so the function returns an empty string silently.
- **Impact:** A failure in the EVP layer (e.g., FIPS mode disabling SHA-256, memory pressure) causes the signing function to produce an empty payload hash or an empty signature, which may be accepted by some S3-compatible servers or cause undefined behavior downstream when the empty string is used as a key in subsequent HMAC calls.
- **Fix:** Check each return value and throw or propagate an error:

    if (EVP_DigestInit_ex(ctx, EVP_sha256(), nullptr) != 1) { EVP_MD_CTX_free(ctx); throw std::runtime_error("EVP_DigestInit_ex failed"); }

---

### MEDIUM Return Value of HMAC Not Checked

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::HmacSha256` (~line 120)
- **Issue:** The `HMAC()` function returns `NULL` on failure; the return value is ignored. If it fails, `result` is uninitialized and `resultLen` is 0, causing `HmacSha256` to return an empty or garbage string used as the signing key in chained HMAC calls.
- **Impact:** Silent failure produces an incorrect or empty signing key, resulting in an invalid signature. Depending on server tolerance this could cause requests to fail silently or, in the worst case, be accepted with a null signature.
- **Fix:** Capture and check the return value:

    if (HMAC(EVP_sha256(), ...) == nullptr) throw std::runtime_error("HMAC failed");

---

### LOW Intermediate Signing Key Material Remains in Heap Memory

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign` (~lines 310–315), `SigV4Signer::HmacSha256`
- **Issue:** The derived signing keys `kDate`, `kRegion`, `kService`, and `kSigning` are plain `std::string` objects. Their memory is not explicitly zeroed before destruction, so the raw HMAC-SHA256 bytes linger in heap memory until overwritten by the allocator.
- **Impact:** A heap disclosure vulnerability elsewhere in the process (e.g., a heartbleed-style read, a use-after-free giving an attacker a heap read) could leak the derived signing key. With `kSigning` an attacker can forge arbitrary AWS SigV4 requests for the same date/region/service without knowing the original `secretKey`.
- **Fix:** Use a custom allocator or explicitly overwrite sensitive strings with `OPENSSL_cleanse()` before they go out of scope, or use `std::unique_ptr` with a custom deleter that calls `OPENSSL_cleanse`.

---

### LOW `dateStamp` Derived from Substring of `amzDate` Without Length Guard

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign` (~line 265)
- **Issue:** `dateStamp = amzDate.substr(0, 8)` assumes `amzDate` is at least 8 characters long. `GetAmzDate` uses `strftime` with a fixed format into a 32-byte buffer, so under normal operation this is always true. However, if `strftime` ever returns 0 (locale issue, clock failure), `amzDate` is empty and `substr(0, 8)` returns an empty string, silently producing an invalid credential scope without any error.
- **Impact:** Low severity under normal conditions; could cause a subtle signing failure that bypasses intended error handling if the system clock or locale is misconfigured.
- **Fix:** Assert or check `amzDate.size() >= 15` after `GetAmzDate()` returns and throw if the invariant is violated.


---

### 42_doc_application_cloud_slackCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="42-doc-application-cloud-slackcloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/42_doc_application_cloud_slackCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Unvalidated `limit` parameter used directly in URL construction (unbounded integer / parameter injection)

- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackRead`, approximately where `url` is assembled after reading `limitVal` from params JSON
- **Issue:** The `limit` value is read from attacker-controlled task params JSON as a raw `int64_t` and interpolated directly into the GET URL via `std::to_string(limit)`. There is no lower-bound or upper-bound check. A value of `-1`, `0`, or `999999999` is passed verbatim to Slack. More importantly, while `std::to_string` of an integer cannot inject URL metacharacters, a negative value such as `-1` is not a valid Slack API `limit` and the code nowhere rejects it. The `oldest` string parameter is more dangerous: it is read from JSON and appended to the URL as `&oldest=<raw string>` with no URL-encoding and no character validation. A value of `1234&token=foo` or `1234 HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: evil.com` would corrupt the URL or, depending on the libcurl version, enable header injection.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply task params (AI-generated workflow, compromised JCWF file) can inject extra query parameters into the Slack API call via `oldest`, potentially overriding `channel`, `token`, or other fields. With an extremely large `limit` the response body could be very large, causing memory exhaustion in the `responseBody` string and the subsequent `messages` vector (no cap on `WriteBodyCallback` accumulation or `messages` vector size).
- **Fix:** (1) URL-encode `oldest` with `curl_easy_escape` before appending. (2) Clamp `limit` to `[1, 1000]` (or whatever the documented Slack maximum is) and reject negative values. (3) Apply the same `ContainsCrlf` check already defined on `ICloudTaskExecutor` to `oldest`, `channel`, and any other string going into URL/headers. (4) Cap `WriteBodyCallback` at a configurable maximum (e.g. 10 MB) and return 0 to abort the transfer when exceeded.

---

### HIGH: `channel` ID not URL-encoded before URL construction

- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackRead`, URL assembly block
- **Issue:** `channel` (e.g. `C01ABCDEF`) is appended verbatim to the query string. The only validation applied is that it does not start with `#`. A value containing `&`, `=`, `%`, or whitespace is not rejected and is not percent-encoded. A value such as `C01ABC&limit=1000` would inject an extra query parameter.
- **Impact:** Parameter injection into the Slack API GET request; an attacker-controlled channel ID could override other query parameters.
- **Fix:** Pass `channel` through `curl_easy_escape` (or an equivalent percent-encoder) before appending to the URL. Alternatively, apply a strict allowlist regex (`[A-Z0-9]{1,20}`) matching Slack's documented channel ID format and reject anything that doesn't match.

---

### HIGH: Unbounded HTTP response body accumulation (DoS via memory exhaustion)

- **Location:** `WriteBodyCallback` / `PerformSlackRequest`
- **Issue:** `WriteBodyCallback` appends all received bytes into a `std::string` with no size limit. A malicious or misconfigured endpoint (or a SSRF-redirected endpoint if `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults` does not fully block redirects) can stream gigabytes, causing `std::bad_alloc` or OOM-kill of the process.
- **Impact:** Denial of service of the workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Check the accumulated size inside `WriteBodyCallback` and return 0 (causing libcurl to abort the transfer with `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`) once a threshold (e.g. 16 MB) is exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM: `credentials.m_Token` not validated for CRLF/control characters before header injection

- **Location:** `PerformSlackRequest`, `authHeader` construction
- **Issue:** `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf` exists and its comment explicitly documents the header-injection risk, but it is never called on `credentials.m_Token` before the token is spliced into `"Authorization: Bearer " + token`. If the token stored in `CloudCredentials` was loaded from a config file, environment variable, or external secret store that was tampered with, an embedded `\r\n` would inject arbitrary additional HTTP headers.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection; could be used to add `Host:`, `X-Forwarded-For:`, or custom headers to the outgoing Slack request, or to split the request.
- **Fix:** Call `ICloudTaskExecutor::ContainsCrlf(credentials.m_Token)` (and check for embedded NUL bytes) at the top of `PerformSlackRequest` and return an error immediately if the check fails.

---

### MEDIUM: `channel` value logged verbatim in `LOG_APP_INFO` (potential log injection)

- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackPost` LOG_APP_INFO call; `ExecuteSlackRead` LOG_APP_INFO call
- **Issue:** `channel`, `connection.m_Name`, `threadTs`, and `latestTs` are written directly into log lines without sanitisation. If any of these strings contain newline characters, log viewers may interpret the injected lines as genuine log entries. `threadTs` and `latestTs` are sourced from Slack API responses (attacker-influenced data).
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery; could mask malicious activity or mislead operators.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape `\r`, `\n`, and other control characters from any externally-sourced string before passing it to a log macro. A small helper `SanitizeForLog(s)` replacing control characters with `_` is sufficient.

---

### MEDIUM: `oldest` Slack timestamp not validated against expected format

- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackRead`, `oldest` parameter extraction
- **Issue:** Beyond not being URL-encoded (noted above), `oldest` has no format check at all. Slack timestamps are decimal strings of the form `<unix_seconds>.<microseconds>` (e.g. `1715000000.123456`). Accepting arbitrary strings allows URL parameter injection and may trigger unexpected Slack API behaviour.
- **Impact:** Combined with the missing URL-encoding, this enables parameter injection. Independently, it allows nonsense values to be sent to Slack, potentially returning unexpected data.
- **Fix:** Validate `oldest` against a strict regex (`^\d{10}\.\d{6}$`) before use and reject non-matching values.

---

### MEDIUM: `workDir` derived from `taskDefinition.m_WorkingDirectory` without visible confinement in `ExecuteSlackPost` / `ExecuteSlackRead`

- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackPost` and `ExecuteSlackRead`, working-directory resolution blocks near the end of each function
- **Issue:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskWorkingDirectoryPath` is called with `taskDefinition.m_WorkingDirectory`, which originates from the task definition (potentially AI-supplied). The resulting `workDir` is used to write output files (`messages_summary.json`, `latest_message.txt`, etc.) directly via `std::ofstream`. Unlike the `text_file` and `thread_ts_file` parameters, there is no `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` call or explicit traversal check on `workDir` before writing. If `ResolveTaskWorkingDirectoryPath` does not enforce confinement internally, this is a path traversal allowing arbitrary file writes.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write to any path writable by the process — configuration overwrite, log poisoning, or writing to a world-readable location.
- **Fix:** After obtaining `workDir`, assert it is under `workflowBaseDir` using the same `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` pattern (or `std::filesystem::relative` with error on failure) before creating directories or opening files. Do not rely solely on `TaskPathResolver` without auditing its implementation.

---

### LOW: `slackError` from Slack API response reflected into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `CheckSlackOk`, error extraction block
- **Issue:** The `error` field from Slack's JSON response is read and stored directly into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` as `slackError`. If this message is later surfaced in a UI, API response, or log, it contains attacker-influenced content (a malicious or spoofed Slack endpoint can return arbitrary strings here).
- **Impact:** Potential downstream injection (log injection, reflected content in a management UI). Low severity because the content is a server-to-server error string, not user-visible HTML, but the attack surface exists.
- **Fix:** Truncate `slackError` to a reasonable length (e.g. 256 chars) and strip control characters before storing it in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`.


---

### 43_doc_application_cloud_slackConnector_h

<a id="43-doc-application-cloud-slackconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/43_doc_application_cloud_slackConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM SSRF via unvalidated user-controlled endpoint

- **Location:** `SlackConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` (~line 45 in the .cpp), consumed by `SlackConnector::TestConnection` (~line 95)
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is accepted as the base URL and only validated in `TestConnection` via `ConnectorHttp::ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint`. However, `GetApiBaseUrl` is a public static method that strips a trailing slash and returns the raw user string with no further sanitization. Any caller of `GetApiBaseUrl` outside `TestConnection` (e.g., a future task executor or a connector method added later) will receive the raw endpoint without the SSRF check having been applied. The validation is positionally correct for the current code path, but the design separates validation from URL construction, making it easy to introduce a bypass when a new method is added that calls `GetApiBaseUrl` directly.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls `m_Endpoint` in a context that bypasses `TestConnection` can direct outbound HTTP requests to internal network addresses (metadata services, internal APIs, localhost), enabling SSRF.
- **Fix:** Move endpoint validation into `GetApiBaseUrl` itself so it is enforced at the point of URL construction regardless of call site. `GetApiBaseUrl` should accept an `std::string&` error output parameter, perform the `ValidatePublicHttpEndpoint` check, and return an empty string on failure. Remove the separate pre-flight call in `TestConnection`.

---

### MEDIUM Slack error string from server response reflected into `errorMessage` without sanitization

- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 155 (the `doc["error"].get_string()` block)
- **Issue:** The string value of the `"error"` field in the Slack JSON response is extracted and concatenated directly into `errorMessage` without any length cap or character filtering. `errorMessage` is returned to callers and, depending on how higher layers log or surface it, may be forwarded to log sinks or UI. If the endpoint is attacker-controlled (see SSRF finding above), the attacker fully controls this string. Even against the legitimate Slack API the field is externally sourced.
- **Impact:** Log injection if `errorMessage` is later written to structured or plaintext logs without escaping; potential UI injection if surfaced to a web front-end; unlimited-length strings could contribute to memory pressure.
- **Fix:** Cap the extracted `slackError` to a reasonable length (e.g., 256 characters) before concatenation, and strip or escape control characters (CR, LF, TAB) before storing into `errorMessage`.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded response body accumulation (DoS / memory exhaustion)

- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 105 (`CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` / `CURLOPT_WRITEDATA`)
- **Issue:** `ConnectorHttp::BoundedStringWriteCallback` is used, which implies a bound exists, but the bound is defined entirely inside `ConnectorHttp` and is invisible here. The `auth.test` endpoint response is tiny (< 1 KB), so any response significantly larger than that should be treated as anomalous and rejected. If `BoundedStringWriteCallback` silently truncates rather than aborting the transfer, parsing a truncated JSON document with simdjson may produce unexpected results. If the bound is large (e.g., several MB as a generic default), a rogue endpoint can still cause significant allocation.
- **Impact:** Against an attacker-controlled endpoint, large response bodies exhaust memory; truncation without transfer abort can cause simdjson to misparse the response and potentially reach an incorrect authentication conclusion.
- **Fix:** Verify that `BoundedStringWriteCallback` aborts the transfer (returns a value other than `size*nmemb`) once the limit is exceeded rather than silently truncating. For this specific call site, pass a small per-call cap (e.g., 16 KB) appropriate for `auth.test` responses.

---

### LOW `CloudCredentials` struct holds secrets in `std::string` with no secure-erase on destruction

- **Location:** `struct CloudCredentials` in `cloudConnector.h`, fields `m_Token`, `m_SecretKey`, `m_Password`
- **Issue:** `std::string` does not zero memory on destruction. After a `CloudCredentials` instance goes out of scope in `TestConnection` (~line 90 of the .cpp), the bearer token remains readable in heap memory until the allocator reuses it. This is relevant for `m_SecretKey` (SigV4) and `m_Password` (BasicAuth) as well.
- **Impact:** A heap-inspection primitive (use-after-free read, memory dump, core file) exposes long-lived credentials.
- **Fix:** Use a custom allocator that zeroes on deallocation, or explicitly zero the string contents in a destructor or RAII wrapper (e.g., `explicit_bzero` / `SecureZeroMemory` before the string is destroyed). At minimum apply this to `m_Token`, `m_SecretKey`, and `m_Password`.

---

### LOW Bearer token value logged indirectly via connection name in CRLF-rejection log

- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 88
- **Issue:** `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` logs `connection.m_Name`. `m_Name` is user-controlled and, while not the token itself, logging it in a security-event context alongside a token-rejection event could leak configuration details. More importantly, `m_Name` is not checked for CRLF or other control characters before being passed to the logger, enabling log injection through the connection name field.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can set `m_Name` can inject arbitrary text into security log entries, potentially forging or suppressing log lines.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `connection.m_Name` (strip CR, LF, and other control characters) before any log statement, or use a structured logging sink that escapes field values automatically.


---

### 44_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="44-doc-application-cloud-snowflakecloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/44_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Unbound memory allocation before response-size cap is enforced

- **Location:** `SnowflakeRequest` → `writeCallback` lambda, approximately lines 135–148
- **Issue:** `buf->append(static_cast<char*>(contents), incoming)` is only guarded by `buf->size() + incoming > kMaxSnowflakeResponseBytes`. The `std::string::append` call itself can throw `std::bad_alloc` or, on platforms where `std::string` grows geometrically, cause the internal buffer to be reallocated to a size considerably larger than `kMaxSnowflakeResponseBytes` before the check triggers on the *next* callback invocation. The check is correct in spirit but slightly racy against the allocator's geometric growth: the actual heap allocation at the moment of append may already be double the notional cap. More critically, `size * nmemb` is not capped before the addition; on a 32-bit build `size * nmemb` can overflow `size_t`, producing a value smaller than `kMaxSnowflakeResponseBytes` that passes the guard while `append` writes the true (large) number of bytes.
- **Impact:** An attacker-controlled or compromised Snowflake endpoint can cause unbounded heap growth (DoS), or on 32-bit targets, bypass the size guard entirely via integer overflow.
- **Fix:** Guard the multiplication: `if (nmemb > kMaxSnowflakeResponseBytes / size) return 0;` before computing `incoming`. Then recheck `buf->size() + incoming > kMaxSnowflakeResponseBytes` as already done.

---

### MEDIUM — `outputFile` path injected into `summary` JSON without escaping

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately lines 420–423
- **Issue:** The `outputFile` string (originating from the task params JSON `"output_file"` key) is concatenated directly into the `summary` JSON string: `",\"output\":\"" + outputFile + "\""`. Even though `ValidateLocalPath` rejects path-traversal attempts, it does not sanitize characters that are meaningful in JSON (backslash, double-quote, control bytes). A value such as `result","injected":true,"x":"` produces syntactically broken or attacker-controlled JSON stored in `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` and potentially surfaced through the dashboard/API.
- **Impact:** JSON injection into `taskState.m_CapturedStdout`; downstream consumers that parse this field without re-validation may misinterpret the structure.
- **Fix:** Apply `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString(outputFile)` when building `summary`, consistent with how all other string fields in this file are JSON-escaped.

---

### MEDIUM — Snowflake error code reflected verbatim into `m_LastErrorMessage`

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud` poll loop, approximately lines 340–350
- **Issue:** When a non-running, non-success status code is detected, `pollCode` (a `std::string_view` over Snowflake server-supplied data) and `errMsg` (likewise) are appended without length-bounding into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. Comments elsewhere in the same function explicitly avoid embedding raw Snowflake responses in `m_LastErrorMessage` for this reason, but the error-code path was missed.
- **Impact:** A compromised or malicious Snowflake endpoint can inject arbitrarily long strings into `m_LastErrorMessage`, which is persisted to the workflow state store and surfaced in dashboards/APIs. This can be used for stored cross-site scripting if consumers render it in HTML without escaping, or for log injection.
- **Fix:** Truncate both `pollCode` and `errMsg` to a safe maximum (e.g., 64 and 256 bytes respectively) before appending, or whitelist the known Snowflake error-code alphabet (digits only) and strip/truncate `errMsg`.

---

### MEDIUM — `resultReady` detection based on fragile string comparison against a localizable message

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines ~295 and ~330
- **Issue:** Result readiness is determined by comparing the `"message"` field against the hardcoded English string `"Statement executed successfully."`. This is not a documented stable API contract from Snowflake; it is a human-readable message. If Snowflake changes wording, adds punctuation, or localises the response, `resultReady` will never become `true` and the function will spin until `statementTimeout`. The proper signal is the `"status"` or `"code"` field (`"090001"` = success is already partially handled in the poll branch). Additionally, when the initial submit response already carries the result, the code sets `resultReady = true` and skips the poll loop entirely — but then parses `responseBody` for the result set. If the `submitDoc` has already been iterated (simdjson `ondemand` is single-pass), the `resultPadded`/`resultDoc` re-parse from the same `responseBody` string works only because a fresh parser/document is created; this is correct but fragile.
- **Impact:** Incorrect readiness detection can cause the task to time out on every query, effectively a self-inflicted DoS on the workflow.
- **Fix:** Use the documented `"status"` field value (e.g. `"success"`) or the `"code"` field (`"090001"`) rather than the `"message"` string to determine completion.

---

### LOW — `curl_slist_append` return value not checked; use of freed `headers` on failure

- **Location:** `SnowflakeRequest`, approximately lines 163–170
- **Issue:** `curl_slist_append` returns `NULL` on allocation failure. If any append fails, `headers` becomes `NULL` and subsequent appends operate on `NULL` (which `curl_slist_append` handles by starting a new list — so it silently loses the prior headers rather than propagating an error). After `curl_easy_perform`, `curl_slist_free_all(headers)` is called; if `headers` is `NULL` at that point, it is a no-op, which is safe, but the request will have been sent without the `Authorization` header.
- **Impact:** In an out-of-memory condition the JWT `Authorization` header is silently dropped; the request is sent unauthenticated. Snowflake will return a 401, which the caller surfaces as a normal failure, but the error message gives no indication that credential injection failed.
- **Fix:** Check the return value of each `curl_slist_append`. If any returns `NULL`, free the list built so far, cleanup curl, and return `false`.

---

### LOW — `WriteResponseJson` writes raw Snowflake response to disk without size check

- **Location:** `ICloudTaskExecutor::WriteResponseJson` (declared in `cloudTaskExecutor.h`, called at approximately line 430 of the `.cpp`)
- **Issue:** The full `responseBody` (up to `kMaxSnowflakeResponseBytes` = 64 MB) is written to `response.json` in the task working directory. There is no per-task disk-quota enforcement visible in this file. A hostile or malfunctioning Snowflake endpoint that streams the maximum allowed response body on every poll iteration can fill the filesystem hosting task working directories.
- **Impact:** Disk exhaustion DoS on the j9t host.
- **Fix:** Either cap `response.json` writes at a smaller limit (e.g. 1–4 MB is sufficient for debugging) or do not write the full response body to disk; write only metadata.


---

### 45_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeConnector_h

<a id="45-doc-application-cloud-snowflakeconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/45_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM: Incomplete CRLF sanitization on JWT — only CR and LF checked, not other header-injection characters

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, approximately at the `credentials.m_Token.find('\r')` check
- **Issue:** The guard rejects `\r` and `\n` in the JWT before it is spliced into the `Authorization: Bearer <token>` header, but it does not reject the null byte (`\0`). Some HTTP/1.1 stacks treat a null byte as a header terminator or silently truncate at it, which can cause the bearer token to be partially transmitted or misinterpreted. Additionally, the CRLF check occurs *after* `ResolveCredentials` has already materialized the private key PEM into a plain `std::string` (`privateKeyPem` in `ResolveCredentials`) and generated the JWT — the check is placed only in `TestConnection`, not in `ResolveCredentials`, so any other call path that invokes `ResolveCredentials` directly and then uses `credentials.m_Token` in a header gets no CRLF protection.
- **Impact:** A crafted or corrupted private key or JWT generator bug that produces a token containing `\r\n` (or `\0`) could inject arbitrary HTTP headers in paths that call `ResolveCredentials` without going through `TestConnection`. In `TestConnection` itself the risk is mitigated, but only there.
- **Fix:** Move the CRLF (and null byte) check into `ResolveCredentials`, immediately after `jwt` is populated and before it is stored in `credentials.m_Token`. Reject tokens containing any of `\r`, `\n`, or `\0`.

---

### MEDIUM: Private key PEM materialized into a plain `std::string` that is not zeroed on destruction

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::ResolveCredentials`, the line `std::string const privateKeyPem(keyPair->m_PrivateKeyPem.Get());`
- **Issue:** The RSA private key is copied out of a `SecureString` (which presumably zeroes its buffer on destruction) into a plain `std::string`. `std::string` does not zero its internal buffer on destruction; the key material will remain in process memory until the allocator reuses that page, and it may be swapped to disk or captured in a core dump.
- **Impact:** Memory forensics, core dump analysis, or a post-exploit heap scan can recover the RSA private key, enabling an attacker to impersonate the service to Snowflake indefinitely.
- **Fix:** Use a zeroing string type (a `std::vector<char>` with a custom allocator that zeroes on dealloc, or a `SecureString` variant) for `privateKeyPem`. Ensure `JwtGenerator::GenerateSnowflakeJwt` accepts such a type, or explicitly `memset`/`explicit_bzero` the `std::string` buffer before it goes out of scope (accepting that the optimizer may elide a plain `memset`; prefer `explicit_bzero` or `OPENSSL_cleanse`).

---

### MEDIUM: `account` and `user` parameter values are not validated before use in JWT subject construction

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::ResolveCredentials`, the `JwtGenerator::GenerateSnowflakeJwt(accountIt->second, userIt->second, ...)` call
- **Issue:** The `account` and `user` values come from `connection.m_Params` (a user-controlled config map) and are passed directly to `JwtGenerator::GenerateSnowflakeJwt` without any character-set or length validation. The Snowflake JWT subject is typically `ACCOUNT.USER` embedded as a JSON string claim. If `JwtGenerator` constructs the JWT payload by string concatenation rather than through a proper JSON serializer, a value containing `"` or `}` could break out of the claim and forge JWT fields. Even if the JWT library serializes correctly, an unbounded account/user string can produce an oversized JWT that triggers DoS in downstream validation.
- **Impact:** Depending on `JwtGenerator` implementation: JWT claim injection or outsized token that causes rejection or log flooding.
- **Fix:** Validate `account` and `user` against an alphanumeric-plus-hyphen-plus-underscore allowlist with a reasonable length cap (e.g., 128 characters each) in `ResolveCredentials`, before calling `JwtGenerator`.

---

### MEDIUM: `connection.m_Name` concatenated into `errorMessage` without sanitization — potential log injection

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::ResolveCredentials` and `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, multiple `errorMessage = "... '" + connection.m_Name + "' ..."` lines
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is a user-controlled string that is concatenated verbatim into `errorMessage`. If callers log `errorMessage` (likely given the pattern), an attacker who controls connection configuration can inject newlines or structured log tokens into the log stream, enabling log forging or log-parser confusion.
- **Impact:** Log injection; fabricated log entries that may hide attacker activity or confuse SIEM parsing.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `m_Name` before embedding it in log-bound strings by stripping or escaping control characters, or log `m_Name` as a structured field rather than interpolating it into a free-form string.

---

### LOW: Integer overflow possible in `writeCallback` response-size accumulation

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, the lambda `writeCallback`, the line `size_t const incoming = size * nmemb;`
- **Issue:** `size * nmemb` is computed in `size_t` without overflow checking. libcurl documents that `size` is always 1, but this is a convention, not a contractual guarantee enforced at compile time. If both values are attacker-influenced (e.g., via a malicious server response in a misconfigured or MITM scenario), overflow produces a small `incoming` that bypasses the cap check, allowing an unbounded write into `responseBody`.
- **Impact:** In an MITM or rogue-server scenario (e.g., if TLS verification were ever relaxed), heap corruption or uncontrolled memory allocation.
- **Fix:** Check for overflow before the multiplication: `if (nmemb > kMaxConnectorResponseBytes / (size ? size : 1)) return 0;` then compute `incoming`.

---

### LOW: `curl_slist_append` return value not checked; `headers` may be partially constructed on allocation failure

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, the five `curl_slist_append` calls building `headers`
- **Issue:** `curl_slist_append` returns `NULL` on allocation failure. The code assigns the return value back to `headers` unconditionally, so a single allocation failure silently discards all previously appended headers and sets `headers` to `NULL`, causing the request to proceed without the `Authorization` header.
- **Impact:** Without the `Authorization: Bearer` header, the Snowflake API returns 401; the test would correctly fail, but the failure path does not indicate that the JWT was never sent. In a future code path that reuses this pattern for actual queries, absence of the auth header could result in unauthenticated requests being silently retried.
- **Fix:** Check the return value of each `curl_slist_append`. On `NULL`, call `curl_slist_free_all` on the last valid pointer, set `errorMessage`, and return `false`.


---
