### 71_doc_application_web_aiJcwfService_h

<a id="71-doc-application-web-aijcwfservice-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/71_doc_application_web_aiJcwfService_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Path traversal via unsanitized `scriptPath` written to disk and read back

- **Location:** `FixFailedScriptAsync`, ~line where `std::ifstream ifs(scriptPath)` is opened; also `RunSingleAiCall` where `queueDir / subfolderName` is constructed
- **Issue:** `scriptPath` is accepted from the caller (ultimately from a WebSocket/HTTP client message) and passed directly to `std::ifstream` with no validation that it stays within an expected base directory. Similarly, `subfolderName` in `RunSingleAiCall` is composed from caller-supplied strings (e.g. `"fix_script_"` + seq, `"gen_" + seqStr + "_script_"`) and used as a filesystem path component via `queueBase / "_ai_jcwf_service" / subfolderName`. If an attacker controls the sequence counter prefix or any other component, crafted `..` segments could escape the intended directory.
- **Impact:** An attacker with access to the WebSocket endpoint can read arbitrary files the process has access to (via `FixFailedScriptAsync`'s `ifstream`) or create directories/files outside the queue base (via `RunSingleAiCall`'s `create_directories`).
- **Fix:** Canonicalize `scriptPath` with `fs::weakly_canonical` and verify it has the expected prefix before opening. For `subfolderName`, validate it contains only `[a-zA-Z0-9_-]` characters before use, or construct the path and assert `resolvedPath.string().starts_with(queueBase.string())`.

---

### [HIGH] `scriptPath` from `ExtractScriptPaths` is used in `fs::exists` check without canonicalization, enabling TOCTOU and path traversal into script generation

- **Location:** `GenerateAsync` lambda, section "Stage 3: Generate companion scripts", `fs::exists(sp)` check and subsequent use of `sp` as a key and in prompt text
- **Issue:** `sp` comes from attacker-controlled JCWF JSON submitted by the user. The check `if (!fs::exists(sp))` is evaluated against a relative path with no base-directory enforcement. A crafted JCWF could supply `"scripts/../../../../etc/passwd"` as a `command` value; `ExtractScriptPaths` would only reject paths that do not start with `scripts/`, but a path like `scripts/../../../etc/shadow` passes the `rfind("scripts/", 0) == 0` check. The path is then passed to `ValidateGeneratedScript`, embedded in AI prompts, and inserted into broadcast JSON.
- **Impact:** Path traversal to read sensitive files via the `FixFailedScriptAsync` flow; injection of arbitrary paths into AI context that could be used to exfiltrate file contents; `fs::exists` on attacker-controlled paths can be used to probe the filesystem.
- **Fix:** After extracting `sp`, call `fs::weakly_canonical(basePath / sp)` and verify the result still starts with the expected scripts base directory. Reject any path containing `..` components before further processing.

---

### [HIGH] AI response content injected unsanitized into broadcast JSON as raw value

- **Location:** `GenerateAsync` lambda, `broadcastResult` lambda, the `ok=true` branch: `ss << R"(,"jcwf":)" << jcwfOrError`
- **Issue:** `jcwfOrError` (which is `generatedJcwf`, raw AI-generated text) is written directly into the JSON payload without any sanitization or re-encoding. The comment even notes "jcwfOrError is raw JSON — embed directly (not string-escaped)". If the AI returns content that is not valid JSON, or returns a JSON object with crafted keys, the resulting broadcast string becomes malformed or adversarially shaped JSON. More critically, if an adversarial AI provider returns content designed to break out of the enclosing JSON structure, downstream WebSocket clients parsing the message may be exposed to JSON injection.
- **Impact:** WebSocket clients receiving the broadcast may parse malformed or maliciously crafted JSON, potentially triggering unexpected behavior in frontend JavaScript. If the frontend acts on injected fields (e.g. a crafted `"type"` key), this could escalate to client-side injection or unexpected workflow execution.
- **Fix:** Parse `generatedJcwf` with simdjson before embedding; only embed if it is valid JSON. Alternatively, base64-encode the JCWF payload and decode on the client side, eliminating the injection surface entirely.

---

### [HIGH] `stderrContent` from failed script included verbatim in AI prompt without size limit

- **Location:** `FixFailedScriptAsync`, construction of `task` string: `"--- stderr ---\n" + stderrContent`
- **Issue:** `stderrContent` is accepted from the caller (HTTP/WebSocket client) with no length validation. There is no cap on `stderrContent` size anywhere visible in this file. An unbounded string is incorporated into the AI prompt and written to disk via `RunSingleAiCall`.
- **Impact:** A malicious client can send a multi-gigabyte `stderrContent`, causing unbounded memory allocation when constructing the prompt string and when writing the TASK file to disk. This is an uncontrolled allocation / DoS vector. It also inflates AI API costs.
- **Fix:** Truncate `stderrContent` to a reasonable maximum (e.g. 64 KB) before use, with a log warning if truncation occurs.

---

### [HIGH] `popen("sw_vers 2>/dev/null", "r")` result embedded in AI prompts with no sanitization

- **Location:** `GetHostOsDescription`, macOS branch, result string built from `fgets` output and then incorporated into AI task prompts throughout `GenerateAsync` and `FixFailedScriptAsync`
- **Issue:** The output of `sw_vers` is read into `result` and returned verbatim. This string is then concatenated into multi-thousand-character AI prompt strings (e.g. `"Host OS: " + GetHostOsDescription()`). If `sw_vers` output were ever controlled or influenced by an attacker (e.g. via a compromised system, symlink attack on the binary, or environment variable manipulation), the content would flow into AI prompts. More concretely, the `sw_vers` output is not sanitized for newlines or special characters before being embedded in structured prompt text, which could confuse prompt structure.
- **Impact:** Prompt injection if `sw_vers` output is attacker-influenced; minor information leakage of OS version to AI provider.
- **Fix:** Validate `GetHostOsDescription()` output contains only printable ASCII and no newlines before embedding it in prompts. Consider returning a static string on macOS rather than executing a subprocess.

---

### [MEDIUM] `userPrompt` from the client embedded directly into AI prompts without any sanitization or size limit

- **Location:** `GenerateAsync` lambda, `decomposeProb`: `"User request: " + userPrompt`; also passed into `scriptCntx`, `sRevCntx`, and other prompt fields
- **Issue:** `userPrompt` is accepted from an external WebSocket/HTTP client and inserted verbatim into AI system/user prompts. There is no length cap and no sanitization. An attacker can craft prompts designed to override AI instructions (prompt injection), cause the AI to generate malicious JCWF or scripts, or exfiltrate context content through the AI response.
- **Impact:** Prompt injection attacks against the AI model; potential for AI to generate harmful shell/Python scripts that are then sent to the frontend for deployment; DoS via excessively large prompts inflating AI API costs.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum length on `userPrompt` (e.g. 4 KB). Consider sanitizing or escaping special characters. Add a system-level instruction that the user prompt is untrusted input and must be treated as data, not instructions.

---

### [MEDIUM] `currentJcwfJson` from the client embedded into AI prompts with no size limit

- **Location:** `GenerateAsync` lambda, `decomposeTask` construction: `"--- Current JCWF ---\n" + currentJcwf`; also `generateCntx`
- **Issue:** Like `userPrompt`, `currentJcwfJson` is caller-supplied with no size validation. A multi-megabyte crafted JCWF will be concatenated into prompt strings, written to disk as CNTX/TASK files, and submitted to the AI API.
- **Impact:** Unbounded memory allocation (DoS); inflated AI API costs; prompt injection via crafted JCWF content embedded in the AI context.
- **Fix:** Validate that `currentJcwfJson` is well-formed JSON (parse it with simdjson and reject on parse error before any processing). Enforce a maximum size limit.

---

### [MEDIUM] `jcwfJsonText` in `ExplainAsync` embedded in AI prompts without size or content validation

- **Location:** `ExplainAsync` lambda, `cntx1`: `"--- JCWF Workflow JSON ---\n" + jcwfText`
- **Issue:** `jcwfJsonText` is caller-supplied and embedded verbatim in AI prompts across two stages. No size limit and no JSON parse validation before use.
- **Impact:** Same as above: DoS via oversized input, AI API cost inflation, prompt injection.
- **Fix:** Parse and validate `jcwfJsonText` with simdjson at the start of the lambda before any AI calls. Enforce a maximum size.

---

### [MEDIUM] `subfolderName` written to disk as a directory name using caller-influenced strings without sanitization

- **Location:** `RunSingleAiCall`, `fs::path const queueDir = queueBase / "_ai_jcwf_service" / subfolderName`; callers supply values like `"explain_" + seqStr + "_stage1"`, `"gen_" + seqStr + "_generate_batch_" + std::to_string(batchIdx)`
- **Issue:** `seqStr` comes from `m_NextRequestSeq`, which is an internal counter and not directly attacker-controlled. However, some callers (e.g. `FixFailedScriptAsync`) use `"fix_script_" + std::to_string(m_NextRequestSeq.fetch_add(1))` — fully internal. The broader issue is that there is no general guard: any future caller that incorporates external input into `subfolderName` would silently introduce path traversal. Currently, the `"gen_" + seqStr + "_script_" + std::to_string(i)` path in `GenerateAsync` uses `i` which is also internal. The structural absence of path validation is the defect.
- **Impact:** If any caller passes attacker-controlled content into `subfolderName` (e.g. a script index derived from attacker-supplied task count), path traversal outside `queueBase` becomes possible.
- **Fix:** Add an assertion or runtime check in `RunSingleAiCall` that the resolved `queueDir` starts with `queueBase`. Use `fs::weakly_canonical` and compare prefixes.

---

### [MEDIUM] Secrets (API key) exposed in `outError` string on failure path

- **Location:** `TestAiInterface`, error path: `outError = "No API key configured for key_name '" + iface.m_KeyName + "'"`. More broadly, `outError` is a `std::string&` returned to the HTTP handler caller.
- **Issue:** While `iface.m_KeyName` is a name rather than a key value, the pattern of embedding credential-related information in error strings that flow back to HTTP responses is present. The `apiKey` variable is not directly leaked here, but the error message confirms the existence and name of a configured key to the caller.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: an unauthenticated caller can enumerate configured key names by calling the test endpoint with various interface indices.
- **Fix:** Return a generic error message ("Authentication not configured") without embedding the key name. Log the detailed error server-side only.

---

### [LOW] `FindTasksObject` and `FindMatchingBrace` perform manual JSON string parsing susceptible to edge cases

- **Location:** `FindMatchingBrace` and `FindTasksObject` anonymous namespace functions; used in `MergeJcwfFragments`, `PatchTasksIntoJcwf`, `FindTaskBlockBounds`
- **Issue:** The hand-rolled JSON brace matcher in `FindMatchingBrace` tracks `inStr` state but does not handle all JSON escape sequences correctly. Specifically, it handles `\\` (escaped backslash) but does not handle `\"` within strings correctly: when `c == '"'` it toggles `inStr` regardless of whether the preceding character was a backslash (the `\\` check only skips the next character, not correctly tracking that `\"` ends nothing). An AI-generated JCWF containing `"\""` in a string value could cause the brace matcher to misparse, leading to incorrect task block extraction or replacement.
- **Impact:** Incorrect merging or patching of JCWF JSON could silently corrupt the workflow before it is sent to the frontend and deployed. Crafted AI output could exploit this to inject content outside intended task boundaries.
- **Fix:** Use simdjson (already a dependency) to extract task blocks rather than manual string scanning. The existing `ExtractTaskIdsFromDecomposition` already uses simdjson correctly and should serve as the model for all structural JSON manipulation.

---

### [LOW] `GenerateRunId` uses `system_clock` seconds as a unique identifier — predictable and collision-prone

- **Location:** `GenerateRunId`, anonymous namespace
- **Issue:** Run IDs are generated as `workflowId + "_" + std::to_string(to_time_t(now))`, which has one-second granularity. Multiple concurrent calls within the same second produce identical run IDs, and the IDs are fully predictable to any observer.
- **Impact:** Run ID collisions cause log entries and queue subdirectories from concurrent requests to collide, potentially overwriting each other's files. Predictable IDs could allow an attacker to guess run IDs and monitor or interfere with ongoing operations if run IDs are exposed elsewhere.
- **Fix:** Incorporate `m_NextRequestSeq` (already available at call sites) or a random suffix (from a cryptographically adequate RNG) into the run ID. Replace `to_time_t` with a higher-resolution source such as `system_clock::now().time_since_epoch().count()`.


---

### 72_doc_application_web_mcpKeyManager_h

<a id="72-doc-application-web-mcpkeymanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/72_doc_application_web_mcpKeyManager_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Authentication check missing in `Authenticate` — disabled and expired keys accepted

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Authenticate`, approximately line 280–300
- **Issue:** After a successful hash match, the function never checks `rec->m_Enabled` and never checks whether `rec->m_ExpiresAt` is in the past. `m_DaysUntilExpiry` is computed and returned to the caller, but the caller is not forced to act on it — `Authenticate` itself returns a successful `AuthResult` for revoked (disabled) or expired keys.
- **Impact:** A key that an admin has disabled (e.g., after a suspected compromise) or that has naturally expired continues to authenticate successfully. Any middleware that relies solely on `Authenticate` returning a value to gate access is silently bypassed.
- **Fix:** Inside `Authenticate`, after the hash comparison passes, add explicit guards:
  ```
  if (!rec->m_Enabled) return std::nullopt;
  if (IsPast(rec->m_ExpiresAt)) return std::nullopt;
  ```
  `SelfRenew` already re-checks `m_Enabled` and `m_DaysUntilExpiry` on the returned `AuthResult`, but that is caller-side policy and is insufficient — the check must live inside `Authenticate`.

---

### [HIGH] `SelfRenew` contains a TOCTOU window — key state can change between `Authenticate` and the renewal write

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::SelfRenew`, approximately line 320–360
- **Issue:** `SelfRenew` calls `Authenticate(currentRawKey)` (which acquires and releases `m_Mutex`), inspects the returned copy of the record, then re-acquires `m_Mutex` to write the new key. Between the two critical sections, another thread can revoke or disable the old key. The stale copy returned from `Authenticate` will still show `m_Enabled = true` and a non-negative `m_DaysUntilExpiry`, so the renewal proceeds against a key that is no longer valid.
- **Impact:** A concurrent admin revocation is silently defeated. An attacker who triggers a renewal at the exact moment of revocation obtains a fresh 90-day key.
- **Fix:** Re-validate the original key while holding `m_Mutex` inside `SelfRenew` rather than relying on the unlocked `Authenticate` copy. Alternatively, fold the entire sequence (authenticate + mint new key + update old key) into a single lock scope with an internal unlocked helper.

---

### [HIGH] `CreateBootstrapAdminKey` hardcodes the identity `"boss"` — no caller-supplied identity

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::CreateBootstrapAdminKey`, approximately line 370–400
- **Issue:** The bootstrap key is always created with `rec.m_User = "boss"`. The first-run handler has no way to supply the actual operator's identity. Every installation in the world will have an admin key with the user field `"boss"`, making identity-based audit logs meaningless and making the key trivially recognisable if the plaintext JSON is ever exposed.
- **Impact:** Audit trails cannot identify which human holds the bootstrap key. If role-based access control elsewhere keys off the `m_User` field, the fixed string is predictable and may be abusable depending on downstream logic.
- **Fix:** Add a `std::string initialUser` parameter to `CreateBootstrapAdminKey` and require the first-run handler to supply the operator's actual identity (e.g., the e-mail entered during master-password setup).

---

### [MEDIUM] `UpdateKey` accepts arbitrary `m_Role` and `m_ExpiresAt` strings without validation

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::UpdateKey`, approximately line 420–435
- **Issue:** `fields.m_Role` is written directly to `rec->m_Role` with no check that it is one of the declared set (`"admin"`, `"operator"`, `"viewer"`). Likewise, `fields.m_ExpiresAt` is written verbatim with no ISO-8601 parse check and no lower-bound guard (a caller could set `m_ExpiresAt` to `"1970-01-01T00:00:00Z"`, immediately expiring a key, or to a date far in the future, bypassing intended rotation policy). This function is called from a web handler that presumably accepts JSON from authenticated users — role escalation is the primary concern.
- **Impact:** An operator-role caller whose web handler passes `UpdateKey` without additional server-side role-restriction could promote their own key to `"admin"` if the handler does not independently validate `fields.m_Role`. Even with handler-level checks, a programming error in any handler would silently result in role escalation stored to disk.
- **Fix:** Inside `UpdateKey`, validate that the supplied role is a member of the allowed set. Parse the supplied `m_ExpiresAt` with `ParseIso8601` and reject empty or unparseable values.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ParseFromJson` performs no bounds validation on numeric fields loaded from disk

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::ParseFromJson`, approximately line 480–540
- **Issue:** `r.m_DiskQuotaMb` and `r.m_KeyExpiryDays` are read as `int64_t` and cast to `int` with no range check. A maliciously crafted (or corrupted) encrypted file could supply `INT64_MAX` or a negative value. Although the file is decrypted with the master password (limiting who can forge it), a padding-oracle, backup-restore mistake, or an admin-level attacker who can write the encrypted file could inject extreme values that cause quota bypasses or unexpected behaviour (e.g., a negative quota allowing unlimited disk use downstream).
- **Impact:** Quota enforcement bypass; potential integer overflow if the quota value is subsequently used in arithmetic.
- **Fix:** After reading, clamp or reject values outside a defined safe range:
  ```
  r.m_DiskQuotaMb = static_cast<int>(std::clamp(ReadInt(obj,"disk_quota_mb",1024), INT64_C(0), INT64_C(1048576)));
  ```

---

### [MEDIUM] `Authenticate` registers the raw key with `SecretRedactor` on every successful call — unbounded memory growth

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Authenticate`, approximately line 310, and similarly `CreateEnrollment` ~line 255, `ActivateEnrollment` ~line 295, `SelfRenew` ~line 360
- **Issue:** Every successful `Authenticate` call unconditionally calls `SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret(rawKey)`. Since the same valid key may be presented on every API request (high-frequency endpoints), the redactor's internal store of secrets grows without bound for the lifetime of the process. `CreateEnrollment` also notes that registration "outlives the enrollment's TTL on purpose," meaning values are never removed.
- **Impact:** Sustained API traffic causes unbounded heap growth in the redactor, leading to process memory exhaustion (DoS). An adversary with a valid key can deliberately exhaust memory by issuing requests at a high rate.
- **Fix:** Check whether the secret is already registered before inserting (use a hash set with O(1) lookup). For `Authenticate` specifically, register once at key-creation time (in `ActivateEnrollment`/`CreateBootstrapAdminKey`) rather than on every authentication call.

---

### [MEDIUM] `Load` reads the entire encrypted file into memory before any size check

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Load`, approximately line 195–215
- **Issue:** The file is read completely into `blob` with `istreambuf_iterator` before any length validation. A `path` pointing to `/dev/zero` or a very large file on disk will exhaust process heap before the empty-check runs.
- **Impact:** An operator who can control the path (e.g., via a config-file injection) or a file that has been replaced with a large file causes a DoS of the engine process.
- **Fix:** Check `std::filesystem::file_size(path)` before reading, and enforce a reasonable upper bound (e.g., 10 MB).

---

### [MEDIUM] `IsPast` returns `false` (permissive) when the timestamp cannot be parsed

- **Location:** `IsPast` anonymous namespace function, approximately line 185
- **Issue:** If `m_ExpiresAt` is empty, malformed, or contains an unrecognised format, `ParseIso8601` returns `std::nullopt` and `IsPast` returns `false` — meaning the token or key is treated as **not** expired. This affects both enrollment TTL enforcement in `ActivateEnrollment` and `PurgeExpiredEnrollments`.
- **Impact:** A corrupt or deliberately empty `m_ExpiresAt` field (injected via `UpdateKey` or a crafted encrypted store) causes the key or enrollment token to never expire, defeating the TTL mechanism.
- **Fix:** Treat an unparseable or empty expiry as expired (return `true`) rather than as valid. At minimum, log a warning when the parse fails.

---

### [LOW] `SerializeToJson` emits `m_DiskQuotaMb` and `m_KeyExpiryDays` as raw integers without range checks

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::SerializeToJson`, approximately line 455–500
- **Issue:** `r.m_DiskQuotaMb` is a plain `int` emitted directly into a JSON string. If it somehow holds a negative value (see ParseFromJson finding), it serialises as a negative integer, which will be faithfully re-read on the next Load, perpetuating the bad value.
- **Impact:** Low on its own; compound risk with the ParseFromJson bounds issue above.
- **Fix:** Assert or clamp the value to the expected non-negative range before serialisation.

---

### [LOW] Timing side-channel in key-ID lookup before constant-time hash comparison

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Authenticate`, approximately line 282–290
- **Issue:** The key-ID prefix (`"mcp_" + first 8 hex chars`) is derived directly from the raw key and used to narrow the lookup to a single record before the constant-time `HashesEqual` comparison runs. This means `Authenticate` returns `std::nullopt` faster for a key-ID that does not exist in the store than for one that does, leaking whether a given key-ID prefix is registered.
- **Impact:** An attacker can enumerate registered key-ID prefixes (8 hex chars = 4 bytes of the raw key) via timing, reducing the brute-force search space for the remaining 28 random bytes.
- **Fix:** When no record is found for the key-ID, still perform a dummy `HashesEqual` call against a fixed sentinel hash to equalise the timing, then return `std::nullopt`.


---

### 73_doc_application_web_webServer_h

<a id="73-doc-application-web-webserver-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/73_doc_application_web_webServer_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Unauthenticated mock-AI endpoint in debug builds accepts arbitrary fixture paths

- **Location:** `HandleDebugMockAiResponsePost`, `RegisterCommonRoutes` (`/api/debug/mock-ai-response`)
- **Issue:** The route comment explicitly states "Auth-free on purpose." In a debug build, any network-reachable client can call this endpoint without any credential. The `header_fixture` and `body_fixture` parameters are validated to be safe basenames, but the endpoint itself is completely open. More critically, the fixture files are loaded from a path derived from `Core::g_Core->GetLaunchCWDAbsolute() / "test" / "dispatch" / "fixtures"`, which is controlled by the server's CWD. While the basename check is correct, an unauthenticated caller can force the server to read arbitrary `.json` files from that directory and return their contents, and can force controlled delays via `delay_ms` with no upper bound (unbounded sleep on the server thread handling the request).
- **Impact:** Any unauthenticated party on the network can: (1) read arbitrary fixture files from the server's filesystem under the fixtures path, (2) cause a request-handling thread to sleep indefinitely via `delay_ms=2147483647`, effectively stalling that Crow thread. In a multithreaded server with a fixed thread pool, enough concurrent calls exhaust available threads and deny service to legitimate users.
- **Fix:** Add `CheckAdminAuth` on this route (consistent with all other debug endpoints). Add a sane cap on `delay_ms` (e.g., `delay_ms = std::clamp(delay_ms, 0, 5000)`).

---

### [HIGH] `HandleLogAnalyzeLastRunGet` opens log file with a hardcoded relative path, bypassing the confinement check used by `ReadLogFile`

- **Location:** `HandleLogAnalyzeLastRunGet`, around the `std::ifstream logFile("log/log.txt", ...)` call
- **Issue:** `ReadLogFile` (called by `HandleLogGet` and `HandleSecurityLogGet`) applies a `ConfinePathUnder` check to ensure the resolved path stays inside `<launchCWD>/log/`. `HandleLogAnalyzeLastRunGet` opens `"log/log.txt"` directly via a relative path without applying the same confinement check. If the process CWD has been changed between startup and this call (e.g., by a sub-process or a misconfigured init system), the relative path resolves to an unintended file. The function also reads the entire log file into a `std::vector<std::string>` with no size cap, allowing a multi-gigabyte log file to exhaust server memory.
- **Impact:** Potential uncontrolled memory allocation leading to OOM/DoS if the log file is large. Inconsistent security posture that could be exploited if CWD changes.
- **Fix:** Use the same absolute path construction as `HandleLogGet` (`Core::g_Core->GetLaunchCWDAbsolute() / "log" / "log.txt"`). Add a line count or byte cap (e.g., read only the last 200,000 lines) before loading into memory.

---

### [HIGH] `ai-write-scripts` WebSocket handler uses `fs::path::lexically_normal()` for path confinement but writes relative to process CWD, not a pinned base

- **Location:** `RegisterWebSocket`, `ai-write-scripts` message handler branch
- **Issue:** The handler checks `normalized.string().rfind("scripts/", 0) != 0` to detect escape, then opens `std::ofstream ofs(normalized, ...)` where `normalized` is a relative path. The file is created relative to the process CWD at the time of the write, not relative to a pinned absolute base directory. If the CWD has drifted, or if a path like `scripts/../../workflows/evil.jcwf` is submitted (the `lexically_normal()` check should catch `..` traversal, but relies on the string prefix check being robust), the write target is not guaranteed. Additionally, `fs::create_directories(parentDir, ec)` can create arbitrary directory trees under the process CWD if the prefix check has any bypass.
- **Impact:** An admin-role WebSocket client could potentially write files outside `scripts/` if there is any path normalization edge case on the target OS (e.g., Windows drive-relative paths, UNC paths, symlinks in the `scripts/` directory itself that were pre-placed).
- **Fix:** Pin the write base to an absolute path: `fs::path const scriptsBase = Core::g_Core->GetLaunchCWDAbsolute() / "scripts"; fs::path const absTarget = (scriptsBase / fs::path(scriptPath).lexically_normal()).lexically_normal();` then verify `absTarget.string().rfind(scriptsBase.string(), 0) == 0` before opening. Use `ConfinePathUnder` (already available in helpers).

---

### [HIGH] `HandleOAuthCallbackGet` leaks `client_secret` via `postBody` if it appears in error logs or exception traces

- **Location:** `HandleOAuthCallbackGet`, construction of `postBody`
- **Issue:** `postBody` is built as a `std::string` concatenation that includes `client_secret` in plaintext. The variable is passed to `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS, postBody.c_str())`. If libcurl verbose logging is on, or if an exception is thrown after `postBody` is constructed and caught by a logging catch block elsewhere, the secret can be written to logs. More concretely, `responseBody` is logged verbatim on HTTP error (including a truncated version up to 1500 bytes), and the error string passed to `crow::response(500, errMsg)` is returned to the browser — if Google/Microsoft echoes back fields from the request body in their error response (which some do), the `client_secret` could appear in the HTTP response body sent to the admin's browser.
- **Impact:** OAuth `client_secret` exposure to the calling browser or log files.
- **Fix:** Never include `client_secret` in variables used for logging. Clear `postBody` after the curl call using `OPENSSL_cleanse` or assign to empty string. Ensure the error path does not return `responseBody` to the client if it could contain reflected request fields; return only a generic error message.

---

### [HIGH] `HandleProvidersSavePost` decrypts the existing keys file to verify the password, but `decrypted` string containing all provider secrets is never zeroed

- **Location:** `HandleProvidersSavePost`, `doSave` lambda
- **Issue:** `std::string decrypted = KeyEncryption::Decrypt(existingBlob, password);` holds the plaintext of the entire decrypted key store (all API keys, OAuth tokens, etc.) in a heap-allocated `std::string`. After the non-empty check, the string goes out of scope and is destroyed normally. `std::string` destruction does not zero memory. The plaintext secrets remain in process heap memory until the allocator reuses that region.
- **Impact:** A process memory dump (crash dump, `/proc/<pid>/mem` read on Linux by a local attacker, core dump written to disk) exposes all provider secrets in plaintext.
- **Fix:** Use a secure string type or call `OPENSSL_cleanse(decrypted.data(), decrypted.size())` before the string goes out of scope.

---

### [MEDIUM] `HandleLoginPost` returns `api_key` from request body in session cookie but does not enforce `Content-Type: application/json`, allowing CSRF via form submission

- **Location:** `HandleLoginPost`
- **Issue:** The login endpoint reads `api_key` from a JSON body parsed with simdjson. There is no `Content-Type` check. A browser can submit a `<form method="POST" action="/api/auth/login" enctype="text/plain">` where the body happens to be valid JSON (or close enough that simdjson skips it and `apiKey` stays empty, causing a 400). More importantly, the session cookie is set with `SameSite=Strict`, which mitigates most CSRF for same-origin flows, but `SameSite=Strict` is not supported in all browsers and older clients. The missing Content-Type check is a defense-in-depth gap.
- **Impact:** Reduced defense-in-depth against CSRF on login for clients not supporting `SameSite=Strict`.
- **Fix:** Reject requests where `Content-Type` is not `application/json`. Add `if (req.get_header_value("Content-Type").find("application/json") == std::string::npos) return MakeJsonResponse(415, ...)`.

---

### [MEDIUM] `ExtractSessionCookie` does not validate the extracted session token format before passing it to `WebSessionManager::Validate`

- **Location:** `ExtractSessionCookie`, `TrySessionAuth`
- **Issue:** The session cookie value is extracted verbatim from the `Cookie` header with no length limit or format check before being passed to `m_WebSessionManager.Validate(sessionId)`. A malicious client can send a `Cookie: session=<10MB string>` header. Crow may truncate headers, but if it does not, this forces `WebSessionManager::Validate` to look up an arbitrarily long string in whatever data structure backs it (likely an `unordered_map`), computing a hash over megabytes.
- **Impact:** Per-request DoS via hash computation cost on an oversized session token. No authentication bypass, but a flood of such requests from the pre-auth rate limiter's perspective still consumes CPU.
- **Fix:** Add a length check: `if (sessionId.size() > 128) return std::nullopt;` in `TrySessionAuth` before calling `Validate`.

---

### [MEDIUM] `HandleRunFilesListGet` and `HandleRunFileGet` ownership check uses a slug-based comparison that can collide between usernames

- **Location:** `HandleRunFilesListGet`, `HandleRunFileGet`, ownership check
- **Issue:** Ownership is verified by comparing `AdhocWorkflowManager::SanitizeUserSlug(auth.m_User)` against `info->m_OwnerSlug`. `SanitizeUserSlug` presumably maps arbitrary usernames to a filesystem-safe slug (replacing or removing non-alphanumeric characters). Two distinct users whose names differ only in characters that are stripped/replaced by the slug function would get the same slug, and one could read the other's run artifacts.
- **Impact:** Horizontal privilege escalation: user `alice@corp.com` and `alice_corp_com` could map to the same slug, allowing one to access the other's run artifacts.
- **Fix:** Store and compare the original user identifier (not the slug) for authorization checks. Use the slug only for filesystem path construction.

---

### [MEDIUM] `HandleOAuthCallbackGet` constructs `redirectUri` using `localhost` hardcoded, making it non-functional and potentially incorrect in production deployments

- **Location:** `HandleOAuthCallbackGet`, `HandleOAuthAuthorizeGet`
- **Issue:** `redirectUri` is always built as `scheme + "://localhost:" + std::to_string(port) + ...`. In a server deployment where j9t is accessed via a hostname (e.g., behind a reverse proxy), the redirect URI registered with the OAuth provider will not match, causing authorization to fail. More importantly from a security perspective: if an attacker can influence the `port` or the `scheme` determination (e.g., by manipulating `config.m_TlsCert`/`config.m_TlsKey` to empty strings), they could downgrade from HTTPS to HTTP, sending the authorization code over plaintext.
- **Impact:** OAuth authorization code sent over HTTP if TLS config is tampered with. The `code` can be intercepted in transit.
- **Fix:** Make the redirect base URL a configurable value (`config.m_OAuthRedirectBase`). Enforce HTTPS in production. Do not derive the scheme from cert file existence at request time.

---

### [MEDIUM] `HandleConfigSettingsPut` — `replaceField` uses string-based in-place JSON patching that can corrupt `config.json` for values containing JSON special characters

- **Location:** `HandleConfigSettingsPut`, `replaceField` lambda
- **Issue:** `replaceField` replaces JSON value regions by direct string substitution without escaping. For boolean and integer fields this is safe. However, the function is also called with `config.m_UseBashOnWindows ? "true" : "false"` and integer `std::to_string(...)` values, so currently safe. But the pattern is fragile: any future caller passing a string value (e.g., a file path containing `"` or `\`) would produce invalid JSON. A post-replacement simdjson validation tripwire exists, but it only prevents the corrupt file from being written, not the in-memory config from being in an inconsistent state.
- **Impact:** If a future maintainer adds a string field to this function, they could silently corrupt `config.json` or (if validation is removed) write an invalid file.
- **Fix:** This is an architectural concern. Use a proper JSON serialization library (e.g., simdjson write path, or nlohmann/json) rather than string-based patching. At minimum, document the restriction that `replaceField` must never be called with string values that require JSON escaping.

---

### [MEDIUM] OAuth PKCE state map (`m_OAuthCodeVerifiers`, `m_OAuthStateTokens`) is unbounded and never pruned

- **Location:** `HandleOAuthAuthorizeGet`, `m_OAuthCodeVerifiers`, `m_OAuthStateTokens` (class members)
- **Issue:** Each call to `HandleOAuthAuthorizeGet` inserts one entry per `connectionName` into both maps under `m_OAuthStateMutex`. Old entries are only removed in `HandleOAuthCallbackGet` when the flow completes. If an admin repeatedly calls the authorize endpoint without completing the OAuth flow (or an attacker calls it with many distinct `connectionName` values if any bypass exists), the maps grow without bound.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion DoS. Admins (or attackers who reach the endpoint) can grow the maps indefinitely. At the current access pattern (admin-only) the risk is low but real.
- **Fix:** Add a max-size check before inserting (e.g., reject if `m_OAuthCodeVerifiers.size() >= 100`). Add a timestamp to each entry and prune entries older than the OAuth flow timeout (e.g., 10 minutes) during cleanup.

---

### [MEDIUM] `HandleAiInterfacesSavePost` bracket-counting JSON scanner does not correctly handle escaped `[` or `]` characters inside string values

- **Location:** `HandleAiInterfacesSavePost`, bracket-counting loop
- **Issue:** The scanner advances past string literals by walking to the closing `"`, honouring `\"` escapes. However, it does not handle the `\\\"` sequence (a literal backslash followed by a quote terminator), meaning a string value like `"path\\["` would be misinterpreted: the scanner sees `\"` (escaped quote, continue) then `[` (bracket inside string, increments depth). This could cause `arrayEnd` to be computed incorrectly, resulting in a malformed replacement and a corrupt `config.json`.
- **Impact:** An admin who creates an AI interface with a URL or description containing `\\[` or `\\]` could corrupt `config.json`. The post-replacement simdjson validation tripwire should catch this and abort the write, but this represents an error in the core logic.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON parser to locate and replace the `"API interfaces"` array rather than a hand-rolled bracket counter. Alternatively, use the write-path of an established JSON library.

---

### [LOW] `HandleWebhookPost` — `callbackUrl` from request body is stored in `ContextMap` without SSRF validation

- **Location:** `HandleWebhookPost`, `callbackUrl` field handling
- **Issue:** The `callbackUrl` value from the webhook request body is stored into the `ContextMap` as `context["callbackUrl"]`. Workflow tasks that use this value to make outbound HTTP requests could be directed to internal network addresses (SSRF), as there is no validation that the URL points to an allowed external host.
- **Impact:** If any workflow task reads `callbackUrl` from context and makes an HTTP request to it, an external webhook caller can pivot to internal services.
- **Fix:** Either validate `callbackUrl` against an allowlist of permitted schemes and hosts at ingestion time, or document that workflow authors are responsible for validating this value before use. Consider stripping or rejecting `callbackUrl` values with RFC-1918 or loopback destinations.

---

### [LOW] `HandleRunFileGet` computes SHA-256 using the deprecated `SHA256()` one-shot function from `openssl/sha.h`

- **Location:** `HandleRunFileGet`, SHA-256 computation block
- **Issue:** `::SHA256(...)` from `<open

### 74_doc_application_web_webSessionManager_h

<a id="74-doc-application-web-websessionmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/74_doc_application_web_webSessionManager_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Timing side-channel in session-ID lookup

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::Validate`, ~line 110 (`m_Sessions.find(sessionId)`)
- **Issue:** `std::unordered_map::find` uses the raw string's equality operator, which is not a constant-time comparison. An attacker who can make many requests and measure response latency can distinguish "session ID prefix matches existing key" from "no match at all," enabling a timing oracle to enumerate or brute-force valid session IDs character by character.
- **Impact:** Statistical timing attacks could allow session token enumeration, reducing effective entropy of the 256-bit token in practice.
- **Fix:** Before the map lookup, apply a constant-time HMAC (e.g., HMAC-SHA256 with a server-side secret) to the incoming token, then look up the HMAC digest as the map key. Alternatively, after the map lookup, always execute a constant-time comparison (e.g., `CRYPTO_memcmp`) between the stored session ID and the supplied one, never short-circuiting on the result of `find` alone.

---

### MEDIUM — `RandomSessionId` returns an empty string on RNG failure; caller stores it

- **Location:** `RandomSessionId`, ~line 91; `WebSessionManager::Create`, ~line 109
- **Issue:** When `RAND_bytes` fails, `RandomSessionId` returns an empty string. `Create` does not check for this; it stores a `Session` with `m_SessionId = ""` and returns `m_SessionId = ""` to the caller. A subsequent call to `Validate("")` short-circuits with `nullopt` at the `sessionId.empty()` guard, so the empty-keyed session sits in `m_Sessions` permanently, leaking memory. More critically, if the HTTP layer sets a cookie with an empty value, any later request that arrives with no session cookie (`sessionId == ""`) would be rejected correctly — but any future code change that removes the `empty()` guard could accidentally validate every cookieless request against the ghost session.
- **Impact:** RNG failure results in a degenerate session that can never be validated or destroyed via `Destroy("")` (which would erase it, but only if the caller happens to call it with an empty string). Depending on caller behaviour this is a DoS accumulation of junk sessions; in a degraded-RNG environment (e.g., early boot) all sessions collapse to the same key, which is a session-fixation/collision risk.
- **Fix:** In `Create`, check that `s.m_SessionId.empty()` after calling `RandomSessionId()` and throw or return a sentinel `LoginResult` indicating failure. Never insert a session with an empty or collision-prone key. Consider also aborting/restarting the process if `RAND_bytes` fails, since a non-functional CSPRNG makes all security guarantees void.

---

### LOW — `m_TimeoutHours` read without lock in `PurgeExpired`

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::PurgeExpired`, ~line 133 (read of `m_TimeoutHours` before acquiring `m_Mutex`)
- **Issue:** `m_TimeoutHours` is read on line ~134 (`auto const idleLimit = std::chrono::hours(m_TimeoutHours)`) **before** `std::lock_guard lock(m_Mutex)` is acquired on line ~136. `SetTimeoutHours` writes `m_TimeoutHours` under the mutex. This is a data race on `m_TimeoutHours` under the C++ memory model (undefined behaviour).
- **Impact:** On weakly-ordered architectures the stale or torn value of `m_TimeoutHours` could cause sessions to never expire (very large value from a torn write) or expire immediately (zero/negative), depending on the race outcome. Incorrect expiry enables either session persistence beyond the intended window or premature invalidation (DoS of legitimate users).
- **Fix:** Move the `idleLimit` calculation to after the lock is acquired, or make `m_TimeoutHours` an `std::atomic<int>`.

---

### LOW — No upper bound on session count (uncontrolled allocation / DoS)

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::Create`, ~line 107
- **Issue:** `m_Sessions` grows without limit. Any authenticated caller (or, if authentication is checked by the caller before `Create` is invoked and that check has a flaw elsewhere) can create an unbounded number of sessions. `PurgeExpired` removes only idle sessions; if sessions are kept alive, memory grows without bound.
- **Impact:** An attacker with valid credentials (or an upstream auth bypass) can exhaust server memory by issuing repeated login requests.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum session count per user and/or a global cap (e.g., 10 000 total sessions). Reject `Create` with an error if the cap is reached; optionally evict the oldest session for the same user on re-login.


---

### 75_doc_application_workflow_adhocWorkflowManager_h

<a id="75-doc-application-workflow-adhocworkflowmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/75_doc_application_workflow_adhocWorkflowManager_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unsanitized `user` Field Used as Filesystem Segment Before `SanitizeUserSlug` Is Applied to `m_User`

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::ReadMeta` / `Init` scan loop, approximately lines 260–290 (`.cpp`); also `OnRunCompleted` and `Reap`
- **Issue:** `ReadMeta` reads `meta.m_User` directly from the JSON file on disk (via the naive `pluck` lambda) and stores the raw string into `RunMeta::m_User` and then into `m_DiskUsageByUser`. `m_DiskUsageByUser` is keyed by the raw user string, not the slug. More critically, in `Init`'s `scanRunFolder` lambda, `meta->m_User` (untrusted, read from a file that could have been tampered with on disk) is used to key `m_DiskUsageByUser` directly. A disk-resident `meta.json` with `"user": "../../../etc/passwd"` or a very long value does not affect filesystem paths here, but the inconsistency between the map key used at stage time (`req.m_User`) and the map key used at reap/read time (`meta->m_User` from disk) means a manipulated `meta.json` can orphan or inflate quota accounting for arbitrary user strings. Additionally `pluck` does no JSON unescaping, so a `\"` in the written value (which `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` should prevent, but is not verified in this file) would be silently misparsed.
- **Impact:** An attacker with write access to the `_adhoc` directory tree (or a bug in `EscapeJsonString`) can craft a `meta.json` that misattributes disk usage to an arbitrary user key, bypassing per-user quota enforcement entirely for the real user while pinning quota to a phantom key.
- **Fix:** After reading `meta.m_User` from disk, pass it through `SanitizeUserSlug` (or a more complete validator) before using it as a map key, and assert it matches the parent directory name. Use a proper JSON parser (simdjson is already available) in `ReadMeta` instead of the fragile `pluck` lambda.

---

### HIGH TOCTOU / Lock Gap Between `WouldExceedQuota` and the Guarded Disk-Usage Update in `Stage`

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::Stage`, approximately lines 340–360 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `WouldExceedQuota` acquires `m_Mutex` internally and releases it, then returns. The actual disk-usage increment (`m_DiskUsageByUser[req.m_User] += meta.m_DiskBytes`) happens later inside a separate `std::lock_guard` scope. Between the two acquisitions, a concurrent `Stage` call for the same user can pass the quota check with stale accounting, then both calls increment the map — potentially doubling the committed usage and exceeding the quota limit.
- **Impact:** Two concurrent adhoc submissions from the same user can both pass the quota gate and together consume up to 2× the allowed disk space, defeating the per-user quota as a DoS / resource-exhaustion control.
- **Fix:** Move the `WouldExceedQuota` check inside the same `lock_guard` that guards the rest of `Stage`, or restructure `Stage` so the entire check-and-commit is atomic under `m_Mutex`.

---

### HIGH Uncapped Uncontrolled Memory Allocation in `RewriteWorkflowId` Output Buffer

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::RewriteWorkflowId`, approximately lines 220–255 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** After simdjson parses the JSON, the function rebuilds the document by iterating every top-level field and streaming each `field.value` through `std::ostringstream`. `field.value` is the simdjson element-to-stream operator, which re-serialises the entire subtree. For a deeply nested or array-heavy JCWF that is, say, 4 MB, the re-serialised output can be substantially larger than the input (escaped strings, whitespace). There is no size cap on `out`. The 4 MB `kMaxJcwfBytes` cap at the top of `Stage` bounds the *input* but not this intermediate buffer.
- **Impact:** A crafted JCWF at the 4 MB ceiling containing many tightly-packed nested arrays could cause the ostringstream to allocate tens of megabytes per request, enabling per-request memory exhaustion (DoS) against the server process.
- **Fix:** After `out.str()` is produced, assert its size does not exceed a reasonable multiple of the input size (e.g. 2× `kMaxJcwfBytes`) before returning; return an error if it does.

---

### MEDIUM `ReadMeta`'s `pluck` Lambda Is Vulnerable to JSON Injection / Value Confusion

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::ReadMeta`, `pluck` lambda, approximately lines 265–280 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `pluck` searches for a raw substring `"field"` in the file content and then grabs the text between the next pair of double-quote characters. It performs no JSON unescaping. If a `meta.json` field value legitimately contains `\"` (escaped quote), `pluck` returns a truncated string. More dangerously, if the file has been tampered with so that a value contains `", "role": "admin"` written as a raw string (possible if `EscapeJsonString` were absent or bypassed), `pluck("user")` could return a value containing embedded JSON syntax, and subsequent `pluck("role")` calls might find the injected key instead of the real one.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write to `meta.json` on disk (e.g. via a path-traversal bug elsewhere, or a symlink race) can manipulate role attribution used by artifact-retrieval ownership checks.
- **Fix:** Replace `pluck` with a proper simdjson parse of `meta.json`. simdjson is already a dependency of this translation unit.

---

### MEDIUM Manifest Paths (`fe.m_RelPath`) Are Not Validated for Path Traversal Before Writing to JSON

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::WriteManifest`, approximately lines 370–420 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `fe.m_RelPath` is derived from `std::filesystem::relative(e.path(), folder)`. On most platforms this is safe, but `std::filesystem::relative` can produce paths containing `..` components if a symlink inside `folder` points outside it (symlink following is the default for `recursive_directory_iterator`). These `..`-containing relative paths are then written verbatim into `manifest.json` as the `"path"` field, which is later served to clients by artifact-listing endpoints.
- **Impact:** If a symlink is planted inside a run folder (e.g. by a concurrent process or a prior adhoc run with a crafted workflow), the manifest served to the client contains `..` paths. A client that naively joins the run folder with the advertised path to fetch the file would traverse outside the run folder.
- **Fix:** After computing `rel`, assert it contains no `..` component (`rel.begin()` iteration check, or check `rel.string()` does not start with `".."`). Also pass `std::filesystem::directory_options::skip_permission_denied` and consider `follow_directory_symlink` explicitly disabled (pass `std::filesystem::directory_options::follow_directory_symlink` negation — use `directory_options::none` instead of the default).

---

### MEDIUM `GenerateFolderName` Calls `m_Counter.fetch_add` Outside `m_Mutex` While Timestamp Is Taken Separately

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::GenerateFolderName`, approximately lines 200–210 (`.cpp`); called from `Stage` under lock
- **Issue:** `GenerateFolderName` is called from `Stage` while `m_Mutex` is held, and the counter is `std::atomic`, so the counter increment itself is safe. However `Iso8601NowCompactUtc()` (called inside `GenerateFolderName`) reads the wall clock, which is independent of the lock. Two rapid concurrent calls could get the *same* timestamp and *different* counter values, producing folder names that sort correctly — but the folder name is also used as the run ID stem, and `Init`'s scan reassembles run IDs from folder names. If two runs share a timestamp+counter (counter wraps at `uint32_t` overflow with only 4-digit formatting), folder name collision and directory-creation failure follow. More specifically the `"%04u"` format truncates counter values ≥ 10000 silently (e.g. counter 10000 formats as "10000" which is 5 chars and overflows the 7-byte `counterBuf` by one null byte — **stack buffer overflow**).
- **Impact:** At counter value 10000, `snprintf` writes `"10000\0"` — 6 bytes — into a 8-byte buffer, which is fine, but at 100000 it writes 7 bytes which still fits. At 1000000 (7 digits + null = 8 bytes) it exactly fills the buffer. At 10000000 it would overflow. Under normal operation this requires 10 million staged runs, but if `Init` reads a corrupted counter from disk the start value could be arbitrarily high.
- **Fix:** Increase `counterBuf` to at least 12 bytes and use `"%010u"` to keep fixed width, or use `std::to_string`. Separately, validate counter values read from folder names in `Init` against a sane upper bound.

---

### MEDIUM Disk-Usage Accounting Integer Overflow in `WouldExceedQuota`

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::WouldExceedQuota`, approximately lines 490–496 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `current + additionalBytes` is an unchecked addition of two `size_t` values. If `current` is near `SIZE_MAX` (possible if accounting has drifted due to the TOCTOU issue above), the addition wraps to a small number, making the quota check return `false` even though the user is far over quota.
- **Impact:** Quota bypass leading to uncontrolled disk consumption.
- **Fix:** Use a saturating add: `if (additionalBytes > limit || current > limit - additionalBytes) return true;`

---

### LOW `SanitizeUserSlug` Allows Empty Output for All-Filtered Input, Which Is Accepted as a Valid Slug

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::Stage`, approximately line 350; `SanitizeUserSlug`, approximately line 185 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `Stage` checks `userSlug.empty()` and returns an error, which is correct. However `SanitizeUserSlug` replaces disallowed chars with `_`, so a user string consisting entirely of Unicode multi-byte characters would produce a slug of all underscores (not empty), passing the empty check. A slug of `____` is still a valid directory name but is not tied to the actual user, enabling one user to pollute another's namespace if two distinct users produce the same all-underscore slug.
- **Impact:** Low-severity namespace collision; quota accounting could be merged between two distinct users who both produce `_` slugs, or one user could list/access another's run folder names.
- **Fix:** After slug generation, additionally verify the slug is not entirely composed of `_` characters, or derive the slug from a canonical identifier (e.g. SHA-256 of the raw user string hex-encoded) rather than character filtering.

---

### LOW `Reap` Reads and Removes Folders Outside `m_Mutex` for Disk-Usage Read, Creating a TOCTOU Window

- **Location:** `AdhocWorkflowManager::Reap`, approximately lines 440–470 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `ReadMeta(folder)` and `MeasureFolderBytes(folder)` are called **after** releasing `m_Mutex` (they run outside the lock scope that collected `toDelete`). A concurrent `OnRunCompleted` for the same folder can remove and update accounting between the unlock and the `ReadMeta` call. The subsequent `lock_guard` block then decrements `m_DiskUsageByUser` a second time, potentially wrapping the `size_t` to near-`SIZE_MAX`.
- **Impact:** Accounting wrap causes quota to read as effectively zero, bypassing all future quota checks for that user.
- **Fix:** Either snapshot the `RunMeta` from `m_RunIdToMeta` (already in memory, under lock) instead of re-reading from disk, or perform the decrement atomically with the folder removal under `m_Mutex`.


---

### 77_doc_application_workflow_aiCallTaskExecutor_h

<a id="77-doc-application-workflow-aicalltaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/77_doc_application_workflow_aiCallTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Shell injection via `popen` despite allowlist — spaces rejected but path not confined before allowlist check

- **Location:** `ConvertWithMarkitdown`, approximately where `command` is built and `popen` is called
- **Issue:** `IsAllowedMarkitdownPath` is checked against the raw `inputPath.string()` value, but the path arriving at `ConvertWithMarkitdown` is already a resolved `std::filesystem::path` from `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding`. The confinement (`ConfineUnderProjectRoot`) happens *before* this call, so the path is safe at that point. However, the allowlist itself permits `:` (colon), which on Linux allows paths like `/proc/self/fd:foo` or enables environment-variable-style tricks if the `markitdown` binary is resolved via `PATH`. More critically, the allowlist permits `=` and `+`, which are benign in paths but the real risk is that **the allowlist is the only shell-injection defense for a `popen("/bin/sh -c ...")` call**. If any future code path reaches `ConvertWithMarkitdown` without first passing through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` (e.g., a direct call from a new caller), the allowlist alone is a single point of failure. The defensive comment acknowledges this and defers a fix to "future migration to argv-based execution."
- **Impact:** If the allowlist is bypassed or a new call site is added without confinement, an attacker who controls a file path can inject shell metacharacters and execute arbitrary commands as the j9t process user.
- **Fix:** Replace `popen` with `posix_spawn` or `fork`+`execvp` passing `markitdown` and the path as separate argv elements. This eliminates the shell entirely and makes the allowlist unnecessary. Until then, remove `:`, `=`, `+` from the allowlist (they are not needed for normal filesystem paths) and add a static assertion or wrapper that enforces `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` is always called before `ConvertWithMarkitdown`.

---

### HIGH: `WriteTextFile` applies no path confinement — arbitrary file write

- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::WriteTextFile`, full function
- **Issue:** `WriteTextFile` is a `public static` method that accepts an arbitrary `std::string const& filePath` and writes caller-supplied content to it. It calls `std::filesystem::create_directories` on the parent and then writes. There is no call to `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` inside `WriteTextFile` itself. All *current* call sites pass paths that were previously resolved (materialization functions, sidecar write), but the public surface is unrestricted. The `WriteInlineQueueFileRefs` function calls `WriteTextFile(fileRef.m_Path, ...)` after only checking that the path is non-empty and logging it — there is no confinement gate before the write for inline files whose `m_Path` is absolute.
- **Impact:** A workflow definition or queue binding that supplies an absolute `m_Path` for an inline file (e.g., `/etc/cron.d/backdoor` or `~/.bashrc`) results in an arbitrary file write with the content of the inline payload, because `WriteInlineQueueFileRefs` does not call `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` before passing the path to `WriteTextFile`.
- **Fix:** Add a `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` check at the top of `WriteTextFile` (or at minimum inside `WriteInlineQueueFileRefs` before the `WriteTextFile` call) and return an error if the resolved path is empty. Making `WriteTextFile` private would reduce the exposed surface.

---

### HIGH: TOCTOU between `file_size` and `open` in `ReadTextFile`

- **Location:** `ReadTextFile`, the `file_size` → `ifstream` sequence
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::file_size` is called, the result is checked against `kMaxReadBytes`, and then `std::ifstream` opens the file in a separate operation. Between these two syscalls the file can be replaced (e.g., via a symlink swap or `rename`) with a much larger file (e.g., `/dev/zero` via a symlink) that bypasses the size cap.
- **Impact:** An attacker or a concurrent process that can write to the working directory can cause unbounded memory allocation in the parsing thread (heap exhaustion / OOM crash), a denial-of-service.
- **Fix:** Open the file first with `O_RDONLY | O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX), then call `fstat` on the open file descriptor to get the size, and refuse if it exceeds `kMaxReadBytes`. Read through the same fd. Alternatively, read incrementally and stop once `kMaxReadBytes` bytes have been consumed, without relying on a pre-check.

---

### HIGH: Glob expansion uses `directory_iterator` (not `recursive_directory_iterator`) but does not follow the confinement gate for matched paths

- **Location:** `ExpandCntxFileGlobs`, the `directory_iterator` loop and the path pushed to `outExpanded`
- **Issue:** Matched paths (`entry.path()`) are pushed directly into `outExpanded` as `m_Path` strings after only `lexically_normal()`, without calling `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`. The `parentDir` is derived from the glob pattern, which itself came from a `QueueFileRef::m_Path` that was resolved via `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath` — but `ResolvePath` is not shown and may not enforce confinement. If a glob pattern resolves to a directory outside the project root (e.g., via a symlink in the working directory), all matched files in that external directory are added as CNTX inputs without a confinement check.
- **Impact:** An attacker who plants a symlink in the task working directory pointing to `/etc/` and supplies a glob pattern `../../<symlink>/*.conf` can exfiltrate arbitrary system files into the AI prompt / output artifacts.
- **Fix:** After collecting `entry.path()` in `ExpandCntxFileGlobs`, call `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(entry.path())` and skip (or error) any entry that returns an empty path. Also apply `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` to `parentDir` before the directory iteration.

---

### MEDIUM: Inline file paths receive template substitution before confinement — prompt injection to path traversal

- **Location:** `Execute`, `substituteInlinePaths` lambda, and subsequently `localizeInlineFileRefs`
- **Issue:** `ApplySimpleTemplate` expands `{{...}}` placeholders in `fileRef.m_Path` using `taskState.m_InputValues` before any path confinement is applied. If workflow input values contain path traversal sequences (e.g., `../../evil`), the substituted path can point outside the working directory. `localizeInlineFileRefs` then resolves relative paths against `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` using `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath`, but no `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` gate is applied here either.
- **Impact:** A malicious workflow input value can redirect an inline file write to an arbitrary location inside or outside the project root, potentially overwriting sensitive files.
- **Fix:** Apply `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` after `localizeInlineFileRefs` for every inline file path that was modified by template substitution, before the path reaches `WriteTextFile`.

---

### MEDIUM: `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `weakly_canonical` — does not resolve symlinks

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` (pathConfinement.cpp), both the `root` and `candidate` canonicalization calls
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` resolves `..` components lexically and resolves symlinks only for the portion of the path that exists. If a symlink inside the project root points to a target outside the root, `weakly_canonical` resolves the symlink and the resulting path will contain the external target — which *will* fail the `lexically_relative` check. However, for paths where intermediate components are symlinks pointing outward and the final component does not yet exist (e.g., a symlink `proj/link → /etc` and path `proj/link/newfile`), `weakly_canonical` resolves `proj/link` to `/etc` but leaves `newfile` as-is, yielding `/etc/newfile` — which correctly fails the check. The *actual* gap is that `weakly_canonical` is called on `current_path()` itself with the same error code, so if `current_path()` returns an error, `root` is set to `fs::weakly_canonical({}, ec)`, and the empty-path check fires. This path is safe. A subtler issue: the `lexically_relative` check compares string prefixes after normalization but does not use `fs::path::string()` with a trailing separator — a root of `/proj` would incorrectly accept `/proj-evil/file` if the comparison were purely string-prefix-based. The current implementation uses `lexically_relative` which is path-component-aware, so this specific variant is not present. The residual concern is symlink loops causing `weakly_canonical` to return an error (which is correctly handled) versus a system where `canonical` would be stronger.
- **Impact:** In edge cases involving dangling symlinks or unusual mount points, the confinement check may be weaker than intended, but the fail-closed design limits practical exploitability.
- **Fix:** Use `std::filesystem::canonical` (which requires the full path to exist) where the path is expected to already exist (read paths). For write paths (which may not exist yet), use `canonical` on the existing prefix and `lexically_normal` on the remainder, then re-apply the `lexically_relative` check. This eliminates the `weakly_canonical` ambiguity for existing paths.

---

### MEDIUM: `BuildDefaultsMap` parses only two levels of nesting — deeper JSON silently dropped, but unescaped values flow into shell/JSON contexts

- **Location:** `BuildDefaultsMap`, the nested `simdjson::ondemand::object` loop
- **Issue:** String values from `workflowDefinition.m_DefaultsJson` are inserted into `defaultsMap` verbatim (as `std::string(nv.get_string().value())`). These values are later used by `ExpandWithDefaults` to fill `provider` and `model` fields. The `provider` and `model` strings then flow into `JsonHelper::EscapeJsonString` (safe for the sidecar JSON), but `envelopeInterfaceName` is used as-is for interface lookup (`apiCandidate.m_Name == envelopeInterfaceName`) — this is a string comparison, not a shell or SQL context, so injection is not directly exploitable there. However, if any downstream consumer logs or embeds these values without escaping, log injection is possible.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls `m_DefaultsJson` (e.g., via a malicious workflow file) can inject CRLF or format-string sequences into log lines, potentially poisoning log aggregators.
- **Fix:** Validate that string values from `BuildDefaultsMap` contain only expected characters (printable ASCII, no CRLF) before inserting into the map. Sanitize or truncate at the point of logging.

---

### MEDIUM: Chunked reduce callback captures raw `AiRequestPool*` — potential use-after-free if shutdown races

- **Location:** `Execute`, `ChunkAggregator::m_RequestPool` raw pointer, and the `onReply` lambda captures
- **Issue:** The `onReply` lambda is submitted to `AiRequestPool::Submit` and may be called from a curl/network callback thread. The comment acknowledges this and states that `Shutdown()` drains callbacks before the pool destructs. However, this guarantee is enforced by convention, not by the type system. If `Shutdown()` is not called (e.g., due to an exception or early exit path in `JarvisAgent`), or if callbacks are dispatched after the drain completes due to a race, `aggregator->m_RequestPool` becomes a dangling pointer. The null-check before deref (`if (agg->m_RequestPool != nullptr)`) does not help against a pointer that is non-null but points to freed memory.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free in the curl callback thread, potentially exploitable for code execution or memory corruption depending on allocator behavior.
- **Fix:** Replace the raw `AiRequestPool*` in `ChunkAggregator` with a `std::weak_ptr<AiRequestPool>` (requiring `AiRequestPool` to be owned via `shared_ptr`), or use a `std::shared_ptr<std::atomic<bool>>` shutdown flag that the pool sets before destruction. The callback then checks the flag before touching the pool pointer.

---

### LOW: Error messages in `ReadTextFile` and materialization functions leak internal absolute paths

- **Location:** `ReadTextFile` (`outErrorMessage = "Failed to open file for reading: " + filePath.filename().string()`), `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` (`errorStream << "Missing CNTX source '" << sourcePath.string()`), `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding`
- **Issue:** `outErrorMessage` is propagated into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and likely surfaced to the workflow caller or logged externally. Some messages include only `filename()` (safe), but `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` includes the full `sourcePath.string()` (absolute path). `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding` uses `sourcePath.filename().string()` which is safer but inconsistent.
- **Impact:** Absolute internal paths in user-visible error messages reveal the server's directory layout, aiding reconnaissance.
- **Fix:** Consistently use `filename()` or a project-root-relative path in error messages that may be returned to callers. Reserve full paths for internal debug logs only.

---

### LOW: `IsAllowedMarkitdownPath` permits `..` detection via substring, not path-component boundary

- **Location:** `IsAllowedMarkitdownPath`, the `path.find("..")` check
- **Issue:** The check `path.find("..") != std::string::npos` would reject `foo..bar` (a valid filename on most systems) as well as `../evil`. While the character allowlist already blocks `/` followed by `..` sequences that would be meaningful at the OS level, the substring check is overly broad (false positives) and the comment says it is "defensive." The character allowlist does not include spaces (correct), but file stems containing `..` are rejected even when they are safe. This is a functional correctness issue rather than a security bypass, but it narrows the safe input space in a way that could cause operators to weaken the allowlist.
- **Impact:** No direct security impact; the allowlist is the binding constraint. However, overly broad rejection may prompt future developers to loosen the allowlist, weakening the real defense.
- **Fix:** Replace the substring `..` check with a path-component-level check: split on `/` and reject any component that equals `..` exactly. This correctly handles `foo..bar` (allowed) vs. `../evil` (rejected).

### 80_doc_application_workflow_aiRequestPool_h

<a id="80-doc-application-workflow-airequestpool-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/80_doc_application_workflow_aiRequestPool_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path-Traversal via `weakly_canonical` in `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, pathConfinement.cpp
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` does not resolve symlinks that exist on disk; it only normalises lexical `.` and `..` components for path elements that do not exist. For path elements that *do* exist, it calls `canonical` internally, which *does* follow symlinks. The consequence is asymmetric: a symlink inside the project tree that points outside it (e.g. `<project>/link -> /etc`) will be followed by `weakly_canonical`, and the resolved path (`/etc/passwd`) will then be checked with `lexically_relative(root)`. That relative path starts with `..`, so the check fires correctly *only if the target is literally outside root*. However, a symlink pointing to another directory still *inside* the tree but created by an attacker (via a world-writable subdirectory) may resolve to an unintended canonical path that passes the prefix check. More critically, the check `relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0` only rejects strings that begin with `..`; a relative component such as `a/../../../etc` is first normalised away by `weakly_canonical`, so the concern is the post-normalisation result — but if the symlink target itself is an absolute path that resolves to a sibling of root (e.g. `/projects/other`), `lexically_relative` returns `../../other`, which starts with `..` and is correctly rejected. The genuine gap is: `weakly_canonical` with a non-existent intermediate directory that contains a symlink component may silently truncate the resolution and return a path that lexically appears inside root but physically is not. This is a known TOCTOU class: the check runs at registration time; by the time `WriteTextFile` or `OnOutputFileCreated` opens the file, a race-created symlink can redirect it.
- **Impact:** A hostile JCWF `expected_output` field or crafted queue-folder path could escape the project tree for reads (`OnOutputFileCreated`) or writes (`WriteTextFile` / `BuildCompletionOutputs`), leading to arbitrary file read or write as the process user.
- **Fix:** Use `fs::canonical` (which requires the full path to exist) rather than `weakly_canonical`. For paths that may not yet exist (output files about to be written), canonicalise only the *parent directory* (which must exist before writing), then append the filename after verification. Additionally, open the file via the verified canonical path (already done in `WriteTextFile`) and re-verify after open via `/proc/self/fd` or `fstat`+`stat` comparison on POSIX to close the TOCTOU window.

---

### HIGH Unbounded Schema-Retry Recursion / Stack Exhaustion

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::Submit`, curlCallback lambda, schema-retry branch (~line in the `curlCallback` that calls `Submit(retryEnvelope, callbackCopy)`)
- **Issue:** When schema validation fails and `nextAttempt < maxAttempts`, the callback calls `this->Submit(retryEnvelope, callbackCopy)` recursively. `Submit` then calls `dispatcher->Submit(...)`, which on some dispatcher implementations (mock, synchronous test shim, or any path where the dispatcher invokes the callback inline before returning) will re-enter the same `curlCallback` immediately on the same stack frame. `m_SchemaAttemptNumber` is incremented each time, so the loop is bounded by `m_Retry.m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts` — but that value comes from the JCWF envelope without a hard upper-bound cap visible in this file. If `m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts` is large (or comes from untrusted workflow input), the recursion depth equals the attempt count, exhausting stack space and crashing the process.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow with a large `output_schema_max_attempts` value and a schema the model never satisfies causes a stack overflow, crashing the engine (DoS).
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard compile-time cap (e.g. 5) on `m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts` before the retry branch, independent of the envelope value. Use an iterative retry loop or post the retry to the dispatcher's work queue rather than calling `Submit` directly from within the callback.

---

### HIGH Secrets Written into `QueryData::m_Params` in Plaintext (`ResolveProviderParams`)

- **Location:** `ResolveProviderParams`, engine.cpp
- **Issue:** `aws->m_SecretAccessKey.Get()` and `aws->m_SessionToken.Get()` are extracted from `SecureString` wrappers and inserted as plain `std::string` values into `params`, which is then copied into `CurlWrapper::QueryData::m_Params` — a plain `std::unordered_map<std::string, std::string>`. This map is captured by value in the `curlCallback` lambda, keeping the secrets alive on the heap for the entire duration of the in-flight request and any retry. If the process memory is dumped (crash, core file, `/proc/<pid>/mem` read by another process) or if `QueryData` is ever logged, the AWS secret access key and session token are exposed in plaintext.
- **Impact:** AWS secret credentials leak from a heap dump, core file, or any future log statement that serialises `QueryData`. Enables full AWS API access under the stolen credentials.
- **Fix:** Do not materialise secrets into plain `std::string`. Pass the `AwsCredential` pointer directly to the SigV4 signer and have the signer read from the `SecureString` accessor only at signing time, clearing its local copy immediately after. Remove the `secret_access_key` and `session_token` keys from `m_Params` entirely.

---

### MEDIUM `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` Prefix Check Is Byte-String Comparison, Not Path-Component Comparison

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`, pathConfinement.cpp
- **Issue:** The escape check is `relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0` — this catches strings that literally begin with `..`. However, `lexically_relative` on some platforms may return a relative path whose first component is a directory name that coincidentally begins with `..` as a substring but is not a traversal (e.g. a directory literally named `..foo`). More importantly, the empty-string check (`relStr.empty()`) accepts the case where `resolved == root` (i.e. the path is the root directory itself). Writing *to* or reading *from* the root directory as a file path is not meaningful and could mask logic errors where an empty filename is constructed.
- **Impact:** Low-severity escape in edge cases; primarily a defence-in-depth gap that weakens the traversal guarantee relied upon throughout the codebase.
- **Fix:** Replace the string prefix check with a proper path-component check: iterate `rel` and reject if the first component equals the path `..`. Accept `resolved == root` only if that is explicitly intended. Use `rel.begin()->string() == ".."` rather than string `rfind`.

---

### MEDIUM `AllocateRequestId` Wraps to 1 Without Invalidating In-Flight Entries

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::AllocateRequestId`, engine.cpp
- **Issue:** On signed 64-bit overflow (`m_NextRequestId <= 0` after increment), the counter resets to 1. Any previously allocated ID that happened to be 1 (or any value in the new sequence) and whose `PendingEntry` is still in `m_PendingRequests` will be aliased by the new allocation. `RegisterPending` inserts by key without checking for collision; the `unordered_map` insert will silently overwrite the existing entry (or, with `operator[]`, replace it), dropping the reference counted by the old `shared_ptr` and orphaning any thread blocked in `WaitForCompletion` on that entry's condition variable.
- **Impact:** After ~9.2 × 10¹⁸ requests (impractical in normal operation but feasible in a fuzz or stress scenario), a new request silently invalidates an in-flight entry; a workflow blocked on `WaitForCompletion` never wakes.
- **Fix:** On wrap detection, log a fatal error and abort, or use a monotonic counter that never wraps (uint64_t with explicit non-reuse policy). At minimum, check for key collision in `RegisterPending` and reject if the key already exists.

---

### MEDIUM `OnOutputFileCreated` Reads File Size via `seekg`/`tellg`, Not `file_size`; Negative Size Not Checked

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::OnOutputFileCreated`, engine.cpp
- **Issue:** File size is obtained via `inputStream.seekg(0, std::ios::end)` followed by `tellg()`. `tellg()` returns `std::streamoff`, which is a signed type. If `tellg()` fails it returns `-1`. The code checks `size > 0` before comparing to `kMaxOutputFileBytes`, but `static_cast<std::size_t>(-1)` wraps to `SIZE_MAX`, passing the cap check and then being passed to `fileContent.resize(SIZE_MAX)`, which throws `std::bad_alloc`. While `bad_alloc` is not caught in this function (only `std::exception` is caught in the outer `curlCallback`; `OnOutputFileCreated` is called outside that try block at several sites), the unhandled exception will propagate and may crash the process.
- **Impact:** A file that causes `tellg()` to fail (e.g., a special filesystem object, or an I/O error mid-seek) triggers an uncaught `bad_alloc`, crashing the engine.
- **Fix:** Check `size >= 0` before the cast. If `size < 0`, treat as an open/read failure and mark the entry failed. Use `fs::file_size()` with an `error_code` overload as a more reliable size probe.

---

### MEDIUM `BuildCompletionOutputs` Called Under `pendingEntry->mutex` (`QueueCompletionIfNeeded`)

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::QueueCompletionIfNeeded`, engine.cpp; `BuildCompletionOutputs` (anonymous namespace)
