- **Issue:** `QueueCompletionIfNeeded` holds `pendingEntry->mutex` while calling `BuildCompletionOutputs`, which calls `WriteTextFile`, which calls `fs::create_directories`, `std::ofstream` open, `outputStream.write`, and `fs::rename`. These are unbounded blocking filesystem calls. Holding the mutex across them means any other thread that tries to acquire `pendingEntry->mutex` (e.g., `TryConsumeResult`, `Update`, `WaitForCompletion`) will block for the entire duration of the file I/O, which can be arbitrarily long on a slow or network-mounted filesystem.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: a slow filesystem stalls the workflow runtime's tick loop (`Update`) and any `WaitForCompletion` caller for the duration of every file write performed under the lock.
- **Fix:** Build the `completion` struct under the lock (as now), set `m_HasQueuedCompletion = true` and release the lock, then call `BuildCompletionOutputs` outside the lock before pushing to `m_Completed`. Since `m_HasQueuedCompletion` is already set, a concurrent second entry to `QueueCompletionIfNeeded` will no-op correctly.

---

### LOW Test-Interface Fixture Path Not Confined to Project Root

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::Submit`, Test interface branch, engine.cpp
- **Issue:** `api->m_Url` is used directly as a `fs::path` for the fixture file without passing through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`. If the configuration allows `m_Url` to be set to an absolute path (e.g., `/etc/passwd`), the test interface will read and return arbitrary filesystem content as the AI reply.
- **Impact:** An operator or attacker who can write the engine configuration can exfiltrate arbitrary files readable by the process via the test interface.
- **Fix:** Apply `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` to `fixturePath` before the `fs::exists` / `std::ifstream` calls and reject if the result is empty.

---

### LOW Error Messages Reflect Internal Path Structure

- **Location:** `WriteTextFile`, engine.cpp; `OnOutputFileCreated`, engine.cpp
- **Issue:** Error strings returned via `outErrorMessage` and logged via `LOG_APP_ERROR` include raw filesystem paths from user-controlled input (e.g., `"path does not resolve under project root: " + filePath`, `"failed to open for writing: " + filesystemPathAbsolute.filename().string()`). These messages are propagated into `AiRequestCompletion::m_ErrorMessage`, which may be surfaced to workflow output or API responses.
- **Impact:** Internal directory structure and absolute paths are disclosed to workflow consumers or API clients, aiding further exploitation attempts.
- **Fix:** Log the full path internally at DEBUG/ERROR level, but return only a generic category string (e.g., "output path rejected", "file write failed") in `outErrorMessage` / `m_ErrorMessage` that reaches external consumers.


---

### 81_doc_application_workflow_aiTranscript_h

<a id="81-doc-application-workflow-aitranscript-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/81_doc_application_workflow_aiTranscript_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal via `transcriptPath` — no canonicalization or root-anchor check

- **Location:** `AppendEntry`, `ReadFile`, `WriteFile` (~lines 130–195); callers `AppendRequest` / `AppendResponse`
- **Issue:** `transcriptPath` is accepted directly from the caller and passed unchanged to `std::filesystem::create_directories`, `std::ifstream`, and `std::ofstream`. No code ever calls `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` or checks that the resolved path is inside any permitted directory. An attacker who controls the workflow configuration (or an AI reply that feeds back into a next-step path computation) can supply a path such as `../../etc/cron.d/evil` or an absolute path outside the intended transcript directory.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write to any location writable by the j9t process. Depending on deployment, this enables cron-job injection, overwriting configuration files, or replacing binaries.
- **Fix:** At the entry point of `AppendEntry` (and therefore transitively of `AppendRequest`/`AppendResponse`), resolve the path with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` and assert it begins with the application's permitted transcript root directory. Reject and log any path that fails this check before touching the filesystem.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU between `file_size` check and `ifstream` open in `ReadFile`

- **Location:** `ReadFile`, approximately lines 109–125
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::file_size` is called, the result is compared against `kMaxTranscriptBytes`, and then a separate `std::ifstream` is opened. Between those two syscalls another process (or a symlink swap) can replace the file with a much larger one. Because the `istream::rdbuf` copy has no size limit, an arbitrarily large file will be read entirely into `outContent`.
- **Impact:** On a host where another user or a compromised subprocess can race the file, this bypasses the 64 MB cap and causes uncontrolled heap allocation, leading to OOM / DoS. On Linux, a symlink race also lets an attacker redirect the read to any file readable by j9t.
- **Fix:** Open the file first, then call `fstat` (POSIX) or `GetFileSizeEx` (Windows) on the open file descriptor to obtain the size on the already-open handle. Alternatively, read incrementally and abort once the byte count exceeds `kMaxTranscriptBytes`, never buffering more than the cap.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled allocation during `AppendEntry` string construction — no cap on `entryJson` size

- **Location:** `AppendEntry` (~line 218), `AppendRequest` (~lines 248–285), `AppendResponse` (~lines 288–330)
- **Issue:** `entryJson` is built by concatenating all message contents (from `envelope.m_Messages`) and the full AI reply text without any per-entry size limit. `kErrorMessageCap` is applied only to error messages; free-form `m_Text`, `m_StructuredJson`, and each `m_Content` field are bounded only by whatever the caller provides. `rebuilt` in `AppendEntry` then concatenates the existing file content with the new entry, again without a total-size cap.
- **Impact:** A malicious or misbehaving AI provider that returns a very large response body, or a workflow configuration with enormous system-prompt content, causes unbounded heap growth during transcript writing, resulting in OOM / DoS.
- **Fix:** Introduce a per-entry size cap (e.g., 4 MB) checked in `AppendEntry` before building `rebuilt`, and apply individual field caps in `AppendRequest` / `AppendResponse` analogous to `kErrorMessageCap`.

---

### MEDIUM Structured JSON injection via `CanonicalizeStructuredJson` when simdjson re-serialization is not guaranteed to escape all Unicode edge cases

- **Location:** `CanonicalizeStructuredJson` (~lines 202–215), called from `AppendResponse` (~line 300)
- **Issue:** After simdjson parses the structured payload, the result is re-serialized with `stream << element` using simdjson's default dump. Simdjson's serializer emits raw UTF-8 and does not escape U+2028 / U+2029 (LINE SEPARATOR / PARAGRAPH SEPARATOR). While this is not a JSON syntax violation, these code points are significant in JavaScript contexts and can break JSON parsers that are not fully spec-compliant. More concretely, if `CanonicalizeStructuredJson` is ever called with a value that simdjson accepts but re-serializes differently than expected (e.g., a top-level array that already contains `]`), the protection is correct — but the current reliance on `operator<<` rather than a dedicated `simdjson::to_string` or a hardened serializer is fragile.
- **Impact:** Limited: a provider-supplied JSON payload could produce unexpected output in downstream consumers of the transcript file. Not directly exploitable for code execution in this C++ layer.
- **Fix:** Use `simdjson::to_json_string` or explicitly validate that the re-serialized output contains no bare U+2028/U+2029 before embedding. At minimum, document the known limitation.

---

### LOW Temp file left on disk after rename failure leaks transcript content

- **Location:** `WriteFile` (~lines 165–193)
- **Issue:** The comment explicitly states the `.tmp` sibling is "left for forensic inspection rather than auto-cleaned." Transcript files contain full AI request messages (including system prompts that may embed secrets injected from the vault) and provider responses. A failed rename leaves this data in a predictably named file (`<path>.tmp`) that may have broader filesystem ACLs than the final path, or may be world-readable depending on the process umask.
- **Impact:** An unprivileged local user or a directory-listing attacker can read partial transcripts containing secret-laden prompts via the predictable `.tmp` name.
- **Fix:** On rename failure, attempt to `std::filesystem::remove` the temp file. If removal also fails, log the temp file path at ERROR level so operators can clean it. Additionally, ensure the file is created with restrictive permissions (owner-read/write only) via `std::filesystem::permissions` after open, or set a restrictive umask before the `ofstream` constructor.

---

### LOW Provider error messages may still leak credential fragments despite truncation

- **Location:** `AppendResponse`, `AiReply::Kind::Error` branch (~lines 305–315)
- **Issue:** `SanitizeUtf8` and `TruncateUtf8Safe` are applied, but neither function (based on their names and the surrounding context) is documented or shown to perform credential-pattern scrubbing (e.g., regex for `sk-...`, `Bearer ...`, `key=...`). The comment acknowledges "provider bodies sometimes include masked API-key suffixes" but the fix is only truncation, not redaction.
- **Impact:** Partial API keys or authorization tokens embedded in provider error bodies are persisted to disk in transcript files, expanding the secret-exposure surface beyond the in-memory lifetime.
- **Fix:** Before calling `TruncateUtf8Safe`, apply a redaction pass that replaces known credential patterns (at minimum: strings matching `sk-[A-Za-z0-9]{10,}`, `Bearer [A-Za-z0-9._\-]{8,}`, `key=[A-Za-z0-9]{8,}`) with a fixed placeholder such as `[REDACTED]`.


---

### 82_doc_application_workflow_dataflowResolver_h

<a id="82-doc-application-workflow-dataflowresolver-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/82_doc_application_workflow_dataflowResolver_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — Resolved input values logged verbatim on template-expansion failure

- **Location:** `DataflowResolver::ResolveInputsForTask`, approximately line 80 (the `ExpandTemplates` failure branch)
- **Issue:** On template-expansion failure, `LOG_APP_ERROR` records `inputValuePair.second` — the fully-resolved input value — directly into the application log. Resolved inputs may contain secrets pulled from `workflowRun.m_Context` (step 2) or from upstream task outputs (step 1/1b), including API keys, tokens, or other sensitive workflow data.
- **Impact:** Any party with read access to application logs can harvest secrets that were resolved into task inputs, even when those secrets were never intended to be persisted.
- **Fix:** Replace `inputValuePair.second` in the log call with a redacted placeholder (e.g., `"<redacted>"`) or log only the input key name and its length. Do not log resolved values at any level below DEBUG, and gate DEBUG-level logging behind an explicit developer flag that is disabled in production builds.

---

### LOW — Dataflow edge error messages expose internal workflow topology

- **Location:** `DataflowResolver::TryResolveFromDataflowEdges`, approximately lines 115–130
- **Issue:** Both `LOG_APP_ERROR` calls emit the exact source task ID, output name, destination task ID, and input name from the workflow definition into the log. If these log lines are surfaced to an unprivileged caller (e.g., via an API response that echoes the last error, or through a shared log sink), they reveal the internal structure of workflow definitions including task names and output field names.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can trigger these error paths (e.g., by submitting a crafted workflow run that references a non-existent task) can enumerate valid task IDs and output field names from the error messages, aiding further targeted attacks against the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Log topology details only at DEBUG level in non-production builds. In production, log a correlation ID and a generic error code; return the detailed message only in an internal audit trail with access controls.

---

### NONE (for all other focus areas)

The remaining focus areas — injection (shell/SQL/HTTP header), authentication/authorization bypass, cryptography misuse, memory safety (no raw pointer ownership transfers, no manual allocations, all iterators validated before dereference), race conditions/TOCTOU, insecure deserialization, SSRF/path traversal, uncontrolled allocation, and TLS configuration — have no exploitable issues identifiable in this translation unit. The two findings above are the only security-relevant observations.


---

### 83_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterEngine_h

<a id="83-doc-application-workflow-filter-filterengine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/83_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterEngine_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unsanitized User-Controlled Paths

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::Evaluate`, approximately lines 100–120; also `EvaluateQuery` around line 270
- **Issue:** `filter.m_Source.m_Path` and `source.m_IndexPath` are taken directly from the `FilterDef` (workflow configuration or user input) and resolved against `workflowBaseDir` using `std::filesystem::path` concatenation without any containment check. A relative path containing `../` sequences will resolve to locations outside the intended base directory. For example, `../../etc/passwd` as `m_Path` produces a fully qualified path that is happily opened by `std::ifstream`.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply or influence a workflow definition can read arbitrary files on the host filesystem that the process has permission to open, including credentials, private keys, and application secrets.
- **Fix:** After resolving the path with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical`, verify it is lexicographically prefixed by the canonical form of `workflowBaseDir`. Reject any path that escapes the base directory before passing it to `EvaluateCsv`, `EvaluateTextLines`, or `EvaluateQuery`. Example check:
  ```
  auto canonical = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(resolvedPath);
  auto base      = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(workflowBaseDir);
  if (canonical.string().rfind(base.string(), 0) != 0) { /* reject */ }
  ```

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled Resource Consumption / DoS via Unbounded File Read

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` (~line 185), `FilterEngine::EvaluateTextLines` (~line 240), `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery` (~line 310)
- **Issue:** When `filter.m_MaxItems == 0` the code reads the entire file into memory with no size limit. A workflow definition pointing at a very large file (or a named pipe / special file that never ends) will cause the process to allocate unbounded memory. The `max_items` guard is optional and controlled by the same untrusted input.
- **Impact:** A malicious or misconfigured workflow can exhaust process memory and crash the engine, causing a denial of service for all concurrent workflows.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard upper bound on total bytes read (e.g., configurable but capped at a safe default such as 512 MB) independently of `m_MaxItems`. Also impose a maximum on the number of items produced regardless of `m_MaxItems` being zero.

---

### HIGH Secrets Leakage in Error Messages and Logs

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluatePolarionQuery` (~lines 360–395); `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` and `EvaluateTextLines` error paths
- **Issue:** `conn->m_KeyName`, `conn->m_Endpoint`, and `filter.m_Source.m_Connection` values are embedded verbatim in `errorMessage` strings returned to the caller and potentially surfaced to end users or written to logs. The `LOG_APP_ERROR` call at ~line 368 logs `filter.m_Id`, and callers may log the full `errorMessage`. If `m_KeyName` or `m_Endpoint` ever contains a credential or internal hostname, it will appear in externally visible error responses. Additionally, `resolvedPath` (which may contain deployment-internal directory structure) is included in error messages.
- **Impact:** Credential names, internal endpoint URLs, and filesystem layout are exposed to anyone who can trigger and observe an error, aiding reconnaissance and potentially leaking partial secret material.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or omit sensitive fields (`m_KeyName`, `m_Endpoint`) from user-facing error messages. Use structured internal logging with severity gating, and return only generic error codes or safe identifiers to callers.

---

### HIGH SSRF via Unvalidated Polarion Connection Endpoint

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluatePolarionQuery` (~lines 380–395), passed to `PolarionClient::FetchAll`
- **Issue:** `conn->m_Endpoint` is copied into `resolvedFilter.m_Source.m_BaseUrl` without any validation of scheme, host, or port before being handed to `PolarionClient::FetchAll`. An attacker controlling the connection configuration can supply an arbitrary URL, including `file://`, `http://169.254.169.254/` (cloud metadata), or internal services.
- **Impact:** Server-side request forgery allowing the attacker to probe or exfiltrate data from internal network services, cloud metadata endpoints, or the local filesystem (via `file://` if the HTTP client permits it).
- **Fix:** Validate `conn->m_Endpoint` against an allowlist of permitted schemes (`https` only) and optionally a hostname allowlist or DNS rebinding guard before use. Reject any URL whose scheme is not `https`.

---

### MEDIUM Log Injection via Unvalidated Filter ID and File Content

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` (~line 225, `LOG_APP_INFO`), `EvaluateTextLines` (~line 260, `LOG_APP_INFO`), `EvaluateQuery` (~line 355, `LOG_APP_INFO`)
- **Issue:** `filter.m_Id` and `resolvedPath` (both user/workflow-controlled) are interpolated directly into log messages via format strings. If the logging backend does not sanitize newlines, a crafted `filter.m_Id` containing `\n` followed by a fake log line can inject false entries into the log stream.
- **Impact:** Log forgery enabling an attacker to hide malicious activity or implant misleading audit records.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape control characters (particularly `\n`, `\r`) from all user-controlled values before passing them to logging macros.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Memory Allocation via CSV Column Count

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv`, inner loop ~line 205, and `SplitCsvLine`
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the number of columns per CSV row. A maliciously crafted CSV with millions of delimiter characters on a single line will cause `SplitCsvLine` to return a vector of millions of strings, each of which is inserted into `item.m_Values` (an `unordered_map`) and retained in the `items` vector. Combined with many such rows, this leads to unbounded allocation.
- **Impact:** Denial of service through memory exhaustion from a single large CSV file.
- **Fix:** Cap the number of columns accepted per row (e.g., 4096) and return an error or truncate if exceeded. Similarly cap the size of any single field value.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Race Between Path Resolution and File Open

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::Evaluate` (~lines 100–115), `EvaluateQuery` (~line 270)
- **Issue:** Path canonicalization (via `std::filesystem::path` operations) and the subsequent `std::ifstream` open are two separate operations. On systems where the path can be influenced between these two steps (e.g., symlink swapping in a world-writable directory), the resolved path at check time may differ from the path opened at use time.
- **Impact:** If an attacker can replace the target with a symlink between resolution and open, they may cause the engine to read a file other than the intended one, potentially exposing sensitive data.
- **Fix:** Open the file first using a trusted base directory file descriptor (e.g., `openat` with a directory fd obtained from the canonical base) rather than constructing a string path and opening it separately. On platforms where this is not feasible, at minimum perform the canonical check on the already-opened file descriptor's `/proc/self/fd/N` link rather than the path.

---

### LOW doc_path Field Overrides SourcePath Without Validation

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery` (~lines 340–345)
- **Issue:** If a document in the index file contains a `doc_path` field, its value is assigned to `item.m_SourcePath` and `item.m_Values["doc_path"]` without any validation. `m_SourcePath` is documented as used for freshness checks; if downstream code opens this path, an attacker controlling index file content can redirect the freshness check to an arbitrary filesystem path.
- **Impact:** Dependent on how `m_SourcePath` is consumed downstream. At minimum an attacker can point freshness checks at arbitrary files; if the consuming code opens and reads the path, this becomes a read-primitive.
- **Fix:** Apply the same path containment check (canonical path must be under `workflowBaseDir`) to `docPathIt->second` before assigning it to `item.m_SourcePath`.


---

### 84_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterManifest_h

<a id="84-doc-application-workflow-filter-filtermanifest-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/84_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterManifest_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — TOCTOU between `file_size()` and `ifstream` open in `ReadManifest`

- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ReadManifest`, the `std::filesystem::file_size` call followed by `std::ifstream file(path, ...)` roughly 20 lines later
- **Issue:** The file size is measured with `std::filesystem::file_size`, and the file is opened for reading in a separate call. Between these two operations an attacker or another process that can write to the manifest directory (e.g., via a race on a world-writable temp area, or via a symlink swap) can replace the file with a larger one. The cap check passes on the small decoy file, and the uncapped read then occurs on the real large file, potentially causing unbounded heap allocation via `oss << file.rdbuf()`.
- **Impact:** An adversary who can write to the manifest directory after the `file_size` check but before the `ifstream` open can cause the process to allocate an arbitrarily large buffer, leading to denial of service or OOM kill.
- **Fix:** Open the file first (`std::ifstream file(path, std::ios::binary | std::ios::ate)`), seek to determine the size on the already-open file descriptor (`file.tellg()`), enforce the cap, then seek back to the beginning and read. This collapses the TOCTOU window to zero because size and read operate on the same open file descriptor.

---

### MEDIUM — `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `fs::weakly_canonical` which does not resolve symlinks to non-existent targets, allowing partial symlink escape

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, the `fs::weakly_canonical(candidate, ec)` call
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` resolves only the portion of the path whose components exist, leaving any non-existent tail lexically normalized. If a symlink that exists inside the project root points to a path outside the root, `weakly_canonical` will follow it and return the external target, and the subsequent `lexically_relative` check will correctly reject it. However, if a path component exists as a directory symlink pointing outside the root and the final file component does not yet exist, `weakly_canonical` resolves the symlink portion and returns an external path that then correctly fails the prefix check. This case is actually handled, but it relies on `weakly_canonical` to follow existing symlinks correctly. The real risk is the opposite: an attacker creates a deep symlink chain where intermediate components are symlinks that together cause `weakly_canonical` to return a path inside the root that lexically looks safe but at open time resolves to an external path because the OS resolves additional symlink layers beyond what `weakly_canonical` traced. The confinement check should use `fs::canonical` (which requires the path to fully exist) for paths that already exist, falling back to the lexical check only for the not-yet-existing tail.
- **Impact:** A crafted symlink tree inside the workflow directory could fool the confinement check into returning a path that the caller then opens or writes to outside the project root, enabling path traversal to arbitrary filesystem locations.
- **Fix:** For paths that fully exist (manifest files being read), use `fs::canonical` instead of `fs::weakly_canonical` so every symlink in the chain is fully resolved and verified. For the write path (manifest files that do not yet exist), apply `fs::canonical` to the parent directory and concatenate only the validated filename component.

---

### MEDIUM — `ComputeQueryHash` canonical string uses unseparated field concatenation, enabling hash collision across distinct filter definitions

- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ComputeQueryHash`, the `canonical` string construction block
- **Issue:** Fields are concatenated in the form `"key=" + value + ";"`. If a field value itself contains `";"` or the substring `"key="`, two structurally distinct `FilterDef` objects can produce the identical canonical string and therefore the same SHA-256 hash. For example, `m_Kind = "a;path=b"` with `m_Path = ""` produces the same string as `m_Kind = "a"` with `m_Path = "b"`. The `m_Fields` vector is appended without a count prefix, enabling further ambiguity. A collision causes `CompareManifests` to skip re-evaluation when the filter definition has actually changed.
- **Impact:** An attacker or misconfigured workflow that can influence filter source fields can construct a new `FilterDef` whose hash collides with an existing manifest, suppressing re-evaluation and causing stale or attacker-controlled data to be used as if it were fresh.
- **Fix:** Use a length-prefixed or properly escaped encoding for each field value (e.g., `"key=" + std::to_string(value.size()) + ":" + value + ";"`), or serialize through a canonical JSON encoder, or use a structured hash-update approach (hash each field separately with a domain separator).

---

### LOW — `ReadManifest` logs `filterId` from the caller without validating it before the `ManifestPath` check succeeds, but error paths that return early also emit `filterId` into log lines

- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ReadManifest`, `LOG_APP_ERROR` calls on the path-rejection branch and inside the item-count-cap branch
- **Issue:** When `ManifestPath` rejects an invalid `filterId`, the value is embedded directly in the error message returned via `errorMessage` and also emitted to the application log. While `IsValidFilterId` does gate the manifest path, the `filterId` string is still echoed to logs. If the logging sink writes to a structured log aggregator or a terminal, an adversary who controls the `filterId` (e.g., through a crafted workflow definition file) could inject log-forging content such as newlines or JSON metacharacters, depending on what the `LOG_APP_ERROR` macro does with the format string argument. This is a log-injection risk, not a code-execution risk.
- **Impact:** Log injection: an attacker who can supply a malicious `filterId` through a workflow file could forge or truncate log entries, potentially hiding other activity or confusing SIEM parsing.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or truncate `filterId` before embedding it in log and error messages: strip or replace any character outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` and cap the displayed length. Since `IsValidFilterId` already enforces this character set, an already-rejected id can simply be replaced with a truncated, hex-escaped rendering such as the first 64 bytes with non-printables hex-encoded.

---

### LOW — `FileMtimeString` uses a two-now() approximation for clock conversion, making mtime-based change detection unreliable under high load

- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::FileMtimeString`, the `fileNow`/`sysNow` offset computation block
- **Issue:** The conversion from `file_clock::time_point` to `system_clock::time_point` is done by computing the difference between two independently read "now" values from different clocks. Under scheduler preemption or high system load the two clock reads can be separated by tens or hundreds of milliseconds. The comment correctly notes this is collapsed by `to_time_t`'s 1-second floor, but the 1-second resolution means that a file modified within the same second as the conversion call could have its mtime recorded as off by one second. Because `m_SourceMtime` is the sole change-detection key in `CompareManifests`, a wrong mtime causes either a missed re-evaluation (stale data used) or a spurious re-evaluation.
- **Impact:** Not directly exploitable for arbitrary code execution, but an attacker who can time file writes to coincide with the clock-read window can suppress change detection, causing the engine to continue processing a stale or modified source file as if it were unchanged.
- **Fix:** Use `std::chrono::file_clock::to_sys` (C++20, available on current supported toolchains) wrapped in a compile-time `#if` for the MSVC versions that lack it, falling back to reading the mtime via platform APIs (`GetFileTime` on Windows, `stat`/`statx` on Linux) and constructing a `system_clock::time_point` directly, eliminating the two-now approximation.


---

### 85_doc_application_workflow_filter_polarionClient_h

<a id="85-doc-application-workflow-filter-polarionclient-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/85_doc_application_workflow_filter_polarionClient_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] SSRF — base URL accepted without scheme or host validation

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::HttpGet`, `HttpRequest`, `HttpDownloadFile`, `HttpUploadFile`; indirectly all public methods that accept `baseUrl` or `url` as a caller-supplied string (polarionClient.cpp, all HTTP helper functions)
- **Issue:** `source.m_BaseUrl` and the `baseUrl` parameters flow directly into `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, ...)` after only stripping a trailing slash and appending path segments. There is no validation that the scheme is `https://`, that the host is on an allowlist, or that the URL does not use a `file://`, `gopher://`, `dict://`, `ftp://`, or other curl-supported scheme. `ConnectorHttp::ApplyHardenedDefaults` is called, but its implementation is not shown; if it does not call `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` / `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to restrict to HTTPS, the full SSRF surface is open.
- **Impact:** An operator-controlled JCWF config (or a compromised config store) can redirect all HTTP calls — including those carrying Bearer tokens — to internal metadata endpoints (AWS IMDSv1, GCP metadata, internal services), to `file://` paths for local file exfiltration, or to attacker-controlled hosts for credential harvesting.
- **Fix:** Before constructing the URL, validate that `baseUrl` starts with `https://` (case-insensitive) and optionally matches an operator-configured host allowlist. Additionally, set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTOCOL_HTTPS)` and `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTOCOL_HTTPS)` in every helper so libcurl refuses non-HTTPS schemes regardless of input.

---

### [HIGH] Bearer token materialised into a plain `std::string` and persists in heap memory

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::FetchAll`, approximately the line `std::string const bearerToken(api->m_ApiKey.Get());` (polarionClient.cpp)
- **Issue:** The PAT is extracted from `SecureString` (which presumably wipes its buffer on destruction) into a `std::string`. `std::string` does not zero its heap allocation on destruction. The token value will remain readable in process memory — and in core dumps, swap, or memory-scraping attacks — for the lifetime of the heap block.
- **Impact:** A process memory dump, heap spray inspection, or core file exposes the live Polarion PAT.
- **Fix:** Use a custom allocator (e.g., a `basic_string` with a zeroing allocator, or a `SecureBuffer` wrapper) for `bearerToken`, or limit its scope as tightly as possible and explicitly overwrite it (`std::fill` + `volatile` pointer trick or `OPENSSL_cleanse`) before it goes out of scope. Pass by reference through helpers rather than copying.

---

### [HIGH] `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `fs::weakly_canonical` — does not resolve existing symlinks

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` (pathConfinement.cpp, throughout)
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` normalises `..` lexically but does **not** follow symlinks that actually exist on disk. A symlink inside the project tree that points to a path outside the tree will be normalised to its own (in-tree) path, pass the `lexically_relative` check, and the returned path will then be used for `fopen`/`ofstream` — which **will** follow the symlink. This defeats the containment guarantee for any existing symlinks under `workflowBaseDir`.
- **Impact:** If an attacker can plant a symlink inside the project output directory (e.g., via a previous partial operation or a race), `WriteItemFile` and `HttpDownloadFile` will write attacker-chosen content to arbitrary paths outside the project root.
- **Fix:** Replace `fs::weakly_canonical` with `fs::canonical` (which requires the path to exist and resolves symlinks) on the final resolved path, or — where the path does not yet exist — call `fs::canonical` on the nearest existing parent and then verify the remainder lexically. Alternatively, open the target directory with `O_NOFOLLOW` / `openat` and perform all writes via the directory file descriptor.

---

### [MEDIUM] Uncontrolled response body growth in `HttpGet` and `HttpRequest` write callbacks

- **Location:** `writeCallback` lambda inside `HttpGet` (polarionClient.cpp ~line 260) and `HttpRequest` (~line 315), and the same pattern in `HttpUploadFile`
- **Issue:** The `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` callback appends every received byte into a `std::string` without any size limit. `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` is only set in `HttpDownloadFile`. A hostile or malfunctioning server can stream an arbitrarily large response body, causing unbounded heap growth and eventual OOM / process termination.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: a single large API response exhausts process heap, killing the workflow engine and any other workflows it is running.
- **Fix:** Add a size cap in the write callback (e.g., reject once `buf->size()` exceeds a configurable limit such as 64 MB) and return 0 (which causes libcurl to abort with `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`). Also set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` (or the newer `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE`) on all `curl` handles, not just the download one.

---

### [MEDIUM] `source.m_Fields` field names concatenated into URL without per-element validation

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::BuildPageUrl`, the `fieldsCsv` loop (polarionClient.cpp ~line 230)
- **Issue:** Individual entries from `source.m_Fields` are concatenated with commas to produce `fieldsCsv`, which is then `UrlEncode`d as a unit. `UrlEncode` will encode commas, brackets, and spaces, so HTTP header injection is not possible at the curl level. However, if any field name contains a comma (`,`) it silently splits into two field selectors, allowing parameter pollution of the `fields[workitems]` query parameter value. More importantly, there is no validation that field names match Polarion's documented identifier shape, so an adversary controlling `m_Fields` entries could inject percent-encoded sequences that survive `UrlEncode` (e.g., already-percent-encoded characters) if libcurl's `curl_easy_escape` double-encodes them in a surprising way. The stronger issue is that `m_Fields` entries are used as **literal keys** when accessing `attributes[fieldName]` in `ParseJsonApiPage` (simdjson key lookup); simdjson's on-demand `operator[]` performs a forward scan, so injecting a crafted key that matches a sensitive attribute could silently extract it.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow config can extract attribute fields the operator did not intend to expose and write them to output JSON files.
- **Fix:** Validate each element of `m_Fields` against an alphanumeric-plus-underscore allowlist before use in both `BuildPageUrl` and `ParseJsonApiPage`.

---

### [MEDIUM] `UploadAttachment` / `HttpUploadFile` — `filePath` not validated against project root before `curl_mime_filedata`

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::UploadAttachment` (polarionClient.cpp) calling `HttpUploadFile`, which calls `curl_mime_filedata(part, filePath.c_str())`
- **Issue:** `UploadAttachment` validates `projectId` and `workItemId` via `IsValidPolarionId` but does **not** call `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` on `filePath` before passing it to `HttpUploadFile`. `curl_mime_filedata` opens and reads the local file at `filePath`. A caller-supplied path such as `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path to a secrets file will be read and uploaded to Polarion.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary local file disclosure: any file readable by the process can be exfiltrated to the Polarion server.
- **Fix:** Apply `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(filePath)` in `UploadAttachment` (mirroring what `DownloadAttachment` already does for `outputPath`) and reject or fail if the result is empty. The same guard should be added inside `HttpUploadFile` as a second layer.

---

### [MEDIUM] `fileName` in multipart upload not sanitised — potential Content-Disposition header injection

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::HttpUploadFile`, `curl_mime_filename(part, fileName.c_str())` (polarionClient.cpp ~line 380)
- **Issue:** `fileName` is passed verbatim to `curl_mime_filename`, which places it in the `Content-Disposition: form-data; filename="..."` MIME header. If `fileName` contains `"`, `\r`, `\n`, or `;`, it can break out of the quoted filename token and inject arbitrary MIME headers or terminate the part header prematurely. libcurl does not sanitise this value before writing the multipart body.
- **Impact:** A crafted `fileName` (e.g., `x"\r\nContent-Type: text/html\r\n\r\n<script>...`) can inject headers into the multipart body sent to the Polarion server, potentially causing Polarion to misinterpret the upload, or — if the response is reflected — create a stored XSS payload in Polarion's attachment system.
- **Fix:** Validate `fileName` to alphanumeric characters, spaces, dots, hyphens, and underscores (strip or reject anything else) before calling `curl_mime_filename`. At minimum, reject any `"`, `\r`, or `\n` character.

---

### [LOW] `UrlEncode` fallback branch invokes `curl_easy_init` and discards a full CURL handle per call

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::UrlEncode` (polarionClient.cpp, entire function)
- **Issue:** `UrlEncode` allocates a `CURL*` handle solely to call `curl_easy_escape`, then immediately cleans it up. This is the documented libcurl usage, so it is not itself a bug. However, if `curl_easy_init` fails (returns `nullptr`), the fallback manual encoder is used. The fallback encoder casts `c` to `unsigned char` correctly, but the `!curl` branch is a silent degradation path — callers have no indication that the canonical encoder was unavailable and the fallback was used. More critically, if `curl_easy_init` fails systemically (e.g., libcurl global state was not initialised via `curl_global_init`), all URL construction silently falls back to the minimal encoder, which may behave differently for edge-case inputs.
- **Impact:** Low: in normal operation this is benign. In a pathological environment the fallback could produce subtly different encoding, potentially allowing query parameter smuggling for characters the fallback handles differently.
- **Fix:** Call `curl_global_init(CURL_GLOBAL_DEFAULT)` once at application startup (guarded). Log a warning if `curl_easy_init` returns null inside `UrlEncode` so the condition is visible. Consider using a single long-lived `CURL*` handle or the global `curl_easy_escape(nullptr, ...)` form (supported in libcurl ≥ 7.82.0) to avoid per-call handle allocation.

---

### [LOW] `goto done` jumps over remaining loop body — last partial page items may be silently dropped without logging

- **Location:** `PolarionClient::FetchAll`, the `goto done` inside the item-writing loop (polarionClient.cpp ~line 195)
- **Issue:** When `maxItems` is reached mid-page, `goto done` exits both loops. The `LOG_APP_INFO` truncation notice is inside the `if` block before the `goto`, so it is emitted once. This is behaviorally correct, but any `pageItems` entries after the truncation point are silently abandoned — their `WriteItemFile` calls never happen. This is by design, but if a caller expects `allItems.size() == maxItems` exactly, partial-page items between the last written item and `maxItems` are not written to disk. There is no security impact by itself, but if `allItems` is used downstream to drive further authenticated API calls (e.g., `UpdateWorkItem`), a mismatch between `allItems` count and on-disk files could cause logic errors.
- **Impact:** Low: logic discrepancy between in-memory list and on-disk files; no direct exploitability.
- **Fix:** Document the invariant explicitly, or restructure to break cleanly without `goto`, ensuring the truncation point is consistent between `allItems` and disk.


---

### 86_doc_application_workflow_filter_queryParser_h

<a id="86-doc-application-workflow-filter-queryparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/86_doc_application_workflow_filter_queryParser_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Unbounded token-vector growth enables memory exhaustion (DoS)

- **Location:** `QueryParser::Tokenize`, quoted-string branch (~line 220) and word-token branch (~line 255)
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the number of tokens produced, no limit on individual token string length, and no limit on the input string length checked before parsing begins. An attacker who can submit query strings can provide a multi-megabyte input such as `"aaa...aaa" OR "bbb...bbb" OR ...` and cause the engine to allocate arbitrarily large `std::vector<Token>` and `std::string` objects on the heap.
- **Impact:** Remote denial of service against any component that feeds attacker-controlled strings into `QueryParser::Parse`.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum input length (e.g. 4 KiB) at the top of `Parse` before calling `Tokenize`. Inside `Tokenize`, cap the token count (e.g. 512 tokens) and cap individual token string length (e.g. 256 bytes), returning an error when either limit is exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded recursion depth allows stack exhaustion (DoS)

- **Location:** `ParseOrExpr` / `ParseAndExpr` / `ParseUnaryExpr` (mutual recursion, ~lines 295–380)
- **Issue:** Deeply nested parentheses such as `(((((...))))` cause `ParseUnaryExpr` → `ParseOrExpr` → `ParseAndExpr` → `ParseUnaryExpr` to recurse once per nesting level with no depth counter. Stack frames are not small (each carries `errorMessage` references and local `unique_ptr`s).
- **Impact:** A crafted query with thousands of nested parentheses crashes the process via stack overflow.
- **Fix:** Add a depth parameter (or a mutable depth counter on `ParserState`) and return an error once depth exceeds a safe threshold (e.g. 64 levels).

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded AST node allocation via repeated OR/AND children

- **Location:** `ParseOrExpr` (~line 305) and `ParseAndExpr` (~line 325), `while` loops consuming OR/AND tokens
- **Issue:** Each iteration of the `while` loop allocates a new `QueryNode` child via `std::make_unique` and pushes it into `m_Children`. With the token-count DoS partially mitigated, this is a second allocation path: a query like `a:1 OR a:1 OR a:1 ... (N times)` produces a single OR node with N heap-allocated children.
- **Impact:** Heap exhaustion proportional to the number of OR/AND terms, independent of token string sizes.
- **Fix:** Cap `m_Children.size()` before each `push_back`, returning an error if a configurable per-node child limit (e.g. 128) is exceeded.

---

### LOW User-supplied field/value text reflected verbatim into error messages

- **Location:** `ParseFieldExpr` (~lines 395, 405, 420), `ParseRangeExpr` (~lines 435, 450, 465), `Parse` (~line 285), `Tokenize` (errorMessage on unterminated string)
- **Issue:** `state.Current().m_Text` (which is directly derived from attacker-controlled query input) is concatenated into `errorMessage` strings without any sanitization or length cap. If the caller logs or returns these error messages to clients (HTTP responses, log sinks), the raw input is reflected outward.
- **Impact:** Log injection (newline / ANSI escape characters in field names contaminate structured log lines) and potential leakage of query content in error responses.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or truncate `m_Text` before embedding it in error messages: strip control characters and cap the reflected substring to a fixed length (e.g. 64 printable characters).

---

### LOW Floating-point parsing of all range values without type context

- **Location:** `QueryParser::CompareValues` (~line 495)
- **Issue:** `std::stod` is called on every range bound without any guard on string length or format. Extremely long numeric strings (e.g. a 10,000-digit mantissa) are accepted by `std::stod` on some platforms and trigger implementation-defined behaviour or disproportionate CPU usage in the parsing routine.
- **Impact:** Localized CPU-time DoS on range evaluation of a crafted document or query.
- **Fix:** Check that both `a` and `b` are within a reasonable length (e.g. ≤ 32 characters) before attempting `std::stod`, or use `std::from_chars` with an explicit length bound which avoids the exception-dispatch overhead and is stricter about accepted input.


---

### 87_doc_application_workflow_internalTaskExecutor_h

<a id="87-doc-application-workflow-internaltaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/87_doc_application_workflow_internalTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated Input and Output Paths

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, path resolution loops (~lines 195–220 in the .cpp block)
- **Issue:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskScopedPath` is called with `inputPathString` and `outputPathString` values that come directly from `taskDefinition.m_FileInputs` and `taskDefinition.m_FileOutputs`. The resolved paths are logged and used immediately but are never checked to confirm they remain under `taskWorkingDirectoryPath`. If a workflow definition is user-controlled or loaded from an untrusted source, values such as `../../etc/passwd` or absolute paths will be accepted without complaint. The `EnsureOutputParentDirectories` function will then call `std::filesystem::create_directories` on an arbitrary path, and `ValidateExistingInputs` will confirm existence of arbitrary filesystem locations.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence workflow definitions can read arbitrary files (by specifying them as inputs and having a task that exfiltrates them), write to arbitrary filesystem locations (by specifying them as outputs), or create arbitrary directories.
- **Fix:** After resolving each path, verify it is lexically under the task working directory. Example: canonicalize both paths with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` (or `canonical` where existence is guaranteed) and assert `resolved.string().starts_with(base.string() + '/')`. Reject any path that fails this check before proceeding.

---

### HIGH Unvalidated `action` Value Used for Registry Dispatch (Authorization Bypass / Enumeration)

- **Location:** `TryGetActionFromParamsJson` (~lines 95–130), `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute` (~line 255)
- **Issue:** The `action` string is extracted from a user/workflow-supplied JSON blob and passed directly to `registry->CreateTask(action)` with no allowlist, length limit, or authorization check. Any string value is accepted as a task name. There is also no check that the calling workflow or run has permission to invoke the requested internal action.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can craft a workflow definition can attempt to invoke any registered internal task by name, potentially triggering privileged operations that the workflow was not intended to perform. The error message "No internal task registered for action: `<action>`" leaks the set of invalid names, enabling enumeration of valid action names by observing success vs. failure.
- **Fix:** Maintain an explicit allowlist of permitted `action` values per workflow type or caller role, and validate against it before dispatch. Apply a maximum length bound on the action string (e.g., 128 characters). Return a generic error that does not echo the supplied action name back to the caller.

---

### MEDIUM Action Name and File Paths Logged Verbatim (Log Injection / Information Leakage)

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, multiple `LOG_APP_INFO` calls (~lines 165–175, 225–240, 260–275)
- **Issue:** Values including `workflowRun.m_RunId`, `workflowRun.m_WorkflowId`, `taskDefinition.m_Id`, `action`, resolved input and output paths, and `workflowDefinition.m_WorkflowFilePath` are interpolated directly into log messages without sanitization. If these values originate from external input, they can contain newlines, ANSI escape codes, or structured log delimiters that corrupt log integrity or enable log injection attacks. Additionally, absolute filesystem paths for every input and output file are emitted at INFO level, which may expose sensitive directory layouts.
- **Impact:** Log injection can allow an attacker to forge log entries, hide malicious activity, or cause log parsers to misinterpret records. Path disclosure aids further attacks.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape newlines and non-printable characters from all externally derived values before logging. Reduce path logging to DEBUG level and gate it behind a debug/verbose build flag. Do not log raw external string values at INFO or higher without sanitization.

---

### MEDIUM `App::g_App` Global Atomic Pointer Accessed Without Lifetime Guarantee (TOCTOU / Use-After-Free)

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute` (~lines 245–255)
- **Issue:** `App::g_App.load(std::memory_order_acquire)` returns a raw pointer to `JarvisAgent`. There is no mechanism shown to prevent the object pointed to from being destroyed between the load and the subsequent call to `app->GetInternalTaskRegistry()`. If `g_App` is a globally shared atomic raw pointer that can be reset to null or pointed at a new object on another thread during execution, the acquired pointer is a dangling reference.
- **Impact:** In a multithreaded engine (which a workflow executor is), a concurrent shutdown or restart sequence could cause `app->GetInternalTaskRegistry()` to dereference freed memory, leading to a crash or, in adversarial conditions, exploitable memory corruption.
- **Fix:** Prefer storing `JarvisAgent` in a `std::shared_ptr` and using an `std::atomic<std::shared_ptr<JarvisAgent>>` (C++20) or a mutex-protected shared pointer so that the object lifetime is extended for the duration of the call. Alternatively, require callers to always supply a non-null `IInternalTaskRegistry` and remove the global fallback path.

---

### LOW Error Messages Echo Internal Structure to Task State

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, multiple `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` assignments (~lines 185, 210, 245, 255, 265, 280)
- **Issue:** Error messages stored in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` include internal details such as working directory paths, registry null diagnostics, and raw `action` strings. If `taskState` is serialized and returned to an external API caller or stored in a log accessible to users, these messages disclose internal filesystem structure and implementation details.
- **Impact:** Information leakage that aids reconnaissance for further attacks.
- **Fix:** Use generic user-facing error messages in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and emit the detailed diagnostic only to the server-side log (where it already goes via `LOG_APP_ERROR`). Never echo the raw `action` value or filesystem paths into the user-visible error field.


---

### 88_doc_application_workflow_jcwfContainer_h

<a id="88-doc-application-workflow-jcwfcontainer-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/88_doc_application_workflow_jcwfContainer_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Zip-Slip bypass via TOCTOU between validation and extraction passes

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract`, validation loop (~line 130) and extraction loop (~line 175)
- **Issue:** The validation pass calls `ValidateContainment` using `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` on each `destPath`, which resolves any *existing* symlinks in ancestor directories at validation time. The extraction pass then runs separately. Between these two passes, `create_directories` is called on `targetDir`, and each iteration of the extraction loop calls `create_directories` on parent paths. A crafted archive can contain an entry that first creates a directory, and a later entry whose resolved path lands inside that directory. More concretely: if a hostile actor can influence the filesystem between the two passes (e.g., by racing a symlink into a directory created during the extraction pass itself), the containment guarantee established during validation no longer holds during extraction. The validation and extraction are not atomic.
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying a crafted `.jcwf` file could write files outside `targetDir`, achieving arbitrary file write on the host.
- **Fix:** Eliminate the two-pass design. Perform the name-shape check (ValidateEntryName, IsSymlinkEntry) in the validation pass as now, but defer `ValidateContainment` to immediately before each `mz_zip_reader_extract_to_file` call in the extraction pass. Open the target directory with `O_NOFOLLOW`-based primitives or use a directory file descriptor and extract relative to it (e.g., via a wrapper that opens the target dir once and uses `openat`/`O_NOFOLLOW` for each output file). This closes the window between resolution and write.

---

### CRITICAL Symlink detection covers only Unix external attributes; zip-from-non-Unix archives bypass it

- **Location:** `IsSymlinkEntry`, ~line 95; `JcwfContainer::Extract` validation loop ~line 150
- **Issue:** `IsSymlinkEntry` inspects bits 28–31 of `m_external_attr`, which are only populated when the archive was created on a Unix host. A zip created on Windows (version-made-by byte = 0, MSDOS attribute set) carries no Unix mode bits. An attacker can create a symlink-containing archive using a Windows zip tool or by zeroing the high byte of external attributes while retaining NTFS reparse-point semantics. The check will return `false` for such entries, allowing symlink entries through to extraction.
- **Impact:** A symlink entry extracted to `targetDir` can point to any absolute path. Subsequent legitimate extraction of another entry to that path will follow the symlink and write outside `targetDir`, enabling arbitrary file write.
- **Fix:** After extracting each entry (or before, using `mz_zip_reader_extract_to_mem`), also check whether the extracted file is a symlink on disk using `std::filesystem::is_symlink`. Alternatively, inspect the entry's content itself: for small files check if the uncompressed data looks like a path string and the Unix mode is set. The most robust fix is to open the output file with `O_NOFOLLOW` and fail if the kernel refuses due to a symlink.

---

### HIGH Unvalidated `internalPath` passed directly to miniz in `ReadFile`

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::ReadFile`, ~line 230
- **Issue:** `internalPath` is a caller-supplied `std::string` passed verbatim to `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap`. There is no call to `ValidateEntryName` or any traversal check. An `internalPath` containing `../` segments, absolute paths, or null bytes is passed directly to miniz's internal filename lookup, which does a string comparison against central-directory filenames. While this doesn't write to disk directly, a null byte in `internalPath` would truncate the C-string passed to miniz, potentially matching an unintended entry and leaking its content to the caller.
- **Impact:** Caller-controlled path can read unintended entries from the archive, including content that should be inaccessible, or trigger unexpected behavior via null-byte truncation.
- **Fix:** Apply `ValidateEntryName(internalPath)` at the top of `ReadFile` and return an error if it fails. Separately, verify `internalPath` contains no null bytes before passing to miniz.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled memory allocation in `ReadFile` via `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap`

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::ReadFile`, ~line 235
- **Issue:** `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap` allocates a heap buffer sized by the uncompressed size field stored in the zip central directory. This field is attacker-controlled. A zip bomb (small compressed data, enormous declared uncompressed size) or a zip with a declared uncompressed size of several GB will cause miniz to attempt to allocate that much memory. There is no size cap before the allocation.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via memory exhaustion; on 32-bit platforms, integer overflow in size calculations within miniz could produce a small allocation followed by a heap overflow.
- **Fix:** Before calling `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap`, call `mz_zip_reader_file_stat` on the located entry to retrieve `m_uncomp_size`. Enforce a hard maximum (e.g., 64 MB for a workflow JSON file). Return an error if the declared size exceeds the limit.

---

### HIGH Same uncontrolled allocation applies during extraction pass

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract`, extraction loop, ~line 185 (`mz_zip_reader_extract_to_file`)
- **Issue:** `mz_zip_reader_extract_to_file` internally decompresses the full entry. The uncompressed size is attacker-controlled. While it writes to disk rather than RAM, there is no cap on individual file size or total extracted size, enabling disk exhaustion (zip bomb).
- **Impact:** Denial of service by filling the target filesystem. If the filesystem is shared with the OS or database, this can crash the host process or the system.
- **Fix:** During the validation pass, accumulate `stat.m_uncomp_size` for all entries and reject the archive if the total exceeds a configured threshold (e.g., 512 MB). Additionally cap any single entry. Check `stat.m_uncomp_size / stat.m_comp_size` ratio and reject extreme compression ratios (e.g., > 100:1).

---

### MEDIUM `canonEc` error code silently ignored after `weakly_canonical` calls

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract`, ~line 120
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::absolute` and `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` are both called with the same `canonEc` variable. If either call fails, `canonEc` is set but never checked; `canonicalTargetDir` will be an empty or partial path. All subsequent `ValidateContainment` calls will compare against this bad canonical path, potentially approving entries that should be rejected.
- **Impact:** If `weakly_canonical` fails (e.g., path component too long, permission denied), the containment check degrades silently, potentially allowing path traversal to pass validation.
- **Fix:** Check `canonEc` after each call. If either fails, call `mz_zip_reader_end` and return an error immediately before entering the validation loop.

---

### MEDIUM `ListEntries` uses a fixed 1024-byte buffer for filenames; miniz truncates silently

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::ListEntries`, ~line 265
- **Issue:** `mz_zip_reader_get_filename` is called with a 1024-byte stack buffer. The zip specification allows filenames up to 65535 bytes. miniz will silently truncate filenames longer than 1023 bytes. The truncated string is then returned to callers who may use it for lookups or display.
- **Impact:** Callers that use `ListEntries` output to locate or validate entries will silently operate on truncated names, potentially failing to find or correctly validate entries with long names. This is a lesser issue in the extraction path because `Extract` uses `MZ_ZIP_MAX_ARCHIVE_FILENAME_SIZE`, but `ListEntries` is a public API.
- **Fix:** Use the same `MZ_ZIP_MAX_ARCHIVE_FILENAME_SIZE` constant as the extraction path, or retrieve the size via `mz_zip_reader_file_stat` and allocate accordingly.

---

### MEDIUM `Pack` silently skips iterator errors; partial archives created without warning

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Pack`, recursive directory iterator loop, ~line 205
- **Issue:** When `std::filesystem::relative` sets `ec`, the entry is silently skipped with `continue`. Similarly, the `recursive_directory_iterator` itself is constructed with an `ec` parameter but that `ec` is shared with the relative-path computation inside the loop and is never checked after iterator construction.
- **Impact:** A packed `.jcwf` may silently omit files, producing an incomplete archive that is returned as success to the caller. If these files contain workflow definitions, the resulting archive is corrupt in a non-obvious way.
- **Fix:** Check `ec` after constructing the iterator and return an error if set. Inside the loop, treat `ec` being set on `relative()` as a fatal error rather than silently skipping, or at minimum log a warning. Use a separate `ec` variable for each fallible call.

---

### LOW Full filesystem paths logged in `LOG_APP_ERROR` and `LOG_APP_INFO`

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract` ~line 110; `JcwfContainer::Pack` ~line 210; `JcwfContainer::ReadFile` ~line 232
- **Issue:** `LOG_APP_ERROR` and `LOG_APP_INFO` calls log full absolute paths (`jcwfPath.string()`, `targetDir.string()`, `sourceDir.string()`). If application logs are accessible to users or exposed via a log-aggregation endpoint, these paths reveal the server-side directory layout.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of installation paths, aiding further attacks such as targeted path traversal or social engineering.
- **Fix:** Log only `filename()` components to user-visible messages (already done for `errorMessage`). Restrict full-path logging to operator-only log levels or ensure the log sink is not externally accessible.


---

### 89_doc_application_workflow_pythonTaskExecutor_h

<a id="89-doc-application-workflow-pythontaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/89_doc_application_workflow_pythonTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path-traversal confinement bypass via `weakly_canonical` on non-existent paths

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, `pathConfinement.cpp`
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` does not require the path to exist; it stops resolving symlinks at the first missing component and normalises the remainder lexically. If an attacker supplies a path such as `existing_dir/../../../etc/passwd`, `weakly_canonical` will resolve the real `existing_dir`, then apply the `../../../etc/passwd` suffix lexically, producing a path outside the root. The subsequent `lexically_relative` prefix check compares the string representation of the result against `root`, but if the resolved string does not literally start with `..` (e.g., on Windows with drive letters, or in edge cases with root-relative paths), the check can pass. More concretely: if `existing_dir` itself is a symlink that points inside the root, `weakly_canonical` follows it; but for paths where the intermediate components do not exist, symlink resolution stops and the `..` segments are folded lexically, which can escape the root without the resulting string starting with `..`.
- **Impact:** A workflow definition that names a file input or working directory containing path components that traverse above the project root may pass the confinement gate, allowing reads (existence checks, Python imports) or writes (`stdout.txt`, `stderr.txt`, `create_directories`) outside the intended sandbox.
- **Fix:** Use `fs::canonical` (which requires the target to exist) for paths that must exist, and reject any path whose target does not yet exist unless creation is intentional. For creation paths, resolve the *parent* directory with `fs::canonical` first, then append only the final filename component after validating it contains no directory separators. Replace the string-prefix check with a loop over the path components of `resolved` vs `root` using `std::mismatch` on the path iterator ranges, which is not fooled by string encoding differences.

---

### MEDIUM `_file_input_N` context keys injected without confinement

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute`, loop starting at approximately line 200 (`contextValues["_file_input_" + std::to_string(i)]`)
- **Issue:** File input paths are resolved via `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath` and injected into `contextValues` that are passed directly to `pythonEnginePool->ExecuteWorkflowTask`. Unlike the earlier `ValidateFileInputsExist` call, this second resolution is **not** passed through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`. If `ResolvePath` behaves differently from the confinement-gated version (e.g., returns an absolute path that was already vetted but the implementation diverges), the Python runtime receives an unconfined path. Even if the implementations agree today, the two separate resolution calls are not atomic: the check and the use are separated, creating a TOCTOU window.
- **Impact:** The Python function receives an unconfined absolute path string in `context['_file_input_N']`, which it can use to open, read, or write files outside the project root.
- **Fix:** Capture the confined path returned from `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` during `ValidateFileInputsExist` (or return the resolved paths from that function), and reuse those already-confined `fs::path` values when populating `contextValues`, eliminating the second unguarded resolution call.

---

### MEDIUM Unconfined `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` used for file writes

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute`, `create_directories` call and `stdout.txt`/`stderr.txt` write, approximately lines 155–280
- **Issue:** `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` is constructed from `workflowDefinition.m_WorkflowBaseDirectory` and `taskDefinition.m_WorkingDirectory` via `TaskPathResolver`, but it is **never passed through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`** before `fs::create_directories`, `std::ofstream` writes, or `fs::remove` calls. The confinement helper is only applied to `m_FileInputs`.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow definition with `working_directory: ../../etc/cron.d` (or equivalent) causes `create_directories` to create directories and subsequent file writes (`stdout.txt`, `stderr.txt`) to land outside the project root. Depending on the process's privileges, this could overwrite system files or plant executable content.
- **Fix:** Apply `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` to `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` immediately after its construction and abort with a `Failed` task state if confinement fails, before any filesystem mutation.

---

### MEDIUM Output file paths passed to Python not confined

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute`, `callArguments[outputName] = outputPath.string()` block, approximately lines 215–235
- **Issue:** `outputPath` is resolved via `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskScopedPath` and injected into `callArguments` (which become kwargs to the Python function) without a `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` check. A workflow that declares an output slot whose name contains "Path" and whose `m_FileOutputs[0]` is a traversal string will hand the Python function an unconfined absolute path.
- **Impact:** The Python function can write its output to an arbitrary location on the filesystem.
- **Fix:** Confine `outputPath` through `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` before storing it in `callArguments`; reject the task if confinement fails.

---

### LOW Absolute paths logged to debug log expose internal filesystem layout

- **Location:** `ValidateFileInputsExist` (`LOG_APP_INFO` with `confined.string()`), `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (`[paths debug]` and `[folder creation debug]` log lines), approximately lines 75–260
- **Issue:** Multiple `LOG_APP_INFO` and `LOG_APP_ERROR` calls emit absolute filesystem paths, workflow IDs, run IDs, working directory layouts, and task IDs. If the log file is accessible to users who submit workflow definitions (i.e., lower-trust operators or, in a multi-tenant scenario, any user), they can enumerate the server's directory structure, discover neighbouring workflows' working directories, and infer path conventions useful for traversal attacks.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of server-side filesystem layout and internal workflow identifiers.
- **Fix:** Gate these `[paths debug]` and `[folder creation debug]` log lines behind a compile-time or runtime verbose/debug flag that is off by default in production builds. For error-level logs that must always appear, log only the relative or task-scoped portion, not the full absolute path.

---

### LOW Uncontrolled size of `capturedStdout`/`capturedStderr` before truncation

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute`, `SanitizeUtf8` and `TruncateUtf8Safe` calls, approximately lines 285–295
- **Issue:** `capturedStdout` and `capturedStderr` are sanitised and then truncated to 1024 characters for the frontend, but the *full* unsanitised buffers are written to `stdout.txt` / `stderr.txt` with no size cap. If a Python task produces unbounded output (e.g., an infinite print loop before timing out, or a task that writes binary data), the write will consume unbounded disk space. There is no evident size limit enforced before or during the `ofstream::write` call.
- **Impact:** A malicious or buggy Python task can exhaust disk space, causing a denial of service affecting the host and all co-located workflows.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum capture size in `PythonEnginePool::ExecuteWorkflowTask` (e.g., stop capturing after N MB) or truncate `capturedStdout`/`capturedStderr` to a configurable maximum before the `ofstream::write` call.


---

### 90_doc_application_workflow_scriptCatalog_h

<a id="90-doc-application-workflow-scriptcatalog-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/90_doc_application_workflow_scriptCatalog_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — `weakly_canonical` does not resolve symlinks; path-containment check is defeatable on some filesystems

- **Location:** `PathIsUnderRoot`, approximately lines 110–135 (header + impl)
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` is documented to normalize the path lexically for components that do not exist, but it does **not** guarantee symlink resolution the way `std::filesystem::canonical` does. If a component of `absolutePath` is a symlink that exists, `weakly_canonical` will resolve it; but if the symlink target itself contains further symlinks, or if the filesystem presents hard-link tricks, the canonical form returned may still not be the true physical path. The code comment says "Resolves symlinks before comparing," which is only partially true. The safer call is `std::filesystem::canonical` (which fails if the path does not exist, but all paths here come from a live directory iterator so they do exist). Using `weakly_canonical` creates a narrow but real gap between the stated guarantee and the actual behavior.
- **Impact:** A carefully constructed nested-symlink scenario could cause `PathIsUnderRoot` to return `true` for a path that physically resolves outside the scripts root, allowing a catalog entry to be created for an out-of-tree file. Downstream consumers that trust `m_Path` or `m_Module` to be contained in the scripts root could then be tricked into reading or executing arbitrary files.
- **Fix:** Replace both `weakly_canonical` calls in `PathIsUnderRoot` with `std::filesystem::canonical`. Because the paths originate from a live directory iterator, they are guaranteed to exist at call time, so `canonical` will not spuriously fail. Keep the `std::error_code` overload and fail-closed behavior as-is.

---

### MEDIUM — `GetByPath` and `GetByModule` perform exact-string lookup on attacker-supplied values with no normalization

- **Location:** `GetByPath` (~line 290), `GetByModule` (~line 302)
- **Issue:** Both public lookup functions accept arbitrary caller-supplied strings and compare them directly against catalog entries. `m_Path` values are stored as `generic_string()` output of `std::filesystem::relative`, and `m_Module` values are built from the same. A caller that supplies a path with a trailing slash, mixed separators, redundant `.` components, or Unicode normalization differences (e.g. NFC vs NFD on macOS) can produce a string that does not match any stored entry yet refers to the same file. More critically, no validation prevents callers from supplying `../` sequences; a miss is returned (`nullopt`) but callers may fall back to a direct filesystem operation using the unvalidated string, enabling path traversal one layer up.
- **Impact:** Depending on how callers handle a `nullopt` result (not visible in this file), a caller could bypass the catalog's safety checks by crafting a path that the catalog rejects as "not found" and then using that same string in a direct file access. Also, non-normalized lookups can cause legitimate catalog entries to appear absent, leading to logic errors.
- **Fix:** In `GetByPath`, validate that `relativePath` does not contain `..` segments and normalize it through `std::filesystem::path(relativePath).lexically_normal().generic_string()` before comparison. Apply equivalent normalization when building `m_Path` during `ParseFile` so stored and queried forms are always comparable. In `GetByModule`, reject any string containing `/`, `\`, or `..`.

---

### MEDIUM — Module name derived from attacker-influenced filename contains no character validation

- **Location:** `ParseFile`, approximately lines 183–197
- **Issue:** `entry.m_Module` is constructed by taking the filesystem path relative to `rootDir.parent_path()`, stripping `.py`, and replacing `/` with `.`. No check is made that the resulting string contains only valid Python identifier characters. A file named `scripts/evil;import os.py` (valid on most filesystems) produces a module string `scripts.evil;import os`. If this module name is later passed to a Python interpreter via `exec`, `importlib.import_module`, or similar without re-validation, it constitutes an injection vector.
- **Impact:** If downstream code uses `m_Module` directly in a Python `import` or `exec` statement (common in workflow engines with embedded Python), an attacker who can place a file with a crafted name in the scripts directory can achieve arbitrary Python code execution.
- **Fix:** After constructing `entry.m_Module`, validate that every dot-separated component matches `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`. If validation fails, set `m_HasJarvisMarker = false` (or skip the entry entirely in `Refresh`) so it cannot be selected by a workflow.

---

### LOW — Script metadata fields (`m_Short`, `m_Description`, `m_Outputs`, `m_Params`) are logged and likely served over HTTP without any sanitization

- **Location:** `ParseFile`, lines throughout the metadata-parsing block (~230–290); `Refresh` log line ~340
- **Issue:** The metadata values read from script comment headers are stored verbatim. The catalog comment says entries are "Served to MCP agents via GET /api/scripts." If these fields are serialized into JSON/HTML responses or written to logs without escaping, a script file with a crafted `@short:` or `@description:` value (e.g. containing `\n`, `\r`, JSON special characters, or HTML) can cause log injection or HTTP response injection. The file itself does not contain the HTTP serving code, but the storage layer performs zero sanitization.
- **Impact:** Log injection can obscure audit trails. If the serving layer does not escape output, stored XSS or HTTP header injection is possible for any user who can write a file into the scripts directory.
- **Fix:** Either sanitize (strip or escape control characters and non-printable bytes) at parse time in `ParseFile`, or document explicitly that all callers of `List()`/`GetByPath()`/`GetByModule()` must escape field values before emitting them into any structured format. Enforce a maximum field length (e.g. 512 bytes) per metadata value to prevent large injected payloads.

---

### LOW — TOCTOU between `is_symlink()` / `is_regular_file()` check and `PathIsUnderRoot` / `ParseFile` open

- **Location:** `Refresh`, approximately lines 320–340
- **Issue:** The directory iterator reads the file's status at iteration time. By the time `PathIsUnderRoot` calls `weakly_canonical` and `ParseFile` calls `std::ifstream::open`, the filesystem entry may have been replaced with a symlink (or the target swapped) by a concurrent process with write access to the scripts directory. This is a classic TOCTOU race.
- **Impact:** On a system where an unprivileged user can write to the scripts directory (e.g. a shared deployment), they could race the scan to substitute a symlink after the `is_symlink()` check passes, causing the catalog to ingest metadata from an arbitrary file outside the scripts root.
- **Fix:** Open the file using `O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX: `open(path, O_RDONLY | O_NOFOLLOW)`) and construct the `std::ifstream` from that file descriptor, or use `openat` with a directory fd obtained from the scripts root. This collapses the check-then-use window. On platforms where this is unavailable, re-check `is_symlink` on the open fd via `fstat` after opening.


---

### 91_doc_application_workflow_shellTaskExecutor_h

<a id="91-doc-application-workflow-shelltaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/91_doc_application_workflow_shellTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: `command` field injected raw into shell string without sanitization or quoting

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput` and `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`, command assembly (~lines building `commandWithRedirect` / passing `command` to `execl`)
- **Issue:** `commandPath` is validated by `ValidateScriptPath` to start with `scripts/` and land under the scripts directory, but after validation it is resolved to an absolute path and placed verbatim into the shell string passed to `popen()` / `sh -c`. The absolute path is not quoted before concatenation. On POSIX, `commandWithRedirect` is built as `"cd <dir> && " + command + stderrRedirect` where `command` is the unquoted absolute path. If the absolute path contains spaces or special characters (e.g., a project root whose path includes a space), the shell will mis-parse it. More importantly, `IsSafeArgument` and `JoinArgumentsForSystem` (which single-quote every argument) are applied only to `args[]`, not to the command itself. The `command` field value, post-path-resolution, reaches the shell string raw.
- **Impact:** If the project is deployed under a path containing shell metacharacters, or if `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` / `ValidateScriptPath` can be made to return a path containing whitespace or special characters through the allowed `scripts/` subtree, the shell interprets those characters, enabling command injection.
- **Fix:** Quote `commandPath` (the resolved absolute path) with `QuoteForPosixShell` before inserting it into `commandWithRedirect`. On the watchdog path, pass it as the literal `argv[3]` argument to `execl` — it already does this correctly via `sh -c <command>`, but the `command` string must be `QuoteForPosixShell(commandPath)` when constructing any popen-based string.

---

### HIGH: Windows PowerShell stderr redirect uses unsanitized path in double-quoted `-Command` string
