- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput`, Windows PowerShell branch, approximately where `commandWithRedirect` is assembled
- **Issue:** The `stderrTmpPath.string()` is inserted into `2>"` + path + `"` inside the outer `cmd.exe` invocation. On Windows, `stderrTmpPath` is built from `workingDirectoryPath / stderrFileName`. `workingDirectoryPath` comes from `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskWorkingDirectoryPath` which is not shown to sanitize against characters that are special to `cmd.exe` argument parsing (e.g., `"`, `^`, `&`). If the working directory path contains a double-quote, the `2>"..."` redirect string breaks out of the double-quoted context and allows injection into the `cmd.exe` command line.
- **Impact:** An operator-controlled working directory path with special characters could allow cmd.exe command injection on Windows.
- **Fix:** Use a temporary file path that is always under a controlled, sanitized directory (e.g., the OS temp directory via `GetTempPath`), or escape the path for `cmd.exe` double-quote context before embedding. Using `%TEMP%` and a process-unique filename sidesteps the issue entirely.

---

### HIGH: `ValidateScriptPath` uses `current_path()` which is process-global and mutable

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::ValidateScriptPath`, approximately lines computing `projectRoot`
- **Issue:** `ValidateScriptPath` calls `std::filesystem::current_path()` to determine the project root against which the `scripts/` directory is anchored. `current_path()` returns the process-wide working directory, which is a mutable global. Although the comment in `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput` says directory changes were moved into the subshell, nothing in the visible code prevents another thread (or future code) from calling `chdir` / `SetCurrentDirectory`. A TOCTOU window exists between the path-confinement check and the actual execution of the script: the working directory could change between the two.
- **Impact:** If the process working directory changes between `ValidateScriptPath` and the `popen`/`execl` call, the confinement boundary shifts and a previously-rejected path might execute, or a previously-accepted absolute path might be interpreted differently.
- **Fix:** Capture the project root once at startup into an immutable global (or pass it explicitly), rather than re-querying `current_path()` on every call. `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` has the same issue and should receive the same treatment.

---

### HIGH: `IsSafeArgument` is not applied to `commandPath` itself before it is injected into the shell string

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::Execute`, after `ValidateScriptPath` returns true and `commandPath` is resolved to an absolute path
- **Issue:** `IsSafeArgument` is explicitly called only on elements of `rawArgs` (the `args[]` array). The `commandPath` — including its post-resolution absolute form — is pushed into `argumentList[0]` and then passed through `JoinArgumentsForSystem`, which does apply `QuoteForPosixShell`. However, on the watchdog path (`ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`), `fullCommand` (the already-joined string, not the individual components) is passed directly to `execl` as the `-c` argument. `fullCommand` is the output of `JoinArgumentsForSystem(argumentList)` which single-quotes `commandPath`. Verify that the single-quoting is correct; however, for the popen path the same `fullCommand` is wrapped inside an additional `sh -c "cd ... && fullCommand"` where `fullCommand` is not re-quoted for the outer shell layer. This double-shell layering means the single-quotes produced by `JoinArgumentsForSystem` are interpreted by the inner `sh -c`, which is correct, but any failure in the outer quoting (the `cd` part) affects argument integrity.
- **Impact:** Path-injection into the `cd` portion of the command string if the working directory path is attacker-influenced and not quoted.
- **Fix:** Apply `QuoteForPosixShell` to `workingDirectoryPath.string()` in the `cd` prefix (this is already done — confirm it is not elided on any build path), and ensure the complete assembled command is reviewed for double-shell-expansion issues.

---

### MEDIUM: Unbounded allocation of `capturedStdout` and `capturedStderr`

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput` (the `stdoutOut.append(buffer, ...)` loop) and `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog` (`stdoutOut.append` / `stderrOut.append` in poll loop)
- **Issue:** Both execution paths accumulate all stdout and stderr into `std::string` objects (`capturedStdout`, `capturedStderr`) with no size cap. A shell task that produces unbounded output (e.g., an infinite-loop script, or one that writes large files to stdout) will cause the parent process to grow its heap without bound until OOM kill or swap exhaustion.
- **Impact:** Any operator-authored (or attacker-triggered via adhoc submission) shell task can exhaust process memory, causing a denial of service to the entire j9t workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum capture size (e.g., 64 MB). Once the cap is reached, stop appending to the accumulator (but continue draining the pipe so the child is not blocked on a full pipe buffer). Log a warning and truncate. The 1024-character truncation applied to `taskState.m_CapturedStdout/Stderr` is only for the frontend tooltip; the full accumulator is written to disk without limit.

---

### MEDIUM: `popen`-based execution on POSIX inherits all open file descriptors

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput`, `OpenPipe` call
- **Issue:** `popen()` on POSIX does not close non-standard file descriptors before exec. Any file descriptors open in the j9t parent process (database connections, TLS sockets, log file handles, secret-bearing memory-mapped files) are inherited by the child shell process. The child shell script can read from or write to these descriptors by number.
- **Impact:** A malicious or compromised script running under the j9t process UID can access open sockets, credential file handles, or IPC endpoints held by the parent, potentially exfiltrating secrets or corrupting internal state.
- **Fix:** Use the `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog` fork/exec path exclusively on POSIX (it is currently only used when a timeout is configured). In the fork child, iterate file descriptors from 3 to `sysconf(_SC_OPEN_MAX)` and close them, or set `FD_CLOEXEC` on all non-stdio descriptors at open time throughout the codebase. Alternatively, use `posix_spawn` with `POSIX_SPAWN_CLOEXEC_DEFAULT` where available.

---

### MEDIUM: TOCTOU between `std::filesystem::exists(stderrTmpPath)` and `std::ifstream` open

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput`, stderr temp-file read block
- **Issue:** The code calls `std::filesystem::exists(stderrTmpPath, ec)` and then opens the file with `std::ifstream`. Between the `exists` check and the `open`, another process with access to the working directory could replace the file with a symlink pointing to an arbitrary path (e.g., `/etc/passwd`), causing the engine to read and log the contents of that file.
- **Impact:** Local privilege escalation / information disclosure if the working directory is world-writable or accessible to another user. In multi-tenant deployments this is a realistic threat.
- **Fix:** Remove the `exists` check and simply attempt to open the file directly; handle the failure case from `ifstream`. To fully close the symlink race, create the temp file with `O_CREAT | O_EXCL` using the process-unique name (already done for naming), and open it by file descriptor rather than by path after the child exits. Alternatively, use `O_NOFOLLOW` when opening for reading.

---

### MEDIUM: `ProbeWindowsShell` uses `std::system()` to probe for bash

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::ProbeWindowsShell`
- **Issue:** `std::system("bash --version >NUL 2>&1")` invokes `cmd.exe /C bash --version >NUL 2>&1`. On Windows, `cmd.exe` searches `PATH` for `bash`. If an attacker can place a `bash.bat` or `bash.exe` earlier on `PATH` than the intended Git bash or WSL bash, this probe executes attacker-controlled code. This runs at startup with the j9t process privileges.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary code execution at engine startup if PATH is partially attacker-controlled (e.g., a compromised user-local directory prepended to system PATH).
- **Fix:** Use `SearchPath` / `PathFindOnPath` (Windows API) to locate `bash.exe` by name, verify its full absolute path against an allowlist or a known installation directory, and check its existence without executing it. Do not use `std::system` for binary probing.

---

### MEDIUM: Inactivity watchdog checks only `watchdog->ElapsedSinceLastKickMs()` but the timeout path does not drain pipes before killing

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`, timeout branch (`timedOut = true; break;`)
- **Issue:** When the inactivity timeout triggers, the code breaks out of the poll loop and sends `SIGTERM` then `SIGKILL` to the process group, then calls `waitpid`. The pipe read ends (`stdoutPipe[0]`, `stderrPipe[0]`) are closed immediately after `waitpid` without draining any remaining data. Any output written by the child before it was killed is silently discarded.
- **Impact:** This is primarily a correctness/reliability issue, but it also means that on timeout, error diagnostic output from the child (which might indicate why it hung, or contain security-relevant audit information) is lost.
- **Fix:** After `waitpid` on the timeout path, drain both pipe fds before closing them, then flush pending line buffers. This is a low-risk change that preserves forensic output.

---

### LOW: `fullCommand` (the complete shell string including quoted arguments) is logged unconditionally

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::Execute`, `LOG_APP_INFO("[shell] Command: {}", fullCommand)` and the `spawnShellTask` debug log
- **Issue:** `fullCommand` contains the fully-expanded, quoted argument list. If any `args[]` element resolves from a template that included a secret value (e.g., an API key passed as an argument), it will appear in the application log in plaintext.
- **Impact:** Secrets passed as positional arguments to shell scripts are written to the log file and any log aggregation system receiving j9t output.
- **Fix:** Either redact arguments that match a configurable secret pattern before logging, or avoid logging the full expanded command and log only the script path and argument count. Template expansion of secrets into `args[]` should be discouraged by design; consider a separate environment-variable injection mechanism for secrets.

---

### LOW: `setenv("JARVIS_TASK_ID", taskId.c_str(), 1)` in fork child — `taskId` is unsanitized external data

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`, child process branch, `setenv("JARVIS_TASK_ID", ...)` call
- **Issue:** `taskId` originates from `taskDefinition.m_Id`, which is parsed from the JCWF (a JSON field). Although `setenv` itself is safe for the environment of the child process, if any code in the child shell script reads `$JARVIS_TASK_ID` and uses it in a shell operation without quoting, an attacker-controlled task ID containing metacharacters could cause injection in that script. This is a second-order risk.
- **Impact:** Shell injection in child scripts that consume `$JARVIS_TASK_ID` unquoted.
- **Fix:** Validate `taskId` against an alphanumeric-plus-hyphen/underscore allowlist before calling `setenv`, and document that `JARVIS_TASK_ID` must always be double-quoted in shell scripts that use it.


---

### 92_doc_application_workflow_subWorkflowTaskExecutor_h

<a id="92-doc-application-workflow-subworkflowtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/92_doc_application_workflow_subWorkflowTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Confinement Bypass via `weakly_canonical` on Non-Existent Paths

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, the `fs::weakly_canonical` call on `candidate`
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` resolves only the existing prefix of a path and leaves the non-existent suffix as-is, without resolving symlinks in that suffix. If an attacker-controlled `m_WorkflowFile` names a path whose existing ancestor directories contain symlinks pointing outside the project root, `weakly_canonical` will resolve those symlinks and may produce a canonical path that lands outside the root. More critically, the subsequent prefix check (`relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0`) only catches the literal string `..` at position 0 of the relative form. On Windows, a path that `lexically_relative` cannot express (e.g., different drive letter) returns an absolute path rather than a `..`-prefixed one, so the check passes and `resolved` — which is outside the root — is returned as valid.
- **Impact:** A hostile workflow file (JCWF) supplying a crafted `workflow_file` value could reference a file outside the project tree, enabling it to enqueue arbitrary registered workflows or, if the registry lookup falls through to a file-read, read arbitrary files under operator privileges.
- **Fix:** Replace `weakly_canonical` with `fs::canonical` (which requires the path to exist and fully resolves all symlinks). After canonicalization, verify containment by checking that `resolved` starts with `root` using a byte-level prefix comparison that also checks for a path separator immediately after the root portion, rather than relying on `lexically_relative`. For example:

        bool isUnder = (resolved.native().rfind(root.native(), 0) == 0) &&
                       (resolved.native().size() == root.native().size() ||
                        resolved.native()[root.native().size()] == fs::path::preferred_separator);

  Reject (return `{}`) if the path does not yet exist, since the engine should only load pre-registered workflow files.

---

### LOW Dangling Raw Pointer — `SetRuntimeManager` / `m_RuntimeManager`

- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::SetRuntimeManager` and `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, header lines ~45–50 and implementation lines ~75–80
- **Issue:** `m_RuntimeManager` is a non-owning raw pointer set via `SetRuntimeManager`. There is no mechanism to invalidate or null this pointer when `WorkflowRuntimeManager` is destroyed. If the runtime manager is torn down while a `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor` instance still holds the pointer and `Execute` is subsequently called (or a concurrent call is mid-flight), the result is a use-after-free / dangling dereference.
- **Impact:** Depending on scheduling, an attacker with the ability to trigger workflow teardown concurrently with task dispatch could cause a crash (DoS) or, under favorable heap layout, achieve controlled memory corruption.
- **Fix:** Use `std::weak_ptr<WorkflowRuntimeManager>` for `m_RuntimeManager`, require `WorkflowRuntimeManager` to be managed by `std::shared_ptr`, and lock the weak pointer inside `Execute` before use. Alternatively, enforce a strict lifetime contract where the executor is destroyed before the runtime manager and enforce this with RAII scoping rather than a raw setter.

---

### LOW Absolute Path Accepted Before Confinement Check

- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, path resolution lambda, implementation lines ~105–115
- **Issue:** If `taskDefinition.m_WorkflowFile` is an absolute path, the code uses it directly (`return raw;`) before passing it to `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`. `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` does handle absolute paths, but the intent expressed in the comment ("Resolve relative to parent workflow directory") is only partially enforced: an absolute path that happens to land inside the project root is silently accepted. The confinement function uses `weakly_canonical` (see above finding), compounding the risk.
- **Impact:** Marginally increases the attack surface for path traversal if the `weakly_canonical` weakness is exploited, because the attacker does not need to construct a relative traversal; an absolute path directly to a sensitive location inside the project root is also accepted.
- **Fix:** Reject absolute paths in `m_WorkflowFile` outright before the confinement call, or always join them against `workflowDefinition.m_WorkflowFileDirectoryAbsolute` and rely solely on confinement for the final check. Add an explicit check:

        if (raw.is_absolute()) { /* set failed state and return false */ }

---

### LOW Internal Path Disclosed in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and `taskState.m_CapturedStdout`

- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, implementation lines ~130, ~145, ~155, ~170
- **Issue:** `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` are populated with full absolute filesystem paths (`absolutePath`) and internal IDs (`childRunId`, `childWorkflowId`). If `taskState` is serialized into API responses, UI output, or logs accessible to end-users, this constitutes information disclosure of server-side directory layout and internal identifiers.
- **Impact:** An attacker with access to workflow run state can enumerate the server's directory structure and internal workflow IDs, aiding further attacks.
- **Fix:** In error messages stored to `m_LastErrorMessage`, use only the filename component (`confinedPath.filename().string()`) rather than the full absolute path. Reserve full-path and run-ID detail exclusively for server-side structured logging (the `LOG_APP_ERROR`/`LOG_APP_INFO` calls), which should be access-controlled separately. For `m_CapturedStdout`, evaluate whether `child_run_id` must be exposed; if it is an opaque token with no privilege attached, disclosure may be acceptable, but it should be documented as intentional.


---

### 93_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutor_h

<a id="93-doc-application-workflow-taskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/93_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

### 94_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutorRegistry_h

<a id="94-doc-application-workflow-taskexecutorregistry-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/94_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutorRegistry_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal on Source File (expandedSource / sourcePath)

- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, approximately the block starting after `ExpandTemplate` succeeds (~line 130–150 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The expanded source path (`expandedSource`) is derived from `sourceTemplate`, which is populated from `taskDefinition.m_Materialize`. When `sourcePath` is absolute, it is merely `lexically_normal()`-ized and used directly; when relative, it is resolved via `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath`. There is no containment check on the **source** path analogous to the one performed on `targetPath`. A workflow definition containing a source template that expands to an absolute path such as `/etc/passwd`, `/proc/self/environ`, or any path outside the workflow tree will be accepted, and the file will be copied into the task working directory and potentially exposed to subsequent task steps.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can author or influence a workflow definition file can exfiltrate arbitrary readable files from the host filesystem by naming them as materialization sources. In a multi-tenant or CI/CD context this includes secrets, SSH keys, and service credentials.
- **Fix:** After resolving `sourcePath` to its final form, apply the same `lexically_relative` containment gate that is applied to `targetPath`, verifying that the source lies within `workflowBaseDirectoryPath` (or a configured set of allowed roots). Reject and log any path that produces a relative component starting with `..` or that is absolute and outside the allowed tree.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Between `exists()` Check and `copy_file()`

- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, the `std::filesystem::exists` call followed by `std::filesystem::copy_file` (~line 170–185 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The existence of `sourcePath` is checked with `std::filesystem::exists`, and then the file is copied in a separate call. Between these two operations another process could replace the file (e.g., swap a regular file for a symlink pointing to a sensitive target), causing `copy_file` to follow the attacker-controlled symlink and copy the symlink's target content into the task working directory.
- **Impact:** On systems where an attacker can write to directories in the workflow path (shared temp directories, world-writable mounts), this is a symlink-race / TOCTOU that can be used to read arbitrary files, compounding the source-path traversal above.
- **Fix:** Open the source file with `O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX) before copying, or use a platform-safe copy primitive. At minimum, verify after opening that the file descriptor refers to a regular file (`fstat` + `S_ISREG`) before reading. Remove the separate `exists()` pre-check and let `copy_file` failure be the authoritative error signal, combined with symlink-safe open flags.

---

### LOW Singleton Registry Has No Thread-Safety on `RegisterExecutor`

- **Location:** `TaskExecutorRegistry::RegisterExecutor` and `TaskExecutorRegistry::Get` (~lines 10–15 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** `m_Executors` is an `std::unordered_map` modified by `RegisterExecutor` without any synchronization. If executors are registered from multiple threads (e.g., plugin loaders running concurrently), concurrent writes or a concurrent write and read race with `Execute` produce undefined behavior.
- **Impact:** In the worst case, a race during map rehashing produces a corrupt internal state that could be exploitable (though exploitation is highly environment-dependent). More likely outcome is a crash or silent data corruption that bypasses the `m_Executors.end()` check, causing the wrong executor to run for a task type.
- **Fix:** Protect `m_Executors` with a `std::shared_mutex`: exclusive lock in `RegisterExecutor`, shared lock in `Execute`. Alternatively, if registration is guaranteed to complete before any `Execute` call (typical startup ordering), document and enforce that contract with an assertion or a `RegisterExecutor`-frozen flag.

---

### LOW Sensitive Workflow Paths Written to Log at INFO Level

- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, `LOG_APP_INFO` call at the end of the copy loop (~line 188 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The full resolved source and target paths, including any path components derived from template expansion of user-supplied values, are emitted at INFO level unconditionally. If those paths encode secrets (e.g., a file named after an API key, a path under a secrets-mounted directory), they are written to application logs in plaintext.
- **Impact:** Log aggregation systems, log forwarders, or any party with log read access can harvest filesystem path information that aids further attacks or directly exposes secret material embedded in filenames.
- **Fix:** Emit path information at DEBUG level, not INFO, or strip any path components that originate from secret-resolution before logging. At minimum, log only the task ID and a success/failure indicator at INFO, and move full path details to DEBUG.


---

### 95_doc_application_workflow_taskFreshnessChecker_h

<a id="95-doc-application-workflow-taskfreshnesschecker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/95_doc_application_workflow_taskFreshnessChecker_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Path information disclosure through debug logging
- **Location:** `TaskFreshnessChecker::IsTaskUpToDate` and `TaskFreshnessChecker::CollectUpstreamOutputTimes`, all `LOG_APP_INFO` call sites throughout both functions
- **Issue:** Every freshness check unconditionally emits absolute filesystem paths, relative paths, task IDs, and file-existence results to the application log at INFO level. The log entries include both the raw (pre-normalization) path and the normalized absolute path, making it straightforward to reconstruct the full directory layout of the workflow and its inputs/outputs.
- **Impact:** If log files are accessible to lower-privileged users, exposed via a log-aggregation service, or included in bug reports, an attacker gains a detailed map of the host filesystem layout, workflow structure, and task dependency graph. This materially assists path-traversal or local privilege-escalation attempts elsewhere in the application.
- **Fix:** Gate these log statements behind a compile-time or runtime debug/verbose flag rather than always-on INFO level. At minimum, avoid logging absolute resolved paths in production builds; log only the task ID and a boolean outcome.

### LOW TOCTOU between `fs::exists` and `fs::last_write_time`
- **Location:** `TaskFreshnessChecker::IsTaskUpToDate` (~lines 110–120 for inputs, ~lines 165–178 for outputs) and `TaskFreshnessChecker::CollectUpstreamOutputTimes` (~lines 250–265)
- **Issue:** Each path is checked with `fs::exists()` and then immediately queried with `fs::last_write_time()` as two separate filesystem operations with no locking between them. A racing process could delete or replace a file between the two calls.
- **Impact:** In a workflow engine that processes untrusted or externally-supplied file paths, a local attacker who can create and delete files in watched directories could manipulate the freshness decision: causing a stale task to appear up-to-date (by deleting an output between the exist-check and the stat), or causing a spurious rebuild to be skipped. The risk is low in typical deployments but is a real TOCTOU window.
- **Fix:** Use a single `fs::last_write_time()` call directly and inspect the returned `std::error_code` for `std::errc::no_such_file_or_directory` to detect absence, eliminating the separate `exists()` call and closing the TOCTOU window.

### LOW Unbounded recursion / stack exhaustion via cyclic or deeply-nested dependency graphs
- **Location:** `TaskFreshnessChecker::CollectUpstreamOutputTimes`, recursive call site (~line 235)
- **Issue:** The `visitedTasks` set prevents revisiting a task node, so true cycles are handled, but the set is only passed by reference from `IsTaskUpToDate` and is not pre-seeded with the root task ID. If workflow validation is skipped (as the comment on line ~225 acknowledges), a graph with a very large but acyclic fan-in (hundreds of chained dependencies) will cause deeply nested recursive calls. There is no depth limit.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow definition with a deep linear dependency chain (e.g., task A → task B → … → task N) will exhaust the call stack, producing a crash (denial of service). Because the workflow definition is read from external input (files, API), this is reachable by an unprivileged user who can submit a workflow.
- **Fix:** Convert the recursion to an explicit stack (iterative DFS) or enforce a maximum recursion depth counter passed through the call, returning an error when exceeded.

### NONE (for all other focus areas)
No issues were identified in the areas of injection, authentication/authorization, cryptography, memory safety (the code uses value types and standard library containers throughout), insecure deserialization, SSRF, or TLS configuration, as none of those capabilities are exercised in this file.


---

### 96_doc_application_workflow_taskPathResolver_h

<a id="96-doc-application-workflow-taskpathresolver-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/96_doc_application_workflow_taskPathResolver_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path traversal — `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `fs::weakly_canonical`, which does not require the path to exist and does not resolve real symlinks

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, implementation file, the two `weakly_canonical` calls (~line 10–18 of the implementation)
- **Issue:** `fs::weakly_canonical` resolves only the portion of the path that exists on disk and lexically normalises the rest. It does not follow symlinks for non-existent components, and critically it does not detect a symlink *that does exist* pointing outside the tree. If an attacker can create a symlink inside the project root that points to an external path (e.g. `/etc/passwd` or `../../../secrets`), `weakly_canonical` will return the symlink's own path (still inside the root), the `lexically_relative` prefix check will pass, and the returned path will still resolve to the external target when handed to `open`/`fstream`/etc. The function's own doc comment claims it handles "symlink pointing out of tree" but the implementation does not.
- **Impact:** Path traversal through a planted symlink; depending on caller behaviour (read, write, delete), an attacker who can create a symlink inside the project root can read or overwrite arbitrary files accessible to the process.
- **Fix:** Replace `weakly_canonical` with `fs::canonical` (which requires the full path to exist and fully resolves all symlink hops). Re-check after resolution:
  ```
  fs::path const resolved = fs::canonical(candidate, ec);
  if (ec || resolved.empty()) return {};
  // then perform the lexically_relative check as now
  ```
  For paths that may not yet exist (e.g. planned output files), canonicalise the deepest existing ancestor, verify it is inside the root, and then re-append the remaining lexically-normalised suffix only after confirming no `..` components remain.

---

### MEDIUM `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` prefix check is bypassable for paths that are proper prefixes of `..`

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot`, `relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0` check (~line 20 of the implementation)
- **Issue:** The escape check is `relStr == ".."` OR `relStr.rfind("..", 0) == 0`. The second condition catches strings that *begin with* `..` but does so on the raw string representation. On Windows with backslash separators, or with unusual path encodings, a relative string like `..X/foo` would pass the `rfind` check. More concretely: the check should be done component-by-component on the `fs::path` object rather than with string prefix matching, which is locale- and separator-sensitive.
- **Impact:** On non-POSIX platforms or with unusual filenames, an attacker-supplied path could escape the root check.
- **Fix:** Iterate the `rel` path object's components and reject if the first component equals `fs::path("..")`:
  ```cpp
  if (!rel.empty() && *rel.begin() == fs::path("..")) return {};
  ```
  This is separator-agnostic and works correctly on all platforms.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded path data from untrusted workflow fields written to info-level logs

- **Location:** `ResolveTaskWorkingDirectoryPath` (~lines 15, 25), `ResolveTaskScopedPath` (~lines 5, 14), `ResolvePath` (multiple `LOG_APP_INFO` calls), `ResolveFreshnessPathsForTask` (multiple `LOG_APP_INFO` calls) — all in the `.cpp` implementation file
- **Issue:** Every path string derived from `WorkflowDefinition` fields (e.g. `m_WorkflowBaseDirectoryAbsolute`, `m_WorkflowFilePath`, `m_WorkingDirectory`, `m_FileInputs`, `m_FileOutputs`) and from template-expanded task inputs/outputs is written verbatim to `LOG_APP_INFO`. These fields are attacker-controlled if workflows are loaded from external or user-supplied sources. Depending on the log sink (file, syslog, network collector), this enables log injection (inserting newlines or format tokens) and information disclosure of internal filesystem layout.
- **Impact:** Log injection to forge log entries; disclosure of absolute path structure to an attacker who can read logs; potential fmt-string issues if the logging macro does not safely format its arguments (cannot confirm without seeing the macro definition).
- **Fix:** (1) Sanitise logged path strings by stripping or escaping newline/carriage-return characters before logging. (2) Ensure the logging macro always uses the path as a *data* argument (i.e. `"{}"` format slot), never interpolated directly into the format string — this appears to be the case here, but must be confirmed in the macro definition. (3) Evaluate whether `[paths debug]` info-level logging should be gated behind a debug/verbose flag disabled in production builds to reduce attack surface.

---

### LOW `ResolvePath` uses `std::filesystem::absolute` without an explicit base, relying on the process CWD

- **Location:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath`, multiple `fs::absolute(path)` and `fs::absolute(baseDirectoryPath / path)` calls (`.cpp` implementation)
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::absolute` with a single argument uses the OS-level process current working directory (`getcwd`). If the process CWD is changed by any thread between launch and the call (e.g. by an embedded Python script via `os.chdir`, or by a concurrent task), the resolved absolute path will silently differ from the intended one. The code already has a `GetLaunchCWDAbsolute()` facility to avoid this, but `fs::absolute` calls do not use it.
- **Impact:** Under a race or after an `os.chdir`, path resolution silently produces a wrong absolute path; if that path is then used for a security-sensitive operation (delete, write), it may target an unintended location.
- **Fix:** Replace all bare `fs::absolute(x)` calls in `ResolvePath` with explicit-base variants: `fs::absolute(x, launchCwd)` where `launchCwd = Core::g_Core->GetLaunchCWDAbsolute()`, falling back to `fs::current_path()` only if `Core::g_Core` is null.

---

### LOW `BuildOutputSlotMap` falls back to reading `m_InputValues` for undeclared output names with a logic error that makes the guard ineffective

- **Location:** `TaskPathResolver::BuildOutputSlotMap`, the `outputSlotMapOut.contains(outputName)` guard (~line 10–15 of that function)
- **Issue:** The guard `if (outputSlotMapOut.contains(outputName)) continue;` is intended to prevent overwriting an already-found output value with the `m_InputValues` fallback. However, at this point in the loop, `outputName` was *not* found in `m_OutputValues` (otherwise the `continue` on the prior branch would have fired), so `outputSlotMapOut` cannot contain `outputName` from a prior iteration unless a previous `taskDefinition.m_Outputs` entry had the same name — which would be a duplicate in the definition. The guard is therefore a no-op in the normal case. More importantly, an attacker who controls `m_InputValues` can populate the output slot map with arbitrary values for any declared output name that happens not to have a real output value, potentially influencing downstream dataflow template expansion (including the path resolution in `ResolveFreshnessPathsForTask`).
- **Impact:** Attacker-controlled input values bleed into the output slot map, potentially influencing path template expansion and downstream freshness decisions.
- **Fix:** Document and enforce that the `m_InputValues` fallback is only acceptable for a known, explicitly allow-listed set of legacy task types. If the set is empty, remove the fallback entirely. If retained, validate the value against the declared output field's type/format constraints before inserting it into `outputSlotMapOut`.


---

### 97_doc_application_workflow_templateEngine_h

<a id="97-doc-application-workflow-templateengine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/97_doc_application_workflow_templateEngine_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Shell Injection via Unvalidated Template Expansion

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, all `replacement = ...` assignment sites (lines ~130, ~150, ~170, ~195, ~215, ~235)
- **Issue:** Values drawn from `m_FileInputs`, `m_FileOutputs`, `m_InputValues`, and `m_EnvVariables` are inserted verbatim into the expanded string with zero sanitization. The engine comment explicitly states it is used for "shell tasks" in Strict mode. If the caller passes the expanded result to `system()`, `popen()`, `execve()` with a shell, or any command-building routine, attacker-controlled values containing shell metacharacters (`;`, `|`, `$()`, backticks, `\n`, etc.) will be interpreted by the shell.
- **Impact:** Remote or local command injection depending on how inputs are sourced (webhooks, AI responses, user-uploaded filenames, environment variables from untrusted config). An attacker who controls any mapped value can execute arbitrary OS commands under the process's UID.
- **Fix:** At expansion time, apply context-appropriate escaping before inserting values that will end up in shell contexts. For POSIX shells, shell-quote each token (wrap in single quotes, escape embedded single quotes). Better: refactor callers that build shell commands to use `execve`-style argument arrays instead of a single expanded string, eliminating the shell entirely.

---

### HIGH Path Traversal via File Path Placeholders

- **Location:** `JoinFileList` (~line 75), `ExpandTemplate` input/output index branches (~lines 130, 150)
- **Issue:** File paths from `m_FileInputs` and `m_FileOutputs` are joined and expanded without any canonicalization or containment check. A value such as `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path like `/etc/shadow` passes through unchanged.
- **Impact:** If the expanded template drives file read, write, or execution operations, an attacker who can influence the file lists can read or overwrite arbitrary files accessible to the process.
- **Fix:** Before accepting any file path into `TemplateContext`, validate that it resolves (via `realpath`/`std::filesystem::canonical`) to a path within the expected working directory or a declared sandbox root. Reject paths that escape the boundary.

---

### HIGH Secrets Leakage via `{{env.NAME}}` Expansion into Logs or Responses

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, `env.` branch (~line 195)
- **Issue:** Any environment variable, including `API_KEY`, `DB_PASSWORD`, `OPENAI_API_KEY`, etc., can be referenced by name in a template and will be emitted verbatim into the expanded output. If that output is logged, returned in an HTTP response, or written to a world-readable file, the secret is disclosed. There is no allowlist of safe variable names.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence the template text (e.g., through a stored workflow definition, a webhook payload, or a user-controlled prompt) can exfiltrate any environment secret the process has access to.
- **Fix:** Maintain an explicit allowlist of environment variable names permitted for expansion, configured at startup. Reject or silently elide any `{{env.NAME}}` reference not on the allowlist.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Allocation / DoS via Unbounded Template Output

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, `expandedOut +=` calls throughout (~lines 110, 115, 240)
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the size of `templateText`, the number of placeholders, or the size of any individual replacement value. A template with thousands of `{{inputs}}` placeholders combined with a large file list, or a single replacement value of hundreds of megabytes, will cause unbounded heap growth with no error path.
- **Impact:** A remote attacker who can submit or influence workflow templates or input values can trigger OOM conditions, crashing the process or degrading service for all users.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum length on `templateText` at entry. Enforce a maximum cumulative length on `expandedOut` (e.g., reject or truncate once it exceeds a configurable cap such as 64 MB). Enforce per-value size limits when populating `TemplateContext`.

---

### MEDIUM Template Injection / Recursive Expansion Not Guarded

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, all replacement branches (~lines 130–240)
- **Issue:** Replacement values are inserted directly into `expandedOut` but the engine iterates over the *original* `templateText`, not the output. However, this also means there is no guard preventing a replacement value from itself containing `{{...}}` syntax. If any caller ever re-feeds `expandedOut` into `ExpandTemplate` (a natural pattern for chained templates), attacker-controlled values will be interpreted as template expressions in the second pass, enabling injection of arbitrary `{{env.NAME}}` or `{{slot.NAME}}` lookups.
- **Impact:** Privilege escalation within the template context: an attacker controlling one low-privilege input value can inject expressions that read other, higher-privilege values (secrets, file paths) on a second expansion pass.
- **Fix:** Document explicitly that expansion output must never be re-expanded. Alternatively, mark expanded regions as inert (escape `{{` to a sentinel before expanding user-controlled data, or strip `{{`/`}}` from all replacement values before insertion).

---

### LOW Error Messages Reflect Attacker-Controlled Input

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, all `errorMessage = ... + indexStr/slotName/inputName/key + ...` assignments (~lines 135, 140, 158, 163, 210, 225, 245)
- **Issue:** Attacker-supplied template keys and index strings are concatenated directly into `errorMessage` without escaping. If this message is serialized to JSON, written to an HTML page, or logged through a structured logger that interprets format strings, injection into those output channels is possible.
- **Impact:** Depending on the downstream consumer: log injection (forging log lines), reflected XSS if rendered in a web UI, or JSON structure breakage if embedded unescaped in a JSON response.
- **Fix:** Escape or quote attacker-controlled substrings before including them in error messages. At minimum, strip or escape newline characters (`\r`, `\n`) to prevent log injection.


---

### 98_doc_application_workflow_triggerEngine_h

<a id="98-doc-application-workflow-triggerengine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/98_doc_application_workflow_triggerEngine_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Open webhook trigger accepted at registration (empty secret permitted)

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::AddWebhookTrigger`, `.cpp` ~line where `SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret(secret)` is called and the instance is pushed with `webhookTriggerInstance.m_Secret = secret`
- **Issue:** The header comment for `AddWebhookTrigger` states "Mandatory — the validator rejects webhook triggers with an empty secret," but the implementation performs no such check. An empty `secret` string skips the `SecretRedactor` call (correct) yet still creates and stores a `WebhookTriggerInstance` with `m_Secret = ""`. Whether the request handler truly enforces the signature in all code paths when `m_Secret` is empty is not visible here, but the engine itself will hand out a `WebhookTriggerInstance` with an empty secret to any caller of `GetWebhookTrigger`, making it trivial for a caller to conclude "no secret, skip HMAC check" rather than "reject this trigger entirely."
- **Impact:** An operator who accidentally omits the `secret` field in a JCWF file gets a live, publicly triggerable webhook with no authentication. Any HTTP client that knows (or guesses) the workflow ID can trigger arbitrary workflow execution.
- **Fix:** Add an explicit guard at the top of `AddWebhookTrigger`: if `secret.empty()`, log an error and `return` without registering the trigger. The comment already documents this contract; enforce it in code.

---

### HIGH: TOCTOU race between `stillActive` check and `eventsToFire.push_back` for email triggers

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::Tick`, email-poll section, the `if (stillActive)` block (~last 20 lines of the email loop)
- **Issue:** The code acquires `m_Mutex`, calls `findEmailTriggerIndexLocked`, releases the lock, and then—outside the lock—pushes to `eventsToFire`. Between the lock release and `FireTrigger` being called at the bottom of `Tick`, `ClearWorkflowTriggers` or `ClearAll` can remove the trigger. This is documented as intentional for the watermark update, but the `stillActive` guard is also dropped before the fire is actually dispatched: the lock is released after setting `stillActive = true`, then `eventsToFire.push_back` happens unlocked, then `FireTrigger` is called completely outside the lock. The trigger (and its backing workflow) may no longer exist when the callback fires.
- **Impact:** The callback (`m_TriggerCallback`) is invoked with a `workflowId` that has been deregistered. Depending on how the Orchestrator's callback handles a missing workflow, this could cause a use-after-free on workflow state, a spurious workflow run of a deleted workflow, or at minimum undefined behavior in the callback.
- **Fix:** Either (a) move the `eventsToFire.push_back` inside the same lock scope as the `stillActive` check so they are atomic, or (b) ensure `FireTrigger`/the callback is safe to call with a stale workflow ID (i.e., the callback itself re-validates existence under its own lock). Option (a) is simpler and consistent with how cron/S3/etc. events are collected under the primary lock.

---

### HIGH: `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` uses `fs::current_path()` as the trust boundary — TOCTOU and CWD manipulation

- **Location:** `ConfineUnderProjectRoot(fs::path const&)`, `pathConfinement.cpp`, the `fs::weakly_canonical(fs::current_path(ec), ec)` call
- **Issue:** The project root is re-evaluated from `fs::current_path()` on every call. Any thread or child process that calls `chdir()` (or equivalent) between two calls to `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` changes the root silently. Additionally, `fs::weakly_canonical` does not resolve symlinks for non-existent path components, so a path like `data/../../outside` where `data` is a symlink into the root may pass if the symlink is created after canonicalization of the `data` component. Specifically: the candidate is resolved with `weakly_canonical` which skips symlink resolution for missing path components — a symlink whose target changes between registration and event processing can point outside the tree.
- **Impact:** Path traversal: a controlled `chdir()` or a symlink swap can cause a watch-path or event-path to be accepted as in-tree when it resolves outside the project root, potentially allowing file-watch triggers to fire on sensitive paths, or (if the confinement is also used for write operations) to access files outside the project.
- **Fix:** (1) Capture and store the project root once at startup (e.g., in a `static` or passed-in `fs::path`) instead of calling `current_path()` on every invocation. (2) Use `fs::canonical` (not `weakly_canonical`) for the candidate resolution so that all symlinks are resolved and the actual target is checked against the root; fall through to rejection on `error_code` for non-existent paths rather than silently proceeding.

---

### MEDIUM: `IsPathMatch` does string-prefix comparison on already-canonicalized paths — partial-component match possible

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::IsPathMatch`, `.cpp`, the `eventPath.compare(0, watchedPath.size(), watchedPath)` branch
- **Issue:** Although both paths have been through `NormalizePath` → `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` → `.generic_string()`, the prefix check compares raw string bytes. On case-insensitive or Unicode-normalizing filesystems (macOS HFS+, Windows NTFS) the canonical form returned by `weakly_canonical` may differ in case or Unicode normalization from the string stored in `m_WatchedPath` for one side but not the other. More concretely: if `watchedPath = "/project/data"` and `eventPath = "/project/data2/file"`, the `compare(0, watchedPath.size(), watchedPath)` check passes (the prefix matches), and then `eventPath[watchedPath.size()] == '/'` is false because the next character is `'2'` — so this specific case is already caught. However, if a platform-specific canonicalization produces a trailing separator on `watchedPath`, the `watchedPath.back() == '/'` branch fires and accepts any path that starts with that string regardless of component boundaries. The `back() == '/'` branch combined with an operator-supplied trailing slash in the JCWF path (which survives `weakly_canonical` on some implementations) could match sibling directories.
- **Impact:** A file event from a sibling path (e.g., `data2/`) could match a trigger registered for `data/`, causing unintended trigger firing.
- **Fix:** After the prefix check, always verify the boundary character is `/` (already done in the non-slash branch) but also strip any trailing `/` from `watchedPath` before storing/comparing. Alternatively, split both paths into `fs::path` components and compare component-by-component rather than as raw strings.

---

### MEDIUM: `CronExpression::Parse` logs the raw operator-supplied expression including potentially sensitive workflow identifiers

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::CronExpression::Parse`, `.cpp`, `LOG_APP_ERROR("CronExpression::Parse: expected 5 fields, got {} in '{}'", tokens.size(), expression)`; and the second error log `LOG_APP_ERROR("CronExpression::Parse: invalid field in expression '{}'", expression)`; and `AddCronTrigger` logs `expression` directly
- **Issue:** The cron expression is operator-supplied from the JCWF JSON. While a bare cron string is not itself secret, the pattern is inconsistent with the project's declared discipline of logging only shape, not value, for operator-supplied fields. If a future workflow definition embeds a secret in the expression string by mistake (unlikely but possible), it will appear verbatim in the log. More practically: this diverges from `SecretRedactor` integration for the webhook secret and creates an inconsistent policy that future maintainers may follow incorrectly for fields that are sensitive.
- **Impact:** Low direct impact; establishes a precedent for logging raw operator values without redaction, increasing risk of accidental secret leakage in adjacent code.
- **Fix:** Continue logging the expression for parse errors (it is useful for diagnosing JCWF mistakes), but register all `Add*Trigger` string parameters through `SecretRedactor` if they could ever contain credentials, or document explicitly which trigger fields are non-sensitive and which must be redacted before logging.

---

### MEDIUM: `SubjectFilter` logged verbatim; could leak sensitive filter patterns or IMAP credentials embedded by mistake

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::AddEmailWatchTrigger`, `.cpp`, `LOG_APP_INFO(... subjectFilter.empty() ? "<all>" : subjectFilter ...)`; and `EmailPollJob.m_SubjectFilter` passed to `EmailConnector::CheckForNewMail` and logged on poll-failure paths
- **Issue:** The `subjectFilter` is operator-supplied and passed into IMAP SEARCH commands. If an operator embeds a credential or PII value in a subject filter (e.g., to watch for a particular password-reset email subject), that value appears in logs. Additionally, an IMAP SEARCH injection is not ruled out: the `subjectFilter` is passed directly to `EmailConnector::CheckForNewMail` with no sanitization or escaping. If `CheckForNewMail` interpolates the filter into an IMAP `SEARCH SUBJECT "..."` command without escaping embedded quotes or special characters, an attacker who can write JCWF files can inject arbitrary IMAP commands.
- **Impact:** IMAP command injection could allow exfiltration of mail beyond the declared folder, or manipulation of the mailbox. Log exposure of filter values could leak PII.
- **Fix:** In `CheckForNewMail` (not visible here but flagged for review), validate and escape `subjectFilter` before incorporating it into IMAP protocol strings. In the log call, consider treating `subjectFilter` as potentially sensitive and either omitting its value or registering it with `SecretRedactor`.

---

### LOW: `AddWebhookTrigger` — `m_WebhookIndex` overwrites existing entry for duplicate `workflowId` without cleaning up the orphaned `m_WebhookTriggers` slot

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::AddWebhookTrigger`, `.cpp`, `m_WebhookIndex[workflowId] = index`
- **Issue:** If `AddWebhookTrigger` is called twice for the same `workflowId` (e.g., during a partial reload that doesn't call `ClearWorkflowTriggers` first), the second call appends a new `WebhookTriggerInstance` and overwrites the index map entry. The first entry remains in `m_WebhookTriggers` but is now unreachable via `GetWebhookTrigger`. Over many reloads this causes unbounded growth of `m_WebhookTriggers`. More importantly, if the first entry had a different secret, callers that obtained a raw pointer from a prior `GetWebhookTrigger` call (before the vector reallocated) now hold a dangling pointer.
- **Impact:** Dangling pointer dereference if any caller caches the `WebhookTriggerInstance*` across a registration cycle; vector unbounded growth (DoS over many reloads).
- **Fix:** Before appending, check whether `m_WebhookIndex` already contains `workflowId` and either reject the duplicate or replace the existing entry in-place. Document that callers must not store the returned `WebhookTriggerInstance*` across any call that could mutate `m_WebhookTriggers`.

---

### LOW: `NormalizePath` / `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` called without holding `m_Mutex` — concurrent CWD change window

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::AddFileWatchTrigger`, `.cpp`, `std::string const normalizedPath = NormalizePath(path)` (called before the `std::scoped_lock`)
- **Issue:** `NormalizePath` is called outside the mutex. If `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` is fixed to snapshot the CWD once (per the HIGH finding above) this becomes moot; but in the current implementation, the CWD could change between the `NormalizePath` call and the subsequent storage of `normalizedPath` under the lock. The stored path was computed against a root that no longer matches the one that will be used on the next `NormalizePath` call (for the event path).
- **Impact:** In an adversarial environment where CWD is manipulated, a trigger could be registered under one root and events matched against another, bypassing the containment check.
- **Fix:** Fix the CWD snapshot issue in `ConfineUnderProjectRoot` first (HIGH finding). Once the root is stable, the lock placement for `NormalizePath` is not a problem.


---

### 99_doc_application_workflow_workflowFileIndex_h

<a id="99-doc-application-workflow-workflowfileindex-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/99_doc_application_workflow_workflowFileIndex_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Path traversal containment relies solely on lexical normalization, not on a canonical filesystem check

- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::FindByRelativePath`, approximately lines 100–125 of the .cpp
- **Issue:** The traversal guard calls `lexically_normal()` and then `lexically_relative()` entirely in memory, without ever calling `std::filesystem::canonical()` or `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical()`. On filesystems that support symlinks, an attacker-controlled `relativePath` that resolves lexically to a path inside `m_RootDirectory` (e.g. `legit_subdir/../../../../etc/passwd` after a symlink inside the tree redirects the intermediate component) will pass the `relStr.rfind("..", 0)` check and reach the `exists()` call with a path that is physically outside the root. The `exists()` call at the end confirms the path is reachable, making this an oracle at minimum.
- **Impact:** A hostile workflow file (JCWF) that places a symlink inside the scanned tree could cause `FindByRelativePath` to confirm the existence of — and return the absolute path of — arbitrary files on the host, leaking structural information and enabling follow-on attacks if callers read the returned path.
- **Fix:** Replace `lexically_normal()` with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical()` (which resolves symlinks) on both `candidateRaw` and `m_RootDirectory` before the containment check. Compare with the canonical root obtained once during `ScanDirectory`. Reject any resolved path that does not have the canonical root as a prefix. Example: `auto canonical = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(candidateRaw, ec); if (ec || canonical.string().rfind(m_CanonicalRoot.string(), 0) != 0) { /* reject */ }`.

---

### LOW Uncontrolled allocation via unbounded directory scan

- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::ScanDirectory`, approximately lines 60–95 of the .cpp
- **Issue:** The recursive directory iterator is given no upper bound on the number of files or total memory consumed. Every regular non-hidden file found anywhere under `rootDir` is unconditionally pushed into `m_AllFiles` and `m_BasenameIndex`. If `rootDir` is user-influenced or is a symlinked mount point with millions of entries, the vectors grow without limit until OOM or until the process is killed.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence the root directory path or who can create many files inside the scanned directory can exhaust process memory or cause a denial of service.
- **Fix:** Introduce a configurable or hardcoded maximum file count (e.g. 100 000). After each `push_back`, check the current size and log an error and break (or return early) when the limit is exceeded.

---

### LOW TOCTOU between `exists()` check and caller use of returned path

- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::FindByRelativePath`, approximately line 122 of the .cpp (`std::filesystem::exists(candidateRaw, ec)`)
- **Issue:** The function returns a path after confirming it exists at query time, but callers will open or read the file later without holding the mutex. Between the `exists()` call and the caller's open, the file can be replaced by a different file or symlink. This is a classic TOCTOU race.
- **Impact:** If callers read the returned path and act on its contents (e.g. inject them into AI prompts or execute them), an attacker who can write to the workflow directory can swap the file after the check, causing the wrong content to be processed.
- **Fix:** Callers should open the file immediately and validate its identity (e.g. compare inode/device number via `fstat` after `open`) rather than trusting a path returned from an earlier check. Alternatively, document clearly that `FindByRelativePath` provides no freshness guarantee and that callers must handle missing/changed files gracefully.

---

### LOW `SerializeMarkdownListing` output injected into AI prompts without sanitization

- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::SerializeMarkdownListing`, approximately lines 135–155 of the .cpp
- **Issue:** File paths from the filesystem are concatenated directly into a Markdown string intended for injection into AI generation prompts (per the comment "Suitable for injecting into AI generation prompts"). A file or directory with a crafted name (e.g. containing backtick sequences, prompt-injection strings, or newline-embedded instructions) will appear verbatim in the prompt. The function does not escape or sanitize path components.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can create files with specially crafted names inside the workflow directory could inject content into AI prompts, potentially manipulating model behavior or exfiltrating context through the model's output.
- **Fix:** Before appending `relPath.generic_string()` to `result`, sanitize or reject path components that contain characters outside a safe allowlist (alphanumerics, `/`, `-`, `_`, `.`). At minimum, strip or escape backticks, angle brackets, and newline characters within individual path components.


---
